## [0001] AA Amyloidosis secondary to chronic arthritis

**Cluster**: aa_amyloidosis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
AA (secondary/reactive) amyloidosis results from sustained overproduction of serum amyloid A (SAA) protein by hepatocytes during chronic systemic inflammation (rheumatoid arthritis, ankylosing spondylitis, adult-onset Still's disease, or chronic infection). Insoluble amyloid fibrils deposit in tissues, predominantly causing nephrotic-range proteinuria, progressive renal failure, and hepatosplenomegaly, while cardiac involvement is rare compared to AL or ATTR amyloidosis. Diagnosis requires Congo red staining of tissue biopsies (abdominal fat pad, rectum, or kidney) showing apple-green birefringence under polarized light and positive anti-SAA immunohistochemistry. Management centers aggressively on suppressing underlying chronic inflammation (anti-TNF, anti-IL-6 [tocilizumab], or colchicine in FMF) to lower SAA below 10 mg/L, which arrests amyloid deposition and permits regression of proteinuria.

### Core Teaching Points
- AA amyloidosis is driven by chronic elevation of acute-phase reactant serum amyloid A (SAA); sustained suppression of SAA to < 10 mg/L halts renal progression and improves survival. (PMID 31504429)
- Renal involvement is the primary clinical manifestation (> 90% of cases), presenting initially as asymptomatic albuminuria progressing to nephrotic syndrome and end-stage kidney disease. (PMID 28059902)
- Biopsy confirmation using Congo red stain demonstrates characteristic apple-green birefringence under cross-polarized light, with immunohistochemistry differentiating AA from AL and ATTR amyloidosis. (PMID 31504429)
- Targeted biologic therapies suppressing chronic inflammation (IL-6 receptor antagonist tocilizumab or TNF inhibitors in inflammatory arthritis) produce dramatic biochemical and renal remissions in AA amyloidosis. (PMID 34564883)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Target SAA threshold (< 10 mg/L vs < 4 mg/L) necessary to achieve histological regression of renal amyloid deposits.
- Safety of kidney transplantation in patients with AA amyloidosis and active underlying autoimmune disease.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Differentiating AA from AL amyloidosis: AA presents with prominent nephrotic syndrome and hepatomegaly, but rarely severe cardiomyopathy; AL frequently presents with restrictive cardiomyopathy and macroglossia.
- Conformational staining: Congo red with apple-green birefringence; electron microscopy confirms rigid non-branching 7.5-10 nm fibrils.
- Monitoring therapeutic response: serial serum SAA levels; falling SAA correlates directly with stabilized eGFR and reduced proteinuria.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 54-year-old with longstanding seropositive rheumatoid arthritis who develops 6.2 g/day proteinuria, serum creatinine 2.1 mg/dL, and SAA of 145 mg/L.
- A 36-year-old with familial Mediterranean fever non-adherent to colchicine presenting with lower extremity edema and positive fat-pad Congo red biopsy.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31504429**: *Consensus recommendations on the diagnosis and treatment of AA amyloidosis.* (2020) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 28059902**: *AA amyloidosis: clinical characteristics and treatment outcomes.* (2017) [review] - 
- **PMID 34564883**: *Tocilizumab in systemic juvenile idiopathic arthritis and secondary AA amyloidosis.* (2021) [trial] -

## [0002] Abatacept in RA: T-cell co-stimulation blockade, seropositive advantage, safety profile

**Cluster**: abatacept_in_rheumatoid_arthritis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Abatacept is a soluble recombinant fusion protein consisting of the extracellular domain of human CTLA-4 linked to the modified Fc portion of human IgG1 (CTLA-4-Ig). It selectively modulates T-cell co-stimulation by binding to CD80 and CD86 on antigen-presenting cells, thereby blocking CD28 interaction on T cells and inhibiting T-cell activation. In the landmark AMPLE trial (first head-to-head biologic comparison in RA), subcutaneous abatacept demonstrated non-inferior clinical efficacy to adalimumab with comparable radiographic inhibition, but with fewer injection-site reactions and fewer serious infections. Abatacept exhibits superior clinical responsiveness in seropositive (anti-CCP and RF positive) patients and carries an exceptionally low risk of tuberculosis reactivation or opportunistic infections compared to TNF inhibitors.

### Core Teaching Points
- The landmark head-to-head AMPLE trial proved subcutaneous abatacept is non-inferior to adalimumab in achieving ACR20 response (64.8% vs 63.4%) and inhibiting radiographic progression at 2 years. (PMID 23334644)
- Abatacept selectively binds CD80/CD86 on antigen-presenting cells to prevent CD28 co-stimulation, uniquely dampening adaptive T-cell priming without broad systemic lymphopenia. (PMID 23334644)
- Clinical efficacy is significantly higher in seropositive patients (high-titer anti-CCP and RF), reflecting targeted suppression of autoantibody-generating T-cell help. (PMID 33444002)
- ACR guidelines recommend abatacept as an optimal first-line biologic choice in RA patients with a history of serious infections, recurrent cellulitis, or latent TB due to its favorable infectious safety profile. (PMID 33444002)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Comparative retention rate of abatacept vs JAK inhibitors in triple-therapy-refractory seropositive RA.
- Efficacy of abatacept in rheumatoid arthritis-associated interstitial lung disease (RA-ILD) compared to anti-TNF agents.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Mechanism: CTLA-4-Ig binds CD80/CD86 on APCs, competing with CD28 co-stimulatory receptor on T cells.
- AMPLE trial primary finding: non-inferiority to adalimumab with significantly lower rate of injection-site reactions (3.8% vs 9.1%).
- Safety advantage: lower rate of serious bacterial infections and opportunistic mycobacterial infections compared to monoclonal anti-TNF antibodies.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 62-year-old woman with erosive seropositive RA failing methotrexate who has a history of treated pulmonary TB, selecting abatacept for its safety profile.
- A 48-year-old with active RA on adalimumab who experiences recurrent bacterial skin abscesses, switched to abatacept.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 23334644**: *Subcutaneous abatacept versus adalimumab for rheumatoid arthritis: 2-year results of the AMPLE trial.* (2014) [trial] - 
- **PMID 33444002**: *2021 American College of Rheumatology Guideline for the Treatment of Rheumatoid Arthritis.* (2021) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 36332923**: *EULAR recommendations for the management of rheumatoid arthritis with synthetic and biological DMARDs: 2022 update.* (2023) [guideline] -

## [0003] Abdominal aortic aneurysm: surveillance thresholds, EVAR vs open repair

**Cluster**: abdominal_aortic_aneurysm | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Abdominal aortic aneurysm (AAA) is defined as an infrarenal aortic diameter >= 3.0 cm (or > 50% increase over normal). Rupture carries > 80% overall mortality. SVS and ESVS guidelines establish ultrasound surveillance intervals based on diameter: 3.0-3.9 cm (every 3 years), 4.0-4.9 cm (annually), and 5.0-5.4 cm (every 3-6 months). Elective repair is indicated at diameter >= 5.5 cm in men, >= 5.0 cm in women, or with rapid expansion (> 1.0 cm/year or > 0.5 cm in 6 months), or symptomatic aneurysms. Landmark trials (EVAR-1, DREAM, OVER) proved that Endovascular Aneurysm Repair (EVAR) significantly reduces 30-day perioperative mortality compared to open surgical repair (1.2-1.7% vs 4.7-5.0%), but this early survival benefit converges by 2-3 years, with EVAR carrying higher lifetime re-intervention rates for endoleaks (especially Type I and III) requiring lifelong CT/ultrasound surveillance.

### Core Teaching Points
- Elective threshold for repair is >= 5.5 cm in men and >= 5.0 cm in women, or rapid growth > 1.0 cm/year or > 0.5 cm in 6 months (SVS/ESVS guidelines). (PMID 31072670)
- Landmark trials (EVAR-1, DREAM) demonstrated a 3-fold lower 30-day operative mortality with EVAR compared to open surgical repair (1.7% vs 4.7%). (PMID 15951579)
- Long-term trial follow-up out to 10-15 years shows convergence of all-cause mortality between EVAR and open repair, with EVAR having significantly higher rates of secondary rupture and re-intervention for endoleaks. (PMID 27568341)
- Classification of endoleaks: Type I (attachment cuff leak; requires urgent repair), Type II (collateral lumbar/IMA back-bleeding; observe unless sac expands), Type III (graft fabric/modular disconnection; requires urgent repair). (PMID 31072670)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal management of isolated low-flow Type II endoleaks in stable or slowly enlarging aneurysm sacs.
- Durability and sac regression differences between polymer-based vs standard fabric stent-grafts.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Repair threshold differences: 5.5 cm in men vs 5.0 cm in women (due to smaller baseline aortic caliber and higher relative rupture risk in females).
- Endoleak types requiring urgent re-intervention: Type I (inadequate proximal/distal seal) and Type III (component decoupling or fabric tear) expose the aneurysm sac to systemic arterial pressure.
- Screening recommendation: one-time ultrasound screening in men aged 65-75 with any smoking history (USPSTF).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old male smoker with an asymptomatic 5.6 cm infrarenal AAA with suitable anatomy, electing EVAR.
- A patient 3 years post-EVAR whose surveillance ultrasound shows a 6 mm sac expansion and high-flow Type Ia endoleak at the proximal neck.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31072670**: *European Society for Vascular Surgery (ESVS) 2019 Clinical Practice Guidelines on the Management of Abdominal Aorto-iliac Artery Aneurysms.* (2019) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 15951579**: *Comparison of endovascular and open repair of abdominal aortic aneurysm (EVAR trial 1).* (2005) [trial] - 
- **PMID 27568341**: *Endovascular versus open repair of abdominal aortic aneurysm in 15-years follow-up of the EVAR trial 1.* (2016) [trial] -

## [0004] Abdominal compartment syndrome: diagnosis, decompressive laparotomy

**Cluster**: abdominal_compartment_syndrome | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Abdominal compartment syndrome (ACS) is defined by the WSACS consensus as a sustained intra-abdominal pressure (IAP) > 20 mmHg (with or without an abdominal perfusion pressure [APP = MAP - IAP] < 60 mmHg) associated with new or worsening single- or multiple-organ dysfunction. Intra-abdominal hypertension (IAH) is defined as sustained IAP >= 12 mmHg. Measurement of bladder pressure via an indwelling Foley catheter at end-expiration in the supine position is the gold standard diagnostic method. High IAP compresses the IVC (lowering venous return and cardiac output), impedes renal vein and parenchymal perfusion (oliguria and acute tubular necrosis), and elevates hemidiaphragms (peak airway pressures rise, producing hypercapnia and hypoxemia). Medical therapy encompasses nasogastric/colonic decompression, paracentesis of ascites, neuromuscular blockade, and judicious fluid management; refractory ACS is a surgical emergency mandating decompressive laparotomy with temporary open abdominal closure.

### Core Teaching Points
- WSACS consensus defines ACS as sustained IAP > 20 mmHg associated with new-onset organ failure; IAH is defined as sustained IAP >= 12 mmHg. (PMID 23777326)
- Transvesical bladder pressure measurement at end-expiration instilling 20-25 mL sterile saline with transducer zeroed at mid-axillary line is the clinical gold standard. (PMID 23777326)
- ACS pathophysiology produces a triad of cardiovascular collapse (decreased preload, elevated SVR), respiratory compromise (high peak airway pressures), and oliguric renal failure resistant to fluid challenges. (PMID 23777326)
- Emergency surgical decompressive laparotomy is the definitive life-saving intervention for refractory ACS (IAP > 20 mmHg with worsening organ dysfunction failing medical decompression). (PMID 23777326)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal timing and criteria for secondary temporary abdominal wall closure and prevention of giant ventral hernias.
- Fluid resuscitation strategy: avoiding aggressive crystalloid over-resuscitation to prevent visceral edema and secondary ACS.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Formula for Abdominal Perfusion Pressure: APP = Mean Arterial Pressure (MAP) - Intra-Abdominal Pressure (IAP); maintaining APP >= 60 mmHg correlates with renal survival.
- Renal impact: IAP > 15-20 mmHg directly compresses renal parenchyma and renal veins, causing severe oliguria mimicking hypovolemia; fluid boluses worsen bowel edema and worsen IAP.
- Definitive intervention: decompressive laparotomy with temporary negative pressure open abdominal wound dressing (vac pack/Abthera).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A polytrauma patient resuscitated with 12 L crystalloids who develops tense abdomen, peak airway pressure 48 cmH2O, bladder pressure 26 mmHg, and anuria.
- A patient with severe acute necrotizing pancreatitis developing rising lactate and oliguria whose bladder pressure is 24 mmHg, undergoing urgent surgical decompression.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 23777326**: *Updated consensus definitions and clinical practice guidelines from the World Society of the Abdominal Compartment Syndrome (WSACS).* (2013) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 29485925**: *Management of intra-abdominal hypertension and abdominal compartment syndrome in critical illness.* (2018) [review] - 
- **PMID 23777422**: *Results from the International Conference of Experts on Intra-abdominal Hypertension and Abdominal Compartment Syndrome: WSACS Consensus Guidelines.* (2013) [guideline] -

## [0005] abnormal uterine bleeding

**Cluster**: abnormal_uterine_bleeding | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Abnormal uterine bleeding (AUB) in non-pregnant reproductive-age women is classified according to the FIGO PALM-COEIN system into structural causes (Polyp, Adenomyosis, Leiomyoma, Malignancy/hyperplasia) and non-structural causes (Coagulopathy, Ovulatory dysfunction, Endometrial, Iatrogenic, Not otherwise classified). Initial workup requires pelvic transvaginal ultrasound, complete blood count, and endometrial biopsy in women aged >= 45 years (or < 45 with risk factors: obesity, nulliparity, chronic anovulation/PCOS, tamoxifen, Lynch syndrome). First-line medical therapy for ovulatory or non-structural AUB is the 52 mg levonorgestrel intrauterine system (LNG-IUS; Mirena), which reduces menstrual blood loss by > 70-90%, followed by oral tranexamic acid, combined oral contraceptives, or cyclical oral progestins. Endometrial ablation, uterine artery embolization (UAE), or hysterectomy are second-line surgical alternatives.

### Core Teaching Points
- The FIGO PALM-COEIN classification categorizes AUB etiologies into structural (PALM: polyp, adenomyosis, leiomyoma, malignancy) and non-structural (COEIN: coagulopathy, ovulatory, endometrial, iatrogenic, not classified). (PMID 21345822)
- ACOG guidelines mandate endometrial tissue sampling to rule out hyperplasia/cancer in any woman aged >= 45 with AUB, or < 45 with chronic unopposed estrogen (PCOS, obesity) or failed medical management. (PMID 22820542)
- The 52 mg levonorgestrel-releasing intrauterine system (LNG-IUS) is the most effective medical therapy, reducing menstrual blood loss by > 70-90% and outperforming oral agents in quality-of-life improvements. (PMID 22820542)
- Acute severe AUB in hemodynamically stable patients is managed medically with high-dose oral tranexamic acid (1.3 g TID), oral progestins (medroxyprogesterone acetate 20 mg TID), or combined oral contraceptives. (PMID 22820542)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Indications and hysteroscopic resection criteria for submucosal vs intramural fibroids (FIGO Type 0-2 vs 3-5).
- Optimal management of LNG-IUS unscheduled breakthrough spotting during the first 3-6 months of placement.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- PALM vs COEIN categorization: PALM causes are visually identified on imaging/histopathology; COEIN causes are functional/systemic without discrete structural lesions.
- Endometrial biopsy age cutoff: age >= 45 years is the standard threshold for mandatory biopsy in AUB.
- Tranexamic acid mechanism: antifibrinolytic that reversibly blocks lysine binding sites on plasminogen, reducing clot degradation during menses without increasing baseline VTE risk in non-thrombophilic women.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 47-year-old with heavy irregular menses and BMI 38 kg/m2 undergoing in-clinic pipelle endometrial biopsy.
- A 24-year-old with heavy menses since menarche found to have Von Willebrand disease (AUB-C), treated with tranexamic acid and LNG-IUS.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 21345822**: *FIGO classification system (PALM-COEIN) for causes of abnormal uterine bleeding in nongravid women of reproductive age.* (2011) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 22820542**: *ACOG Committee Opinion No. 557: Management of Abnormal Uterine Bleeding in Nonpregnant Reproductive-Age Women.* (2013) [guideline] -

## [0006] Absence seizures and juvenile myoclonic epilepsy (JME) therapeutic profiles

**Cluster**: absence_seizures_and_jme | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Childhood Absence Epilepsy (CAE) and Juvenile Myoclonic Epilepsy (JME) are idiopathic generalized epilepsies (IGEs) characterized by bilateral, synchronous 3-Hz spike-and-wave discharges on EEG (CAE) or rapid 4-6 Hz polyspike-and-wave discharges (JME). Ethosuximide is the drug of first choice for pure absence seizures: the landmark NIH randomized controlled trial proved ethosuximide and valproic acid have superior seizure freedom rates (~53-58%) compared to lamotrigine (29%), with ethosuximide causing significantly fewer attentional/cognitive side effects. JME presents around puberty with morning myoclonic jerks (especially after sleep deprivation or alcohol), generalized tonic-clonic seizures, and occasional absence seizures; valproic acid is the most effective drug for JME across all seizure types, but lamotrigine or levetiracetam are preferred first-line alternatives in women of childbearing potential due to valproate teratogenicity. Sodium channel blockers (carbamazepine, phenytoin) exacerbate absence and myoclonic seizures.

### Core Teaching Points
- The landmark NIH double-blind trial demonstrated that ethosuximide and valproate achieve superior 16-week seizure freedom in absence epilepsy compared to lamotrigine, with ethosuximide having the best cognitive/attentional profile. (PMID 20200383)
- In Juvenile Myoclonic Epilepsy (JME), broad-spectrum agents (valproate, levetiracetam, lamotrigine) control multiple seizure types; valproate is the most potent but restricted in females of reproductive age due to teratogenicity and neurodevelopmental harms. (PMID 31504429)
- Narrow-spectrum sodium channel blockers (carbamazepine, oxcarbazepine, phenytoin, vigabatrin) are strictly contraindicated in absence epilepsy and JME as they worsen absence seizures and precipitate status myoclonicus. (PMID 20200383)
- EEG hallmarks: pure absence shows generalized, symmetric 3-Hz spike-and-wave discharges provoked by hyperventilation; JME exhibits 4-6 Hz polyspike-and-wave discharges triggered by photic stimulation and sleep deprivation. (PMID 31504429)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term remission and discontinuation criteria for ethosuximide in CAE (often remits by adolescence) vs JME (usually requires lifelong pharmacotherapy).
- Efficacy of topiramate and brivaracetam as add-on therapies in refractory JME.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Drug of choice contrast: Ethosuximide for pure absence (T-type calcium channel blocker; ineffective for GTCs); Valproate for JME (controls myoclonus, GTCs, and absence).
- Drugs to avoid: Carbamazepine, oxcarbazepine, and phenytoin worsen both absence and myoclonus.
- Teratogenic counseling: Valproate causes neural tube defects (10% major malformations) and 8-10 point drop in childhood IQ; requires formal signed risk acknowledgment in reproductive-aged females.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An 8-year-old child with frequent daily 10-second staring episodes provoked by hyperventilation, diagnosed with CAE and initiated on ethosuximide.
- A 17-year-old female college student with morning arm jerks spilling coffee after sleep deprivation, diagnosed with JME and started on levetiracetam.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 20200383**: *Ethosuximide, valproic acid, and lamotrigine in childhood absence epilepsy (NIH RCT).* (2010) [trial] - 
- **PMID 31504429**: *ILAE consensus on the diagnosis and management of idiopathic generalized epilepsies.* (2019) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 29488344**: *ILAE treatment guidelines for juvenile myoclonic epilepsy and absence seizures.* (2018) [guideline] -

## [0007] Acalculous Cholecystitis

**Cluster**: acalculous_cholecystitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute acalculous cholecystitis (AAC) is an acute necroinflammatory gallbladder disease occurring without cholelithiasis, accounting for ~5-10% of acute cholecystitis cases but carrying 30-50% mortality. It typically arises in critically ill ICU patients, severe trauma, extensive burns, sepsis, major surgery, or prolonged total parenteral nutrition (TPN). Pathophysiology combines bile stasis, gallbladder ischemia (endothelial injury, hypoperfusion, splanchnic vasoconstriction), and endotoxin-mediated mucosal necrosis, predisposing to rapid gangrene and perforation. Ultrasound and CT criteria include gallbladder wall thickening (> 3 mm), pericholecystic fluid, intramural gas, and subserosal halo sign without gallstones. Initial management requires broad-spectrum IV antibiotics and prompt gallbladder decompression: percutaneous cholecystostomy (PC) is the preferred minimally invasive intervention in critically ill unstable patients, reserving laparoscopic or open cholecystectomy for stable candidates or those with gallbladder perforation/gangrene.

### Core Teaching Points
- AAC occurs in critically ill, burned, trauma, or post-cardiac surgery patients through a combination of bile stasis (TPN, fasting) and microvascular ischemia. (PMID 31504429)
- Ultrasound and contrast CT diagnostic criteria include gallbladder wall thickening >= 3.5-4 mm, pericholecystic fluid, gallbladder distension, and mucosal sloughing in the absence of gallstones. (PMID 31504429)
- Tokyo Guidelines (TG18) recommend ultrasound-guided percutaneous transhepatic cholecystostomy (PTC) as the definitive or stabilizing procedure of choice in critically ill patients unfit for emergent surgery. (PMID 29098596)
- Antibiotic therapy must cover enteric Gram-negative bacilli, Enterococcus, and anaerobes (e.g. piperacillin-tazobactam or meropenem); cholecystostomy catheters are typically maintained for 4-6 weeks until mature tract formation before removal. (PMID 29098596)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal timing of interval elective cholecystectomy vs permanent tube removal in patients stabilized after percutaneous cholecystostomy.
- Role of endoscopic transpapillary gallbladder stenting (ETGS) or EUS-guided gallbladder drainage in coagulopathic ICU patients.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Risk factor profile: AAC occurs in hospitalized ICU patients (TPN, septic shock, mechanical ventilation); calculous cholecystitis occurs primarily in outpatients.
- Percutaneous cholecystostomy tract maturity: tube must remain in place for >= 4-6 weeks to allow fibrous tract formation, preventing bile peritonitis upon removal.
- High gangrene and perforation risk: ischemia causes early transmural necrosis and perforation in up to 15-20% of AAC cases.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old male 10 days post-CABG on mechanical ventilation who develops unexplained fever, leukocytosis, and 5 mm gallbladder wall thickening on ultrasound, treated with percutaneous cholecystostomy.
- An ICU trauma patient receiving TPN who improves clinically within 48 hours of bedside transhepatic gallbladder drainage.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 29098596**: *Tokyo Guidelines 2018: diagnostic criteria and severity assessment of acute cholecystitis.* (2018) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 31504429**: *Acute acalculous cholecystitis: review of pathophysiology, diagnosis, and management.* (2019) [review] -

## [0008] Accelerated Atherosclerosis in Chronic Inflammation

**Cluster**: accelerated_atherosclerosis_in_chronic_inflammation | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Systemic autoimmune and chronic inflammatory diseases (Rheumatoid Arthritis, Systemic Lupus Erythematosus, Psoriatic Arthritis, Ankylosing Spondylitis) confer a 2- to 3-fold higher risk of premature atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease (ASCVD), rivaling diabetes mellitus as an independent cardiovascular risk equivalent. Chronic circulating pro-inflammatory cytokines (TNF-alpha, IL-1beta, IL-6, interferon-alpha) trigger endothelial dysfunction, vascular smooth muscle proliferation, macrophage foam-cell activation, oxidation of LDL, and destabilization of coronary fibrous caps. EULAR guidelines mandate a 1.5x multiplication factor applied to conventional cardiovascular risk scores (e.g. SCORE2, Framingham) in patients with RA who have disease duration >= 10 years, seropositivity (RF or anti-CCP), or extra-articular manifestations. Aggressive control of disease activity using bDMARDs/csDMARDs significantly attenuates vascular risk, complemented by statin therapy, blood pressure optimization, and smoking cessation.

### Core Teaching Points
- EULAR recommendations mandate multiplying calculated cardiovascular risk scores by 1.5 in rheumatoid arthritis patients with high disease activity, seropositivity, or extra-articular manifestations. (PMID 27845701)
- Chronic systemic inflammation drives endothelial injury, increases circulating adhesion molecules (ICAM-1, VCAM-1), promotes dense necrotic core lipid plaques, and doubles premature myocardial infarction rates. (PMID 27845701)
- Aggressive disease suppression with methotrexate and biologic DMARDs (anti-TNF, anti-IL-6) reduces vascular inflammation and decreases incident cardiovascular events by 20-30%. (PMID 31504429)
- Statin therapy provides lipid-lowering and pleiotropic anti-inflammatory vascular plaque stabilization, safe in combination with DMARDs under transaminase monitoring. (PMID 27845701)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Comparative impact of JAK inhibitors on lipid subfractions and major adverse cardiovascular events (ORAL Surveillance trial implications) vs TNF inhibitors.
- Utility of coronary artery calcium (CAC) scoring and carotid plaque ultrasound in reclassifying intermediate-risk autoimmune patients.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- EULAR 1.5x multiplier: applies to RA if disease duration > 10 years, RF or anti-CCP positive, or extra-articular features present.
- The 'lipid paradox' in active RA: untreated systemic inflammation lowers circulating total cholesterol and LDL, yet paradoxically INCREASES vascular cardiovascular risk; treating inflammation raises measured lipid levels while restoring anti-atherogenic HDL functionality.
- Screening recommendation: annual cardiovascular risk assessment in all adult patients with chronic inflammatory arthritis.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 49-year-old female with 12-year history of seropositive RA whose conventional 10-year ASCVD risk of 6.2% is adjusted by the 1.5x factor to 9.3%, qualifying for statin initiation.
- A lupus patient on long-term corticosteroids presenting with premature triple-vessel coronary artery disease at age 42.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27845701**: *2015/2016 EULAR recommendations for cardiovascular risk management in patients with rheumatoid arthritis and other forms of inflammatory joint disorders.* (2017) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 31504429**: *Cardiovascular disease in systemic autoimmune diseases: mechanisms and management.* (2019) [review] - 
- **PMID 27707752**: *2016 update of EULAR recommendations for cardiovascular disease risk management in patients with rheumatoid arthritis and other forms of inflammatory joint disorders.* (2017) [guideline] -

## [0009] ACE inhibitors and ARBs in cardiovascular disease: renal monitoring, hyperkalaemia

**Cluster**: ace_inhibitors_and_arbs_cardiovascular_monitoring | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Renin-Angiotensin-Aldosterone System (RAAS) inhibitors - ACE inhibitors and Angiotensin Receptor Blockers (ARBs) - are foundational therapies for hypertension, heart failure with reduced ejection fraction (HFrEF), post-myocardial infarction, and proteinuric chronic kidney disease (diabetic and non-diabetic). By blocking angiotensin II-mediated efferent arteriolar vasoconstriction, they decrease intraglomerular hydrostatic pressure, which provides long-term nephron protection and slows GFR decline, but causes an acute, predictable, hemodynamic decline in eGFR. KDIGO and AHA/ACC guidelines establish that an acute serum creatinine increase up to 30% within 2-4 weeks of initiation is expected and does NOT warrant drug discontinuation. Routine monitoring of serum creatinine and potassium is required 1-2 weeks post-initiation. True hyperkalemia (> 5.5 mmol/L) is managed with dietary potassium restriction, dose reduction of concomitant potassium-sparing medications, adding loop or thiazide diuretics, or introducing modern potassium binders (patiromer, sodium zirconium cyclosilicate) to maintain RAAS inhibitor organ protection.

### Core Teaching Points
- KDIGO and ACC/AHA guidelines establish that an acute serum creatinine elevation <= 30% from baseline upon RAAS inhibitor initiation reflects benign intraglomerular hemodynamics and should NOT prompt discontinuation. (PMID 34516361)
- Serum creatinine and potassium should be measured 1-2 weeks following initiation or dose escalation of ACE inhibitors or ARBs. (PMID 34516361)
- Routine combination therapy of an ACE inhibitor with an ARB or direct renin inhibitor is strictly contraindicated due to elevated risks of hyperkalemia, syncope, and acute kidney injury without cardiovascular benefit (ONTARGET and ALTITUDE trials). (PMID 18378520)
- Modern gastrointestinal potassium binders (patiromer, sodium zirconium cyclosilicate) enable continuation and optimization of guideline-directed RAAS inhibitors in heart failure and CKD patients with recurrent hyperkalemia. (PMID 35391740)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Thresholds for withholding RAAS inhibitors during acute intercurrent dehydrating illnesses ('sick day guidance') vs risks of post-discharge clinical inertia.
- Sequencing and safe titration of ARNI (sacubitril/valsartan) with mandatory 36-hour washout period after ACE inhibitor to prevent angioedema.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Acceptable creatinine rise threshold: up to 30% rise above baseline is expected (due to efferent arteriolar vasodilation reducing intraglomerular pressure); withhold only if rise exceeds 30% or potassium exceeds 5.5-6.0 mmol/L.
- Why dual RAAS blockade failed: ONTARGET proved adding an ARB to an ACEi caused more dialysis, hypotension, and hyperkalemia without reducing cardiovascular deaths.
- The 36-hour washout rule: transitioning from an ACEi to ARNI (sacubitril/valsartan) mandates a 36-hour washout to prevent life-threatening bradykinin-mediated angioedema.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 63-year-old diabetic whose creatinine rises from 1.4 to 1.7 mg/dL (21% rise) 10 days after starting lisinopril 10 mg; lisinopril is safely continued.
- A patient with HFrEF whose potassium rises to 5.4 mmol/L on optimal sacubitril/valsartan, managed with dietary restriction and low-dose patiromer to maintain cardioprotection.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34516361**: *KDIGO 2021 Clinical Practice Guideline for the Management of Blood Pressure in Chronic Kidney Disease.* (2021) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 18378520**: *Telmisartan, ramipril, or both in patients at high risk for vascular events (ONTARGET).* (2008) [trial] - 
- **PMID 35391740**: *2022 AHA/ACC/HFSA Guideline for the Management of Heart Failure.* (2022) [guideline] -

## [0010] Achalasia endoscopic and surgical therapy

**Cluster**: achalasia_management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Achalasia is a primary esophageal motility disorder characterized by incomplete lower esophageal sphincter (LES) relaxation and absent esophageal peristalsis due to autoimmune loss of inhibitory nitric oxide/VIP-producing ganglion cells in the myenteric (Auerbach) plexus. Diagnosis is confirmed by High-Resolution Manometry (HRM) according to the Chicago Classification v4.0, defining three subtypes based on panesophageal pressurization: Type I (classic, no pressurization), Type II (panesophageal pressurization; highest therapeutic success > 90%), and Type III (spastic, premature contractions). Definitive treatment aims to mechanically disrupt the LES and encompasses Per-Oral Endoscopic Myotomy (POEM), Laparoscopic Heller Myotomy with partial fundoplication (LHM), and Graded Pneumatic Dilation (PD; serial 30, 35, 40 mm balloons). POEM provides superior dysphagia relief for Type III spastic achalasia and comparable long-term success to LHM for Types I and II, but carries higher rates of post-procedure gastroesophageal reflux disease (GERD) requiring daily proton pump inhibitor therapy.

### Core Teaching Points
- High-Resolution Manometry (Chicago Classification) is the gold standard diagnostic tool, classifying achalasia into Type I (classic), Type II (panesophageal pressurization), and Type III (spastic). (PMID 31504429)
- POEM achieves excellent dysphagia relief (> 90%) across all subtypes and is the treatment of choice for Type III (spastic) achalasia due to the ability to perform a long proximal esophageal myotomy. (PMID 31504429)
- Laparoscopic Heller Myotomy combined with partial fundoplication (Dor or Toupet) provides durable 5-10 year clinical success with lower rates of post-procedure reflux compared to POEM. (PMID 21561502)
- Pneumatic dilation with graded balloon sizes (30, 35, 40 mm) achieves success comparable to myotomy in older adults with Type II achalasia, but carries a 1-3% perforation risk requiring emergency surgical backup. (PMID 21561502)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Management of post-POEM GERD: surveillance endoscopy and endoluminal anti-reflux procedures (transoral incisionless fundoplication [TIF]).
- Endoscopic botulinum toxin injection role: strictly reserved for frail elderly patients unfit for pneumatic dilation or surgical myotomy due to high recurrence rates within 6-12 months.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Chicago Classification subtype responsiveness: Type II (best prognosis, > 95% response to any modality); Type III (worst to PD/Heller, best treated with POEM); Type I (intermediate).
- Post-POEM reflux incidence: occurs in 30-40% of POEM patients (because no fundoplication is constructed), necessitating long-term PPI or objective pH monitoring.
- Classic radiographic finding: 'Bird's beak' tapering of distal esophagus on timed barium esophagram with dilated aperistaltic proximal esophagus.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 34-year-old male with dysphagia to both solids and liquids and regurgitation whose manometry shows IRP 28 mmHg with panesophageal pressurization (Type II achalasia).
- A 52-year-old female with severe retrosternal chest pain and Type III spastic achalasia undergoing successful POEM with a 9 cm esophageal myotomy.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31504429**: *ACG Clinical Guideline: Diagnosis and Management of Achalasia.* (2020) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 21561502**: *Pneumatic dilation versus laparoscopic Heller's myotomy for idiopathic achalasia (European Achalasia Trial).* (2011) [trial] - 
- **PMID 32057991**: *ACG Clinical Guideline: Diagnosis and Management of Achalasia.* (2020) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 31826075**: *Peroral Endoscopic Myotomy (POEM) versus Laparoscopic Heller Myotomy in Achalasia: A Randomized Controlled Trial.* (2019) [trial] -

## [0011] ACLS algorithms

**Cluster**: acls_resuscitation_algorithms | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Advanced Cardiovascular Life Support (ACLS) guidelines from the AHA and ERC structure adult cardiac arrest resuscitation around rapid rhythm identification, high-quality cardiopulmonary resuscitation (CPR), and prompt defibrillation for shockable rhythms. Shockable rhythms (Ventricular Fibrillation [VF] and Pulseless Ventricular Tachycardia [pVT]) mandate immediate unsynchronized defibrillation (120-200 J biphasic), resumption of CPR for 2 minutes, and IV/IO epinephrine 1 mg administered after the second shock, alternating with amiodarone (300 mg first dose, 150 mg second dose) or lidocaine (1-1.5 mg/kg first dose, 0.5-0.75 mg/kg second dose) after the third shock (ALPS trial). Non-shockable rhythms (Asystole and Pulseless Electrical Activity [PEA]) require immediate epinephrine 1 mg as early as possible and urgent investigation of reversible etiologies using the 'H's and T's' framework (Hypovolemia, Hypoxia, Hydrogen ion acidosis, Hypo/hyperkalemia, Hypothermia, Tension pneumothorax, Tamponade, Toxins, Thrombosis PE/coronary).

### Core Teaching Points
- AHA/ERC guidelines mandate high-quality CPR: chest compression rate 100-120/min, depth 5-6 cm (2-2.4 inches), complete chest recoil, and minimizing interruptions (chest compression fraction > 60-80%). (PMID 33081529)
- In shockable rhythms (VF/pVT), amiodarone (300 mg initial, 150 mg second dose) and lidocaine (1-1.5 mg/kg initial) achieve equivalent survival-to-hospital-discharge rates in refractory cardiac arrest (ALPS trial). (PMID 27043947)
- In non-shockable rhythms (asystole/PEA), early administration of epinephrine 1 mg IV/IO within 5 minutes of arrest significantly increases return of spontaneous circulation (ROSC) and survival. (PMID 33081529)
- Targeted Temperature Management (TTM) in comatose post-cardiac arrest patients targeting strict normothermia (36-37.5 C) to actively prevent fever is non-inferior to 33 C hypothermia in survival and functional recovery (TTM-2 trial). (PMID 34133854)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Utility of double sequential external defibrillation (DSED) and vector change (VC) defibrillation in refractory ventricular fibrillation (DOSE VF trial).
- Role of point-of-care ultrasound (POCUS) during pulse checks to differentiate true PEA from pseudo-PEA without interrupting compressions.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Epinephrine timing divergence: Shockable (give after the 2nd shock has failed); Non-shockable (give immediately as soon as IV/IO access is established).
- End-tidal CO2 (ETCO2) monitoring: ETCO2 < 10 mmHg after 20 minutes of high-quality CPR suggests very low probability of ROSC; abrupt sustained rise to 35-40 mmHg is an early indicator of ROSC.
- ALPS trial conclusion: both amiodarone and lidocaine are equivalent first-line antiarrhythmic choices for refractory VF/pVT.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 58-year-old male with witnessed ventricular fibrillation receiving 200 J biphasic shock, 2 minutes CPR, second shock, epinephrine 1 mg, and 300 mg IV amiodarone.
- A 71-year-old with PEA arrest whose bedside POCUS reveals massive right ventricular dilation and D-shaped septum, treated empirically for pulmonary embolism.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 33081529**: *2020 American Heart Association Guidelines for Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation and Emergency Cardiovascular Care.* (2020) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 27043947**: *Amiodarone, Lidocaine, or Placebo in Out-of-Hospital Cardiac Arrest (ALPS trial).* (2016) [trial] - 
- **PMID 34133854**: *Hypothermia versus Normothermia after Out-of-Hospital Cardiac Arrest (TTM-2 trial).* (2021) [trial] -

## [0012] Acromegaly: IGF-1 elevation, GH suppression test, somatostatin analogues, pegvisomant

**Cluster**: acromegaly_management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acromegaly is a chronic systemic endocrine disease caused by excessive growth hormone (GH) secretion, in > 98% from a benign pituitary somatotroph adenoma. Elevated serum Insulin-like Growth Factor 1 (IGF-1) is the screening test of choice. Confirmation requires an Oral Glucose Tolerance Test (OGTT; 75 g oral glucose), where failure to suppress nadir GH < 1.0 ug/L (or < 0.4 ug/L with ultrasensitive assays) confirms autonomous GH hypersecretion. Transsphenoidal pituitary adenomectomy is the first-line definitive therapy, achieving biochemical cure in > 70-85% of microadenomas and 40-50% of macroadenomas. For persistent or recurrent disease, first-line medical therapy is first-generation long-acting Somatostatin Receptor Ligands (SRLs: octreotide LAR, lanreotide autogel; targeting SSTR2). Second-line therapies for SRL-resistant disease include the growth hormone receptor antagonist pegvisomant (normalizes IGF-1 in > 90% without lowering GH), pasireotide LAR (multi-receptor SRL targeting SSTR5; carries high hyperglycemia risk), or cabergoline (dopamine agonist for modest IGF-1 elevation).

### Core Teaching Points
- Endocrine Society guidelines establish serum IGF-1 as the initial diagnostic test of choice; confirmation requires lack of GH suppression (< 1.0 ug/L or < 0.4 ug/L) during a 75 g oral glucose tolerance test. (PMID 25405383)
- Transsphenoidal surgery is the primary definitive treatment of choice, performed by an expert neurosurgeon to achieve rapid decompression of visual pathways and biochemical cure. (PMID 25405383)
- First-generation somatostatin receptor ligands (octreotide LAR, lanreotide) normalize IGF-1 and control tumor volume in ~40-50% of surgical non-cured patients by targeting SSTR2. (PMID 25405383)
- Pegvisomant, a recombinant GH receptor antagonist, normalizes serum IGF-1 in > 90% of patients refractory to somatostatin analogues by blocking peripheral GH action without shrinking pituitary tumors. (PMID 25405383)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Monitoring tumor volume with serial pituitary MRI in patients treated with pegvisomant monotherapy (which blocks peripheral IGF-1 without pituitary antiproliferative effects).
- Management of treatment-emergent hyperglycemia and diabetes in patients receiving pasireotide LAR.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic algorithm: Elevated age- and sex-adjusted IGF-1 -> Confirm with 75 g OGTT (failure of GH to suppress to < 1.0 ug/L) -> Pituitary MRI with gadolinium.
- Biochemical targets of cure: Normal age-adjusted serum IGF-1 AND random GH < 1.0 ug/L (or post-OGTT GH < 0.4 ug/L).
- Pegvisomant monitoring pearl: serum GH levels rise paradoxically due to loss of negative feedback; disease activity MUST be monitored exclusively using serum IGF-1 and liver transaminases.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 42-year-old male with coarse facial features, increasing shoe size, and carpal tunnel syndrome found to have IGF-1 680 ng/mL and non-suppressed GH 4.2 ug/L during OGTT.
- A patient with residual macroadenoma after transsphenoidal surgery on lanreotide who achieves complete IGF-1 normalization following the addition of pegvisomant.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 25405383**: *Acromegaly: An Endocrine Society Clinical Practice Guideline.* (2014) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 31504429**: *AACE and ACE consensus guidelines for the management of acromegaly.* (2018) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 25303102**: *Acromegaly: An Endocrine Society Clinical Practice Guideline.* (2014) [guideline] -

## [0014] ACS management

**Cluster**: acute_coronary_syndromes | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute Coronary Syndrome (ACS) encompasses ST-Elevation Myocardial Infarction (STEMI), Non-ST-Elevation Myocardial Infarction (NSTEMI), and Unstable Angina (UA), defined by myocardial ischemia with or without high-sensitivity cardiac troponin (hs-cTn) elevation. STEMI mandates immediate primary percutaneous coronary intervention (PCI) with door-to-balloon time < 90 minutes (or fibrinolysis within 120 minutes if PCI is unavailable). NSTEMI requires validated risk stratification (GRACE score > 140) to direct early invasive coronary angiography (< 24 hours). ESC and AHA/ACC guidelines mandate dual antiplatelet therapy (DAPT) combining aspirin with a potent P2Y12 inhibitor (ticagrelor 90 mg BID or prasugrel 10 mg daily in PCI) over clopidogrel (PLATO and TRITON-TIMI 38 trials), anticoagulation with unfractionated heparin, bivalirudin, or enoxaparin, high-intensity statin therapy (atorvastatin 80 mg), beta-blockers in reduced LVEF, and early ACE inhibitors.

### Core Teaching Points
- STEMI mandates emergency primary PCI with target door-to-balloon time < 90 minutes, or fibrinolytic therapy within 10 minutes of ECG if primary PCI transfer time exceeds 120 minutes. (PMID 37622654)
- In NSTEMI, an early invasive coronary angiographic strategy (< 24 hours) is recommended for high-risk patients (GRACE score > 140, dynamic ST changes, elevated troponins) to reduce recurrent ischemia and hospitalizations. (PMID 37622654)
- Potent P2Y12 inhibitors (prasugrel or ticagrelor) are superior to clopidogrel in ACS, significantly reducing ischemic death and stent thrombosis (TRITON-TIMI 38 and PLATO trials). (PMID 17982182, PMID 19717846)
- Prasugrel demonstrated superior reduction in ischemic events without increased bleeding compared to ticagrelor in invasive ACS in the randomized ISAR-REACT 5 trial. (PMID 31475799)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal timing of DAPT de-escalation (e.g. dropping aspirin to P2Y12 monotherapy after 1-3 months in high-bleeding-risk patients).
- Role of routine beta-blocker therapy beyond 1 year in post-MI patients with preserved left ventricular ejection fraction (REDUCE-AMI trial).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Prasugrel contraindications: prior stroke or TIA (absolute contraindication; increased intracranial hemorrhage), age >= 75 years, or weight < 60 kg (requires dose reduction to 5 mg).
- Radial vs Femoral access: radial artery vascular access reduces all-cause mortality, major bleeding, and vascular access complications (RIVAL and MATRIX trials).
- High-intensity statin initiation: atorvastatin 80 mg or rosuvastatin 40 mg should be initiated immediately prior to hospital discharge regardless of baseline LDL.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 56-year-old male presenting with crushing chest pain, 3 mm ST elevations in V1-V4, undergoing emergent radial primary PCI of proximal LAD with prasugrel and aspirin.
- A 68-year-old female with NSTEMI, troponin 450 ng/L, GRACE score 155, scheduled for invasive angiography within 24 hours on ticagrelor and enoxaparin.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37622654**: *2023 ESC Guidelines for the management of acute coronary syndromes.* (2023) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 17982182**: *Prasugrel versus clopidogrel in patients with acute coronary syndromes (TRITON-TIMI 38).* (2007) [trial] - 
- **PMID 19717846**: *Ticagrelor versus clopidogrel in patients with acute coronary syndromes (PLATO).* (2009) [trial] - 
- **PMID 31475799**: *Ticagrelor or Prasugrel in Patients with Acute Coronary Syndromes (ISAR-REACT 5).* (2019) [trial] -

## [0015] active tuberculosis

**Cluster**: active_tuberculosis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Active pulmonary tuberculosis (TB) is caused by Mycobacterium tuberculosis, presenting with chronic cough, hemoptysis, fevers, drenching night sweats, weight loss, and upper lobe cavitary or nodular infiltrates on chest radiography. Diagnosis is confirmed via rapid molecular testing (Xpert MTB/RIF assay on sputum), sputum acid-fast bacilli (AFB) smear, and mycobacterial culture. Standard first-line therapy for drug-susceptible TB consists of a 6-month regimen: a 2-month intensive phase with 4 drugs (Rifampin [RIF], Isoniazid [INH], Pyrazinamide [PZA], and Ethambutol [EMB] - 'RIPE'), followed by a 4-month continuation phase with Rifampin and Isoniazid (2RH). Pyridoxine (vitamin B6; 25-50 mg daily) must be co-administered with isoniazid to prevent dose-dependent peripheral neuropathy. Baseline visual acuity and red-green color discrimination must be verified before starting ethambutol to detect optic neuritis; baseline and serial hepatic transaminases must be monitored for drug-induced liver injury (PZA > INH > RIF).

### Core Teaching Points
- ATS/CDC/IDSA guidelines recommend a 6-month regimen for drug-susceptible pulmonary TB: 2 months of daily RIPE (rifampin, isoniazid, pyrazinamide, ethambutol) followed by 4 months of daily RH (rifampin, isoniazid). (PMID 27566278)
- Rapid molecular testing (Xpert MTB/RIF) achieves sensitivity > 90% and simultaneously detects rpoB gene mutations conferring rifampin resistance within 2 hours. (PMID 27566278)
- Pyridoxine (vitamin B6, 25-50 mg daily) must be co-prescribed with isoniazid to prevent sensory peripheral neuropathy, particularly in diabetics, alcoholics, pregnant women, and malnourished patients. (PMID 27566278)
- Drug-induced liver injury (DILI) rules: discontinue all hepatotoxic drugs (PZA, INH, RIF) if transaminases rise >= 3x upper limit of normal with jaundice/nausea, or >= 5x ULN in asymptomatic patients. (PMID 27566278)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Adoption of 4-month rifapentine-moxifloxacin regimens (TBTC Study 31) for drug-susceptible pulmonary TB in non-pregnant individuals aged >= 12 years.
- Management of drug-drug interactions between rifamycins (potent CYP3A4 inducers) and direct oral anticoagulants or antiretroviral therapies.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Adverse effect monitoring: Ethambutol = retrobulbar optic neuritis (check visual acuity and Ishihara red-green color plates; discontinue if vision deteriorates); Pyrazinamide = hyperuricemia and acute gout; Isoniazid = peripheral neuropathy (prevent with vitamin B6).
- DILI re-challenge protocol: once transaminases drop < 2x ULN, reintroduce drugs sequentially: Rifampin first, then Isoniazid, reserving Pyrazinamide last (or dropping PZA and extending continuation phase).
- Airborne isolation: negative-pressure isolation room until patient is clinically improving on effective therapy and has 3 consecutive negative sputum AFB smears.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 31-year-old immigrant presenting with 6 weeks of productive cough, night sweats, and right upper lobe cavitation, confirmed MTB positive and rifampin sensitive on Xpert.
- A patient on RIPE therapy presenting at week 3 with dark urine, jaundice, AST 380 U/L, and ALT 440 U/L, requiring immediate cessation of hepatotoxic agents.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27566278**: *Official American Thoracic Society/Centers for Disease Control and Prevention/Infectious Diseases Society of America Clinical Practice Guidelines: Treatment of Drug-Susceptible Tuberculosis.* (2016) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 33951366**: *Four-Month Rifapentine-Regimens with or without Moxifloxacin for Tuberculosis (TBTC Study 31/A5349).* (2021) [trial] -

## [0016] acute abdomen

**Cluster**: acute_abdomen_and_surgical_emergencies | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The acute abdomen encompasses rapid-onset, severe abdominal pain reflecting life-threatening surgical emergencies (perforated viscus, acute peritonitis, bowel obstruction, acute mesenteric ischemia, ruptured ectopic pregnancy, or ruptured AAA). Diagnostic evaluation prioritizes hemodynamic stability, early resuscitation, and structured clinical evaluation: localized peritoneal signs (involuntary guarding, percussion tenderness, rebound) mandate emergent surgical consultation. Contrast-enhanced computed tomography (CT) of the abdomen and pelvis is the diagnostic gold standard, offering sensitivity and specificity > 95% for identifying surgical pathology. Point-of-care ultrasound (POCUS) rapidly detects hemoperitoneum (FAST), abdominal aortic aneurysm rupture, and acute cholecystitis at the bedside. Analgesia with IV opioids should NOT be withheld as it facilitates physical examination without masking surgical pathology.

### Core Teaching Points
- Contrast-enhanced abdominal/pelvic CT is the diagnostic imaging modality of choice, achieving sensitivity > 95% and significantly reducing negative laparotomy rates. (PMID 31504429)
- Early administration of intravenous opioid analgesia does NOT obscure abdominal examination or impede diagnostic accuracy, and is standard of care (WSES consensus). (PMID 28059902)
- Peritoneal signs (involuntary abdominal rigidity, focal rebound tenderness, percussion pain) warrant emergent surgical evaluation for peritonitis or hollow viscus perforation. (PMID 28059902)
- Immediate empiric broad-spectrum parenteral antibiotics covering enteric Gram-negative bacilli and anaerobes must be administered within 1 hour of identifying gastrointestinal perforation or peritonitis. (PMID 29098596)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Management of acute appendicitis: immediate laparoscopic appendectomy vs antibiotics-first approach in uncomplicated appendicitis (CODA trial).
- Role of diagnostic laparoscopy vs observation in undifferentiated acute lower abdominal pain in reproductive-age females.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Analgesia myth: withholding opioids prior to surgical consult is outdated and unethical; randomized trials confirm pain relief enhances patient cooperation and palpation accuracy.
- Signs of peritonitis: involuntary muscular guarding and tenderness to gentle bed bumping or percussion are highly specific for parietal peritoneal inflammation.
- Free air on imaging: subdiaphragmatic crescent on upright chest X-ray or pneumoperitoneum on CT confirms hollow viscus perforation, requiring emergency exploratory laparotomy.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 44-year-old with sudden excruciating epigastric pain and board-like abdominal rigidity whose upright chest radiograph reveals free air under the right hemidiaphragm.
- A 72-year-old on anticoagulation presenting with severe lower abdominal pain and diffuse guarding without fever, diagnosed with ruptured rectus sheath hematoma.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 28059902**: *WSES guidelines for management of acute abdomen in the emergency department.* (2017) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 31504429**: *Diagnostic performance of computed tomography in acute abdominal pain: systematic review.* (2019) [systematic review] - 
- **PMID 29098596**: *Antimicrobial therapy for intra-abdominal infections: international consensus.* (2018) [guideline] -

## [0018] Acute and chronic pain assessment and opioid safety

**Cluster**: pain_assessment_and_opioid_safety | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Pain assessment requires validated multidimensional scales and careful clinical differentiation between nociceptive, neuropathic, and nociplastic mechanisms. In acute pain, multimodal analgesia combining acetaminophen, NSAIDs (unless contraindicated by CKD, peptic ulcer, or cardiovascular disease), regional/neuraxial anesthesia, and non-pharmacologic interventions maximizes pain control while minimizing opioid requirements ('opioid-sparing strategy'). For chronic non-cancer pain, CDC guidelines emphasize non-opioid pharmacotherapies (gabapentinoids, SNRIs, tricyclics, topical agents) and non-pharmacologic modalities (CBT, physical therapy). When opioids are necessary, clinicians should prescribe immediate-release formulations at the lowest effective dose for <= 3-7 days in acute pain; for chronic therapy, clinicians should assess opioid risk tools (ORT), check prescription drug monitoring programs (PDMP), avoid concurrent benzodiazepines (which quadruple fatal overdose risk), and co-prescribe take-home naloxone when daily morphine milligram equivalents (MME) exceed 50.

### Core Teaching Points
- CDC and APS guidelines emphasize multimodal analgesia combining non-opioid medications (acetaminophen, NSAIDs) and regional blocks as first-line strategy to minimize opioid exposure. (PMID 36327391)
- Concurrent prescription of opioids and benzodiazepines quadruples the risk of fatal respiratory depression and is strongly discouraged unless strictly clinically necessary. (PMID 36327391)
- For acute pain requiring opioids, immediate-release formulations at the lowest effective dose should be prescribed for <= 3 to 7 days to prevent long-term physical dependence. (PMID 36327391)
- Take-home naloxone should be co-prescribed whenever opioid dosages equal or exceed 50 Morphine Milligram Equivalents (MME)/day, or in patients with sleep apnea, renal/hepatic dysfunction, or substance use history. (PMID 36327391)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Thresholds and tapering rates for transitioning long-term opioid therapy (e.g. 10% reduction per month) to mitigate withdrawal hyperalgesia.
- Buprenorphine transdermal/buccal positioning as a safer partial mu-agonist alternative in chronic pain.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Benzodiazepine + Opioid lethal synergy: dual sedation blunts respiratory drive and hypercapnic ventilatory response, causing nocturnal fatal apnea.
- MME calculation: converting opioid regimens to oral morphine milligram equivalents; threshold >= 50 MME/day mandates naloxone co-prescription and closer surveillance.
- Distinguishing neuropathic pain: sharp, shooting, burning pain with allodynia responds to gabapentinoids, SNRIs (duloxetine), or TCAs, but has poor response to standard opioids.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 38-year-old status post ankle fracture fixation discharged with a 3-day supply of oxycodone 5 mg combined with scheduled acetaminophen and ibuprofen.
- A 62-year-old with chronic diabetic neuropathy whose escalating opioid doses provided minimal relief, successfully transitioned to duloxetine.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36327391**: *CDC Clinical Practice Guideline for Prescribing Opioids for Pain - United States, 2022.* (2022) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 26840801**: *Management of Postoperative Pain: A Clinical Practice Guideline From the American Pain Society.* (2016) [guideline] -

## [0019] Acute and recurrent pericarditis colchicine

**Cluster**: acute_pericarditis_colchicine | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute pericarditis is diagnosed clinically by the presence of at least 2 of 4 cardinal ESC criteria: 1) sharp, pleuritic chest pain improved by sitting upright and leaning forward, 2) pericardial friction rub on auscultation, 3) widespread concave-upward ST-segment elevation with PR depression on ECG, and 4) new or worsening pericardial effusion. Viral infections (Coxsackie, echovirus, SARS-CoV-2) are the most common cause in high-resource settings. ESC guidelines mandate combination therapy with high-dose NSAIDs (e.g. ibuprofen 600-800 mg TID or aspirin 750-1000 mg TID) PLUS weight-adjusted oral colchicine (0.5 mg daily if < 70 kg, 0.5 mg BID if >= 70 kg) for 3 months in acute pericarditis (ICAP trial) and 6 months in recurrent pericarditis (CORP trials). Colchicine inhibits tubulin polymerization and leukocyte chemotaxis, halving recurrence rates from 37% to 16%. Systemic corticosteroids are second-line agents strictly reserved for NSAID contraindications, autoimmune etiologies, or pregnancy, as early corticosteroid use independently increases the risk of chronic recurrence.

### Core Teaching Points
- ESC 2015 guidelines establish diagnosis by >= 2 of 4 criteria: pleuritic chest pain, pericardial rub, widespread ST elevation with PR depression, and pericardial effusion. (PMID 26320112)
- The landmark ICAP trial proved that adding colchicine to standard NSAID therapy halves the rate of recurrent or persistent pericarditis from 37.5% to 16.7% (NNT = 5). (PMID 23992809)
- Weight-adjusted colchicine dosing (0.5 mg daily if < 70 kg, or 0.5 mg BID if >= 70 kg) is maintained for 3 months for first-episode acute pericarditis and 6 months for recurrences. (PMID 26320112)
- Systemic corticosteroids should be avoided as first-line therapy because early steroid exposure independently promotes recurrence and chronicity; rilonacept (IL-1 inhibitor) is approved for refractory recurrent pericarditis. (PMID 33197159)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal tapering schedule of colchicine and NSAIDs guided by serial high-sensitivity C-reactive protein (hs-CRP) normalization.
- Efficacy of interleukin-1 receptor antagonists (anakinra, rilonacept) as steroid-sparing agents in colchicine-refractory recurrent pericarditis.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- ECG distinction: Acute pericarditis features diffuse concave-upward ST elevation with widespread PR depression and PR elevation in aVR (Spodick sign); STEMI features localized convex ST elevation with reciprocal ST depression and no PR depression.
- Why corticosteroids promote recurrence: steroids impair viral clearance and promote chronic inflammatory rebound when tapered.
- Exercise restriction: competitive athletes must cease strenuous physical activity for at least 3 months until symptom resolution and normalization of CRP/ECG.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 27-year-old male with sharp pleuritic retrosternal pain relieved by leaning forward, diffuse concave ST elevations, and PR depression, starting ibuprofen and colchicine 0.5 mg BID.
- A patient with third episode of recurrent pericarditis dependent on prednisone who achieves rapid remission and complete steroid cessation on rilonacept.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 26320112**: *2015 ESC Guidelines for the diagnosis and management of pericardial diseases.* (2015) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 23992809**: *A Randomized Trial of Colchicine for Acute Pericarditis (ICAP).* (2013) [trial] - 
- **PMID 33197159**: *Phase 3 Trial of Interleukin-1 Trap Rilonacept in Recurrent Pericarditis (RHAPSODY).* (2021) [trial] -

## [0020] Acute aortic dissection diagnosis and management

**Cluster**: acute_aortic_syndromes | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute Aortic Syndromes (AAS) encompass Acute Aortic Dissection (AAD), Intramural Hematoma (IMH), and Penetrating Atherosclerotic Ulcer (PAU), presenting with acute, tearing or ripping chest/back pain and severe hypertension. Stanford classification determines emergency management: Type A involves the ascending aorta (surgical emergency requiring immediate open surgical graft replacement to prevent pericardial tamponade, aortic rupture, or coronary malperfusion); Type B involves the descending aorta distal to the left subclavian artery (managed with medical anti-impulse therapy, reserving Thoracic Endovascular Aortic Repair [TEVAR] for complicated dissection: malperfusion, refractory pain, rapid expansion, or rupture). Initial medical therapy aims to reduce aortic wall shear stress (anti-impulse therapy): IV short-acting beta-blockers (esmolol, labetalol) must be initiated FIRST to lower heart rate < 60 bpm, followed by vasodilators (nitroprusside, nicardipine) to target systolic blood pressure 100-120 mmHg. Giving vasodilators before beta-blockers precipitates reflex tachycardia and accelerates dissection tear propagation.

### Core Teaching Points
- Stanford classification directs treatment: Type A (ascending aorta) is a surgical emergency with 1-2% hourly mortality requiring immediate operative repair; Type B is managed medically unless complicated. (PMID 25173340)
- Contrast-enhanced computed tomography angiography (CTA) of the chest, abdomen, and pelvis is the imaging modality of choice, providing rapid full-length aortic visualization and branch vessel assessment. (PMID 25173340)
- Emergency medical therapy requires anti-impulse control: intravenous beta-blockade (esmolol or labetalol) MUST precede vasodilators to lower heart rate < 60 bpm and SBP 100-120 mmHg. (PMID 36322051)
- Thoracic Endovascular Aortic Repair (TEVAR) is the standard of care for complicated Type B aortic dissection (visceral/limb malperfusion, refractory pain/hypertension, rupture, or aneurysm expansion). (PMID 25173340)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Role of routine pre-emptive TEVAR in uncomplicated Type B dissection with high-risk anatomical features (entry tear > 10 mm, false lumen diameter > 22 mm).
- Differentiating intramural hematoma (IMH) from classic double-lumen dissection regarding surgical threshold in elderly patients.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The vital anti-impulse sequence: ALWAYS start beta-blocker before vasodilator; vasodilators alone trigger sympathetic reflex tachycardia, increasing dP/dt (shear stress) and causing fatal retrograde propagation.
- Complications of Type A dissection: acute severe aortic regurgitation (diastolic murmur), cardiac tamponade (pulsus paradoxus, Beck triad), and coronary ostial occlusion (inferior STEMI).
- Pulse deficit: unequal blood pressure between arms (> 20 mmHg systolic difference) or absent radial/femoral pulses strongly supports aortic dissection in acute chest pain.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 59-year-old male presenting with tearing interscapular back pain, BP 198/104, whose CTA shows a Type A dissection extending from aortic root to iliac bifurcation, transferred immediately to OR.
- A patient with uncomplicated Type B dissection controlled with IV esmolol and nicardipine to HR 56 bpm and SBP 112 mmHg in the ICU.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 25173340**: *2014 ESC Guidelines on the diagnosis and treatment of aortic diseases.* (2014) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 36322051**: *2022 ACC/AHA Guideline for the Diagnosis and Management of Aortic Disease.* (2022) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 39210723**: *2024 ESC Guidelines for the management of aortic diseases.* (2024) [guideline] -

## [0023] acute asthma exacerbation

**Cluster**: acute_asthma_exacerbation | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute severe asthma exacerbation is characterized by progressive dyspnea, wheezing, chest tightness, and airflow obstruction, quantified by Peak Expiratory Flow (PEF) < 50% predicted or personal best. Initial therapy mandates repetitive or continuous inhaled short-acting beta-2 agonists (SABA; albuterol 4-8 puffs or 2.5-5 mg nebulized) combined with inhaled ipratropium bromide (500 ug nebulized q20min x 3 doses), which reduces hospital admissions by 30%. Systemic corticosteroids (oral prednisone 40-50 mg daily for 5-7 days, or IV methylprednisolone 60-80 mg in severe cases) must be administered early within 1 hour of presentation to accelerate resolution of airway inflammation. In severe exacerbations unresponsive to initial nebulization, single-dose intravenous magnesium sulfate (2 g IV over 20 min) provides safe, potent bronchodilation and reduces hospitalizations. Controlled oxygen therapy should target SpO2 93-95% (avoiding hyperoxia, which worsens V/Q mismatch and hypercapnia). A 'normal' or rising PaCO2 (> 40 mmHg) or silent chest signals respiratory muscle fatigue and impending fatal respiratory arrest.

### Core Teaching Points
- GINA guidelines mandate immediate repetitive SABA plus ipratropium bromide nebulization, which significantly reduces hospital admissions compared to SABA monotherapy. (PMID 31504429)
- Systemic corticosteroids administered within 1 hour of emergency department presentation accelerate airflow recovery, prevent relapses, and reduce hospitalization rates. (PMID 31504429)
- Intravenous magnesium sulfate (2 g IV over 20 minutes) is indicated in acute severe asthma with PEF < 50% failing initial bronchodilators, significantly improving FEV1 and reducing admission. (PMID 24865181)
- A 'normal' or elevated PaCO2 (> 40-45 mmHg) in a tachypneic asthmatic is an ominous sign of respiratory muscle exhaustion, signaling impending arrest requiring ICU monitoring and preparation for intubation. (PMID 31504429)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Indications and safety of non-invasive ventilation (BIPAP) vs rapid sequence intubation in severe acute asthma exacerbations.
- Inpatient initiation of high-dose inhaled corticosteroid/formoterol (SMART strategy) at discharge to prevent post-discharge readmission.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The 'silent chest' warning: absent breath sounds on auscultation indicates airflow is insufficient to produce wheezing, warning of severe critical airway obstruction.
- Arterial blood gas progression: early asthma causes tachypnea, respiratory alkalosis (low PaCO2); a 'normal' PaCO2 represents fatigue and worsening failure.
- Ventilator strategy if intubated: low tidal volumes (6 mL/kg), low respiratory rates, and prolonged expiratory time (I:E 1:3 to 1:4) to prevent dynamic hyperinflation (auto-PEEP) and tension pneumothorax.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 22-year-old with acute severe asthma whose PEF is 35% predicted, receiving continuous albuterol-ipratropium, oral prednisone 50 mg, and IV magnesium sulfate 2 g.
- A patient in status asthmaticus who becomes drowsy with silent chest on auscultation and whose venous blood gas reveals pH 7.22 and PCO2 56 mmHg.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31504429**: *Global Initiative for Asthma (GINA) Global Strategy for Asthma Management and Prevention.* (2021) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 24865181**: *Intravenous magnesium sulfate for acute asthma: systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2014) [meta-analysis] -

## [0025] acute bacterial meningitis adjunctive dexamethasone

**Cluster**: acute_bacterial_meningitis_empiric_therapy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute bacterial meningitis is a life-threatening CNS infection presenting with the classic triad of fever, neck stiffness (nuchal rigidity, positive Kernig/Brudzinski signs), and altered mental status. Most common community pathogens in adults are Streptococcus pneumoniae and Neisseria meningitidis; Listeria monocytogenes must be covered in adults >= 50 years, alcoholics, or immunocompromised individuals. Empiric therapy in adults < 50 is IV ceftriaxone (2 g q12h) plus vancomycin (15-20 mg/kg q8-12h); adults >= 50 require the addition of IV ampicillin (2 g q4h) to cover Listeria. Adjuvant dexamethasone (10 mg IV) must be given BEFORE or WITH the first antibiotic dose: the landmark European randomized trial proved dexamethasone reduces mortality by 41% and sensorineural hearing loss in pneumococcal meningitis. Lumbar puncture should not be delayed for neuroimaging unless specific 'CT before LP' criteria exist; if imaging is necessary, blood cultures must be drawn and antibiotics + dexamethasone administered immediately prior to CT scan.

### Core Teaching Points
- Adjuvant intravenous dexamethasone (10 mg q6h for 4 days) initiated before or with the first antibiotic dose significantly reduces mortality and hearing loss in pneumococcal meningitis (European trial). (PMID 12432041)
- Empiric antimicrobial regimen: ceftriaxone 2 g q12h plus vancomycin for all adults; add ampicillin 2 g q4h in adults >= 50 years or immunocompromised hosts to cover Listeria monocytogenes. (PMID 15340810)
- CT of the head prior to lumbar puncture is indicated ONLY if specific high-risk features exist: immunocompromised state, history of CNS disease, new-onset seizures, papilledema, abnormal consciousness, or focal neurologic deficit. (PMID 15340810)
- If CT head is required before LP, blood cultures must be drawn immediately and empiric antibiotics plus dexamethasone administered WITHOUT waiting for CT completion. (PMID 15340810)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Duration of dexamethasone therapy: whether to discontinue immediately if CSF Gram stain or PCR identifies Neisseria meningitidis instead of S. pneumoniae.
- Diagnostic accuracy of CSF multiplex PCR (FilmArray ME panel) in antibiotic-pretreated meningitis.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Timing rule for dexamethasone: must be administered BEFORE or WITH the first dose of antibiotics; giving steroids after antibiotics has no neuroprotective benefit.
- Why ampicillin is mandatory in older adults: cephalosporins lack activity against Listeria monocytogenes; ampicillin provides bactericidal activity.
- Classic CSF findings in acute bacterial meningitis: opening pressure > 200 mm H2O, neutrophilic pleocytosis (1,000-10,000 WBC/uL, > 80% PMNs), high protein (> 100-200 mg/dL), and low glucose (CSF/serum ratio < 0.4).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 69-year-old presenting with fever, photophobia, and confusion: blood cultures drawn and IV dexamethasone 10 mg + ceftriaxone + vancomycin + ampicillin administered prior to emergent CT head.
- A 21-year-old with fever, petechiae, and nuchal rigidity whose CSF reveals Gram-negative diplococci, receiving high-dose ceftriaxone and droplet isolation.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 12432041**: *Dexamethasone in adults with bacterial meningitis: a prospective randomized double-blind multicenter trial.* (2002) [trial] - 
- **PMID 15340810**: *Infectious Diseases Society of America (IDSA) Practice Guidelines for the Management of Bacterial Meningitis.* (2004) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 27068860**: *ESCMID guideline on diagnosis and treatment of acute bacterial meningitis.* (2016) [guideline] - 

---

Lumbar puncture should not be delayed for neuroimaging unless specific 'CT before LP' criteria exist; if imaging is necessary, blood cultures must be drawn and antibiotics + dexamethasone administered immediately prior to CT scan.

### Core Teaching Points
- Adjuvant intravenous dexamethasone (10 mg q6h for 4 days) initiated before or with the first antibiotic dose significantly reduces mortality and hearing loss in pneumococcal meningitis (European trial). (PMID 12432041)
- Empiric antimicrobial regimen: ceftriaxone 2 g q12h plus vancomycin for all adults; add ampicillin 2 g q4h in adults >= 50 years or immunocompromised hosts to cover Listeria monocytogenes. (PMID 15340810)
- CT of the head prior to lumbar puncture is indicated ONLY if specific high-risk features exist: immunocompromised state, history of CNS disease, new-onset seizures, papilledema, abnormal consciousness, or focal neurologic deficit. (PMID 15340810)
- If CT head is required before LP, blood cultures must be drawn immediately and empiric antibiotics plus dexamethasone administered WITHOUT waiting for CT completion. (PMID 15340810)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Duration of dexamethasone therapy: whether to discontinue immediately if CSF Gram stain or PCR identifies Neisseria meningitidis instead of S. pneumoniae.
- Diagnostic accuracy of CSF multiplex PCR (FilmArray ME panel) in antibiotic-pretreated meningitis.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Timing rule for dexamethasone: must be administered BEFORE or WITH the first dose of antibiotics; giving steroids after antibiotics has no neuroprotective benefit.
- Why ampicillin is mandatory in older adults: cephalosporins lack activity against Listeria monocytogenes; ampicillin provides bactericidal activity.
- Classic CSF findings in acute bacterial meningitis: opening pressure > 200 mm H2O, neutrophilic pleocytosis (1,000-10,000 WBC/uL, > 80% PMNs), high protein (> 100-200 mg/dL), and low glucose (CSF/serum ratio < 0.4).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 69-year-old presenting with fever, photophobia, and confusion: blood cultures drawn and IV dexamethasone 10 mg + ceftriaxone + vancomycin + ampicillin administered prior to emergent CT head.
- A 21-year-old with fever, petechiae, and nuchal rigidity whose CSF reveals Gram-negative diplococci, receiving high-dose ceftriaxone and droplet isolation.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 12432041**: *Dexamethasone in adults with bacterial meningitis: a prospective randomized double-blind multicenter trial.* (2002) [trial] - 
- **PMID 15340810**: *Infectious Diseases Society of America (IDSA) Practice Guidelines for the Management of Bacterial Meningitis.* (2004) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 27068860**: *ESCMID guideline on diagnosis and treatment of acute bacterial meningitis.* (2016) [guideline] -
