## [0028] acute behavioural disturbance

**Cluster**: acute_behavioural_disturbance_and_delirium | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute behavioural disturbance (ABD) and severe agitation in the emergency and acute inpatient setting represent medical emergencies that threaten patient and staff safety. Most common causes include acute delirium, intoxication (sympathomimetics, alcohol, synthetic cannabinoids), sedative withdrawal, severe psychiatric psychosis, metabolic derangements, and traumatic brain injury. Clinical management follows a staged de-escalation hierarchy: environmental adaptation and verbal de-escalation are attempted first. When non-pharmacologic measures fail and physical danger exists, parenteral pharmacotherapy is deployed: for delirium and undifferentiated agitation, low-dose atypical antipsychotics (haloperidol 2.5-5 mg IM or olanzapine 5-10 mg IM) are first-line. For stimulant toxicity or alcohol withdrawal, intravenous or intramuscular benzodiazepines (lorazepam 1-2 mg or midazolam 2.5-5 mg) are the treatments of choice. For violent, hyperactive excited delirium, intramuscular ketamine (3-4 mg/kg IM) achieves rapid sedation within 5 minutes under strict airway monitoring.

### Core Teaching Points
- A structured stepwise approach begins with non-pharmacologic de-escalation, reserving chemical sedation for patients presenting immediate physical danger to themselves or staff. (PMID 29485925)
- Benzodiazepines (lorazepam, midazolam) are the first-line sedatives for agitation driven by sympathomimetic intoxication (cocaine, methamphetamines) or alcohol withdrawal. (PMID 29485925)
- Antipsychotics (haloperidol, olanzapine, droperidol) provide effective sedation for psychotic agitation and delirium, but require ECG monitoring for QTc prolongation. (PMID 31504429)
- Intramuscular ketamine (3-4 mg/kg IM) produces rapid, reliable dissociation within 3-5 minutes in extreme, life-threatening violent agitation failing other agents, under dedicated airway monitoring. (PMID 31504429)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Comparative incidence of respiratory depression and endotracheal intubation with prehospital ketamine vs droperidol/midazolam.
- Management of acute dystonic reactions and akathisia with anticholinergic agents (diphenhydramine, benztropine).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Etiology-directed sedation selection: Stimulant/Alcohol withdrawal = Benzodiazepines; Primary psychosis/Delirium = Antipsychotics (avoid benzos in elderly delirium); Extreme uncontrolled violence = Ketamine.
- Adverse effect monitoring: QT prolongation and torsades de pointes with IV haloperidol/droperidol; respiratory depression and laryngospasm with ketamine and high-dose benzodiazepines.
- Immediate post-sedation protocol: continuous pulse oximetry, cardiac telemetry, blood glucose check, and temperature monitoring to detect malignant hyperthermia/serotonin syndrome.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 24-year-old brought by EMS with acute cocaine intoxication, hyperthermia, and extreme paranoia, calmed safely with titrated IV lorazepam.
- An 82-year-old post-hip-fracture patient with hyperactive delirium pulling lines, managed with low-dose oral quetiapine and non-pharmacologic orientation.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31504429**: *ACEP Clinical Policy: Critical Issues in the Evaluation and Management of Adult Patients Presenting to the Emergency Department With Acute Agitation.* (2018) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 29485925**: *Acute behavioural disturbance in the emergency department: consensus management guidelines.* (2017) [review] -

## [0029] acute cholecystitis

**Cluster**: acute_cholecystitis_and_ascending_cholangitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute cholecystitis and acute cholangitis represent common biliary infections governed by the international Tokyo Guidelines (TG18). Acute cholecystitis is defined by local inflammation (Murphy's sign, RUQ tenderness), systemic inflammation (fever, elevated CRP/WBC), and confirmatory imaging (ultrasound showing gallbladder wall thickening >= 4 mm, pericholecystic fluid, sonographic Murphy's sign). Laparoscopic cholecystectomy performed early within 72 hours of symptom onset (or during index admission) is the gold standard, achieving shorter hospital stays and fewer complications than delayed surgery. Acute cholangitis is a medical emergency caused by biliary tract obstruction (primarily choledocholithiasis) with bacterial superinfection, presenting with Charcot's triad (fever, jaundice, RUQ pain) or Reynolds' pentad (adding hypotension and altered mental status in septic shock). TG18 mandates immediate resuscitation, empiric broad-spectrum antibiotics, and emergency biliary decompression via Endoscopic Retrograde Cholangiopancreatography (ERCP) within 24 hours (or emergent < 12 hours for severe Grade III cholangitis).

### Core Teaching Points
- Tokyo Guidelines 2018 (TG18) recommend early laparoscopic cholecystectomy within 72 hours of symptom onset as the optimal treatment of choice for acute cholecystitis. (PMID 29098596)
- Acute cholangitis is diagnosed by systemic inflammation (fever, elevated WBC/CRP), cholestasis (jaundice, elevated transaminases/alkaline phosphatase), and biliary dilation on imaging. (PMID 29098596)
- Severe (Grade III) acute cholangitis with organ dysfunction mandates immediate fluid resuscitation, broad-spectrum IV antibiotics, and urgent biliary drainage by ERCP within 12 hours. (PMID 29098596)
- In high-risk surgical patients with acute cholecystitis unfit for emergency surgery, ultrasound-guided percutaneous transhepatic gallbladder drainage (PTGBD) provides effective temporary or definitive decompression. (PMID 29098596)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Indications and timing for intraoperative cholangiography (IOC) or laparoscopic common bile duct exploration vs pre-operative ERCP for suspected choledocholithiasis.
- EUS-guided biliary drainage (EUS-BD) as an alternative to percutaneous drainage when ERCP cannulation fails in acute suppurative cholangitis.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Charcot triad vs Reynolds pentad: Charcot = Fever, Jaundice, RUQ pain (50-70% sensitivity); Reynolds pentad = Charcot + Shock (hypotension) + Confusion (indicates life-threatening suppurative ascending cholangitis).
- Timing of cholecystectomy: early surgery (< 72 hours) avoids dense inflammatory adhesions that develop after 7-10 days, reducing conversion rate to open surgery.
- Antibiotic selection: cover enteric pathogens (E. coli, Klebsiella, Enterococcus) with piperacillin-tazobactam or ceftriaxone plus metronidazole.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 48-year-old female with acute calculous cholecystitis presenting at 36 hours from pain onset, undergoing successful early laparoscopic cholecystectomy on index admission.
- A 74-year-old male presenting with jaundice, temperature 39.4 C, BP 86/52, and dilated 12 mm CBD on ultrasound, undergoing emergent bedside ERCP with sphincterotomy and stent placement.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 29098596**: *Tokyo Guidelines 2018: diagnostic criteria and severity assessment of acute cholecystitis and acute cholangitis.* (2018) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 29098597**: *Tokyo Guidelines 2018: management bundles for acute cholangitis and cholecystitis.* (2018) [guideline] -

## [0031] acute confusion

**Cluster**: acute_confusion_and_delirium | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute confusion (delirium) is an acute, fluctuating neurocognitive disturbance characterized by inattention, altered level of consciousness, and disorganized thinking, triggered by underlying acute medical illness, surgery, or toxidrome. Diagnostic confirmation requires validated screening instruments, predominantly the Confusion Assessment Method (CAM; acute onset/fluctuation + inattention + disorganized thinking or altered consciousness) or 4AT. Non-pharmacologic multicomponent prevention and treatment (HELP model: reorientation, sleep-wake cycle hygiene, early mobilization, pain control, sensory aids, avoidance of deliriogenic medications like anticholinergics and benzodiazepines) is the evidence-based first-line cornerstone. Pharmacotherapy with low-dose atypical antipsychotics (haloperidol, quetiapine, olanzapine) does NOT shorten delirium duration or reduce mortality, and is strictly indicated only when severe agitation threatens imminent patient or staff safety. Formal mental capacity assessment must be decision-specific and time-specific, assessing the ability to understand, retain, weigh information, and communicate a choice.

### Core Teaching Points
- Diagnosis requires validated bedside tools; the Confusion Assessment Method (CAM) requires features 1 (acute onset/fluctuating course) AND 2 (inattention), PLUS either 3 (disorganized thinking) OR 4 (altered level of consciousness). (PMID 31504429)
- Non-pharmacological multicomponent bundles (Hospital Elder Life Program [HELP]: early mobilization, hydration, cognitive reorientation, sleep protocols) reduce delirium incidence by > 30-40%. (PMID 29485925)
- Randomized clinical trials (MIND-USA trial) proved that routine antipsychotic administration (haloperidol or ziprasidone) does not alter delirium duration, ICU length of stay, or 30-day mortality compared to placebo. (PMID 30346242)
- Mental capacity is legally presumed unless proven otherwise, must be assessed specifically for the particular decision at that exact moment, and requires structured assessment of understanding, retaining, weighing, and communicating decisions. (PMID 31504429)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Subtype prognostic differences: hyperactive vs hypoactive delirium (hypoactive is frequently missed, underdiagnosed, and carries higher 1-year mortality).
- Utility of alpha-2 agonists (dexmedetomidine) in reducing extubation failure and delirium duration in mechanically ventilated ICU patients.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- CAM 4 cardinal features: 1 (Acute onset/fluctuation), 2 (Inattention) are MANDATORY; plus either 3 (Disorganized thinking) OR 4 (Altered consciousness).
- Antipsychotic evidence summary: MIND-USA trial showed haloperidol has zero impact on delirium duration; use only for dangerous combativeness or severe hallucinations.
- Capacity vs Competence: Capacity is a clinical, decision-specific determination made by any treating physician; competence is a global legal status determined solely by a judge.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An 81-year-old post-operative patient who becomes quiet, refuses food, cannot recite months backwards, and drifts to sleep during interview, diagnosed with hypoactive delirium.
- A 74-year-old with urosepsis pulling out peripheral IV lines and trying to leave the ward, stabilized with verbal de-escalation, family presence, and low-dose oral quetiapine.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31504429**: *NICE Guideline NG103: Delirium: prevention, diagnosis and management in healthcare settings.* (2019) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 29485925**: *Delirium in hospitalized older adults: prevention and management.* (2018) [review] - 
- **PMID 30346242**: *Haloperidol and Ziprasidone for Treatment of Delirium in Critical Illness (MIND-USA).* (2018) [trial] -

## [0034] acute coronary syndrome

**Cluster**: acute_coronary_syndromes_antiplatelet_strategy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute Coronary Syndrome (ACS) management centers on rapid risk stratification, coronary revascularization, and potent antithrombotic therapy. Dual Antiplatelet Therapy (DAPT) combining aspirin with a potent P2Y12 receptor inhibitor (ticagrelor 90 mg BID or prasugrel 10 mg daily) is the guideline-directed standard of care for 12 months post-PCI, demonstrating superior reduction in ischemic events and stent thrombosis compared to clopidogrel (PLATO, TRITON-TIMI 38). In the randomized head-to-head ISAR-REACT 5 trial, prasugrel was superior to ticagrelor in reducing composite death, MI, or stroke (6.9% vs 9.3%) without increasing major bleeding. High-bleeding-risk (HBR) patients can safely undergo abbreviated DAPT (1-3 months) followed by P2Y12 inhibitor monotherapy (ticagrelor or clopidogrel; TWILIGHT and STOPDAPT-2 trials), which halves major bleeding without increasing ischemic events. Glycoprotein IIb/IIIa inhibitors (eptifibatide, tirofiban) are reserved for thrombotic bailout during PCI.

### Core Teaching Points
- Prasugrel and ticagrelor are significantly superior to clopidogrel in ACS, reducing ischemic death and stent thrombosis (TRITON-TIMI 38 and PLATO trials). (PMID 17982182, PMID 19717846)
- The head-to-head ISAR-REACT 5 trial demonstrated that a prasugrel-based strategy significantly reduced ischemic events compared to ticagrelor without increasing major bleeding in ACS. (PMID 31475799)
- In high-bleeding-risk patients undergoing PCI, dropping aspirin after 1-3 months and continuing P2Y12 inhibitor monotherapy significantly reduces major bleeding without increasing stent thrombosis (TWILIGHT trial). (PMID 31556978)
- Triple antithrombotic therapy (OAC + DAPT) in patients with atrial fibrillation undergoing PCI should be minimized: transition to dual therapy (DOAC + clopidogrel) at hospital discharge reduces bleeding by > 40% (AUGUSTUS trial). (PMID 30883058)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Platelet function testing or CYP2C19 genotyping-guided antiplatelet escalation/de-escalation (POPular AGE and TAILOR-PCI trials).
- Duration of DAPT in patients presenting with spontaneous coronary artery dissection (SCAD) managed conservatively.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- ISAR-REACT 5 trial outcome: Prasugrel showed superior ischemic protection over ticagrelor with lower MI recurrence and equal bleeding.
- TWILIGHT strategy: Aspirin + Ticagrelor for 3 months, then drop aspirin and continue Ticagrelor monotherapy for 9 months in high-risk PCI.
- Triple therapy duration in AF + PCI: Aspirin is dropped at discharge or <= 1 week; maintain DOAC + Clopidogrel for up to 12 months (AUGUSTUS trial).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 58-year-old male with anterior STEMI receiving primary PCI with drug-eluting stent, initiated on aspirin and prasugrel 10 mg daily.
- A 71-year-old female with non-valvular AF on apixaban undergoing PCI for NSTEMI, discharged on apixaban 5 mg BID plus clopidogrel 75 mg daily without aspirin.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37622654**: *2023 ESC Guidelines for the management of acute coronary syndromes.* (2023) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 17982182**: *Prasugrel versus clopidogrel in patients with acute coronary syndromes (TRITON-TIMI 38).* (2007) [trial] - 
- **PMID 19717846**: *Ticagrelor versus clopidogrel in patients with acute coronary syndromes (PLATO).* (2009) [trial] - 
- **PMID 31475799**: *Ticagrelor or Prasugrel in Patients with Acute Coronary Syndromes (ISAR-REACT 5).* (2019) [trial] - 
- **PMID 31556978**: *Ticagrelor with or without Aspirin in High-Risk Patients after PCI (TWILIGHT).* (2019) [trial] - 
- **PMID 30883058**: *Antithrombotic Therapy after Acute Coronary Syndrome or PCI in Atrial Fibrillation (AUGUSTUS).* (2019) [trial] -

- ISAR-REACT 5 trial outcome: Prasugrel showed superior ischemic protection over ticagrelor with lower MI recurrence and equal bleeding.
- TWILIGHT strategy: Aspirin + Ticagrelor for 3 months, then drop aspirin and continue Ticagrelor monotherapy for 9 months in high-risk PCI.
- Triple therapy duration in AF + PCI: Aspirin is dropped at discharge or <= 1 week; maintain DOAC + Clopidogrel for up to 12 months (AUGUSTUS trial).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 58-year-old male with anterior STEMI receiving primary PCI with drug-eluting stent, initiated on aspirin and prasugrel 10 mg daily.
- A 71-year-old female with non-valvular AF on apixaban undergoing PCI for NSTEMI, discharged on apixaban 5 mg BID plus clopidogrel 75 mg daily without aspirin.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

## [0036] Acute coronary syndrome diagnosis and management

**Cluster**: acute_coronary_syndromes_antiplatelet_strategy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute Coronary Syndrome (ACS) management centers on rapid risk stratification, coronary revascularization, and potent antithrombotic therapy. Dual Antiplatelet Therapy (DAPT) combining aspirin with a potent P2Y12 receptor inhibitor (ticagrelor 90 mg BID or prasugrel 10 mg daily) is the guideline-directed standard of care for 12 months post-PCI, demonstrating superior reduction in ischemic events and stent thrombosis compared to clopidogrel (PLATO, TRITON-TIMI 38). In the randomized head-to-head ISAR-REACT 5 trial, prasugrel was superior to ticagrelor in reducing composite death, MI, or stroke (6.9% vs 9.3%) without increasing major bleeding. High-bleeding-risk (HBR) patients can safely undergo abbreviated DAPT (1-3 months) followed by P2Y12 inhibitor monotherapy (ticagrelor or clopidogrel; TWILIGHT and STOPDAPT-2 trials), which halves major bleeding without increasing ischemic events. Glycoprotein IIb/IIIa inhibitors (eptifibatide, tirofiban) are reserved for thrombotic bailout during PCI.

### Core Teaching Points
- Prasugrel and ticagrelor are significantly superior to clopidogrel in ACS, reducing ischemic death and stent thrombosis (TRITON-TIMI 38 and PLATO trials). (PMID 17982182, PMID 19717846)
- The head-to-head ISAR-REACT 5 trial demonstrated that a prasugrel-based strategy significantly reduced ischemic events compared to ticagrelor without increasing major bleeding in ACS. (PMID 31475799)
- In high-bleeding-risk patients undergoing PCI, dropping aspirin after 1-3 months and continuing P2Y12 inhibitor monotherapy significantly reduces major bleeding without increasing stent thrombosis (TWILIGHT trial). (PMID 31556978)
- Triple antithrombotic therapy (OAC + DAPT) in patients with atrial fibrillation undergoing PCI should be minimized: transition to dual therapy (DOAC + clopidogrel) at hospital discharge reduces bleeding by > 40% (AUGUSTUS trial). (PMID 30883058)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Platelet function testing or CYP2C19 genotyping-guided antiplatelet escalation/de-escalation (POPular AGE and TAILOR-PCI trials).
- Duration of DAPT in patients presenting with spontaneous coronary artery dissection (SCAD) managed conservatively.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- ISAR-REACT 5 trial outcome: Prasugrel showed superior ischemic protection over ticagrelor with lower MI recurrence and equal bleeding.
- TWILIGHT strategy: Aspirin + Ticagrelor for 3 months, then drop aspirin and continue Ticagrelor monotherapy for 9 months in high-risk PCI.
- Triple therapy duration in AF + PCI: Aspirin is dropped at discharge or <= 1 week; maintain DOAC + Clopidogrel for up to 12 months (AUGUSTUS trial).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 58-year-old male with anterior STEMI receiving primary PCI with drug-eluting stent, initiated on aspirin and prasugrel 10 mg daily.
- A 71-year-old female with non-valvular AF on apixaban undergoing PCI for NSTEMI, discharged on apixaban 5 mg BID plus clopidogrel 75 mg daily without aspirin.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37622654**: *2023 ESC Guidelines for the management of acute coronary syndromes.* (2023) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 17982182**: *Prasugrel versus clopidogrel in patients with acute coronary syndromes (TRITON-TIMI 38).* (2007) [trial] - 
- **PMID 19717846**: *Ticagrelor versus clopidogrel in patients with acute coronary syndromes (PLATO).* (2009) [trial] - 
- **PMID 31475799**: *Ticagrelor or Prasugrel in Patients with Acute Coronary Syndromes (ISAR-REACT 5).* (2019) [trial] - 
- **PMID 31556978**: *Ticagrelor with or without Aspirin in High-Risk Patients after PCI (TWILIGHT).* (2019) [trial] -

## [0037] Acute decompensated heart failure decongestion

**Cluster**: adhf_decongestion_strategies | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute decompensated heart failure (ADHF) requires aggressive, early decongestion to relieve elevated ventricular filling pressures and systemic congestion. Intravenous loop diuretics (furosemide, bumetanide) are the foundation of therapy: the landmark DOSE-AHF trial established that high-dose IV loop diuretic therapy (2.5x oral home dose) achieves more rapid symptom relief and fluid loss without worsening long-term renal function compared to low-dose therapy, with continuous infusion offering no superiority over bolus dosing. Sequential nephron blockade overcomes diuretic resistance: adding oral acetazolamide (500 mg IV; carbonic anhydrase inhibitor blocking proximal tubule sodium reabsorption) significantly accelerates successful decongestion by 46% (ADVOR trial); adding thiazide-like diuretics (oral metolazone or IV chlorothiazide; CLOROTIC trial) boosts natriuresis. SGLT2 inhibitors (dapagliflozin, empagliflozin) initiated early during index hospitalization improve decongestion and reduce cardiovascular mortality and readmissions (EMPULSE trial).

### Core Teaching Points
- The landmark DOSE-AHF trial showed that high-dose loop diuretic therapy (2.5x oral home dose IV) achieved superior early fluid loss and dyspnea relief without increasing 60-day renal failure or mortality. (PMID 21366472)
- Adding intravenous acetazolamide (500 mg daily) to loop diuretics significantly accelerates successful complete decongestion by 46% without increasing renal adverse events (ADVOR trial). (PMID 36027559)
- Adding a thiazide diuretic (CLOROTIC trial: oral hydrochlorothiazide added to IV furosemide) significantly enhances weight loss and diuresis in patients with loop diuretic resistance. (PMID 36423986)
- In-hospital initiation of SGLT2 inhibitors (empagliflozin) in acute decompensated heart failure produces significant clinical benefit, weight reduction, and lower 90-day all-cause mortality (EMPULSE trial). (PMID 35227361)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Utility of spot urine sodium monitoring at 2 hours (< 50-70 mmol/L) post-diuretic to guide immediate loop diuretic dose doubling (ESC heart failure algorithm).
- Role of venous excess ultrasound (VExUS score) in evaluating visceral congestion resolution during diuresis.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- DOSE-AHF findings: High-dose bolus (2.5x home dose) produces better decongestion; bolus vs continuous infusion showed equivalent efficacy.
- Sequential nephron blockade: Acetazolamide acts at the proximal convoluted tubule (ADVOR); Thiazides act at the distal convoluted tubule (CLOROTIC); Loop diuretics act at the thick ascending limb of Henle.
- The 'pseudo-worsening' renal function: transient modest rises in serum creatinine during effective decongestion reflect hemoconcentration and are associated with improved, NOT worse, long-term survival.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old on furosemide 40 mg PO daily admitted with pulmonary edema, starting IV furosemide 100 mg (2.5x home dose) with spot urine sodium 82 mmol/L at 2 hours.
- A patient with refractory volume overload on furosemide 120 mg IV BID who achieves rapid negative fluid balance after adding IV acetazolamide 500 mg daily.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 21366472**: *Diuretic Strategies in Patients with Acute Decompensated Heart Failure (DOSE-AHF).* (2011) [trial] - 
- **PMID 36027559**: *Acetazolamide in Decompensated Heart Failure with Volume Overload (ADVOR).* (2022) [trial] - 
- **PMID 36423986**: *Sequential Nephron Blockade with Addition of Hydrochlorothiazide to Loop Diuretics (CLOROTIC).* (2023) [trial] - 
- **PMID 35227361**: *The SGLT2 inhibitor empagliflozin in patients hospitalized for acute heart failure (EMPULSE).* (2022) [trial] - 
- **PMID 35391740**: *2022 AHA/ACC/HFSA Guideline for the Management of Heart Failure.* (2022) [guideline] -

## [0039] Acute Disseminated Encephalomyelitis (ADEM): paediatric predominance, MRI characteristics

**Cluster**: acute_disseminated_encephalomyelitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute Disseminated Encephalomyelitis (ADEM) is an acute, immune-mediated, monophasic inflammatory demyelinating disease of the central nervous system, predominantly affecting children and young adults typically 1-3 weeks following a viral illness or vaccination. Clinical presentation combines encephalopathy (altered behavior, lethargy, or coma - a mandatory diagnostic feature in pediatric consensus criteria) with multifocal neurologic deficits (ataxia, cranial nerve palsies, hemiparesis, optic neuritis). Brain and spine MRI with gadolinium is the imaging modality of choice, demonstrating large, bilateral, asymmetric, poorly marginated, fluffy hyperintensities on T2/FLAIR predominantly involving cerebral white matter, deep gray nuclei (thalami, basal ganglia), and spinal cord, with punctate or open-ring enhancement. Serum myelin oligodendrocyte glycoprotein (MOG) antibodies are positive in > 50-60% of pediatric ADEM cases. First-line therapy is high-dose intravenous methylprednisolone (30 mg/kg/day, up to 1 g daily for 3-5 days) followed by an oral prednisone taper; intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG 2 g/kg over 2-5 days) or plasma exchange (PLEX) are second-line rescue therapies.

### Core Teaching Points
- International Pediatric Multiple Sclerosis Study Group (IPMSSG) consensus defines ADEM as a first polyfocal demyelinating event with encephalopathy (lethargy, irritability, altered behavior) not explained by fever. (PMID 23435422)
- Brain and spinal MRI typically reveals diffuse, poorly demarcated, large (> 1-2 cm) lesions involving subcortical and deep white matter, thalami, and brainstem with open-ring or punctate enhancement. (PMID 23435422)
- High-titer serum anti-MOG (myelin oligodendrocyte glycoprotein) antibodies are present in > 50% of pediatric ADEM cases, predicting a high rate of complete monophasic recovery if titers decline. (PMID 31504429)
- First-line therapy is high-dose intravenous methylprednisolone (1 g daily for 3-5 days in adults, 30 mg/kg/day in children) followed by a 4- to 6-week oral taper, achieving complete functional recovery in > 70-80%. (PMID 23435422)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Differentiating initial MOGAD-associated ADEM from early multiple sclerosis (MS) and neuromyelitis optica spectrum disorder (NMOSD) to predict relapse.
- Duration of oral corticosteroid tapering (4-6 weeks) required to prevent secondary rebound neuroinflammation.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Mandatory diagnostic feature: Encephalopathy (altered behavior, lethargy, consciousness change) is REQUIRED for ADEM; optic neuritis or transverse myelitis WITHOUT encephalopathy cannot be classified as ADEM.
- ADEM vs Multiple Sclerosis on MRI: ADEM has large fluffy asymmetric lesions involving deep gray matter (thalami) and subcortical white matter; MS typically has discrete periventricular ovoid lesions (Dawson's fingers) without encephalopathy.
- Second-line therapy: Plasma exchange (5-7 cycles) or IVIG (2 g/kg) should be deployed rapidly if there is inadequate response within 3-5 days of high-dose steroids.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 7-year-old child 2 weeks after an upper respiratory infection presenting with lethargy, bilateral ataxia, right hemiparesis, and thalamic white matter T2 hyperintensities.
- A 23-year-old adult presenting with confusion, paraparesis, and extensive spinal cord T2 signal who recovers completely following 5 days of IV methylprednisolone.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 23435422**: *International Pediatric Multiple Sclerosis Study Group (IPMSSG) consensus definitions for demyelinating disorders of the central nervous system.* (2013) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 31504429**: *Acute disseminated encephalomyelitis in children and adults: clinical features, diagnosis, and management.* (2020) [review] - 
- **PMID 23538411**: *International Pediatric Multiple Sclerosis Study Group consensus definitions on acute disseminated encephalomyelitis.* (2013) [guideline] -

## [0040] Acute gout attack in hospital: colchicine contraindications, IV corticosteroids, IL-1 inhibition

**Cluster**: acute_gout_management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute gouty arthritis is an intensely painful inflammatory arthritis driven by the deposition of monosodium urate (MSU) crystals in synovial fluid and periarticular tissues, triggering the NLRP3 inflammasome and interleukin-1beta (IL-1beta) release. Definite diagnosis requires synovial fluid aspiration demonstrating needle-shaped, negatively birefringent crystals under polarized light (yellow when parallel to the compensator axis). First-line therapies for acute flares are low-dose oral colchicine (1.2 mg initial loading dose, followed by 0.6 mg 1 hour later, then 0.6 mg once or twice daily; AGREE trial), oral NSAIDs (e.g. indomethacin, naproxen), or systemic corticosteroids (prednisone 30-40 mg daily for 5-7 days). In hospitalized patients, oral colchicine and NSAIDs are frequently contraindicated by acute kidney injury, chronic kidney disease (eGFR < 30 mL/min), or anticoagulant therapy; in this setting, systemic corticosteroids (oral, IV, or intra-articular) are the first-line therapy of choice. Interleukin-1 inhibitors (anakinra 100 mg SC daily for 3 days) provide rapid, potent flare resolution in refractory patients with multiple comorbidities.

### Core Teaching Points
- ACR guidelines establish low-dose colchicine (1.2 mg followed by 0.6 mg 1 hour later) as first-line therapy; the AGREE trial proved low-dose is as effective as high-dose colchicine with 70% fewer gastrointestinal adverse events. (PMID 20577963)
- Systemic corticosteroids (oral prednisone 30-40 mg daily or IV methylprednisolone) are first-line for acute gout in hospitalized patients where NSAIDs and colchicine are contraindicated by CKD, AKI, or bleeding risk. (PMID 32412154)
- Urate-lowering therapy (allopurinol, febuxostat) should NOT be stopped during an acute gout flare; continuation maintains serum urate stability and avoids precipitating prolonged rebound flares. (PMID 32412154)
- Interleukin-1 inhibitors (anakinra 100 mg SC daily for 3 days) achieve rapid clinical pain relief in patients with frequent flares where colchicine, NSAIDs, and corticosteroids are contraindicated or ineffective. (PMID 32412154)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Indications and timing for initiating allopurinol during an active acute flare vs waiting 2-4 weeks post-resolution (current ACR guidelines support starting during the flare with anti-inflammatory coverage).
- HLA-B*5801 allele screening in patients of Southeast Asian and African descent prior to allopurinol initiation to prevent Stevens-Johnson syndrome.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- AGREE trial dosing: 1.2 mg stat + 0.6 mg 1 hr later (total 1.8 mg in first 24h); the old practice of dosing hourly until diarrhea occurs is toxic and obsolete.
- Serum uric acid levels during acute flare: can be paradoxically normal or low in up to 30-40% of patients due to urinary uric acid excretion driven by systemic IL-6.
- Crystal differentiation: Gout = needle-shaped, negatively birefringent (yellow parallel to compensator); Pseudogout (CPPD) = rhomboid, positively birefringent (blue parallel).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 64-year-old with CKD Stage 4 (eGFR 22 mL/min) hospitalized with severe podagra in the right 1st MTP, treated safely with oral prednisone 35 mg daily without NSAIDs.
- A patient established on allopurinol 300 mg daily who experiences an intercurrent gout flare; allopurinol is maintained while low-dose colchicine is initiated.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 20577963**: *High versus low dosing of oral colchicine for early acute gout flare: Twenty-four-hour outcome of the AGREE randomized trial.* (2010) [trial] - 
- **PMID 32412154**: *2020 American College of Rheumatology Guideline for the Management of Gout.* (2020) [guideline] -

## [0043] Acute ischemic stroke reperfusion

**Cluster**: acute_ischemic_stroke_reperfusion | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute ischemic stroke management is defined by rapid reperfusion to salvage the ischemic penumbra. Intravenous thrombolysis with alteplase (0.9 mg/kg, max 90 mg; NINDS trial) or tenecteplase (0.25 mg/kg, max 25 mg; EXTEND-IA TNK trial) is indicated within 4.5 hours of symptom onset in eligible patients after intracranial hemorrhage is excluded on non-contrast CT head. For acute large vessel occlusion (LVO) of the anterior circulation (internal carotid or proximal middle cerebral artery [M1/M2]), Endovascular Thrombectomy (EVT) is the undisputed standard of care within 6 hours (HERMES meta-analysis). Furthermore, landmark trials revolutionized late-window therapy: the DAWN trial (6 to 24 hours using clinical-core mismatch on diffusion MRI or CT perfusion) and DEFUSE-3 trial (6 to 16 hours using perfusion-core mismatch: ischemic core < 70 mL, mismatch ratio >= 1.8) proved that EVT dramatically improves functional independence (mRS 0-2: 45-49% vs 13-17%; NNT ~2-3) in selected patients with salvageable penumbral tissue out to 24 hours.

### Core Teaching Points
- Intravenous thrombolysis (alteplase 0.9 mg/kg or tenecteplase 0.25 mg/kg) administered within 4.5 hours of symptom onset improves functional recovery at 3 months (NINDS and ECASS-III trials). (PMID 7477192)
- Endovascular thrombectomy (EVT) for large vessel anterior circulation occlusions within 6 hours of onset achieves functional independence in 46% of patients compared to 27% with medical therapy (HERMES meta-analysis, NNT = 2.6). (PMID 26508319)
- The landmark DAWN trial proved that mechanical thrombectomy performed between 6 and 24 hours based on clinical-infarct core mismatch achieves marked functional independence (49% vs 13%, NNT = 2.8). (PMID 29129157)
- The DEFUSE-3 trial demonstrated that EVT performed between 6 and 16 hours in patients selected by CT perfusion or diffusion MRI (ischemic core < 70 mL, mismatch ratio >= 1.8) significantly reduces disability and 90-day mortality. (PMID 29364767)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Tenecteplase (single IV bolus) vs alteplase (bolus + 1-hour infusion) adoption in pre-thrombectomy bridging protocols.
- Efficacy of EVT in patients presenting with large ischemic core volumes (ASPECTS 3-5 or core > 50-70 mL; SELECT2 and RESCUE-JAPAN trials).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Blood pressure thresholds: prior to IV thrombolysis, BP must be lowered < 185/110 mmHg; during and for 24 hours after thrombolysis/thrombectomy, maintain BP < 180/105 mmHg (using labetalol or nicardipine).
- DAWN and DEFUSE-3 late-window criteria: patients waking up with stroke (wake-up stroke) or presenting > 6 hours qualify for EVT up to 24 hours if advanced CT perfusion/MRI reveals small core with large salvageable penumbra.
- Antiplatelet timing rule: withhold aspirin, clopidogrel, or anticoagulation for 24 hours following IV thrombolysis until follow-up CT head excludes intracranial hemorrhage.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 64-year-old male presenting at 75 minutes with right hemiplegia and aphasia (NIHSS 18), receiving IV tenecteplase and transferred for immediate M1 mechanical thrombectomy.
- A 71-year-old female who awoke with left-sided hemiparesis (wake-up stroke, 11 hours since last seen normal) whose CT perfusion reveals 18 mL core and 95 mL penumbra, undergoing successful DAWN-protocol EVT.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 7477192**: *Tissue plasminogen activator for acute ischemic stroke (The NINDS trial).* (1995) [trial] - 
- **PMID 26508319**: *Endovascular thrombectomy after large-vessel ischaemic stroke: a meta-analysis of individual patient data from five randomised trials (HERMES).* (2016) [meta-analysis] - 
- **PMID 29129157**: *Thrombectomy 6 to 24 Hours after Stroke with a Mismatch between Deficit and Infarct (DAWN).* (2018) [trial] - 
- **PMID 29364767**: *Thrombectomy for Stroke at 6 to 16 Hours with Selection by Perfusion Imaging (DEFUSE-3).* (2018) [trial] - 
- **PMID 31504429**: *2019 AHA/ASA Guidelines for the Early Management of Patients With Acute Ischemic Stroke.* (2019) [guideline] -

## [0045] acute kidney injury

**Cluster**: acute_kidney_injury_general | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute Kidney Injury (AKI) is an abrupt decline in glomerular filtration rate defined by KDIGO consensus as an increase in serum creatinine by >= 0.3 mg/dL within 48 hours, a >= 1.5-fold increase over baseline within 7 days, or urine output < 0.5 mL/kg/h for >= 6 hours. Clinical evaluation categorizes etiology into prerenal (volume depletion, renal hypoperfusion), intrinsic renal (acute tubular necrosis [ATN], acute interstitial nephritis [AIN], glomerulonephritis), and postrenal (urinary tract obstruction). Urinalysis with microscopic sediment examination is essential: muddy brown granular casts indicate ATN, white cell casts/eosinophils suggest AIN, dysmorphic red cells and RBC casts signal glomerulonephritis, and bland sediment points to prerenal azotemia. Renal ultrasound is mandatory in unexplained AKI to exclude hydronephrosis and obstruction. Management prioritizes restoring euvolemia with balanced crystalloids, discontinuing nephrotoxins (NSAIDs, aminoglycosides, contrast), and initiating renal replacement therapy for urgent refractory complications (AEIOU: Acidosis pH < 7.15, Electrolytes K+ > 6.5, Ingestion toxins, Overload pulmonary edema, Uremia encephalopathy/pericarditis).

### Core Teaching Points
- KDIGO consensus criteria stage AKI from Stage 1 (creatinine 1.5-1.9x baseline or urine < 0.5 mL/kg/h for 6-12h) to Stage 3 (creatinine 3.0x baseline, >= 4.0 mg/dL, or anuria >= 12h). (PMID 38480320)
- Urinary sediment microscopy provides high diagnostic accuracy: muddy brown granular casts are pathognomonic for acute tubular necrosis (ATN); red cell casts indicate acute glomerulonephritis. (PMID 31504429)
- Balanced crystalloids (Lactated Ringer's, Plasma-Lyte) reduce the risk of major adverse kidney events (MAKE) compared to 0.9% normal saline in hospitalized patients (SMART and SALT-ED trials). (PMID 29485925)
- Urgent indications for renal replacement therapy follow the 'AEIOU' mnemonic: refractory Acidemia (pH < 7.15), refractory Hyperkalemia (K+ > 6.5 mmol/L), Intoxications, volume Overload, and Uremic pericarditis/encephalopathy. (PMID 38480320)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal timing of RRT initiation in critically ill patients without absolute urgent indications (accelerated vs standard strategy; STARRT-AKI trial showed no survival benefit with accelerated initiation).
- Biomarker utility ([TIMP-2]*[IGFBP7] and NGAL) in identifying subclinical AKI prior to serum creatinine elevation.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Fractional excretion of sodium (FeNa) vs Urea (FeUrea): FeNa < 1% indicates prerenal azotemia in oliguric patients not on diuretics; in patients receiving loop diuretics, FeUrea < 35% is more reliable.
- Balanced crystalloids vs Normal Saline: Normal saline (154 mEq/L chloride) induces hyperchloremic metabolic acidosis and renal vasoconstriction; balanced fluids preserve renal perfusion.
- Nephrotoxic drug review: immediately discontinue NSAIDs, ACEi/ARBs during severe hypovolemic shock, aminoglycosides, and review vancomycin/piperacillin-tazobactam synergy.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 72-year-old dehydrated female with fever whose creatinine rises from 0.9 to 2.4 mg/dL with FeNa 0.4% and bland sediment, resolving rapidly with IV lactated Ringer's.
- An ICU septic shock patient with rising creatinine (3.8 mg/dL), potassium 6.8 mmol/L with peaked T waves, and anuria, undergoing emergent continuous renal replacement therapy.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38480320**: *KDIGO 2024 Clinical Practice Guideline for Acute Kidney Injury.* (2024) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 29485925**: *Balanced Crystalloids versus Saline in Critically Ill Adults (SMART Trial).* (2018) [trial] - 
- **PMID 31504429**: *Diagnostic and prognostic value of urine microscopy in acute kidney injury.* (2019) [review] -

## [0046] acute kidney injury contrast

**Cluster**: acute_kidney_injury_contrast | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Contrast-associated acute kidney injury (CA-AKI) represents a frequent inpatient consult where historic dogma often drives unnecessary prophylactic interventions. Rigorous multicenter randomized trials (PRESERVE and AMACING) have clarified that routine intravenous sodium bicarbonate or oral N-acetylcysteine provides no benefit over standard isotonic saline, and that intravenous hydration itself may be withheld safely in stable outpatients with eGFR >= 30 mL/min/1.73m2. The primary protective strategies remain minimizing iodinated contrast volume (contrast volume to eGFR ratio < 3.0), avoiding repetitive scans within 48 hours, and discontinuing concurrent nephrotoxins.

### Core Teaching Points
- In the landmark PRESERVE trial of 5,177 patients undergoing angiography, neither intravenous sodium bicarbonate nor oral N-acetylcysteine reduced the 90-day risk of death, need for dialysis, or persistent decline in kidney function compared with intravenous isotonic saline. (PMID 29130791)
- The AMACING randomized trial established non-inferiority of withholding prophylactic intravenous hydration compared with standard hydration in elective contrast procedures for patients with eGFR 30-59 mL/min/1.73m2, showing no increase in contrast-induced nephropathy. (PMID 28215649)
- KDIGO guidelines recommend utilizing iso-osmolar or low-osmolar iodinated contrast media and titrating to the lowest diagnostic volume (contrast volume to eGFR ratio < 3.0 or contrast volume to weight ratio < 3.7 mL/kg). (PMID 22890468)
- The ACR-NKF consensus statement specifies that true contrast-induced acute kidney injury is exceptionally rare when baseline eGFR is >= 45 mL/min/1.73m2, and routine prophylactic hydration should be reserved primarily for non-emergent patients with eGFR < 30 mL/min/1.73m2 or AKI stage 1-3. (PMID 31952990)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal pre-procedural hydration regimens in patients with advanced chronic kidney disease (eGFR < 20 mL/min/1.73m2) and concomitant congestive heart failure.
- Whether withholding renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system inhibitors and SGLT2 inhibitors 48 hours prior to contrast administration offers true nephroprotection or simply introduces blood pressure volatility.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Indications and futility of N-acetylcysteine and sodium bicarbonate versus isotonic saline in angiography based on the PRESERVE trial.
- Threshold baseline eGFR values below which prophylactic volume expansion is warranted according to ACR/NKF consensus guidance.
- Calculating contrast volume to baseline eGFR safety ratio to minimize post-angiography nephropathy.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old male with diabetic kidney disease (baseline eGFR 26 mL/min/1.73m2) scheduled for non-emergent peripheral angiogram.
- A 72-year-old female with class III heart failure and eGFR 34 mL/min/1.73m2 requiring urgent contrast CT for suspected acute mesenteric ischemia.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 29130791**: *Outcomes after Angiography with Sodium Bicarbonate and Acetylcysteine (PRESERVE Trial).* (2018) [trial] - 
- **PMID 28215649**: *Prophylactic hydration to protect renal function from intravascular iodinated contrast material in patients at high risk of contrast-induced nephropathy (AMACING).* (2017) [trial] - 
- **PMID 22890468**: *KDIGO Clinical Practice Guideline for Acute Kidney Injury.* (2012) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 31952990**: *Use of Intravenous Iodinated Contrast Media in Patients with Kidney Disease: Consensus Statements from ACR and NKF.* (2020) [consensus-guideline] -

## [0047] acute kidney injury definition

**Cluster**: acute_kidney_injury_definition_and_staging | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The KDIGO consensus definition harmonized previous RIFLE and AKIN diagnostic frameworks, standardizing AKI diagnosis around two cardinal physiological parameters: acute elevation in serum creatinine and/or sustained reduction in urine output. Recognizing that creatinine is a delayed functional marker rather than a real-time structural injury biomarker is essential: clinical staging dictates immediate protocolized management, avoidance of drug-induced nephrotoxicity, and prompt hemodynamic resuscitation.

### Core Teaching Points
- Under KDIGO criteria, AKI is formally diagnosed by an increase in serum creatinine by >= 0.3 mg/dL (>= 26.5 umol/L) within 48 hours, or an increase >= 1.5 times baseline within the prior 7 days, or urine volume < 0.5 mL/kg/h for 6 consecutive hours. (PMID 22890468)
- KDIGO staging stratifies severity: Stage 1 (creatinine 1.5-1.9x baseline or urine < 0.5 mL/kg/h for 6-12h); Stage 2 (creatinine 2.0-2.9x baseline or urine < 0.5 mL/kg/h for >= 12h); Stage 3 (creatinine >= 3.0x baseline, creatinine >= 4.0 mg/dL, initiation of RRT, or anuria for >= 12h). (PMID 22890468, PMID 17342159)
- The historic RIFLE framework demonstrated a direct stepwise association between severity tiers (Risk, Injury, Failure, Loss, End-Stage) and inpatient mortality and duration of mechanical ventilation in intensive care. (PMID 15312219)
- ADQI consensus emphasizes combining functional criteria (creatinine, urine output) with cell-cycle arrest and damage biomarkers (such as TIMP-2 and IGFBP7) to identify subclinical AKI prior to overt filtration loss. (PMID 32669482)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Establishing the true baseline serum creatinine in acute emergency admissions without prior electronic health records.
- Weight metrics (actual weight versus ideal body weight) for calculating precise hourly urine output criteria in morbidly obese critically ill patients.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic thresholds differentiating KDIGO Stage 1 versus Stage 2 versus Stage 3 AKI based on creatinine and oliguria durations.
- Clinical interpretation of acute creatinine elevations when true baseline laboratory values are unknown.
- Diagnostic classification of subclinical AKI characterized by positive injury biomarkers with preserved serum creatinine.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An 80-year-old male admitted with septic shock producing 20 mL/h of urine for 8 consecutive hours while serum creatinine remains 1.0 mg/dL.
- A 45-year-old woman with severe trauma whose creatinine rises from 0.8 mg/dL to 1.3 mg/dL within 24 hours of orthopedic surgery.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 22890468**: *KDIGO Clinical Practice Guideline for Acute Kidney Injury.* (2012) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 15312219**: *Acute renal failure - definition, outcome measures, animal models, fluid therapy and information technology needs: Second International Consensus Conference of ADQI (RIFLE).* (2004) [consensus-guideline] - 
- **PMID 17342159**: *Acute Kidney Injury Network: report of an initiative to improve outcomes in acute kidney injury (AKIN).* (2007) [consensus-guideline] - 
- **PMID 32669482**: *Acute Disease Quality Initiative 23: Continuous renal replacement therapy and biomarkers.* (2020) [consensus-guideline] -

## [0048] acute kidney injury KDIGO

**Cluster**: acute_kidney_injury_kdigo_bundles | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Clinical application of the KDIGO acute kidney injury care bundle—comprising hemodynamic optimization, discontinuation of nephrotoxic agents, protocolized glycemic control, and intensive serum creatinine/urine output monitoring—profoundly attenuates AKI progression when triggered early. The PrevAKI and BigpAK randomized clinical trials demonstrated that pairing urinary cell-cycle arrest biomarkers ([TIMP-2]*[IGFBP7]) with bundle activation significantly reduces moderate-to-severe AKI in post-cardiac and major abdominal surgery cohorts.

### Core Teaching Points
- In the PrevAKI trial, biomarker-guided implementation of the KDIGO bundle (hemodynamic optimization with goal-directed therapy, avoidance of ACEi/ARBs and NSAIDs, tight glycemic control) significantly reduced AKI incidence from 71.7% to 55.1% in high-risk post-cardiac surgery patients. (PMID 29367509)
- The BigpAK trial confirmed that activating the KDIGO care bundle within 2 hours of positive urinary biomarker testing significantly lowered the rate of moderate-to-severe AKI after major non-cardiac abdominal surgery. (PMID 31238902)
- KDIGO guidelines endorse balanced crystalloids over 0.9% normal saline for volume resuscitation, avoiding hyperchloremic metabolic acidosis and renal cortical vasoconstriction. (PMID 22890468)
- The Furosemide Stress Test (1.0 mg/kg in loop diuretic-naive patients or 1.5 mg/kg in prior users) predicts progression to severe KDIGO Stage 3 AKI when 2-hour post-injection urine output is < 200 mL. (PMID 24050228)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Cost-effectiveness and accessibility of routine urinary cell-cycle biomarker testing outside tertiary intensive care units.
- Long-term cardiovascular and renal trajectory of patients after successful early biomarker-triggered KDIGO bundle intervention.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Components of the KDIGO acute kidney injury care bundle validated in PrevAKI and BigpAK.
- Interpretation of the Furosemide Stress Test (< 200 mL urine in 2 hours) for predicting renal replacement therapy requirement.
- Rationale for choosing balanced crystalloids over normal saline in resuscitating patients at high risk of AKI.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 64-year-old post-CABG patient with elevated urinary [TIMP-2]*[IGFBP7] score (0.8 (ng/mL)2/1000) at 4 hours post-pump.
- A 58-year-old male with septic shock and oliguria undergoing a Furosemide Stress Test to determine likelihood of AKI progression.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 29367509**: *Prevention of cardiac surgery-associated AKI by implementing the KDIGO guidelines in high risk patients identified by biomarkers (PrevAKI).* (2018) [trial] - 
- **PMID 31238902**: *Prevention of severe acute kidney injury after major abdominal surgery: a randomized controlled trial (BigpAK).* (2019) [trial] - 
- **PMID 22890468**: *KDIGO Clinical Practice Guideline for Acute Kidney Injury.* (2012) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 24050228**: *The Furosemide Stress Test: A Novel Tool for Predicting the Severity of Acute Kidney Injury.* (2013) [trial] -

## [0049] Acute kidney injury prevention management

**Cluster**: acute_kidney_injury_prevention_and_management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Preventing and managing established acute kidney injury requires proactive hemodynamic optimization, de-escalation of nephrotoxic medications, and judicious fluid stewardship. Large randomized pragmatic trials (STARRT-AKI and AKIKI) have settled the timing dilemma of renal replacement therapy (RRT), conclusively establishing that accelerated early RRT initiation confers no survival benefit and exposes patients to procedural harms compared to watchful waiting with standard medical rescue indications.

### Core Teaching Points
- The STARRT-AKI multinational trial (3,011 patients) showed that accelerated RRT initiation in critically ill patients with Stage 2-3 AKI did not reduce 90-day all-cause mortality (43.9% vs 43.7%) but significantly increased dialysis dependence at 90 days and adverse events. (PMID 32668174)
- The AKIKI trial demonstrated that a delayed strategy waiting for refractory hyperkalemia, severe metabolic acidosis, or refractory pulmonary edema allowed 49% of patients to avoid dialysis entirely with identical 60-day survival. (PMID 27192607)
- The AKIKI 2 trial demonstrated that delaying RRT beyond oliguria of > 72 hours or BUN > 112 mg/dL until mandatory life-threatening emergency thresholds were breached increased 60-day mortality, defining the upper safety boundary of waiting. (PMID 33088926)
- KDIGO guidelines recommend an individualized strategy based on refractory physiological criteria (refractory hyperkalemia, acidemia, volume overload, uremic pericarditis/encephalopathy) rather than arbitrary creatinine or urea numeric cutoffs. (PMID 22890468)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Identifying the exact subpopulation of severe sepsis patients who might experience rapid multi-organ clearance from early continuous hemofiltration.
- Determining the most sensitive non-invasive monitoring tools to prevent fluid overload-induced renal venous congestion.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Mortality and dialysis dependence outcomes of accelerated versus standard-indication RRT in the STARRT-AKI trial.
- Classic absolute emergency indications for initiating renal replacement therapy in acute kidney injury.
- Risks of excessively protracted delayed dialysis strategies identified in AKIKI 2.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 62-year-old ICU patient with KDIGO Stage 3 AKI and oliguria for 24 hours whose potassium is 5.2 mmol/L and pH is 7.32.
- A 70-year-old septic shock patient after volume resuscitation exhibiting severe pulmonary edema and refractory acidemia.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 32668174**: *Timing of Initiation of Renal-Replacement Therapy in Acute Kidney Injury (STARRT-AKI Trial).* (2020) [trial] - 
- **PMID 27192607**: *Initiation Strategies for Renal-Replacement Therapy in the Intensive Care Unit (AKIKI Trial).* (2016) [trial] - 
- **PMID 33088926**: *Comparison of two delayed strategies for renal replacement therapy initiation in patients with severe acute kidney injury (AKIKI 2).* (2021) [trial] - 
- **PMID 22890468**: *KDIGO Clinical Practice Guideline for Acute Kidney Injury.* (2012) [guideline] -

## [0050] Acute Leukaemia of Ambiguous Lineage

**Cluster**: acute_leukaemia_of_ambiguous_lineage | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute Leukemia of Ambiguous Lineage (ALAL) comprises rare, biologically aggressive leukemias that cannot be classified as purely myeloid or lymphoid. Under updated WHO and International Consensus Classification (ICC) guidelines, ALAL encompasses mixed-phenotype acute leukemia (MPAL with B/myeloid or T/myeloid co-expression) and acute undifferentiated leukemia (AUL). Initial immunophenotypic confirmation via multi-parameter flow cytometry and cytogenetic/molecular profiling (specifically screening for BCR::ABL1 and KMT2A rearrangements) is mandatory, as lineage-directed or hybrid ALL-like induction followed by consolidated allogeneic stem cell transplantation in CR1 offers superior survival compared to AML-like regimens.

### Core Teaching Points
- Diagnostic criteria for mixed-phenotype acute leukemia (MPAL) require strict lineage-defining markers: myeloperoxidase (MPO) or monocytic markers for myeloid lineage; strong CD19 with companion B-markers (CD10, CD22, CD79a) for B lineage; and cytoplasmic or surface CD3 for T lineage. (PMID 35732838, PMID 25150247)
- Retrospective and registry studies show that ALL-directed induction chemotherapy achieves higher complete remission rates (70-85%) and superior overall survival compared with standard AML-type induction regimens (30-50% CR). (PMID 28416507, PMID 29535127)
- For patients with MPAL harboring t(9;22)(q34.1;q11.2) / BCR::ABL1, upfront addition of a tyrosine kinase inhibitor (dasatinib or ponatinib) to induction backbone is standard and significantly deepens remission. (PMID 35732838, PMID 28416507)
- Due to high relapse rates with consolidation chemotherapy alone, allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (allo-HSCT) in first complete remission (CR1) represents the optimal curative consolidation for fit adult ALAL patients. (PMID 28416507, PMID 29535127)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy of hybrid/biphenotypic regimens combining vincristine/dexamethasone with cytarabine/anthracyclines versus pure ALL induction.
- Role of novel immunotherapies (blinatumomab or CAR-T cells) in B/myeloid MPAL refractory to induction chemotherapy.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Minimal essential flow cytometry antigen criteria (cytoplasmic CD3 and MPO) to diagnose T/myeloid MPAL.
- Comparison of complete remission rates between ALL-directed versus AML-directed induction therapy for ALAL.
- Management of BCR::ABL1-positive mixed-phenotype acute leukemia with targeted tyrosine kinase inhibitors.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 34-year-old male presenting with pancytopenia and 60% blasts expressing strong cytoplasmic CD3, CD34, and myeloperoxidase.
- A 52-year-old female with leukocytosis and blasts showing co-expression of CD19, CD22, and CD33 with t(9;22) translocation.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 35732838**: *The 5th edition of the World Health Organization Classification of Haematolymphoid Tumours: Myeloid and Histiocytic/Dendritic Neoplasms.* (2022) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 25150247**: *Revisiting the diagnostic criteria and biology of acute leukemias of ambiguous lineage: an international consensus.* (2014) [consensus-guideline] - 
- **PMID 28416507**: *Mixed-phenotype acute leukemia: clinical features and outcomes across disease subgroups.* (2017) [trial] - 
- **PMID 29535127**: *Outcomes of adult patients with acute leukemia of ambiguous lineage: multi-institutional retrospective study.* (2018) [review] -

## [0051] Acute limb ischaemia: Rutherford classification, embolectomy, thrombolysis

**Cluster**: acute_limb_ischaemia_rutherford | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute limb ischemia (ALI) is a vascular emergency characterized by sudden hypoperfusion threatening limb viability within a 4-to-6 hour golden window. The Rutherford classification rapidly stratifies limbs into viable (Stage I), marginally threatened (Stage IIa), immediately threatened (Stage IIb), and irreversibly non-viable (Stage III). Immediate systemic unfractionated heparin anticoagulation must be initiated to arrest thrombus propagation, followed by urgent surgical embolectomy/bypass for Stage IIb or catheter-directed thrombolysis/endovascular thrombectomy for Stage IIa limbs.

### Core Teaching Points
- Rutherford classification defines clinical urgency: Stage I is viable without sensory/motor loss; Stage IIa is marginally threatened with minimal sensory loss and audible arterial Doppler; Stage IIb is immediately threatened with motor weakness and inaudible arterial Doppler; Stage III is irreversible with profound anesthesia and paralysis. (PMID 27840333, PMID 31952972)
- Immediate intravenous unfractionated heparin bolus and infusion prevents microvascular thrombosis propagation and reduces amputation risk, and should be administered immediately upon clinical suspicion before diagnostic imaging. (PMID 27840333)
- The TOPAS and STILE randomized trials demonstrated that for patients presenting within 14 days with viable or marginally threatened limbs (Rutherford IIa), catheter-directed thrombolysis achieved equivalent limb salvage and survival compared to open surgery, while reducing surgical morbidity. (PMID 8900086, PMID 8086000)
- For immediately threatened limbs (Rutherford IIb), emergent open surgical revascularization (balloon catheter embolectomy, endarterectomy, or bypass) is required to restore perfusion within 6 hours to prevent permanent neuromuscular necrosis; prophylactic four-compartment fasciotomy is indicated if ischemia duration exceeds 4-6 hours. (PMID 27840333, PMID 31952972)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Comparative safety and efficacy of modern pharmacomechanical catheter-directed thrombectomy versus classic catheter infusion thrombolysis.
- Long-term patency of endovascular versus open surgical bail-out after initial successful thrombolytic recanalization.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Distinguishing Rutherford Stage IIa versus IIb acute limb ischemia by motor deficit and Doppler arterial signal.
- Indications for emergent surgical revascularization versus catheter-directed thrombolysis based on symptom duration and staging.
- Management of reperfusion injury and clinical indications for urgent four-compartment calf fasciotomy.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 74-year-old male with atrial fibrillation presenting with 3 hours of severe right calf pain, sensory loss over toes, and absent dorsalis pedis Doppler signals with intact motor function.
- A 62-year-old smoker presenting with 12 hours of cold, pale left foot with inability to dorsiflex the great toe and fixed mottling.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

## [0052] acute liver failure

**Cluster**: acute_liver_failure | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute liver failure (ALF) is a rapid-onset syndrome characterized by severe hepatocyte death, international normalized ratio (INR) >= 1.5, and hepatic encephalopathy in a patient without pre-existing cirrhosis, evolving within 26 weeks. Differentiating acetaminophen-induced ALF from non-acetaminophen etiologies (viral hepatitis, drug-induced liver injury, autoimmune, Wilson disease) is critical. Early administration of intravenous N-acetylcysteine improves transplant-free survival even in non-acetaminophen ALF, and aggressive supportive ICU care targeting cerebral edema and multi-organ failure serves as a bridge to spontaneous recovery or emergency liver transplantation.

### Core Teaching Points
- A prospective double-blind randomized controlled trial by Lee et al. demonstrated that intravenous N-acetylcysteine significantly improves transplant-free survival in patients with early-stage non-acetaminophen acute liver failure (coma grade I-II; 52% vs 30%), primarily driven by microvascular hemodynamic benefits. (PMID 19743477)
- ALF diagnosis requires biochemical evidence of acute hepatic injury (transaminitis), coagulopathy (INR >= 1.5), and any degree of hepatic encephalopathy in the absence of pre-existing chronic liver disease. (PMID 22119905, PMID 28461136)
- Infection is the leading cause of death in ALF; surveillance blood, sputum, and urine cultures must be performed routinely, with a low threshold for broad-spectrum empirical antimicrobial and antifungal therapy upon clinical deterioration. (PMID 20638564, PMID 28461136)
- Sedation and endotracheal intubation should be instituted early once Grade 3 or 4 encephalopathy manifests to protect the airway and facilitate neuroprotective management of intracranial hypertension. (PMID 22119905, PMID 28461136)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Utility of plasma exchange / high-volume plasma exchange in improving transplant-free survival across diverse ALF etiologies.
- Safety and efficacy of prophylactic empirical broad-spectrum antifungals in non-transplanted ALF patients.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Evidence for intravenous N-acetylcysteine in non-acetaminophen acute liver failure based on the Lee et al. trial.
- Core diagnostic criteria defining acute liver failure (INR threshold and mental status alteration).
- Indications for emergent intubation and mechanical ventilation in progressive hepatic encephalopathy.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 23-year-old female presenting with jaundice, confusion, INR 2.4, and AST 3,200 U/L five days after starting an herbal supplement.
- A 42-year-old male with acute hepatitis B presenting with asterixis, INR 2.1, and progressive somnolence.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 19743477**: *Intravenous N-acetylcysteine improves transplant-free survival in early stage non-acetaminophen acute liver failure.* (2009) [trial] - 
- **PMID 20638564**: *Acute liver failure: a review.* (2010) [review] - 
- **PMID 22119905**: *AASLD Position Paper: The Management of Acute Liver Failure: Update 2011.* (2012) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 28461136**: *EASL Clinical Practical Guidelines on the management of acute (fulminant) liver failure.* (2017) [guideline] -

## [0053] Acute liver failure in the ICU: King's College criteria, cerebral oedema management

**Cluster**: acute_liver_failure_in_icu | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Managing acute liver failure in the intensive care unit centers on preventing and mitigating severe intracranial hypertension resulting from hyperammonemia, cytotoxic astrocyte swelling, and disrupted cerebral autoregulation. The King's College Criteria provide established, etiology-specific prognostic benchmarks that identify patients unlikely to survive without emergency orthotopic liver transplantation. Neuroprotective interventions—including head elevation, targeted hypertonic saline (serum sodium 145-150 mmol/L), normothermia, and high-volume plasma exchange—reduce cerebral herniation risk.

### Core Teaching Points
- Under King's College Criteria for acetaminophen ALF: arterial blood pH < 7.30 after resuscitation, or all three of INR > 6.5, serum creatinine > 3.4 mg/dL, and Grade 3-4 encephalopathy; for non-acetaminophen ALF: INR > 6.5, or any 3 of unfavorable etiology, age < 10 or > 40, jaundice-to-encephalopathy interval > 7 days, INR > 3.5, bilirubin > 17.5 mg/dL. (PMID 2475730)
- In a randomized trial by Murphy et al., prophylactic infusion of 3% hypertonic saline to maintain serum sodium between 145 and 150 mmol/L significantly reduced the incidence and severity of acute intracranial hypertension in ALF. (PMID 17294435)
- Larsen et al. demonstrated in a multicenter randomized trial that high-volume plasma exchange (HV-PE) significantly improved transplant-free survival (58.7% vs 47.8%) and attenuated systemic inflammatory response syndrome and vasopressor requirements. (PMID 26778409)
- Arterial ammonia levels > 150-200 umol/L correlate strongly with intracranial hypertension and cerebral herniation; continuous renal replacement therapy is recommended early for ammonia clearance even in the absence of traditional dialysis indications. (PMID 28461136)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Invasive intracranial pressure (ICP) monitoring catheter placement versus non-invasive optic nerve sheath ultrasound given bleeding coagulopathy risks.
- Optimal duration and frequency of high-volume plasma exchange sessions before assessing spontaneous liver regeneration.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Specific biochemical and clinical thresholds of the King's College Criteria for acetaminophen versus non-acetaminophen ALF.
- Target serum sodium range (145-150 mmol/L) when infusing hypertonic saline to prevent cerebral edema in ALF.
- Mechanisms and survival impact of high-volume plasma exchange established in the trial by Larsen et al.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 28-year-old female with acetaminophen overdose whose arterial pH is 7.22 despite 3 liters of fluid resuscitation and NAC.
- A 35-year-old male with seronegative ALF exhibiting arterial ammonia of 210 umol/L, decerebrate posturing, and Grade 4 encephalopathy.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 2475730**: *Early indicators of prognosis in fulminant hepatic failure (King's College Criteria).* (1989) [trial] - 
- **PMID 17294435**: *Induction of hypernatremia with hypertonic saline in patients with acute liver failure and cerebral edema.* (2007) [trial] - 
- **PMID 28461136**: *EASL Clinical Practical Guidelines on the management of acute (fulminant) liver failure.* (2017) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 26778409**: *High-volume plasma exchange in patients with acute liver failure: An open label, randomised controlled trial.* (2016) [trial] -

## [0054] Acute liver failure: management and prognostic criteria

**Cluster**: acute_liver_failure_prognostic_criteria | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Accurate, dynamic prognostication in acute liver failure is essential to prevent either premature transplantation in patients who would recover spontaneously or delayed listing resulting in fatal brainstem herniation. While King's College Criteria offer high specificity, their sensitivity is modest. Modern multi-variable prognostic models (such as the ALFSG index, Model for End-Stage Liver Disease [MELD], and serial arterial lactate clearance) complement clinical assessment to guide super-urgent listing decisions in real time.

### Core Teaching Points
- The Model for End-Stage Liver Disease (MELD) score is highly predictive of death in ALF, with MELD scores > 30-33 demonstrating diagnostic sensitivity superior to King's College Criteria for non-acetaminophen etiologies. (PMID 16365825, PMID 21953503)
- Serial arterial lactate kinetics provide critical prognostic insight: in acetaminophen ALF, arterial lactate > 3.5 mmol/L after early fluid resuscitation or > 3.0 mmol/L at 12 hours independently predicts mortality with > 90% positive predictive value. (PMID 20638564, PMID 28461136)
- EASL clinical practice guidelines emphasize combining static scores with dynamic trajectory: lack of reduction in INR and progressive elevation in serum bilirubin over 48-72 hours indicate failure of spontaneous hepatocyte regeneration. (PMID 28461136)
- The Acute Liver Failure Study Group (ALFSG) prognostic index integrates etiology, coma grade, INR, bilirubin, and arterial phosphorus to generate validated 21-day transplant-free survival probabilities. (PMID 21953503)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Prognostic significance of rising alpha-fetoprotein (AFP) as a specific marker of vigorous liver parenchymal regeneration.
- Impact of high-volume plasma exchange on the predictive accuracy of standard MELD and King's College scoring formulas.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Prognostic cutoff of arterial lactate at presentation and post-resuscitation in acetaminophen-induced ALF.
- Comparative sensitivity and specificity between MELD score and King's College Criteria in acute hepatic failure.
- Dynamic clinical and laboratory markers indicating failure of spontaneous liver regeneration.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 32-year-old female with acetaminophen overdose presenting with blood lactate 5.4 mmol/L after 4 hours of fluid loading.
- A 40-year-old male with drug-induced liver injury whose MELD score rises from 24 to 34 over 48 hours without clinical improvement.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 20638564**: *Acute liver failure: a review.* (2010) [review] - 
- **PMID 21953503**: *Prognostication in acute liver failure: external validation of dynamic prediction models.* (2012) [trial] - 
- **PMID 28461136**: *EASL Clinical Practical Guidelines on the management of acute (fulminant) liver failure.* (2017) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 16365825**: *A prospective evaluation of the King's College Criteria and the MELD score in acute liver failure.* (2005) [trial] -

## [0055] Acute liver failure transplant referral

**Cluster**: acute_liver_failure_transplant_referral | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Timely referral to a specialized liver transplant center is the single most vital intervention determining survival in acute liver failure. Listing for emergency liver transplantation (UNOS Status 1A / UK Super-Urgent Scheme) must occur before irreversible multi-organ failure, refractory sepsis, or fixed dilated pupils from cerebral herniation preclude surgery. Understanding the distinct pathophysiological criteria—King's College, Clichy-Villejuif criteria (Factor V < 20-30%), and contraindications to transplantation—enables frontline clinicians to coordinate transfer without fatal delay.

### Core Teaching Points
- Immediate contact with a liver transplant center is mandatory as soon as coagulopathy (INR >= 1.5) and any altered mental status occur, rather than delaying until full King's College Criteria listing benchmarks are reached. (PMID 28461136, PMID 22119905)
- Under the Clichy-Villejuif criteria (widely used in Europe), fulminant viral hepatitis patients with Factor V level < 20% (age < 30) or Factor V < 30% (age >= 30) in the presence of encephalopathy meet emergency transplant listing criteria. (PMID 7619894)
- Super-urgent transplant listing criteria (UNOS Status 1A) require fulminant liver failure with life expectancy < 7 days, manifesting as encephalopathy, mechanical ventilation, renal replacement therapy, or severe coagulopathy (INR > 2.0). (PMID 22119905)
- Absolute contraindications to emergency liver transplantation include irreversible catastrophic brain damage (fixed dilated pupils, sustained cerebral perfusion pressure < 40 mmHg), uncontrolled active septic shock, and severe active cardiopulmonary comorbidities. (PMID 28461136, PMID 22119905)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Transplant eligibility for patients with acute liver failure secondary to intentional substance overdose without established social support.
- Safety and outcomes of auxiliary partial orthotopic liver transplantation allowing subsequent native liver recovery and immunosuppression withdrawal.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Factor V level cutoffs in the Clichy-Villejuif criteria indicating need for emergency liver transplantation.
- UNOS Status 1A emergency liver allocation criteria and eligibility thresholds.
- Clinical contraindications precluding emergency orthotopic liver transplantation in fulminant hepatic failure.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 19-year-old male with acute hepatitis A, somnolence, Factor V level 16%, and INR 3.8 being evaluated for emergency transfer.
- A 45-year-old female with acetaminophen toxicity requiring high norepinephrine doses, refractory hypoxemia, and fixed dilated pupils.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 2475730**: *Early indicators of prognosis in fulminant hepatic failure (King's College Criteria).* (1989) [trial] - 
- **PMID 7619894**: *Emergency liver transplantation for fulminant hepatitis: the Clichy-Villejuif criteria.* (1995) [trial] - 
- **PMID 28461136**: *EASL Clinical Practical Guidelines on the management of acute (fulminant) liver failure.* (2017) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 22119905**: *AASLD Position Paper: The Management of Acute Liver Failure: Update 2011.* (2012) [guideline] -

## [0056] Acute Lymphoblastic Leukaemia

**Cluster**: acute_lymphoblastic_leukaemia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Adult acute lymphoblastic leukemia (ALL) management has been revolutionized by pediatric-inspired multi-agent chemotherapy regimens, molecular risk-stratification, and targeted immunotherapies. In Philadelphia chromosome-positive (Ph+) ALL, combining second- or third-generation tyrosine kinase inhibitors (dasatinib, ponatinib) with blinatumomab has enabled chemotherapy-free induction with high complete molecular remission rates. For relapsed/refractory B-ALL, antibody-drug conjugates (inotuzumab ozogamicin) and bispecific T-cell engagers (blinatumomab) demonstrate marked superiority over conventional salvage.

### Core Teaching Points
- In the phase 3 TOWER trial, blinatumomab (CD19/CD3 bispecific T-cell engager) significantly improved overall survival (median 7.7 vs 4.0 months) and complete remission rates compared to standard salvage chemotherapy in relapsed/refractory B-ALL. (PMID 28249141)
- The INO-VATE trial showed that inotuzumab ozogamicin (anti-CD22 calicheamicin conjugate) achieved significantly higher CR rates (80.7% vs 29.4%) and higher MRD negativity than standard chemotherapy, though requiring careful monitoring for hepatic sinusoidal obstruction syndrome prior to HSCT. (PMID 27294709)
- The D-ALBA trial established that first-line chemotherapy-free induction with dasatinib plus blinatumomab achieved a 98% hematologic complete response rate and high molecular clearance in adult Ph+ B-ALL. (PMID 33085860)
- Pediatric-inspired regimens (such as augmented Berlin-Frankfurt-Münster [BFM] or CALGB protocols) significantly improve event-free survival in adolescents and young adults (AYAs, ages 18-39) with Ph-negative ALL compared to historic adult protocols. (PMID 7686862)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal timing and necessity of consolidative allogeneic stem cell transplantation in adult Ph+ ALL achieving complete molecular remission on blinatumomab and ponatinib.
- Sequencing blinatumomab and CAR-T cell therapy (brexucabtagene autoleucel) to prevent CD19 antigen escape.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Mechanism of action and primary toxicities (cytokine release syndrome, ICANS) associated with blinatumomab infusion.
- Specific hepatic risk (sinusoidal obstruction syndrome) associated with inotuzumab ozogamicin prior to stem cell transplantation.
- Therapeutic benefits of chemotherapy-free combinations (dasatinib/blinatumomab) in Philadelphia chromosome-positive ALL.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 24-year-old male with newly diagnosed Philadelphia-negative B-ALL being considered for pediatric-inspired multi-agent chemotherapy.
- A 48-year-old female with relapsed B-ALL following previous hyper-CVAD, being evaluated for blinatumomab versus inotuzumab salvage.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 28249141**: *Blinatumomab versus Chemotherapy for Advanced Acute Lymphoblastic Leukemia (TOWER Trial).* (2017) [trial] - 
- **PMID 27294709**: *Inotuzumab Ozogamicin versus Standard Therapy for Acute Lymphoblastic Leukemia (INO-VATE Trial).* (2016) [trial] - 
- **PMID 7686862**: *A five-drug remission induction regimen with intensive consolidation for adults with acute lymphoblastic leukemia: CALGB 8811.* (1995) [trial] - 
- **PMID 33085860**: *Dasatinib-Blinatumomab for Ph-Positive Acute Lymphoblastic Leukemia in Adults (D-ALBA Trial).* (2020) [trial] -
