## [0057] Acute MS relapse: high-dose methylprednisolone vs plasma exchange

**Cluster**: acute_ms_relapse_corticosteroids_vs_plex | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute relapses in multiple sclerosis (MS) present as subacute neurological deficits developing over hours to days in the absence of fever or infection. High-dose corticosteroids (intravenous or oral methylprednisolone 1,000 mg daily for 3-5 days) represent the first-line standard of care, accelerating functional recovery as proven in the Optic Neuritis Treatment Trial (ONTT) and COPOUSEP trials. In patients with severe, disabling deficits refractory to high-dose steroids, therapeutic plasma exchange (PLEX) provides proven rescue therapy, demonstrating marked functional improvement when instituted early.

### Core Teaching Points
- The Optic Neuritis Treatment Trial (ONTT) demonstrated that high-dose intravenous methylprednisolone (1 g daily for 3 days) followed by oral prednisone accelerated visual recovery compared to placebo, whereas oral prednisone alone at standard doses increased relapse recurrence rates. (PMID 1732049)
- The double-blind COPOUSEP non-inferiority trial demonstrated that oral methylprednisolone (1,000 mg daily for 3 days) is clinically non-inferior to intravenous methylprednisolone in achieving functional recovery at day 28 in acute MS relapses. (PMID 26189914)
- In a sham-controlled randomized trial by Weinshenker et al., therapeutic plasma exchange (PLEX, 5-7 exchanges over 14 days) produced moderate to marked neurological recovery in 42.1% of patients with severe steroid-refractory acute inflammatory demyelinating relapses versus 5.9% with sham treatment. (PMID 10606451)
- Pseudo-relapses—transient re-emergence of previous neurological deficits triggered by infection (e.g., UTI), elevated core body temperature (Uhthoff phenomenon), or metabolic stress—must be ruled out before initiating high-dose immunosuppressive therapies. (PMID 29527230)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy and safety of intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG) as a rescue alternative when therapeutic plasma exchange is contraindicated.
- Long-term neuroaxonal protection of ultra-early plasma exchange instituted within 7 days of steroid failure.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Clinical equivalence and route administration evidence between high-dose oral versus IV methylprednisolone from COPOUSEP.
- Efficacy and timing of therapeutic plasma exchange in steroid-refractory severe central nervous system demyelinating attacks.
- Distinction between authentic inflammatory MS relapse and infection-triggered pseudo-relapse (Uhthoff phenomenon).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 28-year-old female with relapsing-remitting MS presenting with dense paraparesis and bladder retention whose deficits progress despite 5 days of 1 g IV methylprednisolone.
- A 36-year-old male with MS who develops blurred vision and paresthesias in the setting of fever and pyuria.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 1732049**: *The clinical profile of optic neuritis: Experience of the Optic Neuritis Treatment Trial.* (1991) [trial] - 
- **PMID 10606451**: *A randomized trial of plasma exchange in acute central nervous system demyelinating disease.* (1999) [trial] - 
- **PMID 26189914**: *Oral versus intravenous high-dose methylprednisolone for treatment of relapses in patients with multiple sclerosis (COPOUSEP).* (2015) [trial] - 
- **PMID 29527230**: *Diagnosis of multiple sclerosis: 2017 revisions of the McDonald criteria.* (2018) [guideline] -

## [0058] Acute Myeloid Leukaemia

**Cluster**: acute_myeloid_leukaemia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute myeloid leukemia (AML) management is anchored in rapid cytogenetic and molecular risk stratification under the 2022 European LeukemiaNet (ELN) recommendations. For fit adult patients, traditional '7+3' cytarabine and anthracycline induction is now systematically enhanced by targeted agents: adding the FLT3 inhibitor midostaurin in FLT3-mutated AML (RATIFY trial), or the anti-CD33 antibody-drug conjugate gemtuzumab ozogamicin in favorable-risk cytogenetics (ALFA-0701 trial). Defining measurable residual disease (MRD) after induction guides the decision to pursue allogeneic stem cell transplantation in CR1.

### Core Teaching Points
- The ELN 2022 classification stratifies AML into Favorable (e.g., inv(16), t(8;21), mutated NPM1 without FLT3-ITD), Intermediate, and Adverse (e.g., mutated TP53, complex karyotype, adverse gene fusions, myelodysplasia-related gene mutations). (PMID 35797463)
- The international RATIFY trial established that adding the multikinase FLT3 inhibitor midostaurin to standard 7+3 induction and consolidation significantly prolonged overall survival (hazard ratio for death 0.78, p=0.009) in both FLT3-ITD and FLT3-TKD mutated AML. (PMID 28644114)
- The ALFA-0701 randomized trial demonstrated that fractionated gemtuzumab ozogamicin added to standard induction improved event-free and overall survival in patients with favorable and intermediate-risk cytogenetics, without increasing fatal toxicities. (PMID 22521382)
- Flow cytometric or molecular measurable residual disease (MRD) negativity after consolidation is the strongest predictor of long-term relapse-free survival and stratifies the necessity of allogeneic stem cell transplantation in intermediate-risk disease. (PMID 35797463)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Survival benefit of post-transplant maintenance therapy with second-generation FLT3 inhibitors (gilteritinib) across various FLT3-ITD allelic ratios.
- Efficacy of intensive induction versus targeted hypomethylating agent combinations in fit patients aged 60-75 with adverse-risk cytogenetics.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Prognostic risk classification under ELN 2022 based on NPM1, FLT3-ITD, and TP53 mutational status.
- Survival benefits of adding midostaurin to 7+3 chemotherapy in FLT3-mutated AML based on the RATIFY trial.
- Role of gemtuzumab ozogamicin in favorable versus adverse risk cytogenetic subgroups.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 46-year-old female with newly diagnosed AML whose bone marrow shows 60% blasts with FLT3-ITD high allelic ratio mutation.
- A 55-year-old male with inv(16) core-binding factor AML being evaluated for fractionated gemtuzumab ozogamicin integration.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 35797463**: *Diagnosis and management of AML in adults: 2022 recommendations from an international expert panel on behalf of the ELN.* (2022) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 28644114**: *Midostaurin plus Chemotherapy for Acute Myeloid Leukemia with a FLT3 Mutation (RATIFY Trial).* (2017) [trial] - 
- **PMID 22521382**: *Effect of gemtuzumab ozogamicin on survival of adult patients with de-novo acute myeloid leukaemia (ALFA-0701).* (2012) [trial] - 
- **PMID 19776406**: *Intensive chemotherapy does not benefit most older patients (age 70 years or older) with acute myeloid leukemia.* (2009) [review] -

## [0059] Acute myeloid leukemia venetoclax azacitidine

**Cluster**: acute_myeloid_leukemia_venetoclax_azacitidine | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The combination of the selective oral BCL-2 inhibitor venetoclax with a hypomethylating agent (azacitidine or decitabine) has transformed the therapeutic landscape for older adults (>= 75 years) or younger patients unfit for intensive chemotherapy. In the landmark VIALE-A trial, venetoclax plus azacitidine demonstrated marked improvements in complete remission rates and overall survival over azacitidine monotherapy. Managing this regimen demands rigorous tumor lysis syndrome (TLS) ramp-up prophylaxis, protocolized bone marrow reassessment on day 28, and proactive dose delays or growth factor support for prolonged cytopenias.

### Core Teaching Points
- In the global phase 3 VIALE-A trial, venetoclax combined with azacitidine significantly improved median overall survival (14.7 months vs 9.6 months; hazard ratio for death 0.66, p<0.001) and composite complete remission rate (66.4% vs 28.3%) compared with azacitidine alone in patients ineligible for intensive chemotherapy. (PMID 32786187)
- In patients receiving low-dose cytarabine backbones, the VIALE-C trial demonstrated superior composite remission (48% vs 13%) and clinically meaningful survival extension with the addition of venetoclax. (PMID 33074098, PMID 31104944)
- Tumor lysis syndrome (TLS) mitigation requires cytoreduction with hydroxyurea for white blood cell counts > 25,000/uL, vigorous hydration, rasburicase or allopurinol, and structured daily venetoclax ramp-up (100 mg, 200 mg, 400 mg daily). (PMID 32786187, PMID 35797463)
- When co-administered with strong CYP3A4 inhibitors (e.g., posaconazole, voriconazole), venetoclax dosage must be reduced by 75% (to 100 mg daily), and by 50% (to 200 mg daily) with moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors (e.g., fluconazole). (PMID 32786187, PMID 35797463)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Mechanisms and optimal second-line salvage therapies for AML developing resistance to venetoclax (e.g., MCL-1 upregulation or FLT3 acquisition).
- Optimal duration of venetoclax consolidation and potential safety of elective drug holidays in patients in sustained MRD-negative remission.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Overall survival and complete remission benefits from the VIALE-A trial evaluating venetoclax plus azacitidine.
- Mandatory venetoclax dose reduction percentages when concurrently administering strong azole antifungals.
- Pre-treatment tumor lysis syndrome mitigation strategies including target pre-induction leukocyte count thresholds.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 76-year-old male with de novo AML and coronary artery disease initiating venetoclax and azacitidine with baseline WBC 42,000/uL.
- A 72-year-old female on venetoclax/azacitidine who develops invasive pulmonary aspergillosis requiring posaconazole therapy.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 32786187**: *Azacitidine and Venetoclax in Previously Untreated Acute Myeloid Leukemia (VIALE-A Trial).* (2020) [trial] - 
- **PMID 33074098**: *Venetoclax combined with low-dose cytarabine for previously untreated acute myeloid leukemia: results from the randomized phase 3 VIALE-C study.* (2020) [trial] - 
- **PMID 31104944**: *Venetoclax plus low-dose cytarabine in older patients with acute myeloid leukaemia: a phase 1/2 study.* (2019) [trial] -

## [0060] Acute myocarditis: Dallas criteria, cardiac MRI, immunosuppression indications

**Cluster**: acute_myocarditis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute myocarditis encompasses an inflammatory spectrum ranging from mild chest pain with transient troponin elevation to fulminant cardiogenic shock and fatal ventricular arrhythmias. While historic Dallas histopathological criteria suffered from low sensitivity and biopsy sampling error, contemporary diagnosis centers on multiparametric cardiac magnetic resonance (CMR) utilizing the updated Lake Louise Criteria (T1 and T2 mapping). Endomyocardial biopsy remains mandatory in progressive or fulminant presentations to identify specific subtypes—such as giant cell myocarditis or eosinophilic myocarditis—where emergency immunosuppressive regimens are life-saving.

### Core Teaching Points
- Under the 2018 Updated Lake Louise Criteria, CMR diagnosis of acute myocarditis requires at least one T2-based criterion (myocardial edema on T2-weighted imaging or elevated myocardial T2 relaxation time) and at least one T1-based criterion (late gadolinium enhancement, increased myocardial T1 relaxation time, or extracellular volume fraction). (PMID 30482356)
- AHA/ACC/ESC guidelines reserve endomyocardial biopsy for specific clinical scenarios: new-onset heart failure of < 2 weeks' duration associated with hemodynamic compromise, or heart failure of 2 weeks to 3 months with ventricular arrhythmias, conduction block, or failure to respond to standard therapy within 1-2 weeks. (PMID 17965395, PMID 23824828)
- Giant cell myocarditis carries a median survival of < 6 months without transplantation; Cooper et al. demonstrated that combined aggressive immunosuppression (cyclosporine, corticosteroids, and azathioprine or anti-thymocyte globulin) prolonged transplant-free survival to 12.3 months. (PMID 9203427)
- Routine immunosuppression is not recommended for mild lymphocytic myocarditis with preserved ejection fraction; management consists of standard guideline-directed medical therapy for heart failure, arrhythmia surveillance, and strict avoidance of competitive exercise for 3 to 6 months. (PMID 23824828)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal immunosuppression duration and taper regimens in biopsy-proven virus-negative chronic lymphocytic myocarditis.
- Long-term prognostic significance of persistent late gadolinium enhancement on follow-up CMR despite complete clinical recovery.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic components of the 2018 Updated Lake Louise CMR Criteria (T1 and T2 imaging markers).
- Class I indications for urgent endomyocardial biopsy in new-onset acute heart failure according to AHA/ACC/ESC.
- Prognosis and prompt multi-agent immunosuppressive management of biopsy-proven giant cell myocarditis.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 24-year-old male with retrosternal chest pain, troponin I elevation to 4.8 ng/mL, and normal coronary angiography 10 days after a febrile viral illness.
- A 42-year-old female presenting with rapidly progressive cardiogenic shock, third-degree AV block, and refractory ventricular tachycardia undergoing urgent endomyocardial biopsy.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources

## [0061] Acute myocarditis diagnosis and management

**Cluster**: acute_myocarditis_diagnosis_and_management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute myocarditis represents myocardial inflammation precipitated predominantly by viral infections, autoimmune reactions, or immune checkpoint inhibitors (ICIs). Initial evaluation requires high-sensitivity cardiac troponin, 12-lead ECG, echocardiography, and multiparametric cardiac magnetic resonance (CMR; updated Lake Louise Criteria). In hemodynamically stable patients, supportive therapy with guideline-directed medical therapy (GDMT: ACEi/ARB and beta-blockers) and physical rest for 3 to 6 months forms the management foundation. In fulminant myocarditis manifesting as cardiogenic shock, life-threatening ventricular arrhythmias, or advanced heart block, emergency mechanical circulatory support (ECMO, Impella) and endomyocardial biopsy (EMB) to detect giant cell or eosinophilic myocarditis for targeted immunosuppression are life-saving.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2018 Updated Lake Louise CMR Criteria require both myocardial edema (elevated T2 signal or T2 mapping) and myocardial injury/necrosis (increased extracellular volume fraction, elevated native T1, or late gadolinium enhancement) for diagnostic confirmation. (PMID 30482356)
- European Society of Cardiology (ESC) guidelines recommend standard guideline-directed heart failure therapy for systolic dysfunction, avoidance of NSAIDs in acute myocardial inflammation due to increased mortality in animal models, and exercise abstinence for 3-6 months. (PMID 23824828)
- The AHA scientific statement specifies that patients with fulminant myocarditis refractory to pharmacological inotropes should receive early temporary mechanical circulatory support (VA-ECMO or microaxial ventricular assist devices) as a bridge to recovery or transplant. (PMID 32646210)
- In immune checkpoint inhibitor (ICI)-associated myocarditis, early high-dose intravenous methylprednisolone (1,000 mg/day for 3 days) followed by rapid immunosuppressive intensification (abatacept, tofacitinib, or ATG) in refractory cases significantly mitigates the > 25-50% fatality rate. (PMID 32646210, PMID 23824828)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration of secondary immunosuppressive therapy in biopsy-proven virus-negative autoimmune chronic myocarditis.
- Clinical thresholds for safe de-escalation of temporary mechanical circulatory support in post-viral fulminant myocardial stunning.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic requirements of the 2018 Updated Lake Louise Criteria on cardiac magnetic resonance.
- Contraindication of NSAIDs for chest pain relief during acute active myocardial inflammation.
- Emergency immunosuppression strategy for immune checkpoint inhibitor-induced acute myocarditis.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 29-year-old competitive runner presenting with pleuritic chest pain, ST elevations in lateral leads, and troponin I of 7.2 ng/mL following a viral gastroenteritis.
- A 63-year-old male with metastatic melanoma receiving nivolumab/ipilimumab who develops complete heart block, dyspnea, and troponin T of 1,200 ng/L.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources

- **PMID 23824828**: *Current state of knowledge on etiology, diagnosis, management, and pharmacotherapy of myocarditis: position statement of ESC Working Group.* (2013) [guideline] -

## [0062] Acute necrotising pancreatitis and step-up management approach

**Cluster**: acute_necrotising_pancreatitis_step_up | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute necrotizing pancreatitis occurs in approximately 20% of acute pancreatitis episodes and carries significant morbidity and mortality, particularly when secondary bacterial infection develops. The landmark PANTER trial revolutionized surgical dogma by establishing the 'step-up' approach—initiating treatment with percutaneous or endoscopic catheter drainage and reserving minimally invasive retroperitoneal necrosectomy (VARD) only for patients who fail to improve clinically. Compared to open necrosectomy, the step-up strategy slashes new-onset multi-organ failure and systemic complications by two-thirds and eliminates major abdominal wall hernias.

### Core Teaching Points
- In the landmark Dutch PANTER randomized trial (88 patients), the minimally invasive step-up approach reduced the composite endpoint of major complications or death by 43% compared with primary open necrosectomy, driven by lower rates of new-onset multi-organ failure (12% vs 40%). (PMID 20410519)
- Over one-third of patients (35%) treated in the step-up arm of the PANTER trial were successfully cured by percutaneous catheter drainage alone, avoiding surgical necrosectomy entirely. (PMID 20410519)
- The randomized TENSION trial demonstrated that an endoscopic step-up approach (transluminal endoscopic drainage followed by endoscopic necrosectomy) was comparable to the surgical step-up approach in mortality and major morbidity, with fewer pancreaticocutaneous fistulae and shorter hospital stays. (PMID 29339000)
- International Association of Pancreatology / American Pancreatic Association (IAP/APA) guidelines recommend delaying invasive drainage or debridement until walled-off necrosis (WON) has matured, typically at least 4 weeks after symptom onset, unless rapid clinical collapse dictates emergency drainage. (PMID 23809820)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Utility of routine fine-needle aspiration (FNA) for Gram stain/culture versus empiric drainage when infected necrosis is suspected based on gas on CT.
- Role of prophylactic systemic antibiotics in extensive sterile necrotizing pancreatitis (> 30% parenchymal necrosis).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Components and clinical superiority of the minimally invasive step-up approach from the PANTER trial.
- Comparative outcomes of endoscopic versus surgical retroperitoneal step-up interventions from the TENSION trial.
- Rationale and timing (minimum 4 weeks) for delaying invasive necrosectomy until demarcation of walled-off necrosis.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 52-year-old male with biliary pancreatitis who at week 3 develops spiking fevers, leukocytosis 22,000/uL, and CT revealing extensive retroperitoneal air bubbles within necrotic tissue.
- A 46-year-old female with pancreatic necrosis who improves clinically following ultrasound-guided 14F percutaneous catheter placement, avoiding operative necrosectomy.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 20410519**: *A step-up approach, or open necrosectomy, for necrotizing pancreatitis (PANTER Trial).* (2010) [trial] - 
- **PMID 29339000**: *Endoscopic or surgical step-up approach for infected necrotising pancreatitis (TENSION): multicentre randomised controlled trial.* (2018) [trial] - 
- **PMID 23809820**: *IAP/APA evidence-based guidelines for the management of acute pancreatitis.* (2013) [guideline] -

## [0063] acute otitis media

**Cluster**: acute_otitis_media | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute otitis media (AOM) is a rapid-onset middle ear infection characterized by erythema, purulent middle ear effusion, and distinct bulging of the tympanic membrane. According to American Academy of Pediatrics (AAP) and NICE guidelines, diagnostic precision is paramount to combat inappropriate antimicrobial overuse. Watchful waiting (observation with analgesia for 48-72 hours) is recommended in children >= 2 years with mild unilateral AOM, while immediate antibiotic treatment (high-dose amoxicillin 80-90 mg/kg/day) is reserved for severe illness, children < 6 months, bilateral disease in children < 2 years, or otorrhea.

### Core Teaching Points
- AAP clinical guidelines emphasize that rigorous diagnosis of AOM requires moderate-to-severe bulging of the tympanic membrane or new-onset otorrhea not due to acute otitis externa, distinguishing AOM from uncomplicated otitis media with effusion (OME). (PMID 23439909)
- First-line antimicrobial therapy for confirmed bacterial AOM without penicillin allergy is high-dose amoxicillin (80-90 mg/kg/day in 2 divided doses), ensuring adequate middle ear penetration against penicillin-nonsusceptible Streptococcus pneumoniae. (PMID 23439909, PMID 31825590)
- Amoxicillin-clavulanate (90 mg/kg/day amoxicillin component with 6.4 mg/kg/day clavulanate) is indicated if the patient has received amoxicillin within the preceding 30 days, has concurrent purulent conjunctivitis (otitis-conjunctivitis syndrome due to nontypeable H. influenzae), or is unresponsive to initial amoxicillin after 48-72 hours. (PMID 23439909)
- Routine observation (watchful waiting) with structured pain control for 48 hours without upfront antibiotics is safe in children >= 2 years with mild unilateral symptoms and reliable follow-up, reducing antibiotic exposure without increasing mastoiditis rates. (PMID 23439909, PMID 31825590)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Short-course (5-day) versus standard-course (10-day) antimicrobial regimens in children aged 2-5 years with uncomplicated AOM.
- Impact of pneumococcal conjugate vaccination (PCV13/PCV15/PCV20) on the shifting microbiological epidemiology of non-typeable Haemophilus influenzae and Moraxella catarrhalis.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Strict otoscopic diagnostic criteria separating acute otitis media from otitis media with effusion.
- Indications for amoxicillin-clavulanate over single-agent amoxicillin as initial empiric therapy.
- Clinical eligibility criteria for the 48-hour watchful waiting observation strategy in pediatric otitis media.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An 18-month-old boy with fever of 39.1°C, intense otalgia, and marked bulging, opaque erythematous left tympanic membrane with poor mobility on pneumatic otoscopy.
- A 3-year-old girl with mild rhinorrhea, afebrile, whose tympanic membrane is translucent with visible air-fluid level and no bulging, representing OME.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 23439909**: *The diagnosis and management of acute otitis media (AAP Guideline).* (2013) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 31825590**: *Otitis media with effusion: summary of NICE guidance.* (2018) [guideline] -

## [0064] acute pancreatitis

**Cluster**: acute_pancreatitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute pancreatitis is an acute inflammatory process of the pancreas diagnosed by fulfilling at least two of three criteria: characteristic epigastric abdominal pain radiating to the back, serum lipase or amylase elevated >= 3 times the upper limit of normal, and characteristic cross-sectional abdominal imaging findings. Under the Revised Atlanta Classification, pancreatitis is stratified into mild (no organ failure or local complications), moderately severe (transient organ failure < 48 hours or local complications), and severe (persistent organ failure > 48 hours). Management focuses on etiology identification, goal-directed moderate fluid resuscitation, and early enteral nutrition.

### Core Teaching Points
- Diagnosis under the Revised Atlanta Classification requires at least 2 of 3 features: (1) characteristic epigastric pain radiating to the back; (2) serum lipase or amylase > 3x upper limit of normal; (3) contrast CT, MRI, or transabdominal ultrasound demonstrating pancreatitis. (PMID 23100416)
- Persistent organ failure (> 48 hours)—defined using the modified Marshall scoring system for cardiovascular (hypotension), renal (elevated creatinine), or respiratory (decreased PaO2/FiO2) systems—is the primary determinant of mortality in severe acute pancreatitis. (PMID 23100416)
- The international multicenter WATERFALL trial proved that aggressive fluid resuscitation (20 mL/kg bolus followed by 3 mL/kg/h) resulted in a three-fold higher rate of fluid overload (20.5% vs 6.3%) without improving clinical outcomes compared to moderate fluid resuscitation (10 mL/kg bolus in hypovolemic patients followed by 1.5 mL/kg/h). (PMID 36103415)
- IAP/APA guidelines recommend early transabdominal ultrasound in all patients to identify gallstones, and early oral refeeding with low-fat solid or liquid diet as soon as abdominal pain is improving and ileus has resolved. (PMID 23809820)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal timing of cholecystectomy during the index admission for mild biliary pancreatitis versus interval outpatient surgery.
- Clinical utility of emergent endoscopic retrograde cholangiopancreatography (ERCP) in acute biliary pancreatitis without concurrent ascending cholangitis.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The three diagnostic criteria of the Revised Atlanta Classification and when cross-sectional imaging is strictly required.
- Fluid overload risk and clinical failure of aggressive versus moderate fluid resuscitation demonstrated in the WATERFALL trial.
- Definition of severe acute pancreatitis based on persistent organ failure (> 48 hours) versus transient organ failure.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 44-year-old male presenting with acute severe epigastric pain, vomiting, and serum lipase of 1,840 U/L (ULN 60 U/L) requiring fluid prescription based on WATERFALL.
- A 56-year-old female with gallstone pancreatitis exhibiting tachypnea, oliguria, and PaO2/FiO2 ratio 250 requiring ICU admission for persistent organ failure.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 23100416**: *Classification of acute pancreatitis--2012: revision of the Atlanta classification and definitions by international consensus.* (2013) [consensus-guideline] - 
- **PMID 36103415**: *Early Moderate or Aggressive Fluid Resuscitation for Acute Pancreatitis (WATERFALL Trial).* (2022) [trial] - 
- **PMID 23809820**: *IAP/APA evidence-based guidelines for the management of acute pancreatitis.* (2013) [guideline] -

## [0065] Acute pancreatitis fluid and nutrition strategy

**Cluster**: acute_pancreatitis_fluid_and_nutrition | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Fluid stewardship and nutritional support have undergone a paradigm shift in acute pancreatitis management. The historic practice of aggressive volume resuscitation ('forced hydration') was conclusively refuted by the landmark WATERFALL trial, establishing goal-directed moderate fluid resuscitation with balanced crystalloids (Ringer's lactate) as the modern standard. Similarly, the historic dogma of 'putting the pancreas to rest' via prolonged fasting has been replaced by early enteral nutrition: starting oral feeding immediately in mild pancreatitis and initiating enteral tube feeding (nasogastric or nasojejunal) within 24-72 hours in severe disease, preserving gut mucosal integrity and preventing infected pancreatic necrosis.

### Core Teaching Points
- The WATERFALL randomized trial showed that aggressive hydration doubled the incidence of fluid overload without accelerating clinical resolution; guidelines now advise moderate Ringer's lactate resuscitation (1.5 mL/kg/h maintenance after 10 mL/kg bolus only if hypovolemic). (PMID 36103415)
- In mild acute pancreatitis, immediate oral refeeding with a low-fat solid or soft diet is safe, reduces length of hospital stay by 1.5 to 2 days, and does not increase pain relapse compared to gradual step-up liquid regimens. (PMID 23809820)
- The multicenter Dutch PYTHON randomized trial demonstrated that very early nasoenteric tube feeding (within 24 hours) did not reduce infected necrosis or death compared to an on-demand strategy allowing oral diet at 72 hours in predicted severe pancreatitis, with 69% of patients tolerating oral food without needing a feeding tube. (PMID 25409371)
- Enteral nutrition is vastly superior to total parenteral nutrition (TPN), significantly reducing infectious complications, systemic inflammatory response syndrome (SIRS), multi-organ failure, and overall mortality by preserving intestinal barrier function. (PMID 23809820, PMID 25409371)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Nasogastric versus nasojejunal tube feeding tolerability and aspiration risk in patients with severe pancreatitis-induced gastric ileus.
- Role of immunonutrition (glutamine, omega-3 fatty acids) in reducing systemic inflammatory cascades during severe necrotizing pancreatitis.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Fluid administration rates and targets validated in the WATERFALL trial (1.5 mL/kg/h versus aggressive 3.0 mL/kg/h).
- Findings of the PYTHON trial regarding early routine nasoenteric feeding versus oral diet challenge at 72 hours.
- Immunological and infectious hazards of total parenteral nutrition compared to enteral nutrition in acute pancreatitis.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 38-year-old male with mild alcohol-induced pancreatitis whose abdominal pain and nausea resolve at 18 hours, ready for immediate low-fat solid oral intake.
- A 50-year-old female with predicted severe pancreatitis in the ICU requiring enteral tube feeding due to inability to tolerate oral diet by day 3.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36103415**: *Early Moderate or Aggressive Fluid Resuscitation for Acute Pancreatitis (WATERFALL Trial).* (2022) [trial] - 
- **PMID 25409371**: *Early, on-demand nasoenteric tube feeding versus oral diet in predicted severe acute pancreatitis (PYTHON Trial).* (2014) [trial] - 
- **PMID 23809820**: *IAP/APA evidence-based guidelines for the management of acute pancreatitis.* (2013) [guideline] -

## [0066] Acute pancreatitis in the ICU: Ranson, APACHE, organ support

**Cluster**: acute_pancreatitis_icu_organ_support | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Critically ill patients with severe acute pancreatitis in the ICU suffer high mortality (20-30%) driven by a biphasic clinical trajectory: early multi-organ failure from massive systemic inflammatory response syndrome (SIRS), followed by late sepsis from infected pancreatic necrosis. Early bedside risk stratification utilizing the Bedside Index for Severity in Acute Pancreatitis (BISAP) or APACHE-II score identifies patients at risk of rapid deterioration. Management centers on lung-protective ventilation, restrictive fluid therapy after initial stabilization, avoiding prophylactic antibiotics or routine probiotics (disproven in PROPATRIA), and multidisciplinary coordination.

### Core Teaching Points
- The BISAP score (BUN > 25 mg/dL, Impaired mental status, SIRS >= 2 criteria, Age > 60, Pleural effusion) provides simple, accurate prognostic stratification within 24 hours, with scores >= 3 correlating with a > 7-to-12-fold increase in mortality. (PMID 18725544)
- Routine administration of prophylactic systemic antibiotics for sterile necrotizing pancreatitis is not recommended by IAP/APA guidelines, as randomized trials demonstrate no reduction in infected necrosis or mortality, but an increased risk of multi-drug resistant fungal superinfections. (PMID 23809820)
- The PROPATRIA multicenter randomized trial revealed that prophylactic probiotic administration in predicted severe acute pancreatitis significantly increased bowel ischemia and nearly doubled all-cause mortality (16% vs 6%), leading to a strict black-box contraindication against prophylactic probiotics. (PMID 18279948)
- Intra-abdominal hypertension (IAH, intra-abdominal pressure >= 12 mmHg) and abdominal compartment syndrome (ACS, IAP > 20 mmHg with new organ failure) are frequent in severe pancreatitis; protocolized decompression (nasogastric drainage, sedation, paracentesis, neuromuscular blockade) is critical to preserve renal and splanchnic perfusion. (PMID 23809820)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Comparative accuracy of BISAP versus APACHE-II and Ranson scores in predicting prolonged ICU stay and organ failure duration.
- Surgical versus percutaneous decompressive strategies in refractory abdominal compartment syndrome complicating pancreatitis.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Five components of the BISAP score and their predictive value for severe acute pancreatitis mortality.
- Clinical findings and lethal bowel ischemia risks of probiotic administration identified in the PROPATRIA trial.
- Indications and futility of routine prophylactic antibiotic administration in sterile pancreatic necrosis.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 67-year-old male with pancreatitis, BUN 34 mg/dL, heart rate 108 bpm, respiratory rate 24 bpm, confusion, and right pleural effusion (BISAP 5) admitted to the ICU.
- A 54-year-old female in the ICU on mechanical ventilation whose bladder pressure rises to 23 mmHg with concurrent oliguria and rising airway pressures.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 18725544**: *A prospective evaluation of the bedside index for severity in acute pancreatitis (BISAP) score in assessing mortality and intermediate markers of severity.* (2008) [trial] - 
- **PMID 23809820**: *IAP/APA evidence-based guidelines for the management of acute pancreatitis.* (2013) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 18279948**: *Probiotic prophylaxis in predicted severe acute pancreatitis: a randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial (PROPATRIA).* (2008) [trial] -

## [0067] Acute pericarditis: colchicine, NSAIDs, recurrence prevention

**Cluster**: acute_pericarditis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute pericarditis is diagnosed by fulfilling at least two of four cardinal criteria: pleuritic chest pain, pericardial friction rub, new widespread PR-segment depression or ST-segment elevation, and new or worsening pericardial effusion. High-dose NSAIDs (e.g., ibuprofen 600-800 mg TID or indomethacin 50 mg TID) combined with weight-adjusted colchicine (0.5 mg BID, or 0.5 mg daily if weight < 70 kg) for 3 months represent the first-line evidence-based regimen. The landmark ICAP and COPE trials proved that adding colchicine halves recurrence rates and accelerates symptom remission, whereas systemic corticosteroids should be avoided as first-line therapy due to increased recurrence risk.

### Core Teaching Points
- In the landmark ICAP randomized trial (240 patients), adding colchicine (0.5 mg daily or BID for 3 months) to standard anti-inflammatory therapy halved the rate of recurrent pericarditis from 37.5% to 16.7% (relative risk reduction 56%) and significantly reduced symptom persistence at 72 hours. (PMID 23992556)
- The COPE trial confirmed in first-episode acute pericarditis that colchicine plus aspirin significantly reduced 18-month recurrence rates compared to aspirin alone (10.7% vs 32.3%) and shortened time to clinical remission. (PMID 16183436)
- The 2015 ESC Pericardial Diseases Guidelines recommend high-dose NSAIDs (ibuprofen 600-800 mg TID or aspirin 750-1000 mg TID) with gastroprotection plus colchicine for 3 months as first-line therapy, with tapering guided by symptom resolution and C-reactive protein (CRP) normalization. (PMID 26320112)
- Systemic corticosteroids are classified as second-line therapy for patients with contraindications or intolerance to NSAIDs/colchicine, or specific autoimmune etiologies; upfront steroid use independently predicts disease chronicity and multiple relapses. (PMID 26320112, PMID 23992556)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration and tapering velocity of interleukin-1 receptor antagonists (anakinra, rilonacept) in refractory corticosteroid-dependent recurrent pericarditis.
- Role of exercise restriction duration: whether resting until CRP normalizes is superior to arbitrary 3-month exercise bans in non-athletes.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Four cardinal diagnostic criteria for acute pericarditis under ESC guidelines.
- Magnitude of recurrence reduction achieved by adding colchicine in the ICAP and COPE trials.
- Detrimental effects of early systemic corticosteroid administration on pericarditis recurrence and chronicity.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 31-year-old male presenting with sharp chest pain worse lying flat and relieved leaning forward, widespread PR depressions, and CRP 42 mg/L.
- A 45-year-old female with recurrent pericardial chest pain who was inappropriately treated with a 5-day course of high-dose prednisone without colchicine.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 23992556**: *A Randomized Trial of Colchicine for Acute Pericarditis (ICAP Trial).* (2013) [trial] - 
- **PMID 16183436**: *Colchicine as first-choice therapy for recurrent pericarditis: results of the CORE randomized trial.* (2005) [trial] - 
- **PMID 26320112**: *2015 ESC Guidelines for the diagnosis and management of pericardial diseases.* (2015) [guideline] -

## [0068] Acute Phosphate Nephropathy

**Cluster**: acute_phosphate_nephropathy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute phosphate nephropathy follows oral sodium phosphate bowel preparation, with calcium phosphate deposition in the kidney and often irreversible loss of function; polyethylene glycol preparations are safer.

### Core Teaching Points
- Acute phosphate nephropathy is characterised histologically by calcium phosphate deposition in tubules with tubular atrophy and interstitial fibrosis, without significant glomerular disease (Markowitz). (PMID 16299385)
- The course is often biphasic: an early asymptomatic rise in creatinine followed by progressive loss of renal function, and full recovery to baseline is uncommon. (PMID 16299385, PMID 18285573)
- Risk factors are baseline CKD (eGFR below 60), volume depletion, hypertension, older age, and ACE inhibitors, ARBs, diuretics or NSAIDs. (PMID 18285573, PMID 22890468)
- Oral sodium phosphate bowel preparations carry an FDA boxed warning; they should be avoided in CKD, heart failure, cirrhosis and electrolyte disturbance, and polyethylene glycol preparations are used instead. (PMID 22890468)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether temporary cessation of ACEi/ARBs 48 hours prior to colonoscopy preparation completely prevents crystalline phosphate tubular precipitation.
- Role of urinary calcium phosphate supersaturation indices in identifying patients susceptible to subclinical tubulointerstitial damage.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Risk factors and prevention.
- Characteristic biopsy findings of acute phosphate nephropathy (calcium phosphate tubular casts with Von Kossa positivity).
- Medications and clinical comorbid conditions conferring the highest risk of developing phosphate nephropathy after sodium phosphate purgatives.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old female on lisinopril and hydrochlorothiazide for hypertension who undergoes screening colonoscopy with OSP prep and presents 6 weeks later with creatinine 2.8 mg/dL (baseline 0.9 mg/dL).
- A 72-year-old male with stage 3 CKD requiring colonoscopy preparation, where PEG-electrolyte solution is prescribed to avoid hyperphosphatemia and nephrocalcinosis.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 16299385**: *Acute phosphate nephropathy: An unrecognized cause of acute and chronic kidney injury after oral sodium phosphate bowel purgatives.* (2005) [trial] - 
- **PMID 18285573**: *Acute phosphate nephropathy: a review.* (2008) [review] - 
- **PMID 22890468**: *KDIGO Clinical Practice Guideline for Acute Kidney Injury.* (2012) [guideline] -

## [0069] Acute Promyelocytic Leukaemia

**Cluster**: acute_promyelocytic_leukaemia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute promyelocytic leukemia (APL) is a medical emergency driven by the t(15;17)(q24.1;q21.2) chromosomal translocation encoding the PML::RARA fusion oncoprotein, leading to severe consumptive coagulopathy (disseminated intravascular coagulation and hyperfibrinolysis) and catastrophic hemorrhage. Immediate initiation of all-trans retinoic acid (ATRA) upon morphological suspicion—before cytogenetic confirmation—is mandatory. In standard- and intermediate-risk APL (WBC <= 10,000/uL), chemotherapy-free dual differentiation therapy with ATRA and arsenic trioxide (ATO) is the curative standard of care, achieving 95-98% complete remission and event-free survival rates superior to chemotherapy.

### Core Teaching Points
- In the landmark Italian-German APL0406 randomized phase 3 trial (Lo-Coco et al.), ATRA plus arsenic trioxide (ATO) proved non-inferior and statistically superior to ATRA plus idarubicin chemotherapy in low-to-intermediate risk APL, achieving 97% vs 80% 2-year event-free survival and zero secondary myelodysplasia. (PMID 23841729)
- The UK NCRI AML17 randomized trial confirmed that ATRA plus ATO significantly reduced relapse rates and improved 4-year event-free survival compared to standard chemotherapy across all risk groups, with lower treatment-related mortality. (PMID 26162804)
- European LeukemiaNet (ELN) guidelines emphasize that ATRA must be started immediately at the earliest clinical suspicion of APL without waiting for molecular confirmation, alongside aggressive platelet and cryoprecipitate transfusions to maintain fibrinogen > 150 mg/dL and platelets > 50,000/uL. (PMID 30737248)
- Differentiation syndrome (fever, dyspnea, pulmonary infiltrates, pleural effusions, peripheral edema) occurs in up to 25% of patients during ATRA/ATO therapy; immediate prompt initiation of intravenous dexamethasone (10 mg BID) halts progression without necessitating ATRA/ATO discontinuation unless severe multi-organ failure ensues. (PMID 30737248, PMID 23841729)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal consolidation and maintenance regimens using oral arsenic trioxide formulations to reduce hospitalizations and intravenous infusion requirements.
- Role of upfront prophylactic cytoreductive gemtuzumab ozogamicin or anthracyclines in high-risk APL (WBC > 10,000/uL) combined with ATRA/ATO.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Landmark efficacy results of chemotherapy-free ATRA plus arsenic trioxide from the APL0406 trial.
- Transfusion targets (platelets > 50,000/uL and fibrinogen > 150 mg/dL) to prevent fatal intracranial hemorrhage in acute APL presentation.
- Diagnostic recognition and emergency management of APL differentiation syndrome with dexamethasone.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 32-year-old female presenting with epistaxis, gum bleeding, extensive ecchymoses, WBC 2.4, platelets 18,000/uL, INR 1.9, and fibrinogen 80 mg/dL with promyelocytes on peripheral smear.
- A 40-year-old male with APL on day 5 of ATRA/ATO who develops fevers to 38.8°C, bilateral pulmonary infiltrates, and weight gain of 4 kg.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 23841729**: *Retinoic acid and arsenic trioxide for acute promyelocytic leukemia (APL0406 Trial).* (2013) [trial] - 
- **PMID 26162804**: *Arsenic trioxide and all-trans retinoic acid versus chemotherapy for acute promyelocytic leukaemia (AML17): open-label randomised phase 3 trial.* (2015) [trial] - 
- **PMID 30737248**: *European LeukemiaNet recommendations for the diagnosis and management of acute promyelocytic leukemia.* (2019) [guideline] -

## [0070] Acute psychosis: assessment, management and antipsychotic therapy

**Cluster**: acute_psychosis_assessment_and_therapy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute psychosis is characterized by hallucinations, delusions, thought disorganization, and loss of reality testing, precipitated by primary psychiatric disorders (schizophrenia, bipolar mania), substance intoxication/withdrawal, or underlying medical illness (encephalitis, endocrine disorders, systemic lupus). Initial management prioritizes safety, medical clearance, and calm, structured non-coercive verbal de-escalation. Pharmacotherapy with oral second-generation antipsychotics (e.g., olanzapine, risperidone, aripiprazole) represents the first-line evidence-based standard, while rapid tranquilization protocols are reserved for severe aggression threatening imminent injury.

### Core Teaching Points
- NICE clinical guideline CG178 recommends oral second-generation antipsychotics as first-line therapy for acute psychosis, advising individualized choice based on side-effect profiles (metabolic vs extrapyramidal vs hyperprolactinemia) in collaborative discussion with the patient. (PMID 31825618)
- A comprehensive Cochrane network meta-analysis of 402 randomized trials (Leucht et al., Lancet) established that all antipsychotics significantly reduce psychotic symptoms compared to placebo, with small differences in efficacy favoring clozapine, amisulpride, and olanzapine, but marked differences in weight gain, sedation, and extrapyramidal side effects. (PMID 31295368)
- The TREC randomized trials on rapid tranquilization in acute violent psychotic agitation demonstrated that intramuscular olanzapine or haloperidol combined with promethazine achieved rapid, safe tranquilization within 15-30 minutes with significantly fewer respiratory depressions than intramuscular benzodiazepines alone. (PMID 16905298)
- Physical restraints should be an absolute intervention of last resort, with continuous 1-to-1 nursing observation and regular vital sign/airway monitoring every 15 minutes to prevent restraint-related asphyxia and positional death. (PMID 31825618)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Biomarkers differentiating first-episode primary psychiatric psychosis from autoantibody-mediated autoimmune encephalitis (anti-NMDAR encephalitis).
- Optimal duration of maintenance antipsychotic therapy following complete remission of a first-episode psychosis.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Comparative efficacy and side effect stratification across second-generation antipsychotics established in the Leucht et al. network meta-analysis.
- Pharmacological agents and safety monitoring in rapid tranquilization of severe combative agitation based on the TREC trials.
- Medical 'clearance' investigations required prior to attributing acute psychosis to primary psychiatric etiology.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 21-year-old college student with 3 weeks of persecutory delusions, auditory hallucinations, and social withdrawal without prior psychiatric history.
- A 35-year-old acutely agitated male in the emergency department combative with staff, stabilized using verbal de-escalation followed by oral disintegrating olanzapine.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

## [0071] Acute pulmonary oedema: NIV, morphine controversy, nitrates, ultrafiltration

**Cluster**: acute_pulmonary_oedema | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute pulmonary edema secondary to decompensated heart failure represents an extreme emergency characterized by alveolar flooding, severe dyspnea, tachypnea, hypoxemia, and diaphoresis. Management requires immediate upright positioning, non-invasive positive pressure ventilation (NIV: CPAP or BiPAP), and aggressive peripheral vasodilatation with intravenous nitrates (nitroglycerin or isosorbide dinitrate) in hypertensive presentations. Historic routine administration of intravenous morphine is now strongly discouraged due to increased ICU admission, intubation, and mortality rates. Intravenous loop diuretics should be dosed dynamically, with ultrafiltration reserved for refractory diuretic resistance.

### Core Teaching Points
- The randomized multicenter 3CPO trial (1,069 patients) showed that non-invasive ventilation (either CPAP or BiPAP) induced rapid physiological improvements in dyspnea, acidosis, and hypercapnia compared to standard oxygen therapy, with CPAP being the simplest and most cost-effective initial modality. (PMID 18614781)
- A large-scale analysis from the ADHERE registry (147,362 patients) demonstrated that intravenous morphine administration in acute cardiogenic pulmonary edema was independently associated with a near-doubling of in-hospital mortality (OR 1.8), higher intubation rates, and prolonged ICU stay. (PMID 18037380)
- The UNLOAD trial showed that early venous ultrafiltration in hypervolemic heart failure produced greater weight loss and fluid removal with significantly lower 90-day heart failure rehospitalizations compared to continuous intravenous diuretics. (PMID 17299094)
- The 2021 ESC Heart Failure Guidelines emphasize high-dose intravenous vasodilators (nitrates) as first-line therapy in acute hypertensive pulmonary edema (systolic BP > 110 mmHg) to rapidly reduce cardiac preload and afterload, while restricting opioids to palliative end-of-life care. (PMID 34447992)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether CPAP or bilevel NIV provides superior alveolar recruitment and physiological relief in patients with concurrent COPD and cardiogenic edema.
- Optimal protocol for rapid high-dose intravenous nitroglycerin boluses (e.g., 2 mg IV pushes) in flash pulmonary edema versus continuous infusion.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Physiological benefits and clinical outcomes of non-invasive ventilation from the 3CPO trial.
- Evidence from the ADHERE registry regarding harmful outcomes associated with routine morphine in acute pulmonary edema.
- Role of high-dose intravenous nitrates versus loop diuretics as the primary initial therapy in hypertensive flash pulmonary edema.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 72-year-old female presenting with severe orthopnea, bilateral crackles to apices, BP 210/110 mmHg, and oxygen saturation 82% on room air, initiated on CPAP 10 cmH2O and IV nitroglycerin.
- A 65-year-old male with ischemic cardiomyopathy and refractory severe hypervolemia exhibiting diuretic resistance despite IV bumetanide infusions, evaluated for ultrafiltration.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 18614781**: *Noninvasive ventilation in acute cardiogenic pulmonary edema (3CPO Trial).* (2008) [trial] - 
- **PMID 18037380**: *Association of intravenous morphine use and outcomes in acute decompensated heart failure: a report from the ADHERE registry.* (2008) [trial] - 
- **PMID 17299094**: *Ultrafiltration versus intravenous diuretics for patients hospitalized for acute decompensated heart failure (UNLOAD Trial).* (2007) [trial] - 
- **PMID 34447992**: *2021 ESC Guidelines for the diagnosis and treatment of acute and chronic heart failure.* (2021) [guideline] -

## [0072] Acute red eye: differential diagnosis and emergency management

**Cluster**: acute_red_eye_differential_diagnosis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute red eye ranges from benign conjunctivitis to sight-threatening angle-closure glaucoma, keratitis and uveitis; red flags decide who needs urgent ophthalmology.

### Core Teaching Points
- Red flags for sight-threatening disease are deep or boring pain, photophobia, reduced visual acuity, ciliary flush, a hazy cornea, a fixed mid-dilated pupil or trauma; these need same-day ophthalmology review. (PMID 30580948)
- Acute angle-closure glaucoma causes severe unilateral pain, headache, nausea, a hazy cornea and a fixed mid-dilated pupil; treat with timolol, apraclonidine, IV acetazolamide and, once pressure falls, pilocarpine, followed by laser peripheral iridotomy. (PMID 30580948)
- Fluorescein staining under cobalt blue light is essential in contact lens wearers to detect keratitis (Pseudomonas, Acanthamoeba); topical steroids must not be started without slit-lamp examination. (PMID 30580948)
- Adenoviral conjunctivitis causes watery discharge, follicles and preauricular lymphadenopathy; it is self-limiting and managed with hygiene, not topical antibiotics. (PMID 30580948)
- Acute anterior uveitis causes photophobia, pain and ciliary flush with a small or irregular pupil; scleritis causes severe boring pain and violaceous redness. Both need ophthalmology assessment. (PMID 30580948)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Utility of point-of-care rapid adenovirus antigen testing in reducing inappropriate topical antibiotic prescriptions in acute conjunctivitis.
- Comparative efficacy of topical NSAIDs versus low-potency topical steroids in non-necrotizing anterior episcleritis.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Red flags that exclude benign conjunctivitis.
- Cardinal red flag features distinguishing sight-threatening ocular pathology from uncomplicated superficial conjunctivitis.
- Emergency pharmacological cocktail and definitive surgical procedure (laser peripheral iridotomy) for acute angle-closure glaucoma.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 64-year-old hyperopic female presenting with sudden severe left eye pain, nausea, halos around lights, visual acuity 20/200, cloudy cornea, and IOP of 55 mmHg.
- A 22-year-old extended-wear soft contact lens user presenting with a red eye, foreign body sensation, and a 2-mm white focal stromal infiltrate on the central cornea.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources

## [0073] acute respiratory failure

**Cluster**: acute_respiratory_failure | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute respiratory failure is categorized into Type 1 (hypoxemic: PaO2 < 60 mmHg [8 kPa] with normal or low PaCO2) and Type 2 (hypercapnic: PaCO2 > 45-50 mmHg [6-6.5 kPa] with respiratory acidemia). Type 1 failure results from ventilation-perfusion mismatch, intrapulmonary shunt, or diffusion impairment (e.g., ARDS, pneumonia, pulmonary edema), while Type 2 failure reflects ventilatory pump failure, central hypoventilation, or neuromuscular weakness (e.g., severe COPD, myasthenia gravis, drug overdose). Conservative oxygenation targeting SpO2 92-96% (or 88-92% in patients at hypercapnic risk) avoids oxygen toxicity, while High-Flow Nasal Cannula (HFNC) and non-invasive ventilation provide first-line advanced support.

### Core Teaching Points
- In the landmark FLORALI trial (Frat et al., NEJM), high-flow nasal cannula (HFNC) oxygen therapy significantly reduced 90-day mortality (hazard ratio 0.42) and ventilator days compared to standard oxygen or non-invasive ventilation in patients with acute hypoxemic respiratory failure (PaO2/FiO2 <= 300). (PMID 25981908)
- Multicenter randomized trials (LOCO2 and HOT-ICU) demonstrated that liberal oxygenation strategies targeting supranormal PaO2 levels do not improve survival and can increase mesenteric and myocardial ischemia, establishing a conservative oxygenation target (PaO2 55-80 mmHg or SpO2 92-96%). (PMID 32163690, PMID 33535973)
- British Thoracic Society (BTS) emergency oxygen guidelines recommend target SpO2 ranges of 94-98% for most acutely ill patients, but a restricted target of 88-92% for patients at risk of hypercapnic respiratory failure (COPD, neuromuscular disease, morbid obesity hypoventilation). (PMID 28515286)
- For acute hypercapnic respiratory failure secondary to COPD exacerbation (pH < 7.35 and PaCO2 > 45 mmHg), non-invasive positive pressure ventilation (NIV/BiPAP) reduces the need for intubation by > 60% and reduces hospital mortality by half. (PMID 28515286, PMID 25981908)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- ROX index thresholds (SpO2/FiO2 divided by respiratory rate) predicting successful HFNC weaning versus need for timely endotracheal intubation.
- Role of awake prone positioning combined with HFNC in non-intubated hypoxemic respiratory failure outside COVID-19 ARDS.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Differences in mortality outcomes between HFNC, NIV, and standard oxygen in the FLORALI trial.
- Target oxygen saturation ranges for hypoxemic versus hypercapnia-prone respiratory failure according to BTS guidelines.
- Evidence-based indications and physiological mechanisms of non-invasive ventilation in acute hypercapnic respiratory failure.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 58-year-old male with severe bilateral viral pneumonia, respiratory rate 32 bpm, PaO2/FiO2 ratio 160 on non-rebreather mask, started on HFNC 60 L/min and FiO2 0.60.
- A 68-year-old female with severe COPD admitted with acute somnolence, arterial blood gas showing pH 7.26, PaCO2 68 mmHg, and PaO2 54 mmHg on 4 L nasal cannula, initiated on emergency BiPAP.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 25981908**: *High-flow oxygen through nasal cannula in acute hypoxemic respiratory failure (FLORALI Trial).* (2015) [trial] - 
- **PMID 32163690**: *Conservative versus Liberal Oxygen Therapy in Adults with Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (LOCO2 Trial).* (2020) [trial] - 
- **PMID 33535973**: *Lower versus Higher Oxygenation Target in Adults with Acute Hypoxaemic Respiratory Failure (HOT-ICU Trial).* (2021) [trial] - 
- **PMID 28515286**: *British Thoracic Society Guideline for oxygen use in adults in healthcare and emergency settings.* (2017) [guideline] -

## [0074] Acute severe asthma

**Cluster**: acute_severe_asthma | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute severe asthma is a life-threatening exacerbation defined clinically by peak expiratory flow (PEF) 33-50% of predicted/best, inability to complete sentences in one breath, respiratory rate >= 25 bpm, or heart rate >= 110 bpm. Life-threatening features ('silent chest', cyanosis, exhaustion, altered consciousness, PEF < 33%, or normal/elevated PaCO2) require immediate ICU escalation. First-line pharmacotherapy consists of high-dose inhaled short-acting beta-2 agonists (salbutamol) combined with ipratropium bromide via oxygen-driven nebulization, early systemic corticosteroids (oral prednisolone 40-50 mg or IV hydrocortisone), and intravenous magnesium sulfate.

### Core Teaching Points
- The multicenter 3Mg randomized trial demonstrated that a single infusion of intravenous magnesium sulfate (1.2 to 2 g over 20 minutes) produces rapid, clinically meaningful bronchodilation and reduces hospital admission in patients with acute severe asthma refractory to initial nebulized therapies. (PMID 24707886)
- British Thoracic Society (BTS/SIGN) and GINA guidelines classify asthma exacerbations into Moderate, Severe (PEF 33-50%, RR >= 25, HR >= 110, unable to complete sentences), and Life-threatening (PEF < 33%, SpO2 < 92%, silent chest, cyanosis, exhaustion, confusion, or normal/elevated PaCO2). (PMID 30580795)
- Early administration of systemic corticosteroids within 1 hour of emergency presentation significantly reduces hospital admission rates and prevents relapse; oral prednisolone (40-50 mg daily for 5 days) is as effective as intravenous corticosteroids unless the patient is unable to swallow or vomiting. (PMID 30580795)
- A 'normal' or rising PaCO2 (> 40-42 mmHg [5.3 kPa]) in an acutely tachypneic asthma patient indicates severe respiratory muscle exhaustion and imminent arrest, requiring immediate senior anesthesia consultation for endotracheal intubation. (PMID 30580795, PMID 24707886)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy of intravenous ketamine or aminophylline infusions as second-line rescue bronchodilators in refractory near-fatal asthma.
- Optimal mechanical ventilation strategies (permissive hypercapnia, low tidal volumes, prolonged expiratory time) to prevent dynamic hyperinflation and tension pneumothorax during status asthmaticus.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Clinical and physiological criteria defining Life-Threatening Asthma versus Severe Asthma under BTS/SIGN guidelines.
- Clinical significance of an apparently 'normal' PaCO2 on arterial blood gas during an acute severe asthma attack.
- Evidence and indications for intravenous magnesium sulfate established in the 3Mg trial.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 24-year-old male with asthma presenting with speech limited to single words, respiratory rate 34 bpm, pulse 128 bpm, and PEF 180 L/min (38% predicted) receiving back-to-back salbutamol/ipratropium and IV magnesium.
- A 19-year-old female with acute severe asthma who becomes drowsy; arterial blood gas reveals pH 7.31 and PaCO2 44 mmHg, signaling impending respiratory arrest.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 24707886**: *Intravenous or nebulised magnesium sulphate versus standard therapy for severe acute asthma (3Mg Trial).* (2014) [trial] - 
- **PMID 30580795**: *British guideline on the management of asthma (BTS/SIGN Guideline 158).* (2019) [guideline] -

## [0075] acute thrombolysis

**Cluster**: acute_thrombolysis_indications | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute thrombolytic therapy with recombinant tissue plasminogen activators (alteplase, tenecteplase) offers rapid reperfusion in life-threatening thrombotic emergencies, but carries catastrophic intracranial hemorrhage risks requiring strict patient selection. In acute ischemic stroke, IV alteplase or tenecteplase within 4.5 hours of symptom onset preserves neurological functional independence (NINDS, ECASS-3). In massive pulmonary embolism with hemodynamic instability, systemic thrombolysis reduces all-cause mortality, whereas in intermediate-high risk PE (PEITHO trial), rescue thrombolysis is restricted to hemodynamic decompensation. In STEMI, thrombolysis is indicated within 12 hours when primary PCI cannot be achieved within 120 minutes (STREAM trial).

### Core Teaching Points
- The landmark NINDS rt-PA Stroke Study proved that intravenous alteplase administered within 3 hours of ischemic stroke onset resulted in a 30% absolute increase in patients achieving minimal or no disability at 3 months (OR 1.7) despite a 6.4% symptomatic intracranial hemorrhage rate. (PMID 7477192)
- The multinational PEITHO randomized trial (1,006 patients with intermediate-high-risk PE) showed that tenecteplase significantly prevented hemodynamic collapse (2.6% vs 5.6%) but tripled major extracranial bleeding and ten-folded hemorrhagic stroke (2.0% vs 0.2%), establishing that routine thrombolysis should be withheld in normotensive intermediate-risk PE until overt shock develops. (PMID 24716681)
- The STREAM randomized trial established that in STEMI patients presenting within 3 hours who cannot undergo primary PCI within 60-90 minutes, pre-hospital fibrinolysis with half-dose tenecteplase (in patients >= 75 years) followed by timely catheterization yielded equivalent clinical efficacy to primary PCI. (PMID 23477786)
- Absolute contraindications to systemic thrombolysis include prior intracranial hemorrhage, known structural cerebral vascular lesion or neoplasm, ischemic stroke within 3 months, active internal bleeding, and recent severe head/facial trauma within 3 months. (PMID 7477192, PMID 24716681, PMID 23477786)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Tenecteplase non-inferiority and practical advantages over alteplase in acute ischemic stroke and large-vessel occlusion prior to thrombectomy.
- Safety and efficacy of catheter-directed low-dose ultrasound-accelerated thrombolysis (EKOS) versus systemic thrombolysis in submassive pulmonary embolism.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Time window and disability outcomes from the NINDS rt-PA trial for acute ischemic stroke.
- Clinical findings of the PEITHO trial regarding major hemorrhage and mortality in intermediate-risk submassive pulmonary embolism.
- Pharmacoinvasive strategy parameters and timing from the STREAM trial for STEMI when PCI is delayed.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 63-year-old male presenting 2 hours after acute onset right hemiparesis and aphasia (NIHSS 14) with non-contrast CT head showing no hemorrhage or early infarction.
- A 55-year-old female on day 3 post-knee arthroplasty who develops acute dyspnea, systolic blood pressure 75 mmHg refractory to fluids, and RV strain on echocardiography.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 7477192**: *Tissue plasminogen activator for acute ischemic stroke (The National Institute of Neurological Disorders and Stroke rt-PA Stroke Study).* (1995) [trial] - 
- **PMID 24716681**: *Fibrinolysis for patients with intermediate-risk pulmonary embolism (PEITHO Trial).* (2014) [trial] - 
- **PMID 23477786**: *Fibrinolysis or primary PCI in ST-segment elevation myocardial infarction (STREAM Trial).* (2013) [trial] -

## [0076] Acute Transplant Rejection

**Cluster**: acute_transplant_rejection | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute allograft rejection presents as acute graft dysfunction categorized histologically under the international Banff classification into T cell-mediated rejection (TCMR) and antibody-mediated rejection (ABMR). Indication allograft biopsy is the definitive gold standard to differentiate rejection from calcineurin inhibitor nephrotoxicity, BK polyomavirus nephropathy, or acute tubular necrosis. TCMR responds promptly to high-dose intravenous methylprednisolone pulses (500-1,000 mg daily for 3 days), with anti-thymocyte globulin (ATG) reserved for steroid-resistant or high-grade vascular rejection (Banff II/III). Conversely, ABMR—driven by donor-specific anti-HLA antibodies (DSA) and microvascular inflammation—requires multimodal desensitization combining therapeutic plasma exchange, intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG), and optimization of baseline immunosuppression.

### Core Teaching Points
- KDIGO clinical practice guidelines for kidney transplant recipients mandate prompt percutaneous core biopsy prior to treating acute allograft dysfunction to distinguish TCMR from ABMR and infection. (PMID 20176840)
- First-line therapy for acute TCMR consists of intravenous methylprednisolone (500-1000 mg/day for 3 days), which reverses > 80% of Grade IA/IB rejection episodes; steroid-resistant TCMR or vascular rejection (Banff II/III) warrants rabbit anti-thymocyte globulin (rATG). (PMID 20176840)
- Acute ABMR is diagnosed by the triad of graft dysfunction, histological microvascular inflammation (peritubular capillaritis/glomerulitis or C4d deposition), and circulating donor-specific anti-HLA antibodies (DSA). (PMID 20176840, PMID 30045899)
- Management of acute active ABMR centers on rapid removal and neutralization of donor-specific antibodies using therapeutic plasma exchange (PLEX) coupled with high-dose intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG 1-2 g/kg). (PMID 20176840)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy of IL-6 receptor antagonists (tocilizumab) and complement inhibitors (eculizumab) in chronic active antibody-mediated rejection.
- Clinical utility of donor-derived cell-free DNA (dd-cfDNA) as a non-invasive surveillance biomarker to detect subclinical allograft rejection.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Histopathological differences and treatment pathways between T cell-mediated versus antibody-mediated allograft rejection.
- Diagnostic triad required to establish active antibody-mediated kidney transplant rejection under Banff criteria.
- Indications for rabbit anti-thymocyte globulin versus pulse methylprednisolone in acute cellular rejection.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 42-year-old male 6 months post-renal transplant with serum creatinine rising from 1.2 to 2.3 mg/dL, whose biopsy shows interstitial tubulitis (Banff IB) and negative C4d.
- A 35-year-old female post-living donor kidney transplant presenting with graft tenderness, rising creatinine, positive de novo Class II donor-specific antibodies, and microvascular capillaritis.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 20176840**: *KDIGO clinical practice guideline for the care of kidney transplant recipients.* (2009) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 30045899**: *The Banff 2017 Kidney Meeting Report: Revised diagnostic criteria for chronic active antibody-mediated rejection and tubulitis.* (2018) [consensus-guideline] -

## [0077] Acute uncomplicated diverticulitis management

**Cluster**: acute_uncomplicated_diverticulitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute uncomplicated diverticulitis—defined by localized colonic inflammation without perforation, abscess, fistula, or obstruction (modified Hinchey Stage 0 or Ia)—has undergone an evidence-based demedicalization. Landmark randomized clinical trials (AVOD and DIABOLO) demonstrated that observational management without antibiotics yields equivalent healing, symptom resolution, complication rates, and recurrence compared to routine antibiotic therapy in immunocompetent patients. Contemporary WSES and AGA guidelines recommend symptomatic outpatient care with paracetamol and clear liquids, reserving antibiotics strictly for patients with systemic sepsis, immunosuppression, severe comorbidities, or high-risk features.

### Core Teaching Points
- The landmark Swedish AVOD randomized trial (623 patients) demonstrated that antibiotic treatment for acute uncomplicated diverticulitis did not shorten hospital stay, decrease complications (1.0% vs 1.9%), or reduce 1-year recurrences (16% in both arms). (PMID 22441961)
- The Dutch DIABOLO trial confirmed that observational management without antibiotics resulted in identical 6-month recovery rates, complication rates, and healthcare costs compared to a 10-day course of amoxicillin-clavulanate. (PMID 28062007)
- The 2020 World Society of Emergency Surgery (WSES) guidelines recommend an observational strategy without routine antibiotics for immunocompetent patients with uncomplicated diverticulitis (Stage 0/Ia) and no signs of systemic inflammatory response. (PMID 32660548)
- Antibiotic therapy (e.g., oral amoxicillin-clavulanate or ciprofloxacin plus metronidazole) should be strictly reserved for high-risk patients: immunosuppression, significant comorbidities (cirrhosis, CKD, uncontrolled diabetes), pregnancy, persistent fever, or CRP > 140 mg/L. (PMID 32660548, PMID 28062007)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration of non-antibiotic outpatient observation before repeating cross-sectional imaging in patients with persistent localized pain.
- Risk-benefit ratio of routine interval screening colonoscopy (at 6-8 weeks) following a first episode of CT-verified uncomplicated diverticulitis to exclude occult neoplasia.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Clinical equivalence outcomes between observational treatment versus antibiotics from the AVOD and DIABOLO trials.
- Specific patient cohorts and clinical red flags that mandate antibiotic therapy in acute diverticulitis.
- Diagnostic role and timing of contrast-enhanced CT of the abdomen/pelvis in confirming uncomplicated diverticulitis.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 48-year-old healthy male with CT-confirmed uncomplicated sigmoid diverticulitis (mild wall thickening, localized fat stranding, no abscess), successfully managed as an outpatient on clear fluids and paracetamol.
- A 64-year-old renal transplant recipient on tacrolimus and prednisone presenting with left lower quadrant tenderness and localized diverticulitis, requiring prompt broad-spectrum intravenous antibiotic coverage.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 22441961**: *Antibiotics do not improve recovery rates in acute uncomplicated diverticulitis: results of the AVOD randomized controlled trial.* (2012) [trial] - 
- **PMID 28062007**: *Observation versus antibiotic treatment for first episode of uncomplicated acute diverticulitis: a multicentre, open-label, randomised non-inferiority trial (DIABOLO).* (2017) [trial] - 
- **PMID 32660548**: *2020 WSES guidelines for the management of acute diverticulitis in the emergency setting.* (2020) [guideline] -

## [0078] ADHD pharmacotherapy (stimulants and comparative meta-analysis)

**Cluster**: adhd_pharmacotherapy_stimulants | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (ADHD) pharmacotherapy is guided by comprehensive comparative efficacy and tolerability data across pediatric and adult cohorts. In a landmark network meta-analysis of 133 randomized controlled trials (Cortese et al., Lancet Psychiatry), methylphenidate emerged as the first-choice medication in children and adolescents due to superior efficacy and favorable tolerability, whereas amphetamines (lisdexamfetamine) demonstrated superior efficacy in adults. Non-stimulant alternatives (atomoxetine, guanfacine, clonidine) represent valuable second-line options for patients with substance use disorders, intolerable stimulant-induced anxiety, or severe cardiovascular contraindications.

### Core Teaching Points
- A comprehensive network meta-analysis of 133 double-blind RCTs (Cortese et al., Lancet Psychiatry) established methylphenidate as the first-line medication for children/adolescents (highest balance of efficacy and tolerability), whereas amphetamines are the first-line pharmacotherapy in adults. (PMID 30097390)
- NICE Guideline NG87 recommends central nervous system stimulants (methylphenidate or lisdexamfetamine) as first-line pharmacological treatment for ADHD after environmental and behavioral modifications have been implemented. (PMID 29590458)
- Lisdexamfetamine is a prodrug requiring enzymatic cleavage by red blood cell aminopeptidases to release active d-amphetamine, conferring a smoother pharmacokinetic profile and significantly reduced diversion/abuse potential compared to immediate-release stimulants. (PMID 30097390, PMID 29590458)
- Cardiovascular safety baseline monitoring requires blood pressure, heart rate, and routine personal/family cardiac history screening; routine pre-treatment ECG is indicated only if personal symptoms (syncope, palpitations) or family history of sudden unexplained cardiac death < 40 years are present. (PMID 29590458)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term neurocognitive and cardiovascular safety of continuous stimulant therapy beyond 5-10 years of adult maintenance.
- Optimal clinical strategies for managing stimulant-induced appetite suppression and growth deceleration in prepubertal children.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- First-choice stimulant recommendations differing between children/adolescents (methylphenidate) and adults (amphetamines) based on Cortese et al.
- Mechanism of the prodrug lisdexamfetamine mitigating abuse and rapid peak bioavailability.
- Baseline cardiovascular and growth screening protocols recommended prior to initiating ADHD pharmacotherapy.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 9-year-old boy with severe classroom inattention and hyperactivity whose parents request medication, initiated on osmotic-release methylphenidate (OROS) with baseline height/weight tracking.
- A 27-year-old medical resident diagnosed with adult ADHD exhibiting executive dysfunction, started on low-dose lisdexamfetamine following normal cardiovascular exam.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 30097390**: *Comparative efficacy and tolerability of medications for attention-deficit hyperactivity disorder in children, adolescents, and adults: a systematic review and network meta-analysis.* (2018) [meta-analysis] - 
- **PMID 29590458**: *Attention deficit hyperactivity disorder: diagnosis and management (NICE Guideline NG87).* (2018) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 31826075**: *Attention deficit hyperactivity disorder: diagnosis and management (NICE Guideline NG87).* (2018) [guideline] -
