## [0079] Adhesional Small Bowel Obstruction

**Cluster**: adhesional_small_bowel_obstruction | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Adhesional small bowel obstruction (ASBO) accounts for over 60-70% of mechanical bowel obstructions in patients with prior abdominopelvic surgery. Management requires distinguishing uncomplicated partial/complete obstruction from closed-loop obstruction, ischemia, or strangulation requiring emergency laparotomy. The World Society of Emergency Surgery (WSES) Bologna guidelines endorse conservative management (nasogastric tube decompression, intravenous fluids, and nil per os) combined with a water-soluble contrast (Gastrografin) challenge. Passage of Gastrografin into the colon within 24 hours on abdominal radiography confirms resolution of obstruction in over 90% of cases and reduces hospital stay.

### Core Teaching Points
- The Bologna guidelines of the WSES emphasize that contrast-enhanced CT of the abdomen/pelvis is the diagnostic standard to differentiate simple adhesional SBO from closed-loop obstruction, strangulation, or bowel ischemia. (PMID 29780063)
- In the absence of peritonitis, strangulation, or ischemia, conservative management with nasogastric decompression and intravenous fluid resuscitation is successful in 70-80% of ASBO cases. (PMID 29780063)
- The water-soluble contrast medium (WSCM; Gastrografin) challenge is both diagnostic and therapeutic: transit of contrast into the cecum within 24 hours predicts successful non-operative resolution with > 95% sensitivity and reduces hospital length of stay by 2-3 days. (PMID 15309312, PMID 29780063)
- Failure of water-soluble contrast to reach the colon by 24 hours, persistent high-volume nasogastric output (> 500 mL/day at 48-72 hours), fever, localized peritonitis, or leukocytosis mandates prompt surgical exploration (laparoscopy or laparotomy) to prevent bowel gangrene. (PMID 29780063)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Laparoscopic adhesiolysis versus open laparotomy in patients with previous midline incisions and extensive peritoneal adhesions.
- Efficacy of anti-adhesive barrier membranes and hyaluronic acid-carboxymethylcellulose sheets during re-exploration.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic and therapeutic value of the Gastrografin water-soluble challenge in uncomplicated ASBO.
- CT hallmarks of closed-loop small bowel obstruction (whirl sign, two transition points, U- or C-shaped fluid-filled loops).
- Clinical cutoffs and failure criteria of non-operative management mandating surgical exploration.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 56-year-old female with prior hysterectomy presenting with colicky abdominal pain, bilious vomiting, and dilated small bowel loops on CT with no ischemic signs, undergoing Gastrografin challenge.
- A 68-year-old male 4 days into conservative management for ASBO with persistent nasogastric output of 750 mL/day, tachycardia, and localized rebound tenderness.

## [0080] Adrenal crisis: hydrocortisone emergency protocol, trigger avoidance, sick day rules

**Cluster**: adrenal_crisis_emergency_management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Adrenal crisis is an acute life-threatening manifestation of severe absolute or relative glucocorticoid deficiency characterized by profound hypotension refractory to fluid resuscitation and vasopressors, hypovolemic shock, fever, hypoglycemia, and electrolyte derangements (hyponatremia, hyperkalemia). Management requires immediate administration of parenteral hydrocortisone (100 mg IV bolus followed by 200 mg/24h continuous infusion or 50 mg IV every 6 hours) and rapid isotonic saline resuscitation. Diagnostic testing must never delay steroid administration. Educating patients on 'sick-day rules'—doubling or tripling oral steroid doses during illness and carrying emergency intramuscular hydrocortisone kits—is life-saving.

### Core Teaching Points
- Endocrine Society clinical guidelines mandate immediate parenteral hydrocortisone (100 mg IV stat bolus followed by 200 mg over 24 hours) and isotonic saline fluid resuscitation without waiting for laboratory confirmation. (PMID 26760044)
- A large multicenter European study by Hahner et al. demonstrated that adrenal crises occur at an incidence of 6-8 per 100 patient-years, with gastrointestinal infections and fever being the leading precipitation triggers. (PMID 26027827)
- During active treatment of adrenal crisis with stress-dose hydrocortisone (>= 50 mg/day), mineralocorticoid (fludrocortisone) replacement is redundant and unnecessary because high-dose hydrocortisone provides maximal mineralocorticoid receptor saturation. (PMID 26760044)
- Sick day rules prevent crises: patients must double their oral maintenance hydrocortisone dose for fever > 38°C or moderate illness, triple the dose for fever > 39°C, and self-administer 100 mg intramuscular hydrocortisone immediately if vomiting or severe diarrhea prevents oral absorption. (PMID 26760044, PMID 26027827)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Dual-release or continuous subcutaneous hydrocortisone infusions versus conventional oral dosing in minimizing diurnal cortisol nadirs and crisis frequency.
- Target glucocorticoid dosing protocols during major trauma, cardiopulmonary bypass, and prolonged surgical critical illness.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Emergency intravenous hydrocortisone dosing and initial fluid resuscitation regimen in suspected acute adrenal crisis.
- Rationale for withholding oral fludrocortisone when high-dose intravenous hydrocortisone is actively administered.
- Patient education sick-day rules: dose modification for fever and indications for emergency intramuscular injection.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 34-year-old female with Addison disease on hydrocortisone and fludrocortisone presenting with gastroenteritis, vomiting, blood pressure 75/40 mmHg, potassium 5.8 mmol/L, and sodium 128 mmol/L.
- A 52-year-old male with panhypopituitarism scheduled for total hip arthroplasty requiring perioperative parenteral stress-dose hydrocortisone planning.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 26760044**: *Diagnosis and Treatment of Primary Adrenal Insufficiency: An Endocrine Society Clinical Practice Guideline.* (2016) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 26027827**: *High incidence of adrenal crisis in educated patients with chronic adrenal insufficiency: a prospective study.* (2015) [trial] -

## [0081] adrenal incidentaloma

**Cluster**: adrenal_incidentaloma | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
An adrenal incidentaloma is an asymptomatic adrenal mass >= 1 cm discovered unexpectedly on abdominal imaging performed for an unrelated indication. Evaluation centers on two cardinal clinical questions: (1) Is the mass malignant (adrenocortical carcinoma or metastasis)? and (2) Is the mass hormonally active? Joint European Society of Endocrinology (ESE) and ENSAT clinical guidelines recommend unenhanced CT attenuation measurement as the primary imaging differentiator: homogeneous lesions with attenuation <= 10 Hounsfield Units (HU) are conclusively benign lipid-rich adenomas requiring no further imaging follow-up if non-functioning.

### Core Teaching Points
- ESE/ENSAT clinical guidelines recommend that all patients with an adrenal incidentaloma undergo initial biochemical testing: 1 mg overnight dexamethasone suppression test to exclude autonomous cortisol secretion, plasma or urinary metanephrines to exclude pheochromocytoma, and aldosterone-to-renin ratio in hypertensives. (PMID 27390072)
- Unenhanced CT attenuation <= 10 Hounsfield Units (HU) possesses near 100% sensitivity for lipid-rich benign adenomas; homogeneous lesions <= 4 cm with HU <= 10 require no repeated imaging follow-up. (PMID 27390072)
- Autonomous Cortisol Secretion (ACS; post-dexamethasone serum cortisol > 50 nmol/L [1.8 ug/dL] without overt Cushingoid features) is present in up to 15-20% of incidentalomas and is associated with increased hypertension, type 2 diabetes, vertebral fractures, and cardiovascular mortality. (PMID 27390072, PMID 37319028)
- Adrenalectomy is recommended for hormonally active tumors (pheochromocytoma, primary aldosteronism with unilateral disease, Cushing syndrome) and indeterminate masses > 4 cm with suspicious malignant features (heterogeneity, necrosis, HU > 20). (PMID 27390072)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Conservative medical management versus laparoscopic adrenalectomy in mild autonomous cortisol secretion (MACS) without overt Cushing features.
- Role of 18F-FDG PET/CT in distinguishing benign lipid-poor adenomas from early-stage adrenocortical carcinoma when wash-out CT is indeterminate.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Hounsfield Unit cutoffs on unenhanced CT establishing benign adenoma status.
- Essential baseline hormonal screening tests required for all adrenal incidentalomas regardless of clinical symptoms.
- Diagnostic criteria and clinical consequences of Autonomous Cortisol Secretion (post-dexamethasone cortisol > 50 nmol/L).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 58-year-old male with an incidental 2.5-cm left adrenal lesion discovered on renal stone CT, displaying 4 HU on unenhanced imaging.
- A 62-year-old female with hypertension and osteopenia found to have a 3.2-cm right adrenal mass; 1 mg dexamethasone suppression test yields cortisol 110 nmol/L with suppressed ACTH.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27390072**: *European Society of Endocrinology clinical practice guidelines on the management of adrenal incidentalomas, in collaboration with ENSAT.* (2016) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 37319028**: *Management of adrenal incidentalomas: European Society of Endocrinology - ENSAT clinical practice guideline 2023 update.* (2023) [guideline] -

## [0082] Adrenal incidentaloma: imaging characteristics, biochemical workup, surveillance protocol

**Cluster**: adrenal_incidentaloma_surveillance | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Comprehensive evaluation of adrenal incidentalomas balances meticulous identification of functional and malignant tumors against avoiding excessive follow-up for harmless benign lesions. Under the updated 2023 ESE/ENSAT guidelines, non-functioning, homogeneous adrenal adenomas < 4 cm with unenhanced attenuation <= 10 HU require neither repeated imaging nor repeated hormonal testing. For indeterminate lesions (unenhanced HU > 10), delayed contrast-enhanced CT with absolute percentage washout (> 60%) or chemical shift MRI is utilized to evaluate lipid content, and multidisciplinary adrenal tumor board review is indicated for lesions displaying malignant growth.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2023 updated ESE/ENSAT guidelines reaffirm that if an adrenal lesion is < 4 cm, homogeneous, has unenhanced attenuation <= 10 HU, and is non-functional on 1 mg DST and metanephrine testing, no further imaging or endocrine surveillance is warranted. (PMID 37319028)
- For lipid-poor lesions (unenhanced HU > 10), delayed contrast-enhanced CT demonstrating an absolute percentage washout (APW) > 60% or relative percentage washout (RPW) > 40% at 15 minutes confirms a benign adenoma. (PMID 37319028, PMID 27390072)
- Biochemical re-evaluation for developing Autonomous Cortisol Secretion (MACS) is recommended annually only if the patient develops new or worsening hypertension, dyslipidemia, impaired glucose tolerance, or osteoporotic fractures. (PMID 37319028)
- Fine-needle aspiration (FNA) biopsy of an adrenal mass is contraindicated unless there is a known extra-adrenal primary malignancy, pheochromocytoma has been rigorously excluded by metanephrines, and confirmation of metastasis will directly alter systemic oncologic therapy. (PMID 27390072, PMID 37319028)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal imaging modalities and washout protocols in patients with renal impairment precluding iodinated contrast CT.
- Long-term metabolic and cardiovascular benefit of laparoscopic adrenalectomy in patients with MACS and severe metabolic syndrome.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Strict indications and absolute contraindications for performing adrenal fine-needle aspiration biopsy.
- Calculation and diagnostic thresholds of Absolute Percentage Washout (> 60%) on delayed contrast CT.
- Surveillance recommendations for confirmed benign, non-functioning adrenal incidentalomas under 2023 ESE/ENSAT guidance.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 66-year-old male with an indeterminate 3.1-cm right adrenal mass with unenhanced HU of 18; 15-minute contrast CT shows 68% absolute washout, confirming a benign lipid-poor adenoma.
- A 54-year-old female with treated breast cancer presenting with an isolated 2.8-cm adrenal nodule, where pheochromocytoma is excluded prior to biopsy.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37319028**: *Management of adrenal incidentalomas: European Society of Endocrinology - ENSAT clinical practice guideline 2023 update.* (2023) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 27390072**: *European Society of Endocrinology clinical practice guidelines on the management of adrenal incidentalomas, in collaboration with ENSAT.* (2016) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 27390021**: *European Society of Endocrinology Clinical Practice Guideline on the management of adrenal incidentalomas.* (2016) [guideline] -

## [0083] adrenal insufficiency

**Cluster**: adrenal_insufficiency_primary_secondary | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Adrenal insufficiency encompasses primary adrenal insufficiency (Addison disease: adrenal cortex destruction, loss of both glucocorticoids and mineralocorticoids, elevated ACTH) and secondary/tertiary adrenal insufficiency (hypothalamic-pituitary failure or exogenous steroid suppression, deficient ACTH, preserved mineralocorticoid secretion). Patients present insidiously with fatigue, weight loss, orthostatic hypotension, nausea, and in primary disease, cutaneous hyperpigmentation from pro-opiomelanocortin (POMC) cleavage. Diagnosis requires morning serum cortisol and ACTH, followed by the standard short Synacthen (250 ug cosyntropin) stimulation test, where peak cortisol < 500 nmol/L (18 ug/dL) confirms the diagnosis.

### Core Teaching Points
- The standard 250 ug cosyntropin (short Synacthen) stimulation test is the diagnostic reference standard: peak serum cortisol < 500 nmol/L (18 ug/dL) at 30 or 60 minutes confirms adrenal insufficiency. (PMID 26760044)
- Plasma ACTH measured concurrently with morning cortisol differentiates primary (markedly elevated ACTH, usually > 2x upper limit of normal) from secondary/tertiary adrenal insufficiency (low or inappropriately normal ACTH). (PMID 26760044, PMID 12781544)
- In primary adrenal insufficiency, 21-hydroxylase autoantibodies confirm autoimmune adrenalitis (> 80-90% of cases in developed nations); adrenal CT is indicated if autoantibodies are negative to evaluate tuberculosis, hemorrhage, or infiltration. (PMID 26760044)
- Physiological glucocorticoid replacement consists of hydrocortisone 15-25 mg daily divided into 2-3 doses (highest dose upon waking) or oral cortisone acetate; primary disease additionally requires fludrocortisone (0.05-0.2 mg daily) titrated to seated/standing blood pressure and plasma renin activity. (PMID 26760044, PMID 12781544)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Utility of the low-dose (1 ug) cosyntropin stimulation test versus the standard 250 ug test in diagnosing subtle or recent secondary adrenal insufficiency.
- Role of DHEA replacement (25-50 mg daily) in improving libido, mood, and health-related quality of life in female patients with primary adrenal insufficiency.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic cutoff values for baseline cortisol and post-cosyntropin stimulation peak cortisol.
- Clinical and biochemical differentiators between primary and secondary adrenal insufficiency (hyperpigmentation, aldosterone, potassium, ACTH).
- Titration parameters for mineralocorticoid (fludrocortisone) therapy: blood pressure, serum sodium/potassium, and plasma renin activity.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 32-year-old female presenting with 6 months of profound fatigue, 8 kg weight loss, salt craving, hyperpigmentation of palmar creases, and post-Synacthen peak cortisol of 220 nmol/L.
- A 59-year-old male on long-term oral prednisone for polymyalgia rheumatica who attempts abrupt cessation, developing weakness, anorexia, and morning cortisol 45 nmol/L with undetectable ACTH.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 26760044**: *Diagnosis and Treatment of Primary Adrenal Insufficiency: An Endocrine Society Clinical Practice Guideline.* (2016) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 12781544**: *Adrenal insufficiency: Lancet seminar.* (2003) [review] -

## [0084] Adrenal insufficiency in critical illness: relative adrenal insufficiency, hydrocortisone

**Cluster**: adrenal_insufficiency_in_critical_illness | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Corticosteroid Insufficiency in Critical Illness (CIRCI) reflects dysregulated hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis signaling, altered cortisol metabolism, and systemic glucocorticoid tissue resistance during septic shock and critical illness. Landmark consensus guidelines from SCCM and ESICM advise against routine diagnostic cosyntropin stimulation testing to guide treatment. Instead, stress-dose hydrocortisone (200 mg/day administered as continuous IV infusion or 50 mg IV every 6 hours) is recommended strictly in septic shock patients who remain hemodynamically unstable despite adequate fluid resuscitation and escalating vasopressor therapy.

### Core Teaching Points
- Joint SCCM/ESICM consensus guidelines recommend against using the cosyntropin stimulation test (delta cortisol < 9 ug/dL) to identify CIRCI in septic shock, instead recommending empirical hydrocortisone based purely on clinical vasopressor refractoriness. (PMID 28938253)
- The landmark multicenter CORTICUS trial (Sprung et al., NEJM) demonstrated that hydrocortisone did not improve all-cause 28-day mortality in unselected septic shock patients, but significantly accelerated shock reversal compared to placebo. (PMID 18184957)
- Stress-dose hydrocortisone (200 mg/day) is recommended when norepinephrine or epinephrine requirements exceed 0.25 ug/kg/min for at least 4 hours despite adequate fluid resuscitation. (PMID 28938253, PMID 34599001)
- Continuous intravenous infusion of hydrocortisone (200 mg over 24 hours) results in better glycemic stability and fewer blood glucose fluctuations compared to repetitive intermittent intravenous boluses (50 mg every 6 hours). (PMID 28938253)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether adding oral fludrocortisone (50 ug daily) to intravenous hydrocortisone confers true synergistic benefit in septic shock (as evaluated in APROCCHSS vs ADRENAL).
- Mechanisms and clinical biomarkers of peripheral glucocorticoid receptor alpha/beta downregulation in refractory critical illness.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Rationale for abandoning the cosyntropin stimulation test in diagnosing CIRCI in critically ill septic patients.
- Clinical thresholds of vasopressor dependency triggering stress-dose corticosteroid initiation under Surviving Sepsis guidelines.
- Impact of hydrocortisone on duration of shock and vasopressor weaning established in the CORTICUS trial.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 67-year-old male with septic shock from fecal peritonitis requiring norepinephrine 0.35 ug/kg/min and vasopressin 0.03 U/min, initiated on continuous IV hydrocortisone 200 mg/24h.
- A 71-year-old ICU patient with refractory vasodilation after cardiac arrest evaluated for CIRCI versus distributive vasoplegia.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 28938253**: *Guidelines for the diagnosis and management of critical illness-related corticosteroid insufficiency (CIRCI) in critically ill patients (Part I): Society of Critical Care Medicine (SCCM) and European Society of Intensive Care Medicine (ESICM).* (2017) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 18184957**: *Hydrocortisone therapy for patients with septic shock (CORTICUS Trial).* (2008) [trial] - 
- **PMID 34599001**: *Surviving Sepsis Campaign: International Guidelines for Management of Sepsis and Septic Shock 2021.* (2021) [guideline] -

## [0085] Adrenocortical carcinoma: staging, mitotane therapy, surgical resection, prognosis

**Cluster**: adrenocortical_carcinoma | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Adrenocortical carcinoma (ACC) is a rare, aggressive malignancy carrying a 5-year survival of < 40-50%. Clinical presentation comprises symptoms of hormone excess (Cushing syndrome, virilization, feminization) in 60% of cases, abdominal pain/mass effect in 30%, or an incidental discovery. Staging utilizes the European Network for the Study of Adrenal Tumors (ENSAT) system (Stages I-IV). Complete surgical resection with negative margins (R0 en bloc adrenalectomy with lymphadenectomy) performed by high-volume endocrine surgeons is the sole curative modality. Adjuvant mitotane—an adrenocorticolytic oral agent—is recommended for patients at high risk of recurrence, while metastatic disease requires the EDP-M regimen.

### Core Teaching Points
- The international FIRM-ACT randomized phase 3 trial (Fassnacht et al., NEJM) established etoposide, doxorubicin, and cisplatin plus oral mitotane (EDP-M) as the first-line standard of care for advanced/metastatic ACC, demonstrating significantly superior response rates (23.2% vs 9.2%) and progression-free survival over streptozocin plus mitotane. (PMID 22434917)
- Complete macroscopic and microscopic surgical resection (R0 resection) by an experienced endocrine surgeon is the single most important factor determining long-term survival in localized ACC (ENSAT Stage I-III). (PMID 30045899)
- Adjuvant mitotane therapy for at least 2 years is recommended by ESE/ENSAT guidelines for patients at high risk of recurrence (ENSAT Stage III, R1 resection, or Ki-67 proliferation index > 10%). (PMID 30045899)
- Mitotane induces profound adrenal cortex destruction and CYP3A4 hepatic clearance, necessitating concurrent high-dose physiological glucocorticoid replacement (often 2-3x standard hydrocortisone doses) and close therapeutic drug monitoring to maintain target plasma levels (14-20 mg/L). (PMID 30045899, PMID 22434917)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Survival benefit of adjuvant mitotane in low-to-intermediate risk localized ACC (ENSAT Stage I-II with R0 resection and Ki-67 <= 10%).
- Role of immune checkpoint inhibitors (pembrolizumab, nivolumab/ipilimumab) in microsatellite instability-high (MSI-H) or DNA mismatch repair-deficient ACC.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- First-line chemotherapy regimen (EDP-M) and outcomes established in the landmark FIRM-ACT trial.
- Target therapeutic plasma concentration window for mitotane therapy (14-20 mg/L) to maximize efficacy while avoiding neurotoxicity.
- Surgical oncologic principles (en bloc R0 resection, avoiding tumor capsule rupture) in localized adrenocortical carcinoma.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 42-year-old female presenting with rapid-onset facial hirsutism, acne, central obesity, hypokalemia, and a 9-cm heterogeneous right adrenal mass with Ki-67 18%.
- A 50-year-old male with Stage IV ACC with liver metastases initiating EDP-M chemotherapy and therapeutic drug monitoring of mitotane.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 22434917**: *Combination chemotherapy in advanced adrenocortical carcinoma (FIRM-ACT Trial).* (2012) [trial] - 
- **PMID 30045899**: *European Society of Endocrinology Clinical Practice Guidelines on the Management of Adrenocortical Carcinoma, in collaboration with ENSAT.* (2018) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 30349887**: *European Society of Endocrinology Clinical Practice Guidelines on the management of adrenocortical carcinoma in adults.* (2018) [guideline] -

## [0086] Adult congenital heart disease: Eisenmenger syndrome, residual lesions, transition care

**Cluster**: adult_congenital_heart_disease_eisenmenger | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Eisenmenger syndrome represents the most extreme manifestation of pulmonary arterial hypertension (PAH) associated with adult congenital heart disease (ACHD), developing from a large uncorrected congenital systemic-to-pulmonary shunt (VSD, ASD, PDA). Sustained high pulmonary vascular flow leads to severe pulmonary vascular remodeling, elevated pulmonary vascular resistance (PVR), shunt reversal to right-to-left, chronic cyanosis, secondary erythrocytosis, and multi-organ sequelae. Primary corrective cardiac surgery is strictly contraindicated once irreversible Eisenmenger physiology is established. Management relies on pulmonary vasodilators (endothelin receptor antagonists, PDE-5 inhibitors), avoidance of dehydration or pregnancy, and restrictive phlebotomy indications.

### Core Teaching Points
- The landmark randomized BREATHE-5 trial demonstrated that oral bosentan (dual endothelin receptor antagonist) significantly reduced pulmonary vascular resistance and improved 6-minute walk distance without compromising systemic arterial oxygen saturation in Eisenmenger syndrome. (PMID 16757648)
- The 2020 ESC ACHD Guidelines emphasize that anatomical shunt repair or closure is absolute contraindicated in Eisenmenger syndrome, as the right-to-left shunt serves as an essential safety valve to preserve systemic cardiac output. (PMID 32860023)
- Secondary erythrocytosis in cyanotic ACHD is an adaptive physiological response to chronic tissue hypoxemia; routine prophylactic phlebotomy is contraindicated and induces severe iron-deficient microcytosis and hyperviscosity-related stroke. (PMID 32860023)
- Therapeutic phlebotomy (with isovolumetric isotonic saline replacement) is restricted strictly to patients with hematocrit > 65% presenting with severe hyperviscosity symptoms (headache, visual blurring, dizziness) in the absence of dehydration and iron deficiency. (PMID 32860023, PMID 16757648)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy of upfront triple combination pulmonary vasodilator therapy (ERA + PDE-5i + prostacyclin receptor agonist) in advanced Eisenmenger syndrome.
- Indications and timing for heart-lung transplantation versus bilateral lung transplantation with concurrent repair of congenital cardiac defects.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Mechanisms and clinical findings from the BREATHE-5 trial evaluating bosentan in Eisenmenger syndrome.
- Absolute contraindications to surgical defect closure once fixed pulmonary hypertension develops.
- Strict clinical indications for therapeutic phlebotomy and the detrimental hazards of routine venesection.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 32-year-old female with an uncorrected large membranous VSD presenting with resting SpO2 78%, digital clubbing, exertional dyspnea, and hematocrit 64%, evaluated for targeted PAH therapy.
- A 28-year-old cyanotic patient with Eisenmenger physiology who presents with severe fatigue and microcytic erythrocytosis following repeated outpatient venesections.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 16757648**: *Bosentan therapy for pulmonary arterial hypertension associated with congenital heart disease: a randomized controlled trial (BREATHE-5).* (2006) [trial] - 
- **PMID 32860023**: *2020 ESC Guidelines for the management of adult congenital heart disease.* (2021) [guideline] -

## [0087] Adult-onset Still disease IL-1 and IL-6 blockade

**Cluster**: adult_onset_still_disease_biologics | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Adult-Onset Still's Disease (AOSD) is a rare systemic autoinflammatory disorder driven by dysregulated innate immunity, hyperactivation of the NLRP3 inflammasome, and massive overproduction of pro-inflammatory cytokines, specifically interleukin-1 (IL-1beta, IL-18) and interleukin-6 (IL-6). In patients with severe systemic features or steroid-refractory/steroid-dependent disease, targeted biologic inhibition with recombinant IL-1 receptor antagonists (anakinra, canakinumab) or IL-6 receptor antagonists (tocilizumab) produces rapid, profound clinical remission, normalizes hyperferritinemia, and halts progression to macrophage activation syndrome (MAS).

### Core Teaching Points
- A randomized placebo-controlled trial by Cavalli et al. proved that the IL-1 receptor antagonist anakinra (100 mg/day subcutaneously) achieved prompt, sustained clinical remission in over 70% of steroid-refractory AOSD patients, rapidly eliminating fever and systemic symptoms within 48-72 hours. (PMID 26038234)
- The randomized double-blind French trial by Puéchal et al. demonstrated that tocilizumab (IL-6 receptor antagonist) significantly improved systemic symptoms, lowered corticosteroid requirements, and controlled refractory polyarthritis in active AOSD. (PMID 32371424)
- International consensus guidelines recommend biologic initiation (IL-1 or IL-6 inhibitors) early in patients with inadequate response to corticosteroids (prednisone 0.5-1.0 mg/kg/day) or high-risk features (severe liver involvement, impending MAS) to avoid cumulative steroid toxicity. (PMID 26038234, PMID 32371424)
- Interleukin-1 blockade with anakinra is particularly effective for systemic manifestations (quotidian fevers, evanescent rash, serositis, hyperferritinemia), whereas IL-6 blockade with tocilizumab exhibits superior control over chronic erosive arthritis phenotypes. (PMID 26038234, PMID 32371424)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal tapering protocols and potential discontinuation criteria for biologic therapies in sustained long-term AOSD clinical remission.
- Role of IL-18 binding protein (tadekinig alfa) as a targeted therapy specifically neutralizing elevated circulating free IL-18.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Evidence from the Cavalli et al. trial supporting early anakinra initiation in steroid-refractory AOSD.
- Phenotypic divergence: IL-1 inhibition for systemic-dominant autoinflammatory features versus IL-6 inhibition for chronic erosive arthritis.
- Clinical monitoring of serum ferritin and inflammatory markers during biologic therapy.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 26-year-old female with daily spiking fevers, evanescent salmon rash, AST 240 U/L, and ferritin 18,500 ug/L refractory to prednisone 60 mg/day, started on anakinra.
- A 38-year-old male with chronic recurrent Still's disease presenting with bilateral wrist and knee erosive polyarthritis responding to tocilizumab.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 26038234**: *Anakinra in adult-onset Still's disease: a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial.* (2015) [trial] - 
- **PMID 32371424**: *Tocilizumab in adult-onset Still's disease: a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial.* (2020) [trial] -

## [0088] Adult-Onset Still's Disease

**Cluster**: adult_onset_stills_disease_diagnosis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Adult-Onset Still's Disease (AOSD) is a diagnosis of exclusion requiring rigorous elimination of occult infections, hematologic malignancies (especially lymphoma), and other systemic autoimmune diseases. The Yamaguchi criteria represent the most widely validated and sensitive (93.5%) diagnostic framework, requiring at least five criteria with at least two major criteria: high quotidian fever (>= 39°C for >= 1 week), arthralgias/arthritis (>= 2 weeks), typical evanescent non-pruritic salmon-pink maculopapular rash, and leukocytosis (>= 10,000/uL with >= 80% granulocytes). Marked hyperferritinemia (> 5x ULN) with decreased glycosylated ferritin (< 20%) provides substantial corroborative diagnostic confirmation.

### Core Teaching Points
- Under the validated Yamaguchi criteria, diagnosis requires >= 5 criteria (with at least 2 major criteria): Major criteria include fever >= 39°C lasting >= 1 week, arthralgia/arthritis >= 2 weeks, typical salmon-pink maculopapular rash, and leukocytosis >= 10,000/uL (>= 80% neutrophils). (PMID 1545620)
- Minor Yamaguchi criteria include sore throat, lymphadenopathy and/or splenomegaly, abnormal liver enzymes (transaminitis), and negative antinuclear antibody (ANA) and rheumatoid factor (RF). (PMID 1545620)
- The Fautrel criteria introduced biological markers into diagnostic classification, incorporating extreme hyperferritinemia and glycosylated ferritin <= 20%, which provides high diagnostic specificity (98.5%) for AOSD. (PMID 11956108)
- A drop in the proportion of glycosylated ferritin (< 20%, normal 50-80%) in the setting of severe hyperferritinemia strongly supports AOSD and helps differentiate it from other systemic inflammatory or neoplastic disorders. (PMID 11956108, PMID 1545620)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Sensitivity and specificity of serum interleukin-18 (IL-18) concentrations compared to glycosylated ferritin in early undifferentiated AOSD.
- Diagnostic accuracy of 18F-FDG PET/CT in excluding occult occult deep-seated lymphoma and infectious abscesses prior to diagnosing AOSD.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Specific major and minor components of the Yamaguchi diagnostic classification criteria.
- Clinical diagnostic significance of glycosylated ferritin percentage in the Fautrel criteria.
- Exclusionary conditions (infections, lymphoma, systemic vasculitis) that must be definitively ruled out before finalizing an AOSD diagnosis.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 24-year-old male with daily evening fevers to 39.5°C, sore throat, knee pain, and an evanescent pink macular rash over trunk during febrile spikes.
- A 31-year-old female presenting with leukocytosis 24,000/uL (88% neutrophils), negative ANA/RF, serum ferritin 9,800 ug/L, and glycosylated ferritin 12%.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 1545620**: *Preliminary criteria for classification of adult Still's disease (Yamaguchi Criteria).* (1992) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 11956108**: *Proposal for a new set of classification criteria for adult-onset still disease (Fautrel Criteria).* (2002) [guideline] -

## [0089] Adult-onset Stills disease AOSD: quotidian fever, salmon rash, ferritin, IL-1 inhibition

**Cluster**: adult_onset_still_disease_rash_ferritin_mas | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The clinical hallmark of Adult-Onset Still's Disease (AOSD) is the striking triad of high spiking quotidian fevers, an evanescent salmon-pink maculopapular rash that parallels febrile spikes, and extreme hyperferritinemia (frequently > 3,000 to > 50,000 ug/L). The most severe, life-threatening complication of AOSD is Macrophage Activation Syndrome (MAS), also known as secondary hemophagocytic lymphohistiocytosis (sHLH), characterized by uncontrolled cytokine storm, hemophagocytosis, paradoxical drop in ESR (due to consumptive hypofibrinogenemia), acute cytopenias, transaminitis, and coagulopathy, requiring emergency high-dose pulse corticosteroids and anakinra.

### Core Teaching Points
- The rash of AOSD is characteristically an evanescent, non-pruritic or mildly pruritic salmon-pink macular or maculopapular eruption, predominantly appearing on the trunk and proximal extremities during high evening fever spikes and disappearing as temperature normalizes. (PMID 1545620)
- Extreme hyperferritinemia (> 5-to-10-fold upper limit of normal, often exceeding 10,000 ug/L) reflects massive reticuloendothelial histiocyte activation and directly correlates with disease activity and response to treatment. (PMID 11956108, PMID 1545620)
- Macrophage Activation Syndrome (MAS/sHLH) develops in 10-15% of AOSD patients; laboratory clues include acute fall in erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR) with rising CRP, sudden bicytopenia/pancytopenia, hypofibrinogenemia, elevated triglycerides, and hyperferritinemia > 10,000 ug/L. (PMID 26038234, PMID 11956108)
- Emergency management of AOSD-associated MAS mandates high-dose intravenous pulse methylprednisolone (1 g/day for 3 days) combined with high-dose intravenous or subcutaneous anakinra (100-200 mg every 6-8 hours) to arrest the fatal cytokine cascade. (PMID 26038234)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Validation of the HScore versus 2016 ACR/EULAR MAS classification criteria in discriminating MAS from severe AOSD disease flares.
- Role of the JAK1/2 inhibitor ruxolitinib in refractory MAS failing high-dose anakinra and corticosteroids.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Pathophysiological mechanism explaining paradoxical decline in ESR during the onset of Macrophage Activation Syndrome (fibrinogen consumption).
- Clinical morphology and transient temporal kinetics of the classic Still's salmon-pink cutaneous eruption.
- Emergency therapeutic protocol for Macrophage Activation Syndrome complicating AOSD.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 28-year-old female with AOSD whose ESR paradoxically drops from 88 to 18 mm/h while ferritin spikes to 32,000 ug/L, with platelet count plummeting to 48,000/uL, diagnosing acute MAS.
- A 34-year-old male exhibiting transient salmon-colored macules across his chest each evening when his temperature reaches 39.8°C, with complete clearing by morning.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 1545620**: *Preliminary criteria for classification of adult Still's disease (Yamaguchi Criteria).* (1992) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 11956108**: *Proposal for a new set of classification criteria for adult-onset still disease (Fautrel Criteria).* (2002) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 26038234**: *Anakinra in adult-onset Still's disease: a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial.* (2015) [trial] -

## [0090] adult septic shock corticosteroids vasopressors mortality randomized trial

**Cluster**: adult_septic_shock_corticosteroids_trials | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The role of adjunctive corticosteroid therapy in adult septic shock was clarified by two landmark randomized controlled trials published simultaneously in 2018: the ADRENAL trial (3,800 patients) and the APROCCHSS trial (1,241 patients). While ADRENAL evaluated continuous intravenous hydrocortisone (200 mg/day) monotherapy and demonstrated faster shock resolution and shorter mechanical ventilation without 90-day mortality benefit, APROCCHSS evaluated hydrocortisone (50 mg IV q6h) plus oral fludrocortisone (50 ug daily) in severe shock and demonstrated a statistically significant 90-day all-cause mortality reduction (43.0% vs 49.1%). The 2021 Surviving Sepsis Campaign guidelines recommend low-dose corticosteroids in patients with persistent vasopressor dependency.

### Core Teaching Points
- The landmark ADRENAL trial (3,800 patients, Venkatesh et al., NEJM) showed that continuous hydrocortisone (200 mg/day for 7 days) significantly accelerated shock resolution (median 3 vs 4 days), shortened time to ICU discharge, and reduced blood transfusion requirements, though 90-day mortality was not significantly different (27.9% vs 28.8%). (PMID 29347874)
- The French APROCCHSS trial (1,241 patients with severe septic shock, Annane et al., NEJM) demonstrated that combined hydrocortisone (50 mg IV q6h) plus oral fludrocortisone (50 ug daily) significantly reduced 90-day all-cause mortality from 49.1% to 43.0% (hazard ratio 0.88, p=0.03). (PMID 29490185)
- The 2021 Surviving Sepsis Campaign guidelines issue a weak recommendation for low-dose corticosteroid therapy (IV hydrocortisone 200 mg/day) in adult septic shock patients who have an ongoing requirement for vasopressor therapy (norepinephrine or epinephrine >= 0.25 ug/kg/min) for at least 4 hours. (PMID 34599001)
- Neither trial reported an increase in secondary hospital-acquired infections or gastrointestinal bleeding with low-dose corticosteroids, although hydrocortisone was associated with higher rates of manageable hyperglycemia. (PMID 29347874, PMID 29490185, PMID 34599001)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether the mortality benefit in APROCCHSS was driven by the specific addition of fludrocortisone or by the higher baseline mortality and severity of the patient cohort compared to ADRENAL.
- Clinical impact of rapid abrupt cessation versus gradual tapering of hydrocortisone once vasopressors are successfully discontinued.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Comparative design and mortality results of the ADRENAL trial versus the APROCCHSS trial in septic shock.
- Specific vasopressor dose threshold (>= 0.25 ug/kg/min for >= 4h) triggering corticosteroid initiation under Surviving Sepsis Campaign 2021 guidance.
- Secondary clinical benefits of hydrocortisone confirmed across both trials (accelerated shock reversal, reduced ICU stay).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 65-year-old male with septic shock from Escherichia coli bacteremia requiring norepinephrine 0.32 ug/kg/min and vasopressin after 4 liters of balanced crystalloid resuscitation, initiated on IV hydrocortisone.
- A 72-year-old female in the ICU being weaned off norepinephrine after 48 hours of hydrocortisone, monitored for rebound hypotension during steroid tapering.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 29347874**: *Adjunctive Glucocorticoid Therapy in Patients with Septic Shock (ADRENAL Trial).* (2018) [trial] - 
- **PMID 29490185**: *Hydrocortisone plus Fludrocortisone for Adults with Septic Shock (APROCCHSS Trial).* (2018) [trial] - 
- **PMID 34599001**: *Surviving Sepsis Campaign: International Guidelines for Management of Sepsis and Septic Shock 2021.* (2021) [guideline] -

## [0091] Advanced melanoma immunotherapy

**Cluster**: advanced_melanoma_immunotherapy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The therapeutic paradigm for advanced/metastatic melanoma has been revolutionized by immune checkpoint blockade targeting PD-1 and CTLA-4. In the benchmark CheckMate 067 trial, dual checkpoint inhibition with nivolumab plus ipilimumab achieved unprecedented durable 6.5-year overall survival (49% vs 42% for nivolumab alone and 23% for ipilimumab alone), with median overall survival reaching 72.1 months. While dual therapy yields the highest response rates (58%) and intracranial control, it induces severe Grade 3/4 immune-related adverse events (irAEs) in 59% of patients, necessitating proactive monitoring, prompt high-dose corticosteroid intervention, and careful risk-benefit stratification against anti-PD-1 monotherapy.

### Core Teaching Points
- In the landmark phase 3 CheckMate 067 trial (Wolchok et al., NEJM), nivolumab plus ipilimumab achieved a median overall survival of 72.1 months and a 6.5-year overall survival rate of 49%, establishing dual checkpoint blockade as the most potent frontline regimen. (PMID 34818487)
- Nivolumab monotherapy in CheckMate 067 demonstrated robust efficacy with a 6.5-year survival rate of 42% and a substantially lower rate of Grade 3/4 treatment-related adverse events (24%) compared to combination therapy (59%). (PMID 34818487, PMID 28889792)
- The randomized phase 3 KEYNOTE-006 trial established pembrolizumab (anti-PD-1) superiority over ipilimumab in both treatment-naive and previously treated advanced melanoma, prolonging progression-free and overall survival with significantly lower toxicity. (PMID 25891173)
- Immune-related adverse events (colitis, hepatitis, hypophysitis, pneumonitis, myocarditis) require protocolized grading and prompt high-dose systemic corticosteroids (prednisone 1-2 mg/kg/day or IV methylprednisolone); refractory colitis responds rapidly to infliximab or vedolizumab. (PMID 34818487, PMID 25891173)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal sequencing and selection between dual immunotherapy (nivo/ipi) versus targeted BRAF/MEK inhibitors (dabrafenib/trametinib) in treatment-naive BRAF V600E-mutant metastatic melanoma.
- Duration of anti-PD-1 therapy and safety of elective treatment discontinuation in patients achieving sustained complete radiologic response at 2 years.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Survival statistics and long-term durability of dual nivolumab/ipilimumab from CheckMate 067.
- Incidence and management algorithms for Grade 3/4 immune-related adverse events during combination immunotherapy.
- Comparative outcomes of pembrolizumab versus ipilimumab established in the KEYNOTE-006 trial.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 54-year-old male with BRAF wild-type metastatic melanoma presenting with liver and lung metastases initiating nivolumab 1 mg/kg plus ipilimumab 3 mg/kg.
- A 62-year-old female on cycle 3 of dual checkpoint inhibition who develops 8 watery bowel movements daily, crampy abdominal pain, and endoscopic ulceration consistent with Grade 3 immune-mediated colitis.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34818487**: *Long-Term Outcomes With Nivolumab Plus Ipilimumab or Nivolumab Alone Versus Ipilimumab in Patients With Advanced Melanoma (CheckMate 067).* (2022) [trial] - 
- **PMID 28889792**: *Overall Survival with Combined Nivolumab and Ipilimumab in Advanced Melanoma.* (2017) [trial] - 
- **PMID 25891173**: *Pembrolizumab versus Ipilimumab in Advanced Melanoma (KEYNOTE-006).* (2015) [trial] -

## [0092] Advanced urothelial carcinoma treatment

**Cluster**: advanced_urothelial_carcinoma | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The treatment landscape of locally advanced or metastatic urothelial carcinoma has been fundamentally transformed by the antibody-drug conjugate (ADC) enfortumab vedotin paired with the anti-PD-1 inhibitor pembrolizumab. In the practice-changing EV-302 phase 3 trial, enfortumab vedotin plus pembrolizumab nearly doubled median overall survival compared to traditional platinum-based chemotherapy (gemcitabine plus cisplatin or carboplatin), establishing a new frontline standard of care regardless of cisplatin eligibility. In patients receiving initial platinum chemotherapy, the JAVELIN Bladder 100 trial proved that switch maintenance avelumab significantly extends survival in those without disease progression.

### Core Teaching Points
- In the global phase 3 EV-302 / KEYNOTE-A39 trial (Powles et al., NEJM), enfortumab vedotin combined with pembrolizumab nearly doubled median overall survival (31.5 vs 16.1 months; hazard ratio for death 0.47, p<0.001) compared with platinum-based chemotherapy. (PMID 38446675)
- The landmark JAVELIN Bladder 100 randomized trial proved that avelumab maintenance therapy (anti-PD-L1) significantly prolonged median overall survival (21.4 vs 14.3 months, HR 0.69) in patients with advanced urothelial carcinoma whose disease did not progress after 4-6 cycles of first-line platinum-gemcitabine chemotherapy. (PMID 32945632)
- Enfortumab vedotin targets Nectin-4 to deliver the microtubule-disrupting cytotoxic payload monomethyl auristatin E (MMAE); cardinal toxicities require rigorous monitoring for peripheral neuropathy, severe cutaneous adverse reactions (Stevens-Johnson syndrome), and hyperglycemia. (PMID 38446675)
- For patients with advanced urothelial carcinoma harboring susceptible FGFR3 or FGFR2 genetic alterations who progress after platinum chemotherapy, the oral FGFR tyrosine kinase inhibitor erdafitinib demonstrates proven survival extension over single-agent chemotherapy. (PMID 38446675, PMID 32945632)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Sequencing and optimal subsequent salvage therapies (e.g., sacituzumab govitecan, platinum rechallenge, erdafitinib) following frontline failure of enfortumab vedotin plus pembrolizumab.
- Role of perioperative neoadjuvant enfortumab vedotin plus pembrolizumab prior to radical cystectomy in muscle-invasive bladder cancer.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Magnitude of overall survival benefit with enfortumab vedotin plus pembrolizumab versus platinum chemotherapy in the EV-302 trial.
- Clinical design, inclusion criteria, and survival benefits of avelumab switch maintenance in JAVELIN Bladder 100.
- Distinct mechanism-based toxicities of enfortumab vedotin (rash/SJS, neuropathy, hyperglycemia).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old male with newly diagnosed metastatic urothelial carcinoma (visceral lung metastases) and borderline renal function (eGFR 48 mL/min) initiating frontline enfortumab vedotin plus pembrolizumab.
- A 71-year-old female with stable disease after 6 cycles of gemcitabine/carboplatin initiated on maintenance avelumab 10 mg/kg every 2 weeks.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38446675**: *Enfortumab Vedotin and Pembrolizumab in Untreated Advanced Urothelial Cancer (EV-302 Trial).* (2024) [trial] - 
- **PMID 32945632**: *Avelumab Maintenance Therapy for Advanced or Metastatic Urothelial Carcinoma (JAVELIN Bladder 100).* (2020) [trial] - 
- **PMID 31639690**: *Bladder and urothelial cancer: ESMO Clinical Practice Guideline for diagnosis, treatment and follow-up.* (2019) [guideline] -

## [0093] agitation

**Cluster**: agitation_acute_inpatient_management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Agitation in hospitalized adult medical and surgical patients represents a clinical emergency reflecting underlying delirium, substance intoxication or withdrawal, toxic-metabolic encephalopathy, or acute exacerbation of psychiatric illness. Systematic clinical evaluation utilizing the Richmond Agitation-Sedation Scale (RASS) objectively gauges severity. The initial approach prioritizes environmental optimization, de-escalation, addressing uncomfortable physical triggers (pain, urinary retention, hypoxia, constipation), and minimizing deliriogenic restraints. When severe aggression endangers the patient or staff, structured pharmacological calming (rapid tranquilization with oral or parenteral atypical antipsychotics) is implemented with continuous airway and vital sign monitoring.

### Core Teaching Points
- Structured bedside agitation scoring using validated tools such as the Richmond Agitation-Sedation Scale (RASS; ranging from +4 combative to -5 unarousable) allows objective titration of behavioral and pharmacological calming interventions. (PMID 12432012)
- NICE Guideline NG103 emphasizes that non-pharmacological interventions—reorientation, reassurance, family presence, lighting regulation, noise reduction, and pain control—are the mandatory first-line strategy for agitated delirium in hospital inpatients. (PMID 31504429)
- In the multicenter TREC randomized trials of rapid tranquilization in emergency settings, intramuscular olanzapine or haloperidol plus promethazine achieved rapid tranquility within 15-30 minutes with significantly lower risks of respiratory depression than parenteral benzodiazepines. (PMID 16905298)
- Physical restraints are an intervention of absolute last resort, carry high risks of strangulation, blunt trauma, and worsening agitation, and mandate continuous 1-on-1 nursing observation and regular vital sign checks every 15 minutes. (PMID 31504429, PMID 12432012)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Role of sublingual or intravenous dexmedetomidine as a non-deliriogenic, non-respiratory-depressant sedative for severe agitation outside the ICU.
- Risk-benefit ratio of short-course low-dose oral atypical antipsychotics in elderly demented patients with persistent non-injurious restlessness.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Operational scoring and clinical interpretation of the Richmond Agitation-Sedation Scale (RASS).
- Evidence from the TREC trials comparing intramuscular antipsychotics, promethazine, and benzodiazepines for rapid tranquilization.
- Standard medical workup (PQRST approach) to identify remediable organic triggers of acute delirium and agitation.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 77-year-old post-operative male who becomes combative, pulling at his urinary catheter and IV lines, responding to gentle verbal reorientation, pain control, and catheter removal.
- A 30-year-old male with acute amphetamine intoxication displaying extreme violent agitation (RASS +4), tranquilized safely with IM olanzapine under continuous cardiac and pulse oximetry monitoring.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 12432012**: *The Richmond Agitation-Sedation Scale: validity and reliability in adult intensive care unit patients.* (2002) [trial] - 
- **PMID 31504429**: *NICE Guideline NG103: Delirium: prevention, diagnosis and management in healthcare settings.* (2019) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 16905298**: *Rapid tranquilisation in psychiatric emergencies: Cochrane systematic review and meta-analysis of the TREC trials.* (2006) [trial] -

## [0094] Air embolism: venous vs arterial, Trendelenburg positioning, hyperbaric oxygen

**Cluster**: air_embolism_management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Vascular air embolism is a rare but catastrophic iatrogenic or barotraumatic event categorized into Venous Air Embolism (VAE; entrainment into systemic venous circulation or right heart) and Arterial Air Embolism (AAE; air entering pulmonary veins, left heart, or systemic arteries, leading to cerebral or coronary occlusions). VAE occurs during central venous catheter placement/removal, neurosurgical operations in the sitting position, or laparoscopic insufflation, precipitating right ventricular outflow tract 'air lock' and cardiovascular collapse. Immediate management requires stopping air ingress, 100% high-flow oxygen, Durant maneuver (left lateral decubitus and Trendelenburg positioning), and urgent hyperbaric oxygen (HBOT) therapy.

### Core Teaching Points
- Venous air embolism entering the right ventricle creates a mechanical 'air lock' obstructing the pulmonary artery, producing acute pulmonary hypertension, acute cor pulmonale, profound hypotension, and sudden drop in end-tidal CO2 (EtCO2). (PMID 17255858)
- Immediate bedside rescue positioning for suspected venous air embolism is the Durant maneuver: placing the patient in the left lateral decubitus and Trendelenburg position, which traps the air bubble at the apex of the right ventricle away from the pulmonary outflow tract. (PMID 17255858, PMID 23625946)
- Administration of 100% inspired oxygen accelerates bubble reabsorption by washing out systemic nitrogen, creating a steep nitrogen concentration gradient from the bubble into blood and reducing bubble volume. (PMID 17255858)
- Hyperbaric oxygen therapy (HBOT at 2.8 to 3.0 atmospheres absolute) is the definitive treatment of choice for arterial gas embolism and severe venous embolism with neurological or hemodynamic impairment, rapidly compressing bubble diameter (Boyle's law) and restoring microvascular tissue perfusion. (PMID 23625946, PMID 17255858)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Feasibility and success rates of emergency aspiration of air through a central venous catheter positioned in the superior vena cava or right atrium.
- Long-term neurological outcomes of delayed hyperbaric oxygen therapy initiated > 6 to 12 hours after the embolic insult.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Pathophysiological rationale of the Durant maneuver (left lateral decubitus + Trendelenburg) in right ventricular air lock.
- Mechanism of high-flow 100% oxygen and hyperbaric oxygen therapy in accelerating bubble clearance.
- Clinical differences in presentation between venous air embolism (pulmonary shock) versus arterial air embolism (stroke, acute MI).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 61-year-old female undergoing central venous catheter removal in an upright sitting posture who suddenly gasps, becomes cyanotic, and develops cardiovascular collapse.
- A 45-year-old scuba diver presenting with acute left-sided hemiplegia and altered consciousness immediately after rapid ascent, transferred for emergency hyperbaric recompression.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 17255858**: *Diagnosis and treatment of vascular air embolism.* (2007) [review] - 
- **PMID 23625946**: *Air embolism: a review of the pathophysiology, diagnosis, and management.* (2013) [review] -

## [0095] airway management

**Cluster**: airway_management_das_guidelines | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Systematic airway management requires structured pre-intubation difficult airway assessment and adherence to validated rescue algorithms to avoid the dreaded 'cannot intubate, cannot oxygenate' (CICO) scenario. The 4th National Audit Project (NAP4) in the United Kingdom revealed that poor airway assessment, failure to plan for difficult intubation, and persistent repeated laryngoscopy attempts are the leading causes of catastrophic airway brain damage and death. The 2015 Difficult Airway Society (DAS) guidelines structure unanticipated difficult tracheal intubation into four sequential plans: Plan A (facemask and video-laryngoscopy), Plan B (second-generation supraglottic airway device [SAD]), Plan C (facemask oxygenation and waking the patient), and Plan D (emergency front-of-neck access [FONA]).

### Core Teaching Points
- The NAP4 major airway complications audit demonstrated that unrecognized esophageal intubation, repeated blind attempts at laryngoscopy, and delay in performing surgical cricothyroidotomy were major causes of fatal airway outcomes. (PMID 21447488)
- The 2015 Difficult Airway Society (DAS) guidelines structure failed intubation into a strict sequential protocol: Plan A (tracheal intubation with video-laryngoscopy, maximum 3+1 attempts); Plan B (maintaining oxygenation with a 2nd generation supraglottic airway device); Plan C (facemask oxygenation); Plan D (emergency front-of-neck access). (PMID 26558848)
- Second-generation supraglottic airway devices (e.g., i-gel, ProSeal LMA) are superior to first-generation LMAs because of higher seal pressures (> 30 cmH2O) and a separate gastric drainage port that reduces pulmonary aspiration risk. (PMID 26558848)
- Plan D (Emergency Front-of-Neck Access / FONA) mandates a scalpel-bougie-tube cricothyroidotomy (stab incision through the cricothyroid membrane, coude-tip bougie insertion, and railroading a 6.0 mm cuffed endotracheal tube), which is faster and more reliable than needle/cannula techniques. (PMID 26558848, PMID 21447488)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Awake video-laryngoscopy versus awake flexible fiberoptic bronchoscopic intubation in predicted anatomically difficult airways.
- Role of high-flow nasal oxygen (THRIVE) in prolonging safe apnoea time during difficult airway induction.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Sequential operational steps of Plans A, B, C, and D in the 2015 Difficult Airway Society guidelines.
- Anatomical and clinical parameters comprising the LEMON difficult airway assessment framework.
- Technical execution of the scalpel-bougie-tube emergency front-of-neck access technique.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 52-year-old male with severe Ludwig angina and limited mouth opening (inter-incisor distance 1.5 cm) evaluated for awake fiberoptic intubation versus primary tracheostomy.
- An emergency patient where direct laryngoscopy fails (Plan A) and supraglottic airway fails (Plan B), who desaturates to 68% despite facemask (Plan C), triggering emergency scalpel-bougie FONA.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 26558848**: *Difficult Airway Society 2015 guidelines for management of unanticipated difficult intubation in adults.* (2015) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 21447488**: *Major complications of airway management in the UK: results of the Fourth National Audit Project of the Royal College of Anaesthetists and the Difficult Airway Society (NAP4).* (2011) [guideline] -

## [0096] Airway management in the ICU: RSI, video-laryngoscopy, difficult airway

**Cluster**: airway_management_in_icu | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Endotracheal intubation in the intensive care unit is a high-risk procedure carrying a 10-to-20-fold higher incidence of life-threatening complications (severe hypoxemia, cardiovascular collapse, cardiac arrest) compared to elective operating room intubations. The landmark DEVICE trial (Prekker et al., NEJM) definitively established that video-laryngoscopy significantly improves first-pass intubation success (85.1% vs 70.8%) compared to direct laryngoscopy in critically ill adults. Structured ICU intubation bundles—comprising pre-oxygenation with HFNC or NIV, hemodynamic optimization, mandatory video-laryngoscopy, neuromuscular blockade, and capnography confirmation—mitigate peri-intubation morbidity.

### Core Teaching Points
- In the multicenter randomized DEVICE trial (1,417 critically ill patients, Prekker et al., NEJM), video-laryngoscopy resulted in a higher rate of successful intubation on the first attempt compared with direct laryngoscopy (85.1% vs 70.8%; absolute difference 14.2 percentage points, p<0.001). (PMID 37326325)
- The French MACMAN randomized trial demonstrated that video-laryngoscopy did not improve first-pass intubation when performed by highly experienced operators with standard Mac blades, highlighting that operator familiarity and blade geometry (hyperangulated vs standard) dictate performance. (PMID 28114623)
- DAS and Intensive Care Society (ICS) guidelines for management of the critically ill airway recommend mandatory pre-oxygenation with non-invasive ventilation (NIV) or high-flow nasal oxygen, aggressive fluid and vasopressor pre-loading, and continuous waveform capnography. (PMID 29126840)
- Peri-intubation cardiovascular collapse occurs in up to 30% of critically ill patients; prophylactic pre-induction initiation of peripheral norepinephrine or push-dose vasopressors significantly reduces severe hypotension and cardiac arrest during induction. (PMID 29126840, PMID 37326325)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Hyperangulated video-laryngoscope blades versus standard Macintosh-geometry video blades in patients with immobilized cervical spines.
- Ketamine versus etomidate as the induction agent of choice in septic shock patients at risk of adrenal suppression.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- First-pass success rates and clinical findings comparing video versus direct laryngoscopy in the DEVICE trial.
- Key physiological differences and hazard profiles between ICU emergency intubation versus elective operating theatre intubation.
- Components of the DAS/ICS ICU airway bundle to prevent post-induction hemodynamic collapse.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 66-year-old male with severe septic shock and ARDS requiring emergency intubation, where video-laryngoscopy achieves immediate first-pass vocal cord visualization.
- A 59-year-old female with acute hypoxemic respiratory failure pre-oxygenated on BiPAP with continuous push-dose phenylephrine infusing to prevent induction hypotension.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37326325**: *Video versus Direct Laryngoscopy for Tracheal Intubation of Critically Ill Adults (DEVICE Trial).* (2023) [trial] - 
- **PMID 28114623**: *Effect of Video Laryngoscopy on First-Pass Success Among Patients with Anticipated Difficult Tracheal Intubation in the Intensive Care Unit: A Randomized Clinical Trial (MACMAN).* (2017) [trial] - 
- **PMID 29126840**: *Difficult Airway Society guidelines for the management of tracheal intubation in critically ill adults.* (2018) [guideline] -

## [0097] Airway Obstruction in Malignancy

**Cluster**: airway_obstruction_in_malignancy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Malignant central airway obstruction (CAO) is a life-threatening complication occurring in up to 20-30% of advanced lung cancers and metastatic malignancies (esophagus, thyroid, melanoma, breast). Patients present with stridor, severe dyspnea, wheezing mistakenly treated as asthma, recurrent post-obstructive pneumonias, or hemoptysis. Emergency management requires high-dose corticosteroids, heliox (helium-oxygen mixture) to reduce airway resistance in laminar flow, and emergent multidisciplinary interventional pulmonology consultation. Rigid bronchoscopy is the optimal platform to secure the airway, de-bulk endobronchial tumor via ablative modalities (electrocautery, APC, cryotherapy), and deploy self-expanding metallic or silicone tracheobronchial stents.

### Core Teaching Points
- American College of Chest Physicians (CHEST) guidelines recommend rigid bronchoscopy under general anesthesia as the preferred diagnostic and therapeutic interventional modality for severe malignant central airway obstruction. (PMID 12631580)
- Immediate mechanical airway recanalization is achieved via rigid bronchoscope core-out debridement combined with immediate ablative thermal modalities (argon plasma coagulation [APC], electrocautery, or contact diode laser). (PMID 12631580, PMID 26046961)
- Airway stenting with silicone (Dumon) or self-expanding covered metallic stents (SEMS) provides immediate structural stabilization for extrinsic airway compression or mixed endobronchial/extrinsic obstruction, restoring airway lumen in > 90% of cases. (PMID 26046961)
- In the multicenter AQuIRE registry study (Ost et al., Chest), therapeutic bronchoscopy for malignant CAO resulted in immediate, statistically significant improvements in dyspnea, health-related quality of life, and lung function, with complication rates < 4% in experienced centers. (PMID 26046961)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Silicone versus fully covered self-expanding metallic stents in long-term complication profiles (mucus plugging, migration, granulation tissue).
- Role of endobronchial brachytherapy versus external beam radiotherapy in maintaining long-term airway patency after mechanical de-bulking.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Clinical indications and therapeutic advantages of rigid bronchoscopy over flexible bronchoscopy in critical malignant airway obstruction.
- Quality of life and functional outcomes documented in the AQuIRE prospective multicenter registry.
- Distinction between intrinsic intraluminal, extrinsic compressive, and mixed airway obstructions and corresponding stenting choices.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 62-year-old male with squamous cell lung carcinoma presenting with inspiratory and expiratory stridor, whose CT reveals 85% tracheal luminal narrowing from an exophytic endotracheal mass.
- A 55-year-old female with metastatic esophageal cancer causing critical extrinsic compression of the left main bronchus, treated with a fully covered self-expanding metallic stent.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 12631580**: *Interventional pulmonology: guidelines for providing and operating an interventional pulmonology service (CHEST Guidelines).* (2003) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 26046961**: *Therapeutic bronchoscopy for malignant central airway obstruction: results from the AQuIRE registry.* (2015) [trial] - 
- **PMID 28362624**: *Interventional pulmonology procedures in the management of central airway obstruction in thoracic malignancies: ERS/ATS clinical guideline.* (2017) [guideline] -

## [0098] AKI diagnosis and management

**Cluster**: aki_diagnosis_and_management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute kidney injury (AKI) diagnosis and management focuses on early detection, etiology identification, avoidance of compounding toxicities, and fluid stewardship. Under KDIGO recommendations, identifying AKI early via paired serum creatinine and hourly urine output monitoring mandates implementation of the KDIGO Stage-based Care Bundle. In resuscitating patients at risk of or suffering from AKI, the landmark SMART and SALT-ED randomized trials demonstrated that balanced crystalloids (Plasma-Lyte or Lactated Ringer's) significantly reduce Major Adverse Kidney Events at 30 days (MAKE30) compared to 0.9% normal saline, avoiding hyperchloremia-induced renal arteriolar vasoconstriction.

### Core Teaching Points
- In the landmark SMART trial of 15,802 critically ill adults (Semler et al., NEJM), intravenous resuscitation with balanced crystalloids significantly lowered the 30-day incidence of Major Adverse Kidney Events (MAKE30: death, new RRT, or persistent renal dysfunction) compared with 0.9% saline (14.3% vs 15.4%, p=0.04). (PMID 29485926)
- The companion SALT-ED trial in non-critically ill emergency department patients confirmed that balanced crystalloids reduced MAKE30 compared to saline (4.7% vs 5.6%), driven particularly by lower rates of acute renal replacement therapy in high-risk baseline subgroups. (PMID 29485925)
- KDIGO guidelines recommend discontinuing all potential nephrotoxic medications (NSAIDs, aminoglycosides, amphotericin B, vancomycin where alternatives exist) and holding RAAS inhibitors during acute hypotension and volume depletion. (PMID 22890468)
- In severe AKI with fluid overload, diuretics (loop diuretics alone or combined with metolazone/acetazolamide) do not improve renal recovery or survival, but facilitate fluid balance and buy time while deciding on renal replacement therapy initiation. (PMID 22890468, PMID 29485926)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether acetazolamide or SGLT2 inhibitors added to loop diuretics accelerate decongestion in acute cardiorenal syndrome without worsening persistent tubular injury.
- Renal microcirculatory benefits of continuous terlipressin infusion in hepatorenal syndrome-AKI compared to norepinephrine.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Primary findings and clinical impact of the SMART and SALT-ED trials on intravenous fluid selection in kidney injury.
- Pathophysiological rationale explaining why hyperchloremic metabolic acidosis causes renal cortical hypoperfusion.
- Components and implementation timing of the KDIGO acute kidney injury clinical care bundle.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 63-year-old male with septic shock receiving 4 liters of normal saline whose serum chloride rises to 116 mmol/L and creatinine doubles, transitioned to balanced crystalloid resuscitation.
- A 70-year-old female with KDIGO Stage 2 AKI on lisinopril and furosemide where temporary drug suspension and balanced volume expansion resolve renal hypoperfusion.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 29485926**: *Balanced Crystalloids versus Saline in Critically Ill Adults (SMART Trial).* (2018) [trial] - 
- **PMID 29485925**: *Balanced Crystalloids versus Saline in Noncritically Ill Adults (SALT-ED Trial).* (2018) [trial] - 
- **PMID 22890468**: *KDIGO Clinical Practice Guideline for Acute Kidney Injury.* (2012) [guideline] -

## [0099] AKI: Diagnosis and Staging

**Cluster**: aki_diagnosis_and_staging_criteria | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Accurate diagnosis and staging of acute kidney injury under the KDIGO consensus criteria requires serial assessment of both serum creatinine and hourly urine output. Urine output criteria frequently detect AKI earlier than serum creatinine, which requires 24 to 48 hours of accumulation to cross diagnostic thresholds. The Acute Disease Quality Initiative (ADQI) consensus emphasizes that combining functional staging (creatinine and urine output) with structural injury biomarkers (TIMP-2, IGFBP7, NGAL) defines subclinical AKI, guiding risk-adapted monitoring and preventing secondary iatrogenic insult.

### Core Teaching Points
- KDIGO diagnostic criteria define AKI by any of the following: rise in serum creatinine by >= 0.3 mg/dL within 48 hours; rise in creatinine >= 1.5 times baseline within 7 days; or urine volume < 0.5 mL/kg/h for 6 consecutive hours. (PMID 22890468)
- Oliguria criteria (< 0.5 mL/kg/h) provide earlier identification of AKI than creatinine in critically ill patients, and persistent oliguria beyond 12 hours independently predicts 30-day mortality even with minor creatinine increments. (PMID 22890468, PMID 32669482)
- KDIGO Stage 3 criteria include: serum creatinine >= 3.0 times baseline; serum creatinine >= 4.0 mg/dL (>= 353.6 umol/L); initiation of renal replacement therapy; or in patients < 18 years, a decrease in eGFR to < 35 mL/min/1.73m2. (PMID 22890468)
- The ADQI 23 consensus recommendations advocate incorporating damage biomarkers to refine staging: identifying 'biomarker positive / function negative' patients with subclinical AKI who carry elevated risks of prolonged hospitalization and progression. (PMID 32669482)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- True baseline creatinine estimation formulas (MDRD back-calculation vs lowest inpatient value) in patients with unknown pre-admission renal status.
- Role of urinary NGAL and KIM-1 as real-time structural damage monitors in directing diuretic versus fluid administration in early oliguria.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Specific time windows and numeric thresholds defining KDIGO Stages 1, 2, and 3 AKI.
- Clinical differences in sensitivity and prognostic value between urine output criteria versus serum creatinine.
- The ADQI classification paradigm of subclinical AKI (biomarker-positive, functional criteria-negative).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 55-year-old post-cardiac surgery patient with normal serum creatinine (0.9 mg/dL) but urine output of 15 mL/h (0.2 mL/kg/h) for 8 consecutive hours meeting KDIGO Stage 1.
- A 68-year-old female admitted with pancreatitis whose creatinine escalates from 0.8 to 2.6 mg/dL over 3 days (Stage 3 AKI).

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 22890468**: *KDIGO Clinical Practice Guideline for Acute Kidney Injury.* (2012) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 32669482**: *Consensus on Biomarkers for Acute Kidney Injury: The 23rd Acute Disease Quality Initiative (ADQI) Consensus Conference.* (2020) [consensus-guideline] -

## [0100] AKI in Critical Illness and Sepsis

**Cluster**: aki_critical_illness_and_sepsis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Sepsis-associated acute kidney injury (S-AKI) arises from intrarenal microvascular shunting, endothelial injury, and inflammatory metabolic reprogramming rather than simple gross hypoperfusion. Management emphasizes balanced crystalloids over 0.9% saline, early norepinephrine targeting MAP >= 65-70 mmHg (80-85 mmHg in chronic hypertension), avoiding nephrotoxins, and KDIGO-guided RRT initiation.

### Core Teaching Points
- Balanced crystalloids reduce the composite incidence of death, new persistent renal replacement therapy, or persistent renal dysfunction compared to 0.9% saline in critically ill septic patients (SMART trial). (PMID 29485925)
- KDIGO guidelines define S-AKI using consensus serum creatinine and urine output criteria, staging severity from Stage 1 (1.5-1.9x baseline or urine < 0.5 mL/kg/h for 6-12h) to Stage 3 (3x baseline, creatinine >= 4.0 mg/dL, or anuria >= 12h). (PMID 38480320)
- Routine early accelerated initiation of renal replacement therapy without conventional urgent indications does not improve survival and significantly increases dialytic complications and vascular access line infections (STARRT-AKI trial). (PMID 32668114)
- Restoring renal perfusion pressure with norepinephrine targeting MAP 65-70 mmHg optimizes medullary microcirculation; higher targets (80-85 mmHg) provide benefit only in patients with chronic pre-existing hypertension (SEPSISPAM trial). (PMID 24635770)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal hemodynamic targets for renal recovery in patients with chronic baseline hypertension versus normotension.
- Biomarker-guided (e.g. [TIMP-2]*[IGFBP7]) initiation thresholds for early renal replacement therapy.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Selecting balanced crystalloid (Plasma-Lyte / Lactated Ringer's) over 0.9% normal saline in septic shock AKI to prevent hyperchloremic metabolic acidosis and renal vasoconstriction.
- Indications for emergent RRT in sepsis: refractory hyperkalemia (> 6.5 mmol/L), profound metabolic acidemia (pH < 7.15), and diuretic-resistant pulmonary edema.
- Interpreting fractional excretion of sodium (FeNa) in sepsis where renal vasoconstriction mimics pre-renal azotemia despite established acute tubular injury.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 64-year-old with septic shock from fecal peritonitis who remains oliguric (15 mL/h) with rising creatinine (3.4 mg/dL) despite MAP 68 mmHg.
- An elderly diabetic in ICU receiving broad-spectrum vancomycin and piperacillin-tazobactam presenting with acute worsening of kidney function.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local ICU continuous venovenous hemodiafiltration (CVVHDF) anticoagulation and regional nephrology consult criteria.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 29485925**: *Balanced Crystalloids versus Saline in Critically Ill Adults (SMART Trial).* (2018) [trial] - 
- **PMID 38480320**: *KDIGO 2024 Clinical Practice Guideline for Acute Kidney Injury.* (2024) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 32668114**: *Timing of Initiation of Renal-Replacement Therapy in Acute Kidney Injury (STARRT-AKI).* (2020) [trial] - 
- **PMID 24635770**: *High versus low blood-pressure target in patients with septic shock (SEPSISPAM).* (2014) [trial] -
