## [0101] AKI Recovery and Long-Term Outcomes

**Cluster**: aki_recovery_and_long_term_outcomes | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute kidney disease (AKD) describes the critical window between 7 and 90 days post-insult where acute injury either resolves or progresses to permanent chronic kidney disease. Standard of care includes post-discharge nephrology follow-up within 90 days, blood pressure optimization, monitoring proteinuria, and reassessing cardioprotective medications.

### Core Teaching Points
- ADQI 24 consensus defines acute kidney disease (AKD) as persistent kidney dysfunction lasting 7 to 90 days after an initial AKI episode. (PMID 33504933)
- Post-AKI survivors experience a 3-fold higher long-term risk of incident end-stage kidney disease and accelerated cardiovascular mortality. (PMID 30765178)
- Timely post-discharge nephrology evaluation within 90 days of severe AKI is associated with a 24% relative reduction in 1-year all-cause mortality. (PMID 32514120)
- Careful re-initiation of RAAS inhibitors after recovery from hemodynamic AKI reduces secondary cardiovascular events and mortality without increasing recurrent AKI. (PMID 33504933)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Exact timing and safety criteria for re-starting ACE inhibitors, ARBs, and SGLT2 inhibitors following severe acute tubular necrosis.
- Stratifying which post-AKI patients benefit most from specialized multidisciplinary kidney transition clinics.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Differentiating complete AKI recovery from AKD and CKD based on the 7-day and 90-day time points.
- Long-term post-AKI surveillance schedule: urinalysis, urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio (uACR), and serum creatinine at 30, 90, and 365 days.
- Recognizing cardiovascular risk as the leading cause of mortality following hospital discharge for dialysis-requiring AKI.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 58-year-old discharged after severe post-cardiac surgery AKI who asks when his lisinopril and dapagliflozin can be safely restarted.
- A patient with unresolved Stage 2 AKI at day 28 post-pneumonia presenting to primary care for follow-up testing.

**Reviewer Gap**: Confirm follow-up protocols for monitoring renal functional reserve and transition to chronic kidney disease clinics.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 33504933**: *Acute Kidney Disease and Renal Recovery: Consensus Report of the ADQI 24 Workgroup.* (2021) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 30765178**: *Long-term outcomes after acute kidney injury: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2019) [meta-analysis] - 
- **PMID 32514120**: *Nephrologist follow-up and outcomes in patients with severe acute kidney injury: a population-based cohort study.* (2020) [systematic review] -

## [0102] Alcohol-associated hepatitis corticosteroids

**Cluster**: alcohol_associated_hepatitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Severe alcohol-associated hepatitis carries up to 30-50% 1-month mortality. It is defined by Maddrey Discriminant Function (mDF) >= 32 or MELD > 20. Oral prednisolone (40 mg daily for 28 days) improves 28-day survival provided active infection, gastrointestinal bleeding, and viral hepatitis are rigorously excluded before initiation.

### Core Teaching Points
- AASLD guidelines recommend oral prednisolone 40 mg daily for 28 days in patients with severe alcohol-associated hepatitis (mDF >= 32 or MELD > 20). (PMID 38241123)
- The Lille Model assessed on day 7 calculates corticosteroid responsiveness; a score >= 0.45 indicates non-response and warrants stopping steroids to avoid fatal fungal or bacterial sepsis. (PMID 17514371)
- Pentoxifylline does not improve 28-day or 6-month survival in severe alcoholic hepatitis and is no longer recommended (STOPAH trial). (PMID 25898143)
- Early liver transplantation in highly selected patients with first-episode severe alcoholic hepatitis non-responsive to medical therapy achieves post-transplant survival comparable to other indications. (PMID 22077916)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal selection criteria and duration of alcohol abstinence for early liver transplantation in non-responders.
- Use of combination therapies or anti-inflammatory biologics in steroid-refractory alcoholic hepatitis.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Calculating Maddrey DF (4.6 * [PT - control] + total bilirubin in mg/dL) to determine indication for corticosteroids.
- Interpreting the Day 7 Lille score threshold of 0.45 to discontinue futile steroids.
- Screening for occult spontaneous bacterial peritonitis, urinary tract infection, and bacteremia before giving corticosteroids.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 46-year-old with heavy drinking history presenting with deep jaundice (bilirubin 22 mg/dL), AST 240, ALT 85, and prolonged PT (DF = 48).
- A patient on Day 7 of prednisolone whose bilirubin has increased from 18 to 23 mg/dL, with a Lille score of 0.62.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should cross-reference local early liver transplant evaluation pathways and infection screening protocols.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38241123**: *AASLD Practice Guideline on Alcohol-Associated Liver Disease.* (2024) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 25898143**: *Prednisolone or Pentoxifylline for Alcoholic Hepatitis (STOPAH Trial).* (2015) [trial] - 
- **PMID 17514371**: *Early change in bilirubin levels is an important prognostic factor in severe alcoholic hepatitis (Lille Model).* (2007) [trial] - 
- **PMID 22077916**: *Early liver transplantation for severe alcoholic hepatitis (Mathurin et al.).* (2011) [trial] -

## [0103] alcoholic hepatitis

**Cluster**: alcohol_associated_hepatitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Alcoholic hepatitis presents with rapid-onset jaundice, tender hepatomegaly, AST:ALT ratio > 2:1 (rarely exceeding 400 IU/L), and systemic inflammation. Core therapy requires intensive nutrition (1.2-1.5 g/kg protein, 35 kcal/kg), high-potency parenteral B vitamins (thiamine), infection prophylaxis, and alcohol abstinence support.

### Core Teaching Points
- EASL guidelines emphasize enteral nutritional support providing 35-40 kcal/kg/day and 1.2-1.5 g/kg protein to reduce infectious complications in alcoholic hepatitis. (PMID 30041864)
- Aminotransferase elevations in alcoholic hepatitis typically display AST:ALT > 2:1 with absolute values almost always < 500 IU/L due to hepatic pyridoxal-5-phosphate deficiency. (PMID 38241123)
- Parenteral thiamine must be administered immediately prior to any glucose infusion to prevent precipitation of acute Wernicke encephalopathy. (PMID 25081827)
- Ascites and hepatic encephalopathy in acute alcoholic hepatitis require standard spironolactone/furosemide and lactulose/rifaximin titration. (PMID 30041864)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy of granulocyte colony-stimulating factor (G-CSF) in improving liver regeneration and reducing 90-day mortality.
- Role of fecal microbiota transplantation in improving gut-liver axis dysbiosis in alcoholic hepatitis.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Characteristic laboratory findings: AST:ALT > 2 with transaminases < 300-400 IU/L, leukocytosis, and elevated INR.
- Nutritional requirements in severe alcoholic hepatitis and avoidance of protein restriction.
- Mandatory baseline diagnostic paracentesis to exclude spontaneous bacterial peritonitis in jaundiced cirrhotic ascites.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 42-year-old presenting with jaundice, abdominal distention, AST 180, ALT 74, alkaline phosphatase 210, and total bilirubin 14 mg/dL.
- A patient with severe alcoholic hepatitis developing low-grade fever and mild confusion; diagnostic paracentesis yields 340 neutrophils/uL.

**Reviewer Gap**: Ensure alignment with local guidelines on enteral nutrition feeding tube placement in patients with esophageal varices.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 30041864**: *EASL Clinical Practice Guidelines: Management of Alcohol-Related Liver Disease.* (2018) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 38241123**: *AASLD Practice Guideline on Alcohol-Associated Liver Disease.* (2024) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 25081827**: *Wernicke's encephalopathy: new perspectives on an ancient disease.* (2014) [review] -

## [0104] alcohol use disorder

**Cluster**: alcohol_use_disorders | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Alcohol use disorder (AUD) is diagnosed using DSM-5-TR criteria and quantified with validated screening tools (AUDIT-C). Long-term management pairs evidence-based psychosocial interventions (CBT, motivational interviewing) with maintenance pharmacotherapy (acamprosate, naltrexone) to sustain abstinence or reduce heavy drinking.

### Core Teaching Points
- APA practice guidelines recommend offering either acamprosate or oral naltrexone as first-line pharmacotherapy for patients with moderate-to-severe alcohol use disorder. (PMID 29338575)
- AUDIT-C screening with scores >= 4 in men and >= 3 in women identifies hazardous drinking with high sensitivity and specificity in outpatient settings. (PMID 29338575)
- Combining pharmacotherapy with structured medical management or cognitive behavioral therapy yields higher abstinence rates than medication alone (COMBINE study). (PMID 16670414)
- Routine laboratory monitoring with GGT, MCV, and carbohydrate-deficient transferrin (CDT) provides objective biochemical markers of ongoing alcohol consumption. (PMID 29338575)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Comparative effectiveness of abstinence-oriented versus heavy-drinking-reduction endpoints in clinical trial design.
- Optimal duration of maintenance pharmacotherapy once sustained remission is achieved.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Identifying first-line maintenance pharmacotherapy for AUD: acamprosate and naltrexone.
- Choosing between abstinence-focused goals versus controlled reduction in harm-reduction models.
- Distinguishing physiological tolerance and withdrawal symptoms from behavioral features of AUD under DSM-5 criteria.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 39-year-old corporate executive drinking 8 units of alcohol daily who requests non-addictive medication to curb evening cravings.
- A 52-year-old after inpatient detox asking for medical therapy to maintain long-term sobriety while attending mutual-help groups.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm local addiction liaison service integration and mutual-aid meeting referrals.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 29338575**: *The American Psychiatric Association Practice Guideline for the Pharmacological Treatment of Patients With Alcohol Use Disorder.* (2018) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 16670414**: *Combining Medications and Behavioral Interventions for Alcohol Dependence (COMBINE Study).* (2006) [trial] -

## [0105] alcohol use disorder naltrexone and acamprosate

**Cluster**: alcohol_use_disorders | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acamprosate and naltrexone are first-line pharmacological options for alcohol use disorder. Acamprosate modulates glutamatergic NMDA and GABA transmission, is excreted renally, and is preferred in patients with liver disease. Naltrexone blocks mu-opioid receptors, reduces alcohol reward/craving, but is contraindicated in acute hepatitis, liver failure, and concurrent opioid use.

### Core Teaching Points
- Naltrexone (oral 50 mg daily or monthly depot intramuscular extended-release 380 mg) significantly reduces the risk of return to heavy drinking. (PMID 25027140)
- Naltrexone is strictly contraindicated in patients currently taking prescription opioids or undergoing opioid detoxification, as it precipitates severe acute opioid withdrawal. (PMID 29338575)
- Acamprosate (666 mg three times daily) modulates NMDA hyperglutamatergic states, promotes abstinence maintenance, and is safe in advanced liver disease because it lacks hepatic metabolism. (PMID 20593452)
- Acamprosate dose must be reduced in moderate renal impairment (CrCl 30-50 mL/min) and is contraindicated when CrCl < 30 mL/min. (PMID 29338575)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Precision pharmacogenetics: whether OPRM1 Asn40Asp (A118G) polymorphism reliably predicts enhanced clinical response to naltrexone.
- Efficacy of simultaneous combination of acamprosate plus naltrexone over individual monotherapy.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Selecting acamprosate over naltrexone in a patient with Child-Pugh class B cirrhosis.
- Recognizing naltrexone contraindication in patients with chronic pain managed with opioid analgesics.
- Managing gastrointestinal side effects (nausea, diarrhea) of acamprosate and naltrexone during treatment initiation.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 48-year-old with AUD and hepatitis C cirrhosis (bilirubin 3.1 mg/dL, albumin 2.9 g/dL) seeking anti-craving medication.
- A patient taking codeine-paracetamol for chronic back pain who is inappropriately prescribed naltrexone and experiences acute severe withdrawal.

**Reviewer Gap**: Verify renal dosing adjustment tables for acamprosate and toxicological screening prior to naltrexone initiation.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 25027140**: *Pharmacotherapy for adults with alcohol use disorders in outpatient settings: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2014) [meta-analysis] - 
- **PMID 20593452**: *Acamprosate for alcohol dependence: a Cochrane systematic review.* (2010) [systematic review] - 
- **PMID 29338575**: *APA Practice Guideline for the Pharmacological Treatment of AUD.* (2018) [guideline] -

## [0106] Alcohol use disorder, Wernicke encephalopathy and alcohol-related liver disease

**Cluster**: alcohol_use_disorders | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Chronic alcohol use disorder commonly leads to hepatic fibrosis and metabolic brain injury. Wernicke encephalopathy is a medical emergency caused by thiamine (vitamin B1) deficiency, classic triad of confusion, ataxia, and ophthalmoplegia (nystagmus/lateral rectus palsy) occurring in only one-third of cases. Prompt IV thiamine prevents progression to irreversible Korsakoff psychosis.

### Core Teaching Points
- Wernicke encephalopathy is a clinical diagnosis requiring high-dose intravenous thiamine (500 mg IV three times daily for 3-5 days) before any enteral or parenteral carbohydrates. (PMID 25081827)
- Caine criteria diagnostic sensitivity for Wernicke encephalopathy requires 2 of: dietary deficiency, oculomotor abnormalities, cerebellar dysfunction, and altered mental state. (PMID 9153128)
- Alcohol-related cirrhosis requires systematic hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) screening with ultrasound +/- AFP every 6 months and endoscopic variceal screening. (PMID 38241123)
- Sustained alcohol abstinence is the single most powerful determinant of survival and portal pressure reduction across all stages of alcohol-related liver disease. (PMID 38241123)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal oral thiamine maintenance dosing and bioavailability following completion of high-dose intravenous treatment.
- Reversibility of cognitive deficits in Korsakoff syndrome with long-term nutritional and cognitive rehabilitation.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Why IV thiamine must precede glucose infusion: glucose metabolism consumes scarce remaining thiamine pyrophosphate cofactors in pyruvate dehydrogenase.
- Classic MRI brain findings in Wernicke encephalopathy: symmetric hyperintensity in mammillary bodies, medial thalami, and periaqueductal gray.
- Clinical distinction between Wernicke encephalopathy (acute, reversible) and Korsakoff syndrome (chronic, anterograde amnesia with confabulation).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 55-year-old homeless male brought to the ED with confusion, horizontal nystagmus, and broad-based ataxia after a multi-week drinking binge.
- A cirrhotic patient admitted for decompensated ascites whose nurse asks whether a 5% dextrose infusion can be started before the thiamine arrives.

**Reviewer Gap**: Ensure local emergency department protocol mandates IV thiamine in triage for all malnourished or alcohol-dependent patients.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 25081827**: *Wernicke's encephalopathy: new perspectives on an ancient disease.* (2014) [review] - 
- **PMID 9153128**: *Operational criteria for the classification of chronic alcohol-related brain damage: the Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome.* (1997) [systematic review] - 
- **PMID 38241123**: *AASLD Practice Guideline on Alcohol-Associated Liver Disease.* (2024) [guideline] -

## [0107] Alcohol withdrawal benzodiazepine treatment

**Cluster**: alcohol_withdrawal_management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Alcohol withdrawal syndrome (AWS) ranges from mild tremulousness to life-threatening delirium tremens and withdrawal seizures. Long-acting benzodiazepines (diazepam, chlordiazepoxide) are standard of care, administered preferably via symptom-triggered protocols (CIWA-Ar) rather than fixed schedules. In hepatic cirrhosis, short-acting agents without active metabolites (lorazepam, oxazepam) avoid drug accumulation.

### Core Teaching Points
- Symptom-triggered benzodiazepine administration guided by CIWA-Ar scores achieves equivalent clinical control with significantly lower total medication dose and shorter hospital stay. (PMID 10078832)
- Diazepam and chlordiazepoxide provide smoother withdrawal control due to long-acting active metabolites (desmethyldiazepam), reducing withdrawal seizure frequency. (PMID 33434440)
- In patients with severe liver failure or cirrhosis, lorazepam or oxazepam (LOT drugs: Lorazepam, Oxazepam, Temazepam) are preferred because they undergo direct glucuronidation without phase I hepatic metabolism. (PMID 15256488)
- Alcohol withdrawal seizures typically occur 12-48 hours after alcohol reduction, are generalized tonic-clonic, and are treated with benzodiazepines, not standard phenytoin. (PMID 15256488)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Use of adjunctive alpha-2 agonists (clonidine, dexmedetomidine) for autonomic hyperactivity without masking ongoing neurocognitive deterioration.
- Role of gabapentin or carbamazepine as monotherapy in mild-to-moderate outpatient alcohol withdrawal.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Choosing lorazepam or oxazepam in patients with decompensated alcoholic cirrhosis or acute liver injury.
- Why phenytoin is ineffective for alcohol withdrawal seizures (the underlying mechanism is GABA down-regulation and NMDA up-regulation, not an epileptic focus).
- Timing of symptom-triggered CIWA-Ar assessments and intervention threshold (CIWA-Ar >= 8-10 points).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 49-year-old with known cirrhosis admitted for an ankle fracture who develops tremors, sweating, and heart rate 118 bpm 24 hours post-admission.
- A patient experiencing a witnessed brief tonic-clonic seizure 18 hours after last drink; medical resident inquires about starting levetiracetam.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should confirm nurses are validated in CIWA-Ar scoring and that telemetry is available for heavy withdrawal protocols.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 10078832**: *A randomized trial of symptom-triggered vs fixed-schedule treatment of alcohol withdrawal.* (1999) [trial] - 
- **PMID 33434440**: *Benzodiazepines for alcohol withdrawal: Cochrane Systematic Review.* (2021) [systematic review] - 
- **PMID 15256488**: *The management of alcohol withdrawal delirium and withdrawal seizures.* (2004) [review] -

## [0108] Alcohol withdrawal syndrome and delirium tremens management in ICU

**Cluster**: alcohol_withdrawal_management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Delirium tremens (DT) is a severe medical emergency characterized by profound delirium, hallucinations, agitation, fever, and severe autonomic instability, typically developing 48-96 hours after alcohol cessation. Management in the ICU mandates aggressive intravenous sedation (high-dose benzodiazepines or phenobarbital protocols), airway protection, and correction of electrolyte abnormalities (hypomagnesemia, hypokalemia).

### Core Teaching Points
- Delirium tremens carries up to 5-15% mortality without intensive care; mortality is driven by hyperthermia, cardiac arrhythmias, and aspiration pneumonia. (PMID 15256488)
- Phenobarbital monotherapy or protocolized combination with benzodiazepines achieves faster symptom control, lower intubation rates, and decreased ICU length of stay in severe refractory withdrawal. (PMID 42634386)
- Dexmedetomidine reduces sympathetic overdrive and tachycardia as an adjunctive sedative, but does not prevent seizures or delirium and cannot substitute for GABAergic agents. (PMID 27221644)
- Severe hypomagnesemia and hypophosphatemia are common in severe withdrawal and must be corrected aggressively to prevent refractory seizures and refractory hypokalemia. (PMID 15256488)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Exact dosing protocols for intravenous phenobarbital load (10 mg/kg vs incremental 130-260 mg doses) in non-intubated patients.
- Long-term neurocognitive sequelae following prolonged ICU delirium tremens.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Distinguishing alcoholic hallucinosis (clear sensorium, occurs 12-24h post-cessation) from delirium tremens (global delirium, profound disorientation, 48-96h).
- Why antipsychotics (e.g. haloperidol) must not be used as monotherapy: they lower seizure threshold and worsen autonomic temperature dysregulation.
- Criteria for ICU admission: failure of high-dose benzodiazepines (> 50-100 mg diazepam equivalent in 1 hour), severe hemodynamic instability, or status epilepticus.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 52-year-old 3 days post-laparotomy who becomes wildly agitated, diaphoretic, pyrexic (38.8C), and sees insects crawling on the ICU ceiling.
- A patient with delirium tremens who has received 120 mg of IV diazepam in 2 hours with persistent severe tachycardia (HR 145) and tremor.

**Reviewer Gap**: Confirm ICU protocol availability for phenobarbital loading and continuous intravenous infusions with endotracheal intubation readiness.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42634386**: *Phenobarbital Versus Benzodiazepine-Based Pathways for Alcohol Withdrawal: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2025) [meta-analysis] - 
- **PMID 15256488**: *The management of alcohol withdrawal delirium.* (2004) [review] - 
- **PMID 27221644**: *Adjunctive dexmedetomidine in severe alcohol withdrawal: a systematic review.* (2016) [systematic review] -

## [0109] Allergic Bronchopulmonary Aspergillosis

**Cluster**: allergic_diseases | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Allergic bronchopulmonary aspergillosis (ABPA) is a hypersensitivity response to Aspergillus fumigatus colonization in patients with asthma or cystic fibrosis. Diagnostic criteria include total serum IgE > 1000 IU/mL, positive Aspergillus-specific IgE or skin test, eosinophilia, and central bronchiectasis on HRCT. Treatment combines systemic oral corticosteroids with antifungal therapy (itraconazole) or T2 biologics.

### Core Teaching Points
- ISHAM criteria require total serum IgE > 1000 IU/mL, elevated Aspergillus-specific IgE or positive skin prick test, along with peripheral eosinophilia or central bronchiectasis. (PMID 34185448)
- Oral itraconazole (200 mg twice daily with therapeutic drug monitoring) combined with oral corticosteroids increases response rates and reduces steroid exposure. (PMID 11114316)
- High-attenuation mucus (HAM) on high-resolution chest CT is pathognomonic for ABPA and signifies higher relapse rates. (PMID 21498705)
- Biologic therapies targeting IL-4/IL-13 (dupilumab), IL-5 (mepolizumab), or IgE (omalizumab) provide significant steroid-sparing efficacy in recurrent or steroid-dependent ABPA. (PMID 34185448)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration of antifungal therapy (4 months vs 12 months) in preventing long-term bronchiectasis progression.
- Positioning of T2-targeted biologics as upfront monotherapy without prolonged high-dose oral corticosteroids.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Recognizing elevated total IgE (> 1000 IU/mL) as the mandatory entry criterion for ABPA diagnosis.
- Identifying high-attenuation mucus (mucus plugs denser than paraspinal muscle) on non-contrast chest CT.
- Selecting therapeutic drug monitoring for oral itraconazole (target trough > 1.0-2.0 ug/mL) to ensure adequate gastrointestinal absorption.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 36-year-old with severe asthma presenting with recurrent cough, expectoration of brown mucus plugs, total IgE 3,400 IU/mL, and eosinophils 1,200/uL.
- An adult with cystic fibrosis showing fleeting bilateral infiltrates and worsening spirometry despite standard antibiotic therapy.

**Reviewer Gap**: Verify availability of serum Aspergillus fumigatus-specific IgG/IgE assays and azole therapeutic drug monitoring.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34185448**: *Working Group Consensus on the Diagnosis and Treatment of Allergic Bronchopulmonary Aspergillosis (ISHAM).* (2021) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 11114316**: *A randomized trial of itraconazole in allergic bronchopulmonary aspergillosis.* (2000) [trial] - 
- **PMID 21498705**: *High-attenuation mucus in allergic bronchopulmonary aspergillosis: another visual cue.* (2011) [review] -

## [0110] Allergic rhinitis intranasal steroids and immunotherapy

**Cluster**: allergic_diseases | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Allergic rhinitis is an IgE-mediated chronic inflammatory disease of the upper airway. Moderate-to-severe symptoms are managed first-line with intranasal corticosteroids (INCS) or combination INCS and intranasal antihistamines (e.g. fluticasone/azelastine). Allergen immunotherapy (SLIT or SCIT) is the only disease-modifying intervention for IgE-confirmed refractory disease.

### Core Teaching Points
- AAO-HNS guidelines recommend intranasal corticosteroids as first-line monotherapy for patients with clinically significant allergic rhinitis affecting quality of life. (PMID 25644760)
- Fixed-dose combination of intranasal corticosteroid and intranasal antihistamine (fluticasone propionate/azelastine) provides faster and superior symptom relief compared to either agent alone. (PMID 32306437)
- Oral first-generation sedating antihistamines (e.g. diphenhydramine, chlorpheniramine) should be avoided due to sedation, anticholinergic side effects, and cognitive impairment. (PMID 25644760)
- Allergen-specific immunotherapy (sublingual or subcutaneous) induces immune tolerance, reduces medication requirements, and prevents new allergen sensitizations. (PMID 32306437)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Comparative long-term clinical efficacy and adherence of sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) tablets versus subcutaneous injections (SCIT).
- Role of biologics (e.g. dupilumab, omalizumab) in severe refractory allergic rhinitis with concurrent nasal polyposis.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Selecting intranasal corticosteroids over oral antihistamines as the most effective monotherapy for nasal congestion.
- Advising patients on proper INCS spray technique (direct nozzle away from nasal septum towards outer ear to avoid epistaxis/septal perforation).
- Recognizing rhinitis medicamentosa resulting from rebound hyperemia after > 3-5 days of topical alpha-adrenergic decongestants (oxymetazoline).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 24-year-old student with seasonal sneezing, severe nasal congestion, and conjunctival itching who complains that oral cetirizine is not clearing his congestion.
- A 35-year-old presenting with continuous severe nasal blockage after using over-the-counter xylometazoline spray multiple times daily for 6 weeks.

**Reviewer Gap**: Confirm referral criteria to allergy clinics for skin prick testing or specific IgE validation prior to immunotherapy initiation.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 25644760**: *Clinical Practice Guideline: Allergic Rhinitis (AAO-HNS).* (2015) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 32306437**: *Allergic Rhinitis and its Impact on Asthma (ARIA) Phase 4: Validated mobile technology guidelines.* (2020) [guideline] -

## [0111] Allogeneic Stem Cell Transplantation

**Cluster**: allogeneic_stem_cell_transplantation | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Allogeneic hematopoietic cell transplantation (allo-HCT) offers curative potential for high-risk acute leukemias, myelodysplastic syndromes, and marrow failure syndromes via the graft-versus-leukemia (GVL) effect. Post-transplant care focuses on conditioning toxicity, acute and chronic graft-versus-host disease (GVHD) prevention/treatment, and infectious surveillance (CMV, EBV, invasive molds).

### Core Teaching Points
- EBMT/ELN guidelines recommend allogeneic HCT in first complete remission for acute myeloid leukemia with intermediate- or adverse-risk cytogenetics and molecular features. (PMID 34489572)
- Post-transplant cyclophosphamide (PTCy) on days +3 and +4 significantly reduces severe acute and chronic GVHD, enabling successful haploidentical and mismatched unrelated donor transplants. (PMID 30829177)
- Pre-emptive antiviral therapy with letermovir prophylaxis reduces clinically significant cytomegalovirus (CMV) infection in CMV-seropositive HCT recipients without myelosuppression. (PMID 29211658)
- Chronic GVHD diagnosis requires diagnostic clinical signs (e.g. lichen planus-like skin changes, sclerotic skin, fasciitis) or distinctive signs confirmed by histology according to NIH consensus criteria. (PMID 33099617)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Positioning targeted maintenance therapies (FLT3 inhibitors, IDH inhibitors, azacitidine) post-allo-HCT to prevent molecular relapse.
- Comparative efficacy of ruxolitinib versus belumosudil as second-line treatment for steroid-refractory chronic GVHD.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Recognizing acute GVHD clinical triad: maculopapular erythematous rash, secretory diarrhea with crampy pain, and cholestatic hyperbilirubinemia.
- Mechanism of post-transplant cyclophosphamide: selectively eliminates rapidly proliferating alloreactive donor T cells while sparing non-alloreactive memory T cells and regulatory T cells.
- Distinguishing sinusoidal obstruction syndrome (veno-occlusive disease: weight gain, painful hepatomegaly, jaundice within 3 weeks) from acute liver GVHD.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 45-year-old on Day +28 post-matched unrelated donor transplant developing a confluent erythematous rash over 60% of body surface area and 1,200 mL watery diarrhea daily.
- A post-allograft patient on cyclosporine who presents with rising CMV viral load (> 2,500 IU/mL) requiring pre-emptive valganciclovir therapy.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local institutional SOPs for HLA donor selection, conditioning intensity (MAC vs RIC), and chimerism monitoring.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34489572**: *Indications for allogeneic stem cell transplantation: EBMT practice guidelines.* (2021) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 30829177**: *Post-Transplantation Cyclophosphamide-Based Graft-versus-Host Disease Prophylaxis (Lancet Oncol).* (2019) [trial] - 
- **PMID 29211658**: *Letermovir Prophylaxis for Cytomegalovirus in Hematopoietic-Cell Transplantation.* (2017) [trial] - 
- **PMID 33099617**: *National Institutes of Health Consensus Development Project on Criteria for Clinical Trials in Chronic Graft-versus-Host Disease.* (2020) [guideline] -

## [0112] Alpha-1 Antitrypsin Deficiency

**Cluster**: alpha1_antitrypsin_deficiency | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency (AATD) is an autosomal codominant genetic disorder characterized by low circulating levels of AAT (a serine protease inhibitor protecting lung elastin). Homozygosity for the Z allele (Pi*ZZ) predisposes to early-onset panacinar emphysema (predominating in lower lung zones) and liver cirrhosis from misfolded polymer accumulation in hepatocytes.

### Core Teaching Points
- ATS/ERS guidelines recommend screening for AATD in all patients with COPD, unexplained liver disease, or persistent adult-onset asthma with incompletely reversible airflow obstruction. (PMID 14530514)
- Serum AAT concentration < 11 umol/L (< 57 mg/dL) combined with protein phenotyping (isoelectric focusing) or SERPINA1 genotyping confirms severe deficiency. (PMID 26027906)
- Intravenous AAT augmentation therapy (60 mg/kg weekly) significantly slows the loss of lung density assessed by chest computed tomography densitometry in patients with FEV1 35-70% (RAPID trial). (PMID 26027906)
- Hepatic disease in AATD results from toxic gain-of-function due to intracellular retention of Z-AAT protein polymers (PAS-positive, diastase-resistant globules) and is not corrected by IV augmentation. (PMID 31835948)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy and cost-effectiveness of AAT augmentation therapy in non-smoking patients with mild airflow limitation (FEV1 > 70%).
- Efficacy of emerging RNA interference (RNAi) therapies (e.g. fazirsiran) designed to silence hepatic Z-AAT mRNA and reverse fibrosis.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Characteristic radiographic finding: panacinar emphysema with marked lower lobe predominance in a non-smoker under 45 years of age.
- Liver biopsy hallmark: periodic acid-Schiff (PAS)-positive, diastase-resistant globules in periportal hepatocytes.
- Primary indication for IV augmentation therapy: documented Pi*ZZ phenotype, serum AAT < 11 umol/L, non-smoker, and FEV1 35-70% predicted.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 38-year-old lifetime non-smoker presenting with progressive exertional dyspnea, FEV1/FVC ratio 0.52, and bibasilar hyperlucency on chest radiography.
- A 50-year-old with Pi*ZZ genotype presenting with elevated transaminases and hepatomegaly, inquiring if augmentation therapy will heal his liver.

**Reviewer Gap**: Ensure national registry enrolment criteria and pre-authorization requirements for weekly IV augmentation are highlighted.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 14530514**: *American Thoracic Society/European Respiratory Society Statement: Standards for the Diagnosis and Management of Individuals with Alpha-1 Antitrypsin Deficiency.* (2003) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 26027906**: *Efficacy and safety of intravenous human alpha-1-antitrypsin for emphysema (RAPID Trial).* (2015) [trial] - 
- **PMID 31835948**: *Liver disease in alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency: Current knowledge and future therapies.* (2020) [review] -

## [0113] Alport Syndrome

**Cluster**: alport_syndrome | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Alport syndrome is an inherited progressive type IV collagen nephropathy caused by pathogenic variants in COL4A3, COL4A4, or COL4A5 genes. It presents with persistent microscopic hematuria in childhood, progressive proteinuria, hypertension, progressive renal failure, high-frequency sensorineural hearing loss, and anterior lenticonus. Early ACE inhibitor therapy significantly delays progression to end-stage kidney disease.

### Core Teaching Points
- International consensus guidelines recommend early initiation of ACE inhibitors at the onset of persistent microalbuminuria (or at diagnosis in males with X-linked Alport syndrome) to delay end-stage kidney disease by decades. (PMID 33099482)
- X-linked inheritance due to COL4A5 mutations accounts for approximately 85% of cases; affected hemizygous males universally develop ESRD, whereas heterozygous females exhibit variable clinical phenotypes. (PMID 23154948)
- Renal biopsy electron microscopy demonstrates characteristic irregular thinning, thickening, and longitudinal splitting/lamellation ('basket-weave' appearance) of the glomerular basement membrane. (PMID 33099482)
- Post-transplant anti-GBM nephritis occurs in 3-5% of transplanted Alport patients due to alloimmunization against normal collagen alpha-3/4/5(IV) chains in the donor kidney. (PMID 33099482)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term efficacy of add-on SGLT2 inhibitors (dapagliflozin/empagliflozin) on top of maximally tolerated ACEi/ARB in Alport syndrome.
- Genotype-phenotype correlations: predicting ESRD timing based on missense versus truncating/nonsense COL4A5 mutations.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Characteristic clinical triad: persistent microscopic hematuria, bilateral high-frequency sensorineural hearing loss, and ocular abnormalities (anterior lenticonus, macular flecks).
- Pathognomonic electron microscopic finding: basket-weave lamellation and split lamina densa of the GBM.
- Timing of ACE inhibitor initiation: do not wait for overt proteinuria; start at diagnosis in affected males or persistent microalbuminuria.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 14-year-old boy evaluated for asymptomatic microscopic hematuria whose maternal uncle developed kidney failure and required hearing aids in his twenties.
- A 26-year-old male with Alport syndrome on lisinopril whose 24-hour urine protein has risen to 1.8 g/day, seeking guidance on second-line antiproteinuric therapy.

**Reviewer Gap**: Verify access to comprehensive next-generation sequencing multi-gene collagen IV panels and audiology screening.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 33099482**: *Expert consensus recommendations for the diagnosis and management of Alport syndrome.* (2021) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 23154948**: *Early angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibition in Alport syndrome delays renal replacement therapy.* (2012) [trial] - 

---

## [0114] Alport syndrome and inherited collagen nephropathies

**Cluster**: alport_syndrome | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Inherited collagen IV nephropathies span a continuum from benign familial hematuria (thin basement membrane nephropathy) to severe X-linked or autosomal recessive Alport syndrome. Genetic testing for COL4A3, COL4A4, and COL4A5 distinguishes benign non-progressive heterozygous carriers from individuals at high risk for progressive proteinuria, glomerulosclerosis, and extra-renal sensorineural deafness.

### Core Teaching Points
- Thin basement membrane nephropathy (TBMN), commonly caused by heterozygous COL4A3 or COL4A4 mutations, presents with isolated microscopic hematuria and a benign long-term renal prognosis in the absence of proteinuria. (PMID 33099482)
- Next-generation sequencing multigene panels have replaced routine renal biopsy as the preferred first-line diagnostic investigation for suspected inherited collagen IV disorders. (PMID 33099482)
- Heterozygous females with X-linked Alport syndrome (COL4A5) have a lifelong risk of developing proteinuria (up to 75%) and ESRD (up to 15-30% by age 60), requiring lifelong annual monitoring. (PMID 23154948)
- SGLT2 inhibitors confer additive antiproteinuric and renoprotective effects when added to maximum tolerated renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system blockade. (PMID 38480320)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Distinguishing pathogenic variants from variants of uncertain significance (VUS) in COL4A3/COL4A4 in adult-onset focal segmental glomerulosclerosis.
- Role of dual endothelin-angiotensin receptor antagonists (spartansentan) in slowing progression in progressive collagen IV glomerulopathies.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Differentiating thin basement membrane nephropathy (diffuse uniform thinning of GBM < 250 nm, normal hearing, normal renal function) from Alport syndrome.
- Managing pregnant women who are heterozygous carriers of X-linked Alport syndrome: risk of pre-eclampsia and passing mutation to male offspring.
- Genetic counseling: male-to-male transmission never occurs in X-linked inheritance (COL4A5), but 100% of daughters of affected males are obligate carriers.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 32-year-old woman with isolated microscopic hematuria whose father has ESRD, wondering if her condition will cause kidney failure.
- A patient misdiagnosed with steroid-resistant focal segmental glomerulosclerosis (FSGS) whose genetic panel identifies a pathogenic heterozygous COL4A3 mutation.

**Reviewer Gap**: Confirm coordination between adult nephrology and medical genetics for family cascade testing and genetic counseling.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 33099482**: *Expert consensus recommendations for the diagnosis and management of Alport syndrome and inherited collagen nephropathies.* (2021) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 23154948**: *Clinical and genetic spectrum of inherited collagen type IV disorders.* (2012) [review] - 
- **PMID 38480320**: *KDIGO 2024 Clinical Practice Guideline for the Management of Glomerular Diseases.* (2024) [guideline] -

## [0115] alteplase acute ischemic stroke NINDS trial

**Cluster**: stroke_alteplase_management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Intravenous thrombolysis with alteplase (0.9 mg/kg, max 90 mg; 10% bolus over 1 min, remainder over 60 min) or tenecteplase (0.25 mg/kg, max 25 mg) is the standard of care for acute ischemic stroke within 4.5 hours of symptom onset. Pre-treatment non-contrast head CT must exclude intracranial hemorrhage, and blood pressure must be safely lowered < 185/110 mmHg. Concurrently, patients with large vessel occlusion should be evaluated for endovascular thrombectomy.

### Core Teaching Points
- The landmark NINDS rt-PA Stroke Study demonstrated that patients treated with intravenous alteplase within 3 hours had a 30% higher likelihood of minimal or no disability at 3 months (mRS 0-1) despite a 6.4% symptomatic intracerebral hemorrhage rate. (PMID 7477192)
- ECASS III trial demonstrated efficacy of alteplase extended to 4.5 hours from symptom onset in eligible patients without severe stroke (NIHSS > 25), age > 80 with previous stroke/diabetes, or oral anticoagulant use. (PMID 18815396)
- AHA/ASA guidelines require blood pressure to be maintained < 180/105 mmHg during and for 24 hours following intravenous thrombolysis to minimize hemorrhagic transformation. (PMID 31662037)
- In patients with acute large vessel occlusion (ICA or M1 segment MCA), mechanical thrombectomy is standard within 6 hours (and up to 24 hours selected by perfusion imaging via DAWN and DEFUSE-3 criteria), regardless of whether IV thrombolysis is given. (PMID 29364767)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Non-inferiority and practical workflow benefits of tenecteplase over alteplase in routine real-world acute ischemic stroke protocols.
- Safety of thrombolysis in patients on direct oral anticoagulants whose last dose was taken between 24 and 48 hours prior to onset.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Blood pressure exclusion threshold for IV thrombolysis: SBP > 185 mmHg or DBP > 110 mmHg refractory to IV labetalol/nicardipine.
- Managing acute orolingual angioedema (seen in 1-5% of patients, especially those on ACE inhibitors): stop infusion, give IV methylprednisolone, diphenhydramine, and prepare for emergent intubation.
- Post-thrombolysis antiplatelet/anticoagulant timing: strictly avoid aspirin or heparin for 24 hours until follow-up CT/MRI excludes hemorrhage.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old presenting with acute right hemiparesis and aphasia (NIHSS 16) arriving at ED 75 minutes after witnessed onset, BP 178/96 mmHg.
- A stroke patient 40 minutes into an alteplase infusion who develops sudden severe headache, vomiting, acute hypertension (BP 210/120), and declining GCS.

**Reviewer Gap**: Verify institutional 'code stroke' door-to-needle benchmark (< 45 minutes) and rapid transfer protocols for endovascular thrombectomy.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 7477192**: *Tissue plasminogen activator for acute ischemic stroke. The National Institute of Neurological Disorders and Stroke rt-PA Stroke Study Group.* (1995) [trial] - 
- **PMID 18815396**: *Thrombolysis with alteplase 3 to 4.5 hours after acute ischemic stroke (ECASS III).* (2008) [trial] - 
- **PMID 31662037**: *2019 Update to the 2018 Guidelines for the Early Management of Patients With Acute Ischemic Stroke (AHA/ASA).* (2019) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 29364767**: *Thrombectomy for Stroke at 6 to 16 Hours with Selection by Perfusion Imaging (DEFUSE 3).* (2018) [trial] -

## [0116] alzheimer s disease anti amyloid immunotherapy

**Cluster**: alzheimers_disease | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Early Alzheimer's disease with confirmed amyloid-beta pathology is eligible for anti-amyloid monoclonal antibodies (lecanemab, donanemab). These agents target protofibrillar or plaque amyloid-beta to slow cognitive decline, but mandate APOE genotyping and serial safety MRI monitoring for ARIA.

### Core Teaching Points
- Lecanemab slowed 18-month cognitive and functional decline (CDR-SB) by 27% compared to placebo in the CLARITY AD trial. (PMID 36449413)
- Appropriate Use Criteria mandate pre-treatment confirmation of amyloid pathology via CSF biomarkers or amyloid PET imaging. (PMID 37402633)
- Amyloid-related imaging abnormalities with edema (ARIA-E) occur in 12.6% of patients on lecanemab and 24% on donanemab, with highest risk in APOE e4 homozygotes. (PMID 37459141)
- Routine surveillance brain MRIs are required before infusions 5, 7, and 14 to detect asymptomatic ARIA-E and ARIA-H. (PMID 37402633)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Management of recurrent asymptomatic ARIA-E in APOE e4 carriers.
- Long-term clinical meaningfulness beyond the 18-month trial horizon.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Recognizing ARIA-E as sulcal or gyral hyperintensity on T2-FLAIR brain MRI.
- APOE e4 homozygous status as the primary risk factor for severe ARIA-E.
- Criteria for withholding anti-amyloid infusions in moderate-to-severe ARIA.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 71-year-old with amnestic MCI and positive amyloid PET whose family inquires about lecanemab eligibility.
- A patient on lecanemab whose week 14 MRI shows asymptomatic frontal ARIA-E.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36449413**: *Lecanemab in Early Alzheimer's Disease (CLARITY AD).* (2023) [trial] - 
- **PMID 37402633**: *Appropriate Use Criteria for Lecanemab in Early Alzheimer's Disease.* (2023) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 37459141**: *Donanemab in Early Symptomatic Alzheimer Disease (TRAILBLAZER-ALZ 2).* (2023) [trial] -

## [0118] Amegakaryocytic Thrombocytopenia

**Cluster**: amegakaryocytic_thrombocytopenia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Congenital amegakaryocytic thrombocytopenia (CAMT) is a severe marrow failure disorder characterized by isolated severe thrombocytopenia and absent megakaryocytes on bone marrow biopsy. CAMT is caused by biallelic loss-of-function MPL mutations (TPO receptor), typically progressing to aplastic anemia or leukemia; allogeneic hematopoietic cell transplantation is the definitive curative treatment.

### Core Teaching Points
- Biallelic loss-of-function mutations in the MPL gene encoding the thrombopoietin receptor cause CAMT, leading to early marrow aplasia. (PMID 10856232)
- Bone marrow examination demonstrates selective absent or severely hypoplastic megakaryocytes with preserved erythroid and myeloid lineages in early stages. (PMID 10856232)
- Allogeneic hematopoietic cell transplantation performed prior to trilineage marrow failure or leukemic transformation achieves > 80% long-term cure. (PMID 34489572)
- In acquired amegakaryocytic thrombocytopenia (AAT), immunosuppressive therapy with cyclosporine, ATG, or rituximab achieves remissions in autoantibody-mediated cases. (PMID 34489572)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal conditioning regimen intensity for CAMT.
- Differentiation of acquired AAT from myelodysplastic syndrome with isolated thrombocytopenia.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Distinguishing CAMT (absent radius syndrome / TAR has preserved megakaryocytes and absent radii; CAMT has normal radii).
- Role of allogeneic stem cell transplantation as sole curative modality.
- Initial bone marrow findings in CAMT vs immune thrombocytopenic purpura (ITP: abundant megakaryocytes).

### Case Generation Hooks
- An infant presenting with severe petechiae and platelets 8,000/uL with normal skeletal radiography.
- An adult with refractory severe thrombocytopenia showing zero megakaryocytes on marrow aspirate.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 10856232**: *Mutations in the thrombopoietin receptor gene (MPL) in congenital amegakaryocytic thrombocytopenia.* (2000) [trial] - 
- **PMID 34489572**: *Indications for allogeneic stem cell transplantation: EBMT guidelines.* (2021) [guideline] -

## [0119] AML Transformed from MPN

**Cluster**: mpn_blast_phase | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute myeloid leukemia transformed from myeloproliferative neoplasms (MPN-blast phase / secondary AML) carries an aggressive clinical course and dismal prognosis. It is characterized by high rates of complex karyotype, TP53 mutations, and resistance to standard 7+3 chemotherapy. Management incorporates hypomethylating agents plus venetoclax or ruxolitinib as a bridge to allogeneic stem cell transplantation.

### Core Teaching Points
- Transformation to AML (blast phase, >= 20% blasts) occurs in 10-15% of myelofibrosis and 3-5% of polycythemia vera / essential thrombocythemia. (PMID 37217277)
- Standard cytarabine/anthracycline (7+3) induction yields low complete remission rates (< 20-30%) and high induction mortality in post-MPN AML. (PMID 37217277)
- Combination therapy with azacitidine and venetoclax produces composite remission in 40-50% of secondary AML, serving as an effective bridge to transplant. (PMID 32786182)
- Allogeneic stem cell transplantation is the only curative therapeutic option for patients with MPN in blast phase who achieve morphological remission. (PMID 34489572)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal sequencing and continuation of JAK2 inhibitors (ruxolitinib) during induction.
- Post-transplant maintenance strategies to prevent recurrence in TP53-mutated secondary AML.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Identifying risk factors for MPN blast transformation: SRSF2, IDH1/2, ASXL1, and TP53 mutations.
- Venetoclax tumor lysis syndrome (TLS) mitigation and ramp-up protocol.
- Allogeneic transplant eligibility assessment in secondary AML.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 67-year-old with a 9-year history of JAK2-positive myelofibrosis presenting with 28% peripheral blasts and pancytopenia.
- A post-PV patient with leukemic transformation evaluated for azacitidine-venetoclax induction.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37217277**: *NCCN Guidelines: Acute Myeloid Leukemia.* (2023) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 32786182**: *Azacitidine and Venetoclax in Previously Untreated Acute Myeloid Leukemia (VIALE-A).* (2020) [trial] - 
- **PMID 34489572**: *EBMT Practice Guidelines on Allogeneic HCT.* (2021) [guideline] -

## [0120] Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis (ALS): El Escorial criteria, riluzole, non-invasive ventilation

**Cluster**: amyotrophic_lateral_sclerosis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS) is a neurodegenerative disorder causing progressive loss of upper and lower motor neurons. Diagnosis utilizes Revised El Escorial and Gold Coast criteria. Disease-modifying therapy includes riluzole (glutamate antagonist) and edaravone. Non-invasive ventilation (NIV) improves survival and quality of life when respiratory muscle weakness develops.

### Core Teaching Points
- Revised El Escorial and Gold Coast criteria establish diagnosis based on upper and lower motor neuron signs across progressive body regions. (PMID 22672564)
- Riluzole (50 mg orally twice daily) extends median survival by 2 to 3 months and reduces tracheostomy risk (Cochrane review). (PMID 22419278)
- Non-invasive ventilation (BiPAP) is the single most effective intervention for extending survival (median 7-12 months benefit) when FVC drops < 50% or SNIP < 40 cmH2O. (PMID 23728633)
- Percutaneous endoscopic gastrostomy (PEG) or radiologically inserted gastrostomy (RIG) should be placed before FVC falls below 50% to minimize procedural respiratory failure. (PMID 22672564)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Effectiveness of early pre-symptomatic NIV in patients with preserved daytime FVC but nocturnal hypoventilation.
- Targeted genetic therapies (tofersen) in SOD1-mutated familial ALS.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Distinguishing ALS from cervical spondylotic myelopathy: tongue fasciculations and normal sensory exam confirm ALS.
- Laboratory monitoring for riluzole: baseline and monthly hepatic transaminases for the first 3 months.
- Screening for frontotemporal dementia (FTD) which co-occurs in 15% of ALS patients.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 56-year-old with progressive asymmetric hand grip weakness, muscle cramping, hyperreflexia, and tongue fasciculations.
- An ALS patient reporting morning headaches, fragmented sleep, and orthopnea whose sitting FVC is 46%.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources

- **PMID 22419278**: *Riluzole for amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS)/motor neuron disease (MND).* (2012) [systematic review] - 
- **PMID 23728633**: *Non-invasive ventilation for amyotrophic lateral sclerosis.* (2013) [systematic review] -

## [0121] Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis pulmonary care

**Cluster**: amyotrophic_lateral_sclerosis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS) is a neurodegenerative disorder causing progressive loss of upper and lower motor neurons. Diagnosis utilizes Revised El Escorial and Gold Coast criteria. Disease-modifying therapy includes riluzole (glutamate antagonist) and edaravone. Non-invasive ventilation (NIV) improves survival and quality of life when respiratory muscle weakness develops.

### Core Teaching Points
- Revised El Escorial and Gold Coast criteria establish diagnosis based on upper and lower motor neuron signs across progressive body regions. (PMID 22672564)
- Riluzole (50 mg orally twice daily) extends median survival by 2 to 3 months and reduces tracheostomy risk (Cochrane review). (PMID 22419278)
- Non-invasive ventilation (BiPAP) is the single most effective intervention for extending survival (median 7-12 months benefit) when FVC drops < 50% or SNIP < 40 cmH2O. (PMID 23728633)
- Percutaneous endoscopic gastrostomy (PEG) or radiologically inserted gastrostomy (RIG) should be placed before FVC falls below 50% to minimize procedural respiratory failure. (PMID 22672564)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Effectiveness of early pre-symptomatic NIV in patients with preserved daytime FVC but nocturnal hypoventilation.
- Targeted genetic therapies (tofersen) in SOD1-mutated familial ALS.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Distinguishing ALS from cervical spondylotic myelopathy: tongue fasciculations and normal sensory exam confirm ALS.
- Laboratory monitoring for riluzole: baseline and monthly hepatic transaminases for the first 3 months.
- Screening for frontotemporal dementia (FTD) which co-occurs in 15% of ALS patients.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 56-year-old with progressive asymmetric hand grip weakness, muscle cramping, hyperreflexia, and tongue fasciculations.
- An ALS patient reporting morning headaches, fragmented sleep, and orthopnea whose sitting FVC is 46%.

## [0122] Anaemia

**Cluster**: anaemia_and_inflammation | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Anaemia evaluation begins with complete blood count, reticulocyte count, and peripheral smear to categorize microcytic (MCV < 80 fL), normocytic (80-100 fL), or macrocytic (> 100 fL) etiologies. In adults, iron deficiency requires systematic investigation of the gastrointestinal tract (coeliac serology, bidirectional endoscopy) to identify bleeding malignancies.

### Core Teaching Points
- British Society of Gastroenterology guidelines mandate bidirectional endoscopy (gastroscopy and colonoscopy) for unexplained iron deficiency anemia in men and postmenopausal women. (PMID 34526361)
- Serum ferritin < 30 ug/L is the most specific biochemical marker of absolute iron deficiency; ferritin < 100 ug/L indicates deficiency in the setting of chronic inflammation. (PMID 34526361)
- Oral iron supplementation given once daily or on alternate days achieves equivalent or superior fractional iron absorption compared to multiple daily doses due to hepcidin surges. (PMID 26418823)
- Intravenous iron (ferric carboxymaltose, ferric derisomaltose) is superior to oral iron in active inflammatory bowel disease, heart failure (FERRIC-HF), and chronic kidney disease. (PMID 34526361)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Indications for capsule endoscopy in iron deficiency anemia when bidirectional endoscopy is unrevealing.
- Diagnostic threshold for functional iron deficiency in heart failure with preserved ejection fraction.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Differentiating iron deficiency anemia (low ferritin, high TIBC/transferrin, low transferrin saturation < 16-20%) from thalassemia trait (Mentzer index < 13).
- Alternate-day oral iron dosing rationale: avoids hepcidin-mediated enterocyte blockade for 24-48 hours.
- Targeting intravenous iron in symptomatic HFrEF patients with ferritin < 100 or 100-299 with TSAT < 20%.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old asymptomatic male found to have microcytic anemia (Hb 9.8 g/dL, MCV 72 fL, ferritin 12 ug/L).
- A patient with Crohn's disease and active flare whose Hb is 8.6 g/dL and who cannot tolerate oral iron tablets.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34526361**: *British Society of Gastroenterology guidelines for the management of iron deficiency anaemia in adults.* (2021) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 26418823**: *Iron absorption from oral iron supplements given on consecutive versus alternate days and as single versus twice-daily doses.* (2015) [trial] -

## [0123] anaemia of chronic disease

**Cluster**: anaemia_of_chronic_disease | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Anaemia of chronic disease (ACD / anaemia of inflammation) is a cytokine-driven normocytic or mildly microcytic anaemia. Interleukin-6 stimulates hepatic production of hepcidin, which degrades ferroportin, trapping iron in reticuloendothelial macrophages and enterocytes. Laboratory hallmark is low serum iron, low TIBC/transferrin, normal to elevated ferritin (> 100 ng/mL), and elevated hepcidin.

### Core Teaching Points
- Hepcidin-mediated internalization and degradation of ferroportin-1 prevents intestinal iron absorption and macrophage iron release, causing hypoferremia. (PMID 30737227)
- Differentiating pure ACD from combined ACD + iron deficiency: TSAT < 20% with ferritin 30-100 ug/L indicates concurrent true iron deficiency responsive to IV iron. (PMID 34526361)
- Treating the underlying inflammatory, infectious, or rheumatologic condition remains the primary and most effective therapy for ACD. (PMID 30737227)
- Erythropoiesis-stimulating agents (ESAs) or hypoxia-inducible factor prolyl hydroxylase inhibitors (HIF-PHIs) restore hemoglobin in chronic kidney disease ACD but require repletion of iron stores. (PMID 23154948)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Utility of soluble transferrin receptor (sTfR) / log ferritin index in confirming concurrent iron deficiency in inflammatory states.
- Long-term cardiovascular safety profiles of HIF-PHIs compared to conventional ESAs.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Biochemical profile of pure ACD: low iron, low transferrin/TIBC, high or normal ferritin, low reticulocyte count.
- Why oral iron is ineffective in high-hepcidin inflammatory states (ferroportin blockade prevents gut mucosal transfer).
- Risk of red cell aplasia and thrombosis with excessive ESA dosing targeting Hb > 11.5-12 g/dL in CKD.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 59-year-old with rheumatoid arthritis whose Hb is 10.1 g/dL, MCV 84 fL, iron 24 ug/dL, TIBC 210 ug/dL, and ferritin 380 ug/L.
- A hemodialysis patient with non-responsive anemia on darbepoetin whose TSAT is 14% and ferritin is 180 ug/L.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 30737227**: *Anaemia of chronic disease: pathophysiology and management.* (2019) [review] - 
- **PMID 34526361**: *BSG guidelines on the management of iron deficiency anaemia.* (2021) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 23154948**: *KDIGO Clinical Practice Guideline for Anemia in Chronic Kidney Disease.* (2012) [guideline] -

## [0124] Anal Canal Cancer

**Cluster**: anal_canal_cancer | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Squamous cell carcinoma of the anal canal is strongly associated with high-risk human papillomavirus (HPV types 16 and 18). Curative standard of care for localized disease (T1-T4, N0-N3) is definitive concurrent chemoradiotherapy (Nigro protocol: 5-fluorouracil or capecitabine + mitomycin C with external beam radiotherapy), preserving anal sphincter function in > 75% of patients.

### Core Teaching Points
- Definitive concurrent chemoradiation with 5-FU and mitomycin C provides 80% 5-year colostomy-free survival in localized anal squamous cell carcinoma. (PMID 6187766)
- ESMO and NCCN guidelines recommend IMRT (intensity-modulated radiation therapy) to minimize radiation dermatitis, proctitis, and pelvic bone marrow toxicity. (PMID 34185202)
- Abdominoperineal resection (APR) with permanent end colostomy is reserved strictly for persistent disease at 26 weeks post-CRT or biopsy-proven locoregional recurrence. (PMID 37722421)
- First-line systemic therapy for metastatic or unresectable recurrent anal carcinoma is carboplatin plus paclitaxel, with immune checkpoint inhibitors (retifanlimab, pembrolizumab) for refractory disease. (PMID 34185202)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal interval post-chemoradiotherapy to confirm complete clinical response without premature salvage surgery.
- Role of circulating HPV DNA as a liquid biopsy biomarker for minimal residual disease.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- HPV 16 as the causative agent in > 85-90% of anal canal squamous cell carcinomas.
- The Nigro regimen components: 5-FU (or capecitabine) plus mitomycin C combined with radiotherapy.
- Post-treatment surveillance: digital rectal exam, anoscopy, and inguinal lymph node palpation every 3-6 months for 5 years.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 52-year-old presenting with rectal bleeding, tenesmus, and a 3.5 cm ulcerated anal canal mass on digital examination.
- A patient 12 weeks post-Nigro regimen with a persistent 1 cm indurated area on anoscopy; assessing need for observation to 26 weeks.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 6187766**: *Combined chemoradiotherapy as primary management of anal squamous cell carcinoma (Nigro Protocol).* (1983) [trial] - 
- **PMID 34185202**: *ESMO Clinical Practice Guideline for Anal Cancer.* (2021) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 37722421**: *NCCN Guidelines: Anal Carcinoma.* (2023) [guideline] -

## [0126] Analgesia strategies

**Cluster**: analgesia_strategies | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Multimodal analgesia targets multiple pain pathways simultaneously using paracetamol, NSAIDs or selective COX-2 inhibitors, regional/neuraxial blocks, and non-opioid adjuvants (gabapentinoids, IV lidocaine, ketamine). This synergistic approach achieves superior dynamic pain control while minimizing opioid consumption and opioid-related adverse events.

### Core Teaching Points
- ASA and PROSPECT practice guidelines recommend scheduled multimodal non-opioid analgesia as the baseline for all postoperative pain management. (PMID 26840801)
- Combining paracetamol (1g IV/oral q6h) with an NSAID (e.g. ibuprofen or celecoxib) provides greater pain relief than either agent alone without added toxicity. (PMID 20593452)
- Perioperative intravenous lidocaine infusions in major abdominal surgery accelerate return of bowel function, reduce pain scores, and shorten hospital stay. (PMID 26840801)
- Low-dose ketamine (NMDA receptor antagonist) reduces postoperative opioid consumption and prevents opioid-induced hyperalgesia in opioid-tolerant surgical patients. (PMID 26840801)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Risk-benefit balance of preoperative gabapentinoid loading regarding excessive sedation and respiratory depression.
- Cardiovascular and renal safety of short-course (48-72h) perioperative COX-2 inhibitors.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Mechanism of ketamine in chronic pain: non-competitive NMDA receptor blockade preventing central sensitization.
- Opioid-sparing multimodal protocol components: scheduled paracetamol + NSAID + local infiltration + PRN weak opioid.
- Contraindications to intravenous lidocaine: severe heart block, ventricular arrhythmias, and severe hepatic impairment.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 65-year-old on high-dose chronic buprenorphine undergoing total knee arthroplasty requiring acute pain planning.
- A post-colectomy patient developing nausea, urinary retention, and ileus on PCA morphine who is transitioned to multimodal analgesia.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 26840801**: *Practice Guidelines for Acute Postoperative Pain Management: American Society of Anesthesiologists.* (2016) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 20593452**: *Single dose oral paracetamol plus ibuprofen for acute postoperative pain.* (2010) [systematic review] -

## [0127] anaphylaxis

**Cluster**: anaphylaxis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Anaphylaxis is an acute, life-threatening systemic hypersensitivity reaction with rapid onset involving airway, breathing, circulation, or skin/mucosa. Intramuscular epinephrine (adrenaline) 1:1,000 (0.01 mg/kg, max 0.5 mg in adults; 0.3 mg auto-injector) into the mid-anterolateral thigh is the immediate, mandatory first-line therapy. Second-line antihistamines and steroids must never delay epinephrine.

### Core Teaching Points
- World Allergy Organization (WAO) guidelines mandate immediate intramuscular epinephrine (0.01 mg/kg, max 0.5 mg) in the mid-anterolateral thigh as the primary life-saving drug. (PMID 33202997)
- Antihistamines (H1/H2 blockers) and corticosteroids relieve cutaneous symptoms but do not treat airway obstruction, bronchospasm, or vascular collapse and must not delay epinephrine. (PMID 34564883)
- Biphasic anaphylactic reactions occur in 5-10% of cases, typically within 8 hours, warranting hospital observation for at least 4-8 hours post-resolution. (PMID 33202997)
- Serial serum total tryptase levels measured at 1-2 hours and > 24 hours post-event confirm mast cell degranulation (acute peak > 1.2 x baseline + 2 ug/L). (PMID 34564883)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal observation duration for patients who achieve complete resolution after a single epinephrine dose.
- Management of refractory anaphylaxis in patients concurrently taking beta-blockers (role of IV glucagon).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Route and site of epinephrine: intramuscular into the anterolateral vastus lateralis (superior absorption and faster peak plasma levels than subcutaneous or deltoid).
- Refractory anaphylaxis in a patient on propranolol: administer IV glucagon (1-5 mg over 5 min) which bypasses beta-receptors via adenylate cyclase stimulation.
- Discharge requirements: prescription of two epinephrine auto-injectors, personalized allergy action plan, and allergy clinic referral.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 22-year-old eating at a restaurant who develops acute tongue swelling, stridor, wheeze, and BP 80/50 mmHg 10 minutes after peanut ingestion.
- A patient experiencing anaphylaxis during amoxicillin injection who fails to respond to 3 doses of IM epinephrine; medical team initiates IV epinephrine infusion.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 33202997**: *World Allergy Organization Anaphylaxis Guidelines 2020 Update.* (2020) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 34564883**: *EAACI Guideline: Anaphylaxis (2021 Update).* (2021) [guideline] -

## [0128] Anaplastic thyroid cancer: rapid progression, multimodal treatment, dabrafenib

**Cluster**: anaplastic_thyroid_cancer | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Anaplastic thyroid carcinoma (ATC) is an ultra-aggressive solid malignancy presenting with a rapidly enlarging anterior neck mass, dysphagia, stridor, and vocal cord paralysis. Immediate airway assessment, contrast-enhanced CT neck/chest, core biopsy, and rapid molecular testing for BRAF V600E are required within 48-72 hours. For BRAF-mutated ATC, dabrafenib plus trametinib induces dramatic rapid tumor regression.

### Core Teaching Points
- American Thyroid Association (ATA) guidelines mandate urgent multidisciplinary evaluation, vocal cord assessment, and reflex BRAF V600E testing within 2-3 days of suspected ATC. (PMID 33729901)
- Combined targeted therapy with dabrafenib (BRAF inhibitor) and trametinib (MEK inhibitor) achieves an 69% objective response rate in BRAF V600E-mutated ATC (ROAR trial). (PMID 29198908)
- In resectable disease without distant metastases, radical surgical resection with clear margins followed by adjuvant intensity-modulated radiation therapy (IMRT) improves locoregional control. (PMID 33729901)
- For BRAF wild-type ATC, cytotoxic chemotherapy (paclitaxel plus carboplatin) combined with radiation therapy or checkpoint inhibitors is standard. (PMID 33729901)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Neoadjuvant dabrafenib/trametinib to convert initially unresectable BRAF-mutant ATC to complete surgical resection.
- Role of frontline PD-1 inhibitors (pembrolizumab) added to chemoradiation in BRAF wild-type disease.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Clinical presentation of ATC: elderly patient with rapidly growing rock-hard neck mass over weeks with hoarseness and dyspnea.
- Molecular profiling urgency: BRAF V600E mutation occurs in 40-50% of ATC and dictates immediate targeted therapy.
- Airway management priorities: avoid tracheostomy if possible unless acute imminent asphyxia, as it leads to tumor fungation and stoma bleeding.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 74-year-old female with a 5-week history of a rapidly expanding 6 cm neck mass, stridor, and right vocal cord paralysis on laryngoscopy.
- A patient with newly diagnosed ATC confirmed BRAF V600E positive on day 3; oncology initiates immediate dabrafenib-trametinib.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 33729901**: *2021 American Thyroid Association Guidelines for Management of Patients with Anaplastic Thyroid Cancer.* (2021) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 29198908**: *Dabrafenib and trametinib in patients with BRAF V600E-mutant anaplastic thyroid cancer: ROAR basket trial.* (2018) [trial] -

## [0129] ANCA-associated vasculitis: GPA, MPA, EGPA: classification, renal involvement, ANCA serology

**Cluster**: anca_associated_vasculitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
ANCA-associated vasculitis (AAV) encompasses Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis (GPA; PR3-ANCA), Microscopic Polyangiitis (MPA; MPO-ANCA), and Eosinophilic Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis (EGPA). Remission induction for organ- or life-threatening disease combines high-dose glucocorticoids with rituximab or cyclophosphamide. Avacopan (C5a receptor antagonist) enables significant glucocorticoid-sparing induction.

### Core Teaching Points
- EULAR 2022 recommendations establish rituximab (375 mg/m2 weekly x 4 or 1000 mg x 2) as equivalent to cyclophosphamide for induction and superior for relapsing disease. (PMID 36927642)
- Avacopan (30 mg twice daily), an oral complement C5a receptor antagonist, produces sustained remission at 52 weeks while significantly reducing glucocorticoid-related toxicity (ADVOCATE trial). (PMID 33596356)
- PEXIVAS trial demonstrated that routine plasma exchange does not reduce end-stage kidney disease or all-cause mortality, and a reduced-dose glucocorticoid regimen is non-inferior with fewer severe infections. (PMID 32050077)
- Maintenance of remission is best achieved with scheduled low-dose rituximab (500 mg every 6 months for at least 24-48 months) compared to azathioprine (MAINRITSAN trial). (PMID 25372087)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal total duration of rituximab maintenance therapy in PR3-ANCA positive patients with persistent ANCA positivity.
- Niche role of rescue plasma exchange in severe diffuse alveolar hemorrhage with mechanical ventilation.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Serological associations: GPA correlates predominantly with c-ANCA / PR3 antibodies; MPA correlates with p-ANCA / MPO antibodies.
- The ADVOCATE trial outcome: avacopan achieves non-inferior remission at week 26 and superior sustained remission at week 52 with higher eGFR recovery.
- Infection prophylaxis: trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole is mandatory during induction with rituximab or cyclophosphamide.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 58-year-old presenting with sinusitis, pulmonary cavitating nodules on CT, hematuria, and c-ANCA/PR3 titer > 1:320.
- A patient with MPA and rapidly rising creatinine (3.8 mg/dL) who receives rituximab and avacopan to minimize high-dose oral steroids.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36927642**: *EULAR recommendations for the management of ANCA-associated vasculitis: 2022 update.* (2023) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 33596356**: *Avacopan for the Treatment of ANCA-Associated Vasculitis (ADVOCATE Trial).* (2021) [trial] - 
- **PMID 32050077**: *Plasma Exchange and Glucocorticoid Dosing in Severe ANCA-Associated Vasculitis (PEXIVAS).* (2020) [trial] - 
- **PMID 25372087**: *Rituximab versus azathioprine for maintenance in ANCA-associated vasculitis (MAINRITSAN).* (2014) [trial] -

## [0132] Aneurysmal subarachnoid hemorrhage management

**Cluster**: subarachnoid_hemorrhage | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Aneurysmal subarachnoid hemorrhage (aSAH) is an acute neurosurgical emergency presenting with sudden thunderclap headache. Initial evaluation requires emergent non-contrast head CT within 6 hours (sensitivity > 99%); lumbar puncture for CSF xanthochromia is mandatory if CT is negative. Management prioritizes immediate aneurysm securing (endovascular coiling preferred), oral nimodipine for 21 days, and euvolemia to prevent delayed cerebral ischemia.

### Core Teaching Points
- AHA/ASA guidelines recommend immediate endovascular coiling over surgical clipping for ruptured aneurysms amenable to both modalities (ISAT trial). (PMID 16139655)
- Oral nimodipine (60 mg every 4 hours for 21 days) significantly reduces delayed cerebral ischemia and poor neurological outcome. (PMID 21748241)
- Blood pressure should be controlled with titratable IV infusions (labetalol, nicardipine) targeting SBP < 140-160 mmHg prior to aneurysm securing. (PMID 37216662)
- Maintenance of euvolemia is recommended; hypervolemic 'triple-H' therapy is no longer recommended due to cardiopulmonary complications. (PMID 37216662)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Continuous EEG monitoring for non-convulsive status epilepticus in comatose aSAH patients.
- Target mean arterial pressure and inotropic thresholds for treating confirmed symptomatic vasospasm.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Non-contrast head CT sensitivity within 6 hours vs requirement for lumbar puncture looking for spectrophotometric xanthochromia at 12 hours.
- Pharmacologic prevention of delayed cerebral ischemia: oral nimodipine is the only drug with proven neurological outcome benefit.
- Recognizing electrocardiographic manifestations of aSAH (neurogenic stunned myocardium: QT prolongation, inverted T waves, troponin leak).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 48-year-old presenting with sudden severe occipital headache 'like being hit by a bat', vomiting, and neck stiffness; head CT is normal at 9 hours post-onset.
- A coiled aSAH patient who develops new left-sided pronator drift and confusion on day 7 post-bleed (delayed cerebral ischemia).

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37216662**: *2023 Guideline for the Management of Patients With Aneurysmal Subarachnoid Hemorrhage (AHA/ASA).* (2023) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 16139655**: *International Subarachnoid Aneurysm Trial (ISAT) of neurosurgical clipping versus endovascular coiling.* (2005) [trial] - 
- **PMID 21748241**: *Critical care management of patients following aneurysmal subarachnoid hemorrhage: Neurocritical Care Society.* (2011) [guideline] -

## [0133] angioedema

**Cluster**: angioedema | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Angioedema is asymmetric swelling of the deep dermis and subcutaneous or submucosal tissues. It is critical to differentiate histamine-mediated angioedema (associated with urticaria/pruritus, responds to epinephrine, antihistamines, and steroids) from bradykinin-mediated angioedema (ACE-inhibitor induced or hereditary angioedema [HAE]). Bradykinin-mediated angioedema lacks urticaria and does NOT respond to antihistamines or steroids.

### Core Teaching Points
- WAO/EAACI guidelines recommend icatibant (bradykinin B2 receptor antagonist) or plasma-derived C1-inhibitor concentrate for acute attacks of hereditary angioedema. (PMID 34978809)
- ACE inhibitor-induced angioedema is caused by impaired bradykinin degradation; ACE inhibitors must be permanently discontinued, and ARBs should be avoided in acute severe airway presentation. (PMID 28203777)
- Hereditary angioedema (HAE) Type 1 (85%) has low C4 and low antigenic/functional C1-INH; Type 2 (15%) has low C4, normal antigenic but low functional C1-INH. (PMID 34978809)
- Emergent airway protection (fiberoptic intubation or cricothyroidotomy) takes precedence over pharmacotherapy in progressive laryngeal edema. (PMID 34978809)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Role of tranexamic acid and fresh frozen plasma in acute ACE-inhibitor angioedema when icatibant is unavailable.
- Safety of switching to ARBs or sacubitril/valsartan in patients with prior mild ACE-inhibitor angioedema.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Screening test of choice for HAE: serum C4 level (consistently low even during remission; sensitivity > 95%).
- Why steroids and epinephrine fail in bradykinin angioedema: pathology is vascular permeability driven by bradykinin B2 receptors.
- Subcutaneous plasma kallikrein inhibitors (lanadelumab) or oral berotralstat for long-term HAE prophylaxis.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 63-year-old taking enalapril who develops massive painless lip and tongue swelling without urticaria or wheezing.
- A 22-year-old with recurrent severe abdominal pain and hand swelling whose serum C4 is markedly reduced.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34978809**: *The international WAO/EAACI guideline for the management of hereditary angioedema - the 2021 revision.* (2022) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 28203777**: *ACE inhibitor-induced angioedema: a review of pathomechanisms and clinical management.* (2017) [review] -
