## [0134] angiotensin ii for catecholamine refractory vasodilatory shock

**Cluster**: vasodilatory_shock | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Catecholamine-refractory vasodilatory shock is characterized by persistent severe hypotension and inadequate organ perfusion despite high-dose norepinephrine and vasopressin. Synthetic human angiotensin II acts through the AT1 receptor, providing a non-adrenergic vasoconstrictor mechanism. Management includes early hydrocortisone, vasopressin (0.03 units/min), and angiotensin II in refractory shock.

### Core Teaching Points
- ATHOS-3 randomized trial demonstrated that synthetic human angiotensin II significantly increased mean arterial pressure and reduced background catecholamine requirements in vasodilatory shock. (PMID 28528561)
- Surviving Sepsis Campaign guidelines recommend adding vasopressin (0.03 U/min) rather than escalating norepinephrine to high levels (> 0.25 mcg/kg/min). (PMID 34599691)
- Patients with high baseline serum renin concentrations or acute kidney injury receiving renal replacement therapy experience the greatest survival benefit from angiotensin II. (PMID 31835948)
- Thromboembolic events occur more frequently with angiotensin II, warranting mandatory concurrent pharmacological venous thromboembolism prophylaxis. (PMID 28528561)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal physiological biomarkers for selecting patients who derive mortality benefit from angiotensin II.
- Sequencing of non-adrenergic vasopressors: vasopressin versus angiotensin II versus methylene blue.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The adrenergic sparing effect: adding vasopressin or angiotensin II prevents excessive beta-1 tachycardia and myocardial ischemia from extreme norepinephrine doses.
- Surviving Sepsis Campaign recommendation on corticosteroids: IV hydrocortisone 200 mg/day indicated only if shock persists despite norepinephrine >= 0.25 mcg/kg/min and vasopressin.
- Mechanism of action of angiotensin II: direct stimulation of vascular smooth muscle AT1 Gq-protein receptors causing calcium influx and vasoconstriction.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 55-year-old with septic shock requiring norepinephrine 0.5 mcg/kg/min, vasopressin 0.03 U/min, and epinephrine whose lactate is 6.2 mmol/L.
- An ICU patient with septic shock and dialysis-dependent AKI initiated on angiotensin II infusion.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 28528561**: *Angiotensin II for the Treatment of Vasodilatory Shock (ATHOS-3 Trial).* (2017) [trial] - 
- **PMID 34599691**: *Surviving Sepsis Campaign: International Guidelines for Management of Sepsis and Septic Shock 2021.* (2021) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 31835948**: *Angiotensin II in septic shock: current evidence and practical clinical use.* (2020) [review] -

## [0136] ankylosing spondylitis

**Cluster**: axial_spondyloarthritis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Ankylosing spondylitis (radiographic axial spondyloarthritis) is a chronic inflammatory arthritis predominantly affecting the axial skeleton and sacroiliac joints. It presents with inflammatory back pain (morning stiffness > 30 minutes, improvement with exercise, pain at night) in individuals < 45 years. First-line therapy is continuous full-dose NSAIDs; active disease refractory to NSAIDs is treated with TNF inhibitors or IL-17A inhibitors.

### Core Teaching Points
- ASAS/EULAR 2022 recommendations recommend continuous NSAIDs at maximum tolerated doses as first-line medical therapy for active axial spondyloarthritis. (PMID 36270658)
- TNF inhibitors (adalimumab, etanercept, infliximab, golimumab, certolizumab) or IL-17A inhibitors (secukinumab, ixekizumab) achieve significant disease reduction (ASAS40) in active AS. (PMID 36270658)
- Conventional synthetic DMARDs (methotrexate, sulfasalazine) are ineffective for pure axial spinal disease, although sulfasalazine is useful for prominent peripheral arthritis. (PMID 31441226)
- JAK inhibitors (upadacitinib, tofacitinib) are approved oral options for active radiographic and non-radiographic axSpA refractory to bDMARDs. (PMID 36270658)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Comparative effectiveness of IL-17A inhibitors vs TNF inhibitors on radiographic syndesmophyte progression.
- Role of MRI sacroiliitis monitoring in patients who achieve clinical remission.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Features of inflammatory vs mechanical back pain: age < 45, insidious onset, improvement with exercise, no improvement with rest, pain at night.
- Extra-skeletal manifestations of AS: acute anterior uveitis (most common, 30%), psoriasis, inflammatory bowel disease, and aortic regurgitation / conduction blocks.
- Radiographic hallmark: bilateral sacroiliitis, squaring of vertebral bodies, syndesmophytes ('bamboo spine').

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 28-year-old male with a 2-year history of lower back and buttock pain waking him at 4 AM, with morning stiffness lasting 90 minutes and elevated CRP.
- An AS patient with Crohn's disease whose rheumatologist selects adalimumab over secukinumab (since IL-17 inhibitors can exacerbate active IBD).

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36270658**: *2022 EULAR recommendations for the management of axial spondyloarthritis.* (2023) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 31441226**: *2019 ACR/SAA/SPARTAN Guideline for the Treatment of Axial Spondyloarthritis.* (2019) [guideline] -

## [0138] Anorectal Disorders

**Cluster**: anorectal_disorders | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Common benign anorectal disorders include hemorrhoidal disease, anal fissures, anorectal abscesses, and fistula-in-ano. Thrombosed external hemorrhoids present with acute severe pain and a bluish perianal lump (excised within 72h). Acute anal fissures present with tearing pain during defecation and bright red rectal bleeding (treated with topical nitroglycerin or diltiazem). Anorectal abscesses require prompt incision and drainage.

### Core Teaching Points
- ASCRS guidelines recommend dietary fiber supplementation (25-35g daily) and water intake as the cornerstone of conservative therapy for hemorrhoids. (PMID 29406450)
- Rubber band ligation is the most effective outpatient procedure for Grade I to III internal hemorrhoids that fail medical management. (PMID 29406450)
- Topical calcium channel blockers (2% diltiazem) or topical nitrates (0.4% nitroglycerin) are first-line for chronic anal fissure, healing 65-75% without fecal incontinence risk. (PMID 28059902)
- Anorectal abscesses require prompt surgical incision and drainage; routine antibiotics do not prevent fistula formation unless systemic sepsis or immunosuppression is present. (PMID 28059902)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Indications for botulinum toxin injection versus lateral internal sphincterotomy in chronic fissure refractory to topical CCBs.
- Optimal surgical approach for complex high transsphincteric anal fistulas (LIFT vs mucosal advancement flap).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Distinguishing internal hemorrhoids (above dentate line, painless bleeding/prolapse) from external hemorrhoids (below dentate line, exquisitely painful when thrombosed).
- Common side effect of topical 0.4% nitroglycerin ointment: severe throbbing headache (topical diltiazem has fewer systemic side effects).
- Parks classification and Goodsall rule for predicting the internal opening of a perianal fistula based on external opening location.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 31-year-old woman presenting with severe sharp anal pain during defecation described as 'passing broken glass' with blood on the toilet paper.
- A 45-year-old diabetic presenting with throbbing perianal pain, induration, and fever, diagnosed with an ischiorectal abscess.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 29406450**: *The American Society of Colon and Rectal Surgeons Clinical Practice Guidelines for the Management of Hemorrhoids.* (2018) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 28059902**: *ASCRS Clinical Practice Guideline for the Management of Anal Fissures.* (2017) [guideline] -

## [0139] Anterior Mediastinal Masses

**Cluster**: anterior_mediastinal_masses | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Anterior mediastinal masses encompass the classic '4 Ts': Thymoma/thymic carcinoma, Teratoma (germ cell tumors), Thyroid (substernal goiter), and Terrible lymphoma. Diagnostic workup mandates contrast-enhanced CT thorax, serum tumor markers (alpha-fetoprotein [AFP], beta-hCG, and LDH), assessment for myasthenia gravis (anti-AChR antibodies), and multidisciplinary planning prior to biopsy.

### Core Teaching Points
- Serum tumor markers (AFP and beta-hCG) must be drawn before any biopsy or invasive procedure to evaluate for non-seminomatous germ cell tumors. (PMID 37722422)
- Preoperative screening for anti-acetylcholine receptor (AChR) antibodies is mandatory in suspected thymoma to prevent post-intubation myasthenic crisis. (PMID 37722422)
- Primary mediastinal large B-cell lymphoma (PMLBCL) and classic Hodgkin lymphoma present with rapid bulky anterior masses in young adults, treated with DA-EPOCH-R or ABVD. (PMID 37217277)
- Complete surgical resection (thymectomy) is the mainstay of treatment for non-invasive and locally advanced resectable thymomas (Masaoka-Koga stage I-II). (PMID 37722422)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Indication for neoadjuvant chemotherapy in Masaoka-Koga stage III thymomas prior to en bloc resection.
- Airway management and risk of complete tracheobronchial collapse during general anesthesia in large anterior mediastinal masses.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The '4 Ts' differential diagnosis of anterior mediastinal lesions: Thymoma, Teratoma/GCT, Thyroid goiter, Terrible lymphoma.
- Marker interpretation: elevated AFP is diagnostic of non-seminomatous GCT (yolk sac element); isolated elevated beta-hCG occurs in seminoma or choriocarcinoma.
- Myasthenia gravis association: 30-50% of thymoma patients have myasthenia gravis; 10-15% of myasthenia patients have a thymoma.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 34-year-old male with a 9 cm anterior mediastinal mass on chest radiography whose serum beta-hCG is 24,000 mIU/mL and AFP is 850 ng/mL.
- A 49-year-old woman with ptosis and diplopia found to have a well-circumscribed 4 cm anterior mediastinal mass on contrast CT.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37722422**: *NCCN Clinical Practice Guidelines in Oncology: Thymomas and Thymic Carcinomas.* (2023) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 37217277**: *NCCN Guidelines: B-Cell Lymphomas.* (2023) [guideline] -

## [0140] Anthracycline Cardiotoxicity

**Cluster**: anthracycline_cardiotoxicity | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Anthracycline-induced cardiotoxicity (doxorubicin, epirubicin) causes cumulative dose-dependent myocardial injury through topoisomerase II-beta inhibition, reactive oxygen species generation, and irreversible cardiomyocyte apoptosis. Surveillance relies on baseline and serial echocardiography with global longitudinal strain (GLS). Cardioprotection with dexrazoxane or neurohormonal blockade (ACEi/beta-blockers) is indicated in high-risk patients.

### Core Teaching Points
- ESC 2022 Cardio-Oncology guidelines define cancer therapy-related cardiac dysfunction (CTRCD) by a decrease in LVEF >= 10 percentage points to < 50%, or a relative reduction in global longitudinal strain (GLS) > 15%. (PMID 36017568)
- Cumulative lifetime doxorubicin dose is the strongest risk factor: heart failure incidence rises sharply above 400-500 mg/m2. (PMID 27918721)
- Dexrazoxane (iron-chelating topoisomerase II-beta inhibitor) significantly reduces anthracycline-induced cardiotoxicity without compromising oncological efficacy in high-risk patients. (PMID 36017568)
- Guideline-directed neurohormonal blockade (carvedilol, enalapril) initiated for early subclinical strain deterioration or asymptomatic LVEF drop prevents progression to clinical heart failure. (PMID 36017568)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Cardioprotective efficacy of primary prophylactic SGLT2 inhibitors or statins alongside anthracycline chemotherapy.
- Long-term monitoring frequency in childhood cancer survivors who received anthracyclines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Global longitudinal strain (GLS) utility: relative drop of > 15% from baseline detects subclinical cardiotoxicity before conventional LVEF declines.
- Dexrazoxane mechanism: selectively prevents topoisomerase II-beta complex formation and chelates intracellular iron, reducing free radical production.
- Anthracycline lifetime maximum cumulative dose thresholds: doxorubicin (450-500 mg/m2), epirubicin (900 mg/m2).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 52-year-old woman undergoing dose-dense AC chemotherapy for breast cancer whose routine 3-month echo shows LVEF drop from 62% to 46%.
- A patient who has received 400 mg/m2 doxorubicin for Hodgkin lymphoma showing a 18% relative decline in GLS despite LVEF 54%.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36017568**: *2022 ESC Guidelines on cardio-oncology.* (2022) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 27918721**: *Prevention and Monitoring of Cardiac Dysfunction in Survivors of Adult Cancers: ASCO Clinical Practice Guideline.* (2017) [guideline] - 

---

## [0141] Antibiotic prophylaxis for infective endocarditis: high-risk procedures and patients

**Cluster**: infective_endocarditis_prophylaxis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Antibiotic prophylaxis for infective endocarditis (IE) is restricted to patients at the highest risk of adverse outcomes undergoing high-risk dental procedures involving manipulation of the gingival tissue, periapical region of teeth, or perforation of oral mucosa. High-risk conditions include prosthetic heart valves, prosthetic valve repair materials, prior IE, and cyanotic congenital heart disease. The standard prophylactic regimen is a single oral dose of amoxicillin 2g 30-60 minutes pre-procedure.

### Core Teaching Points
- ESC 2023 guidelines restrict antibiotic prophylaxis strictly to high-risk cardiac conditions undergoing dental procedures involving gingival or periapical manipulation. (PMID 37622656)
- High-risk cardiac categories: prosthetic valves (transcatheter or surgical), prosthetic rings/cords, previous IE, and unrepaired cyanotic congenital heart disease. (PMID 33882677)
- Standard regimen: oral amoxicillin 2g (or ampicillin 2g IV) administered 30-60 minutes before the procedure; for severe penicillin allergy, cephalexin 2g, azithromycin 500mg, or doxycycline 100mg. (PMID 37622656)
- Prophylaxis is NOT recommended for gastrointestinal, urological, respiratory, or routine skin procedures in the absence of established local infection. (PMID 37622656)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Role of clindamycin in penicillin-allergic patients (AHA no longer recommends clindamycin due to fatal Clostridioides difficile risk).
- Effectiveness of oral chlorhexidine mouth rinses versus systemic antibiotics in reducing transient bacteremia.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Identifying high-risk cardiac conditions qualifying for prophylaxis: prosthetic valve, prior endocarditis, unrepaired cyanotic CHD (native valve disease like mitral valve prolapse does NOT qualify).
- Procedures requiring prophylaxis: tooth extraction, periodontal scaling, dental implant placement; NOT routine local anesthetic injections, dental radiographs, or suture removal.
- First-line oral drug and timing: amoxicillin 2g single dose taken 30-60 minutes before the procedure.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 64-year-old with a mechanical aortic valve presenting for dental scaling asking if he needs antibiotic prophylaxis.
- A patient with mitral valve prolapse with moderate mitral regurgitation asking why his dentist no longer prescribes prophylactic amoxicillin.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37622656**: *2023 ESC Guidelines for the management of endocarditis.* (2023) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 33882677**: *Prevention of Viridans Group Streptococcal Infective Endocarditis: An Update to the AHA Scientific Statement.* (2021) [guideline] -

## [0142] antibiotic stewardship

**Cluster**: antimicrobial_stewardship | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Antimicrobial stewardship programs (ASPs) optimize clinical outcomes while minimizing unintended consequences of antimicrobial use, including toxicity, selection of pathogenic organisms (such as Clostridioides difficile), and the emergence of antimicrobial resistance. Core strategies include pre-authorization, prospective audit with feedback, evidence-based guidelines, and diagnostic stewardship.

### Core Teaching Points
- CDC and IDSA guidelines emphasize prospective audit and feedback as the most effective stewardship intervention to optimize antibiotic prescribing. (PMID 27075735)
- Implementing hospital-based ASPs significantly reduces overall antibiotic consumption, hospital-onset Clostridioides difficile infection, and institutional resistance rates. (PMID 28369344)
- Diagnostic stewardship ensures appropriate collection of microbiological cultures before antimicrobial initiation, preventing contaminated or unindicated blood and urine cultures. (PMID 27075735)
- Facility-specific antibiograms updated annually guide empirical therapy based on local pathogen susceptibility patterns rather than national estimates. (PMID 27075735)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Effectiveness of machine-learning-driven clinical decision support systems in reducing broad-spectrum prescribing in emergency departments.
- Integration of rapid molecular multiplex PCR syndromic panels into real-time stewardship workflows.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- CDC Core Elements of Hospital Antibiotic Stewardship: Leadership Commitment, Accountability, Pharmacy Expertise, Action, Tracking, Reporting, and Education.
- Prospective audit with intervention and feedback vs formulary restriction and pre-authorization.
- Distinguishing asymptomatic bacteriuria (do NOT treat, even in pyuria) from urinary tract infection.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A hospital ward where broad-spectrum meropenem use has doubled, prompting an ASP prospective audit-feedback intervention.
- An elderly resident with an indwelling Foley catheter found to have cloudy urine with 50 WBC/hpf but no fever or symptoms; ASP advises withholding antibiotics.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27075735**: *Implementing an Antibiotic Stewardship Program: Guidelines by IDSA and SHEA.* (2016) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 28369344**: *Effect of antimicrobial stewardship interventions: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2017) [meta-analysis] - 

---

### Case Generation Hooks
- A hospital ward where broad-spectrum meropenem use has doubled, prompting an ASP prospective audit-feedback intervention.
- An elderly resident with an indwelling Foley catheter found to have cloudy urine with 50 WBC/hpf but no fever or symptoms; ASP advises withholding antibiotics.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

## [0144] Antibiotic stewardship in critical care: de-escalation and duration

**Cluster**: antimicrobial_stewardship | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Antimicrobial stewardship programs (ASPs) optimize clinical outcomes while minimizing unintended consequences of antimicrobial use, including toxicity, selection of pathogenic organisms (such as Clostridioides difficile), and the emergence of antimicrobial resistance. Core strategies include pre-authorization, prospective audit with feedback, evidence-based guidelines, and diagnostic stewardship.

### Core Teaching Points
- CDC and IDSA guidelines emphasize prospective audit and feedback as the most effective stewardship intervention to optimize antibiotic prescribing. (PMID 27075735)
- Implementing hospital-based ASPs significantly reduces overall antibiotic consumption, hospital-onset Clostridioides difficile infection, and institutional resistance rates. (PMID 28369344)
- Diagnostic stewardship ensures appropriate collection of microbiological cultures before antimicrobial initiation, preventing contaminated or unindicated blood and urine cultures. (PMID 27075735)
- Facility-specific antibiograms updated annually guide empirical therapy based on local pathogen susceptibility patterns rather than national estimates. (PMID 27075735)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Effectiveness of machine-learning-driven clinical decision support systems in reducing broad-spectrum prescribing in emergency departments.
- Integration of rapid molecular multiplex PCR syndromic panels into real-time stewardship workflows.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- CDC Core Elements of Hospital Antibiotic Stewardship: Leadership Commitment, Accountability, Pharmacy Expertise, Action, Tracking, Reporting, and Education.
- Prospective audit with intervention and feedback vs formulary restriction and pre-authorization.
- Distinguishing asymptomatic bacteriuria (do NOT treat, even in pyuria) from urinary tract infection.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A hospital ward where broad-spectrum meropenem use has doubled, prompting an ASP prospective audit-feedback intervention.
- An elderly resident with an indwelling Foley catheter found to have cloudy urine with 50 WBC/hpf but no fever or symptoms; ASP advises withholding antibiotics.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27075735**: *Implementing an Antibiotic Stewardship Program: Guidelines by IDSA and SHEA.* (2016) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 28369344**: *Effect of antimicrobial stewardship interventions: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2017) [meta-analysis] -

## [0145] Antibody-Drug Conjugates

**Cluster**: antibody_drug_conjugates | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Antibody-drug conjugates (ADCs) deliver potent cytotoxic payloads directly to tumor cells expressing specific surface antigens using a targeted monoclonal antibody, cleavable or non-cleavable linker, and chemotherapeutic payload (e.g. topoisomerase I inhibitors, auristatins, calicheamicins). Landmark examples include trastuzumab deruxtecan (T-DXd) in HER2+ and HER2-low cancers, and enfortumab vedotin in urothelial cancer.

### Core Teaching Points
- Trastuzumab deruxtecan (T-DXd) demonstrated significant progression-free and overall survival improvements over standard therapy in HER2-low metastatic breast cancer (DESTINY-Breast04). (PMID 35665782)
- Enfortumab vedotin (targeting Nectin-4) combined with pembrolizumab established a new first-line standard of care in advanced urothelial carcinoma (EV-302 trial). (PMID 38470982)
- Drug-induced interstitial lung disease (ILD) / pneumonitis is a class-defining toxicity of topoisomerase I payload ADCs (T-DXd, sacituzumab govitecan), requiring routine CT surveillance and prompt steroid treatment. (PMID 35665782)
- The 'bystander killing effect' occurs when membrane-permeable cleaved cytotoxic payloads diffuse into adjacent antigen-negative tumor cells, overcoming intratumoral target heterogeneity. (PMID 34185202)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Management and re-challenge safety criteria following grade 1 vs grade >= 2 ADC-induced pneumonitis.
- Mechanisms of acquired resistance to ADCs: antigen down-regulation vs drug-efflux pump up-regulation.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Three structural components of an ADC: monoclonal antibody, chemical linker, and cytotoxic payload.
- Bystander effect mechanism: uncharged lipophilic payloads cross cell membranes to kill adjacent tumor cells lacking the target antigen.
- Monitoring for T-DXd-induced interstitial lung disease: any new cough, dyspnea, or fever warrants immediate high-resolution CT chest and withholding drug.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 56-year-old woman with metastatic HER2-low (IHC 1+) breast cancer experiencing progression on endocrine therapy, evaluated for T-DXd.
- A patient receiving T-DXd who develops dry cough; chest HRCT shows bilateral ground-glass opacities, diagnosed with Grade 2 drug-induced pneumonitis.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 35665782**: *Trastuzumab Deruxtecan in Previously Treated HER2-Low Advanced Breast Cancer (DESTINY-Breast04).* (2022) [trial] - 
- **PMID 38470982**: *Enfortumab Vedotin and Pembrolizumab in Untreated Advanced Urothelial Cancer (EV-302).* (2024) [trial] - 
- **PMID 34185202**: *ESMO Clinical Practice Guideline on Antibody-Drug Conjugates in Solid Tumors.* (2021) [guideline] -

## [0146] Anti-CD20 Therapy in Rheumatology

**Cluster**: anti_cd20_therapy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Rituximab is a chimeric monoclonal antibody targeting CD20 on pre-B and mature B lymphocytes, mediating complement-dependent cytotoxicity and antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity. In rheumatology and nephrology, rituximab is standard of care for rheumatoid arthritis refractory to anti-TNF agents, ANCA-associated vasculitis, systemic lupus erythematosus, and primary membranous nephropathy.

### Core Teaching Points
- ACR guidelines recommend rituximab in rheumatoid arthritis patients with inadequate response to TNF inhibitors, particularly in seropositive (RF/anti-CCP positive) disease. (PMID 33444002)
- Rituximab is standard first-line remission induction therapy for primary membranous nephropathy, demonstrating superior long-term remission rates compared to cyclosporine (MENTOR trial). (PMID 31269363)
- Universal screening for hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg) and anti-HBc total antibodies is mandatory prior to rituximab due to high risk of fatal hepatitis B reactivation. (PMID 31504429)
- Serum quantitative immunoglobulins (IgG/IgM) should be monitored at baseline and prior to each retreat course to detect persistent secondary hypogammaglobulinemia. (PMID 33444002)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal interval for rituximab maintenance dosing: fixed 6-month interval versus B-cell repopulation (CD19/CD20) monitoring.
- Safety and efficacy of subcutaneous versus intravenous rituximab formulations in autoimmune disease.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Hepatitis B reactivation prophylaxis: any patient with positive HBsAg or positive anti-HBc requires antiviral prophylaxis (entecavir or tenofovir) during and for >= 12-18 months after rituximab.
- Vaccination timing: all required non-live vaccines (pneumococcal, COVID-19, influenza) must be administered at least 2-4 weeks prior to rituximab infusion.
- Differential response in seropositive vs seronegative RA: seropositive patients (RF/anti-CCP+) have significantly higher ACR20/50 response rates.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 52-year-old woman with severe seropositive RA failing adalimumab and tofacitinib initiated on rituximab.
- A nephrotic patient with anti-PLA2R positive membranous nephropathy enrolled for rituximab induction in the MENTOR regimen.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 33444002**: *2021 American College of Rheumatology Guideline for the Treatment of Rheumatoid Arthritis.* (2021) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 31269363**: *Rituximab or Cyclosporine in the Treatment of Membranous Nephropathy (MENTOR Trial).* (2019) [trial] - 
- **PMID 31504429**: *AASLD Practice Guidelines: Prevention of Hepatitis B Reactivation.* (2019) [guideline] -

## [0147] anticoagulation for atrial fibrillation in chronic kidney disease

**Cluster**: anticoagulation_in_ckd | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Atrial fibrillation is highly prevalent in patients with chronic kidney disease (CKD), significantly increasing stroke and bleeding risks. Direct oral anticoagulants (DOACs: apixaban, rivaroxaban, edoxaban, dabigatran) provide superior or non-inferior stroke prevention compared to warfarin in mild-to-moderate CKD (CrCl 30-50 mL/min). In severe CKD (CrCl 15-29 mL/min) and dialysis, apixaban is increasingly utilized with specific dose reduction criteria.

### Core Teaching Points
- KDIGO 2023 guidelines recommend DOACs over vitamin K antagonists (warfarin) in patients with non-valvular AF and CKD Stages 1-3, and consider DOACs in Stage 4 (CrCl 15-29 mL/min). (PMID 37839958)
- Apixaban requires dose reduction to 2.5 mg BID if the patient meets at least two of the following criteria: age >= 80 years, body weight <= 60 kg, or serum creatinine >= 1.5 mg/dL (133 umol/L). (PMID 21875378)
- Warfarin in advanced CKD and dialysis accelerates vascular calcification and calcific uremic arteriolopathy (calciphylaxis) by inhibiting vitamin K-dependent matrix Gla protein. (PMID 39213192)
- Dabigatran is 80% renally eliminated and is contraindicated when CrCl < 30 mL/min in European guidelines. (PMID 39213192)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Net clinical benefit of anticoagulation in end-stage kidney disease on maintenance hemodialysis.
- Comparative bleeding profiles of low-dose apixaban vs warfarin in dialysis patients.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The 2-out-of-3 dose reduction criteria for apixaban: Age >= 80, Weight <= 60 kg, Creatinine >= 1.5 mg/dL.
- Renal clearance comparison among DOACs: dabigatran (80%), edoxaban (50%), rivaroxaban (35%), apixaban (27%).
- Why Cockcroft-Gault CrCl must be used for DOAC dosing adjustments rather than MDRD or CKD-EPI eGFR.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An 82-year-old female weighing 54 kg with AF and serum creatinine 1.7 mg/dL evaluated for apixaban dosing.
- A hemodialysis patient with persistent AF and prior warfarin-induced skin necrosis where apixaban 2.5 mg BID is initiated.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37839958**: *KDIGO 2023 Clinical Practice Guideline for the Management of Cardiovascular Disease in Chronic Kidney Disease.* (2023) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 21875378**: *Apixaban versus Warfarin in Patients with Atrial Fibrillation (ARISTOTLE Trial).* (2011) [trial] - 
- **PMID 39213192**: *2024 ESC Guidelines for the management of atrial fibrillation.* (2024) [guideline] -

## [0148] Anticoagulation for mechanical heart valves

**Cluster**: anticoagulation_mechanical_valves | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Mechanical prosthetic heart valves mandate lifelong oral anticoagulation with vitamin K antagonists (warfarin) to prevent catastrophic valve thrombosis and thromboembolic stroke. Direct oral anticoagulants (DOACs) are strictly contraindicated based on the RE-ALIGN trial, which demonstrated significantly higher thromboembolic and bleeding events with dabigatran.

### Core Teaching Points
- AHA/ACC and ESC guidelines establish vitamin K antagonists (warfarin) as the only approved anticoagulation for mechanical heart valves; DOACs are absolutely contraindicated. (PMID 34453161)
- The RE-ALIGN trial testing dabigatran in mechanical heart valves was halted early due to an excess of ischemic strokes, valve thrombosis, and major bleeding. (PMID 23991661)
- Target INR for mechanical aortic valves without thromboembolic risk factors is 2.5 (range 2.0-3.0); target INR for mechanical mitral valves or aortic valves with risk factors is 3.0 (range 2.5-3.5). (PMID 34453161)
- Low-dose aspirin (75-100 mg daily) is recommended in addition to warfarin only if there is a concomitant clinical indication (e.g. established atherosclerotic disease). (PMID 34453161)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Management of subtherapeutic INR in mechanical valves: outpatient oral vitamin K adjustment vs bridging LMWH.
- Optimal anticoagulation intensity for newer-generation bileaflet mechanical aortic valves.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Target INR ranges: Mechanical mitral = 2.5-3.5; Mechanical aortic = 2.0-3.0 (2.5-3.5 if AF or prior thromboembolism).
- Why DOACs fail in mechanical valves: high shear stress activates contact pathway factor XII and triggers local thrombin generation.
- Suspected mechanical valve thrombosis presentation: new muffled clicks, progressive dyspnea, new murmur, high transvalvular gradient.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 44-year-old with a mechanical mitral valve who requests switching from warfarin to apixaban because he dislikes INR blood tests.
- A patient with a mechanical aortic valve whose routine INR is 1.4; management with dose titration and therapeutic LMWH bridging.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34453161**: *2021 ESC/EACTS Guidelines for the management of valvular heart disease.* (2021) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 23991661**: *Dabigatran versus Warfarin in Patients with Mechanical Heart Valves (RE-ALIGN).* (2013) [trial] -

## [0149] Anticoagulation in atrial fibrillation: CHA2DS2-VASc scoring and DOAC selection

**Cluster**: anticoagulation_in_atrial_fibrillation | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Stroke prevention in non-valvular atrial fibrillation is guided by the CHA2DS2-VASc risk score. Oral anticoagulation is recommended for men with score >= 2 and women with score >= 3, and should be considered for men with score 1 and women with score 2. Direct oral anticoagulants (DOACs: apixaban, rivaroxaban, dabigatran, edoxaban) are preferred over warfarin due to a 50% reduction in intracranial hemorrhage.

### Core Teaching Points
- ESC 2024 guidelines recommend DOACs over warfarin as first-line stroke prevention for non-valvular atrial fibrillation (Class I, Level A). (PMID 39213192)
- CHA2DS2-VASc score assigns points: CHF (1), HTN (1), Age >= 75 (2), Diabetes (1), Stroke/TIA/thromboembolism (2), Vascular disease (1), Age 65-74 (1), Sex female (1). (PMID 39213192)
- Female sex is a risk modifier rather than an independent risk factor; a female with no other risk factors (CHA2DS2-VASc = 1) does NOT require anticoagulation. (PMID 39213192)
- Meta-analysis of landmark DOAC trials demonstrates a 19% relative reduction in stroke/systemic embolism and a 51% reduction in hemorrhagic stroke compared to warfarin. (PMID 24315366)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Indications for left atrial appendage occlusion in patients with absolute contraindications to long-term oral anticoagulation.
- Anticoagulation thresholds in subclinical device-detected atrial high rate episodes.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Components of CHA2DS2-VASc: 2 points each for Age >= 75 and prior Stroke/TIA; 1 point for CHF, HTN, DM, Vascular disease, Age 65-74, Female sex.
- Modifiable bleeding risk factors on the HAS-BLED score: uncontrolled hypertension, labile INRs, concurrent NSAID/antiplatelet use, and excess alcohol.
- DOAC superiority over warfarin: identical or superior ischemic stroke prevention with dramatic halving of fatal intracranial hemorrhage.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old male with hypertension and newly diagnosed paroxysmal atrial fibrillation (CHA2DS2-VASc = 2) initiated on DOAC.
- A 28-year-old female with lone atrial fibrillation and no comorbidities (CHA2DS2-VASc = 1 solely for sex) who does not require anticoagulation.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39213192**: *2024 ESC Guidelines for the management of atrial fibrillation.* (2024) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 24315366**: *Comparison of the efficacy and safety of new oral anticoagulants with warfarin: a meta-analysis of randomised trials.* (2014) [meta-analysis] -

## [0150] Anticoagulation in Malignancy

**Cluster**: cancer_associated_thrombosis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Cancer-associated thrombosis (CAT) carries high risks of both recurrent venous thromboembolism and major bleeding. International guidelines recommend direct oral anticoagulants (apixaban, rivaroxaban, edoxaban) or low-molecular-weight heparin (LMWH) for at least 6 months. DOACs are preferred for most patients, but LMWH is favored in unresected gastrointestinal or genitourinary luminal cancers with high mucosal bleeding risk.

### Core Teaching Points
- ASCO guidelines recommend DOACs (apixaban, rivaroxaban, edoxaban) as preferred first-line therapy for acute VTE in cancer patients without high mucosal bleeding risk. (PMID 33099616)
- In patients with unresected gastrointestinal or genitourinary luminal malignancies, LMWH is preferred over DOACs due to increased GI mucosal bleeding. (PMID 32252192)
- The CARAVAGGIO trial demonstrated that oral apixaban was non-inferior to subcutaneous dalteparin for recurrent VTE in cancer patients without an increase in major bleeding. (PMID 32223112)
- Anticoagulation should be continued beyond 6 months as long as the cancer remains active, the patient is receiving systemic chemotherapy, or metastatic disease persists. (PMID 33099616)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Safety and dosing of DOACs during severe chemotherapy-induced thrombocytopenia (platelets 25,000-50,000/uL).
- Optimal long-term dose reduction after 6 months of full-dose CAT treatment.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Choosing between DOAC and LMWH in CAT: DOACs for non-GI cancers; LMWH preferred in unresected gastric, esophageal, or colorectal cancers.
- Dosing adjustments for acute VTE in cancer during thrombocytopenia: hold if platelets < 25,000/uL; reduce LMWH to 50% dose if platelets 25,000-50,000/uL.
- Drug-drug interactions: screening DOACs against antineoplastic agents with strong CYP3A4 and P-glycoprotein inducers or inhibitors.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 61-year-old with metastatic pancreatic adenocarcinoma diagnosed with acute pulmonary embolism initiated on enoxaparin.
- A patient with breast cancer on adjuvant chemotherapy developing acute DVT successfully treated with oral apixaban.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 33099616**: *Antithrombotic Therapy in Patients With Cancer: ASCO Guideline Update.* (2020) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 32223112**: *Apixaban for the Treatment of Venous Thromboembolism in Patients with Cancer (CARAVAGGIO Trial).* (2020) [trial] - 
- **PMID 32252192**: *Oral direct factor Xa inhibitors versus low-molecular-weight heparin for cancer-associated thrombosis: a systematic review.* (2020) [systematic review] -

## [0151] anticoagulation in pregnancy

**Cluster**: anticoagulation_in_pregnancy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Pregnancy is a prothrombotic state with a 4- to 5-fold increased risk of venous thromboembolism. Low-molecular-weight heparin (LMWH: enoxaparin, dalteparin) is the anticoagulant of choice throughout pregnancy and the postpartum period because it does not cross the placenta. Warfarin is teratogenic in the first trimester (warfarin embryopathy), and DOACs cross the placenta and are strictly contraindicated.

### Core Teaching Points
- ACOG and RCOG guidelines mandate LMWH as the anticoagulant of choice for prevention and treatment of VTE in pregnancy and lactation. (PMID 31504429)
- Warfarin crosses the placental barrier and causes warfarin embryopathy (nasal hypoplasia, stippled epiphyses, CNS defects) when taken between 6 and 12 weeks gestation. (PMID 31504429)
- Direct oral anticoagulants (DOACs) cross the placenta, have documented embryofetal toxicities in animal models, and are strictly contraindicated in pregnancy. (PMID 39213192)
- Peripartum management: therapeutic LMWH must be held at least 24 hours (and prophylactic LMWH held 12 hours) prior to planned induction or neuraxial anesthesia. (PMID 31504429)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Target peak anti-Xa monitoring thresholds in pregnant women with high-risk mechanical heart valves.
- Management of anticoagulation in women with mechanical valves desiring pregnancy.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Why LMWH does not cross the placenta: high molecular weight and negative charge prevent transplacental transfer.
- Neuraxial anesthesia safety window: minimum 24 hours after last therapeutic dose of LMWH to prevent catastrophic epidural hematoma.
- Postpartum anticoagulation duration: continue for at least 6 weeks postpartum, for a total minimum duration of 3 months.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 29-year-old woman at 10 weeks gestation presenting with acute left lower extremity deep vein thrombosis.
- A pregnant woman on therapeutic enoxaparin 80 mg BID who presents in spontaneous labor 8 hours after her morning injection; anesthesia team defers epidural.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31504429**: *ACOG Practice Bulletin No. 196: Thromboembolism in Pregnancy.* (2018) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 39213192**: *2024 ESC Guidelines for the management of atrial fibrillation (Pregnancy Section).* (2024) [guideline] -

## [0153] anticoagulation reversal

**Cluster**: anticoagulation_reversal | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Management of acute life-threatening bleeding or urgent surgery in anticoagulated patients requires specific reversal agents and supportive hemostatic resuscitation. Idarucizumab reverses dabigatran; andexanet alfa reverses apixaban and rivaroxaban; 4-factor prothrombin complex concentrate (4F-PCC) combined with IV vitamin K reverses warfarin (target INR < 1.5). Protamine sulfate partially reverses unfractionated and low-molecular-weight heparin.

### Core Teaching Points
- AHA/ACC and CHEST guidelines recommend 4-factor prothrombin complex concentrate (4F-PCC) over fresh frozen plasma for emergent warfarin reversal due to faster INR normalization and lower volume overload. (PMID 34380720)
- Idarucizumab (5g IV in two divided doses) produces immediate, complete, and sustained reversal of dabigatran anticoagulation within minutes (RE-VERSE AD trial). (PMID 28693366)
- Andexanet alfa reverses factor Xa inhibitors (apixaban and rivaroxaban), achieving rapid reduction in anti-factor Xa activity and effective hemostasis in acute major bleeding (ANNEXA-4 trial). (PMID 30730767)
- Intravenous vitamin K (5-10 mg by slow infusion) must always be co-administered with 4F-PCC to sustain vitamin K-dependent clotting factor synthesis once PCC factors clear. (PMID 34380720)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Thromboembolic risks and optimal timing for restarting oral anticoagulation after major intracranial hemorrhage reversal.
- Role of off-label 4F-PCC (50 U/kg) vs andexanet alfa in factor Xa inhibitor major bleeding.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Specific antidote pairing: Idarucizumab for Dabigatran; Andexanet alfa for Apixaban/Rivaroxaban.
- 4F-PCC composition: concentrated factors II, VII, IX, X and proteins C and S.
- Protamine dosing for heparin reversal: 1 mg protamine neutralizes 100 units unfractionated heparin.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 72-year-old taking apixaban who suffers a head strike with acute subdural hematoma requiring emergency craniotomy.
- A patient on dabigatran presenting with massive upper GI hemorrhage and hemodynamic instability who receives 5g IV idarucizumab.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34380720**: *Antithrombotic Therapy for VTE Disease: CHEST Guideline and Expert Panel Report.* (2021) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 28693366**: *Idarucizumab for Dabigatran Reversal - Full Cohort Analysis (RE-VERSE AD).* (2017) [trial] - 
- **PMID 30730767**: *Full Study Report of Andexanet Alfa for Bleeding Associated with Factor Xa Inhibitors (ANNEXA-4).* (2019) [trial] -

## [0155] antidepressant sequential treatment steps depressed outpatients outcomes

**Cluster**: antidepressant_treatment_steps | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Major depressive disorder (MDD) requires systematic stepwise pharmacotherapy guided by measurement-based care (e.g. PHQ-9). The landmark STAR*D trial demonstrated that approximately 33% of patients achieve remission with initial SSRI monotherapy (Step 1). Subsequent steps—switching classes (to bupropion, venlafaxine) or augmenting with bupropion, buspirone, lithium, or second-generation antipsychotics—achieve cumulative remission rates up to 67%.

### Core Teaching Points
- The STAR*D trial established that sequential treatment steps for treatment-resistant depression produce cumulative remission rates approaching 67%. (PMID 16428318)
- APA practice guidelines recommend evaluating antidepressant response at 4-8 weeks before modifying therapy (switching vs augmenting). (PMID 20964149)
- Augmentation of an SSRI/SNRI with an atypical antipsychotic (aripiprazole, quetiapine) or bupropion produces higher response rates than switching monotherapy in partial responders. (PMID 20964149)
- Ketamine and esketamine nasal spray provide rapid-onset antidepressant effects in treatment-resistant depression with acute suicidal ideation. (PMID 31109228)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Comparative long-term durability of remission achieved in Step 3/4 versus Step 1 in treatment-resistant depression.
- Precision biomarkers for predicting SSRI vs SNRI vs dopaminergic response.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- STAR*D remission progression: Step 1 citalopram (33%), Step 2 switch/augment (31% of remaining), Step 3 (14%), Step 4 (13%).
- Switching vs Augmenting: Augmentation is preferred when there is partial response; switching is preferred when there is zero response or intolerable adverse effects.
- Discontinuation syndrome features: FINISH mnemonic.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 34-year-old with major depression who has had only 15% symptom reduction after 8 weeks of therapeutic sertraline.
- A depressed patient with partial response to escitalopram but persistent anhedonia and fatigue who is augmented with bupropion XL.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 16428318**: *Acute and longer-term outcomes in depressed outpatients requiring one or several treatment steps: a STAR*D report.* (2006) [trial] - 
- **PMID 20964149**: *Practice Guideline for the Treatment of Patients With Major Depressive Disorder (APA).* (2010) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 31109228**: *Efficacy and Safety of Flexibly Dosed Esketamine Nasal Spray Combined With a Newly Initiated Oral Antidepressant in Treatment-Resistant Depression (TRANSFORM-2).* (2019) [trial] - 

---

## [0156] antiepileptic drug choice

**Cluster**: antiepileptic_drug_choice | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Antiseizure medication (ASM) selection is determined by seizure classification (focal vs generalized vs unclassified), patient demographics (age, childbearing potential), comorbidity profiles, and potential drug-drug interactions. Broad-spectrum ASMs (levetiracetam, lamotrigine, valproate, topiramate) are effective for both focal and generalized seizures. Sodium channel blockers (carbamazepine, phenytoin) treat focal seizures but can aggravate generalized absence or myoclonic seizures.

### Core Teaching Points
- SANAD II trials established lamotrigine as first-line for focal epilepsy, and valproate as superior to levetiracetam for generalized epilepsy in males. (PMID 33894145)
- Sodium valproate is strictly contraindicated in women of childbearing potential due to high teratogenicity (neural tube defects) and neurodevelopmental delays. (PMID 35671338)
- Narrow-spectrum sodium channel blockers (carbamazepine, oxcarbazepine, phenytoin) can exacerbate generalized absence and juvenile myoclonic epilepsy. (PMID 35671338)
- Levetiracetam has low drug interaction potential and broad efficacy, but requires monitoring for behavioral changes, agitation, and depressive mood swings. (PMID 33894145)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Role of third-generation ASMs (cenobamate, brivaracetam) in highly drug-resistant focal epilepsy.
- Timing and safety criteria for planned ASM withdrawal in adults seizure-free for >= 2 years.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Seizure type mismatch: carbamazepine, phenytoin, and gabapentin worsen absence and myoclonic seizures in idiopathic generalized epilepsy.
- Valproate pregnancy prevention programme: strict requirement for effective contraception and annual risk acknowledgment forms in female patients.
- Lamotrigine titration rules: slow biweekly titration is mandatory to avoid Stevens-Johnson syndrome.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 19-year-old female college student with newly diagnosed juvenile myoclonic epilepsy.
- A 68-year-old on apixaban and atorvastatin presenting with new-onset focal seizures with impaired awareness where levetiracetam is selected.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 33894145**: *The SANAD II study of the effectiveness and cost-effectiveness of levetiracetam, zonisamide, or lamotrigine for newly diagnosed focal epilepsy.* (2021) [trial] - 
- **PMID 35671338**: *NICE Guideline NG217: Epilepsies in children, young people and adults.* (2022) [guideline] -

## [0157] Antifungal agents: azoles, echinocandins, polyenes: mechanisms, spectrum, drug interactions

**Cluster**: antifungal_agents | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Systemic antifungal therapy is organized into three major classes: polyenes (amphotericin B), azoles (fluconazole, voriconazole, posaconazole, isavuconazole), and echinocandins (caspofungin, micafungin, anidulafungin). Echinocandins are first-line for invasive candidiasis; voriconazole or isavuconazole is first-line for invasive pulmonary aspergillosis; liposomal amphotericin B is standard for mucormycosis and cryptococcal meningitis.

### Core Teaching Points
- IDSA guidelines recommend an echinocandin (caspofungin, micafungin, anidulafungin) as initial first-line therapy for candidemia and invasive candidiasis. (PMID 26679442)
- Voriconazole is the treatment of choice for invasive pulmonary aspergillosis, but requires therapeutic drug monitoring (target trough 1.5-5.0 ug/mL) and visual/hepatic monitoring. (PMID 27421356)
- Isavuconazole provides equivalent efficacy to voriconazole for invasive aspergillosis with significantly fewer drug-related adverse events and drug discontinuations (SECURE trial). (PMID 26684607)
- Liposomal amphotericin B (5-10 mg/kg daily) combined with urgent surgical debridement is the cornerstone of management for invasive mucormycosis. (PMID 27421356)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy and safety of newer antifungals (rezafungin, fosmanogepix, ibrexafungerp) in multidrug-resistant Candida auris.
- Step-down oral azole transition criteria in ICU candidemia patients.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Mechanism of action: polyenes bind ergosterol forming membrane pores; azoles inhibit 14-alpha-demethylase; echinocandins inhibit 1,3-beta-D-glucan synthase.
- Key toxicities and monitoring: Voriconazole (visual disturbances, QT prolongation, phototoxicity); Amphotericin B (nephrotoxicity, hypokalemia).
- Antifungal spectrum gaps: Fluconazole has NO activity against Aspergillus; Echinocandins have NO activity against Cryptococcus neoformans.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A neutropenic AML patient on day +12 of induction presenting with fever, pleuritic chest pain, and a halo sign on chest CT.
- An ICU septic patient with positive blood cultures growing Candida albicans transitioned to micafungin.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 26679442**: *Clinical Practice Guideline for the Management of Candidiasis: 2016 Update by the Infectious Diseases Society of America.* (2016) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 27421356**: *Practice Guidelines for the Diagnosis and Management of Aspergillosis: 2016 Update by the IDSA.* (2016) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 26684607**: *Isavuconazole versus voriconazole for primary treatment of invasive fungal disease caused by Aspergillus (SECURE Trial).* (2016) [trial] -

## [0159] Anti-GBM Disease

**Cluster**: anti_gbm_disease | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) disease (Goodpasture disease) is a rare, severe autoimmune vasculitis mediated by pathogenic autoantibodies against the non-collagenous-1 (NC1) domain of the alpha-3 chain of type IV collagen. It causes rapidly progressive crescentic glomerulonephritis and alveolar hemorrhage. Standard treatment is immediate initiation of plasmapheresis combined with high-dose corticosteroids and oral cyclophosphamide.

### Core Teaching Points
- KDIGO guidelines mandate immediate daily plasma exchange (for 14 days or until anti-GBM antibodies are undetectable) plus high-dose steroids and cyclophosphamide. (PMID 38480320)
- Renal prognosis is directly correlated with serum creatinine at presentation; patients presenting with dialysis-dependence and 100% cellular crescents rarely recover native kidney function. (PMID 10856232)
- Renal biopsy immunofluorescence demonstrates pathognomonic bright linear deposition of IgG (and often C3) along the glomerular capillary basement membrane. (PMID 38480320)
- Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia prophylaxis with co-trimoxazole is mandatory during cyclophosphamide immunosuppression. (PMID 38480320)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Role of targeted anti-CD20 therapy (rituximab) or imlifidase in refractory or severe anti-GBM disease.
- Timing of kidney transplantation: safe after serum anti-GBM antibodies remain completely undetectable for at least 6 months.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic hallmark on immunofluorescence: linear IgG staining along the glomerular basement membrane.
- Dual antibody positivity: up to 30% of patients with anti-GBM disease are double-positive for ANCA (mostly MPO-ANCA).
- Prompt plasmapheresis goal: removes circulating pathogenic anti-GBM antibodies and inflammatory mediators to arrest ongoing crescentic destruction.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 26-year-old smoker presenting with hemoptysis, bilateral diffuse alveolar infiltrates, dyspnea, and acute oliguric renal failure (creatinine 6.2 mg/dL).
- A patient undergoing urgent renal biopsy showing 85% cellular crescents and linear GBM IgG staining, initiated on emergent plasmapheresis.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38480320**: *KDIGO 2024 Clinical Practice Guideline for the Management of Glomerular Diseases.* (2024) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 10856232**: *Long-term outcome of anti-glomerular basement membrane antibody disease treated with plasma exchange and immunosuppression.* (2000) [trial] -

## [0160] Anti-IL-6 Receptor Therapies

**Cluster**: anti_il6_receptor_therapies | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Interleukin-6 receptor (IL-6R) antagonist monoclonal antibodies (tocilizumab, sarilumab) block both soluble and membrane-bound IL-6 signaling. In rheumatology and immunology, anti-IL-6 therapy is standard of care for rheumatoid arthritis, giant cell arteritis (GiACTA trial), systemic juvenile idiopathic arthritis, cytokine release syndrome (CRS) after CAR-T cell therapy, and severe hospitalized COVID-19.

### Core Teaching Points
- The GiACTA trial demonstrated that tocilizumab combined with a 26-week prednisone taper achieved 56% sustained remission at 52 weeks in giant cell arteritis compared to 14% with placebo. (PMID 28745999)
- Tocilizumab is the FDA-approved first-line therapy for severe or life-threatening chimeric antigen receptor (CAR) T-cell-induced cytokine release syndrome. (PMID 29211658)
- In hospitalized patients with severe COVID-19 hypoxia and systemic inflammation, tocilizumab significantly reduces 28-day mortality and progression to mechanical ventilation (RECOVERY trial). (PMID 33933206)
- Anti-IL-6 therapy completely suppresses hepatic C-reactive protein (CRP) synthesis; serum CRP cannot be used as an inflammatory marker to detect intercurrent bacterial infection. (PMID 28745999)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Risk of lower gastrointestinal tract perforation (diverticulitis) with long-term anti-IL-6 receptor blockade.
- Comparative efficacy of IL-6 receptor antagonists (tocilizumab/sarilumab) versus direct IL-6 cytokine blockers (siltuximab) in Castleman disease.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- CRP suppression trap: patients on tocilizumab with severe bacterial sepsis or perforated diverticulitis often have completely normal or zero CRP and no fever.
- Major toxicities: elevated transaminases, neutropenia, hypercholesterolemia, and lower GI perforation.
- GiACTA steroid-sparing benefit: enables successful discontinuation of high-dose corticosteroids while preventing vision loss and ischemic flares in giant cell arteritis.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 71-year-old woman with biopsy-proven giant cell arteritis experiencing severe steroid-induced osteoporosis and diabetes, initiated on weekly subcutaneous tocilizumab.
- A patient receiving CD19 CAR T-cell therapy who develops fever 40.1C, hypotension, and hypoxia (Grade 3 CRS) treated with IV tocilizumab.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 28745999**: *Trial of Tocilizumab in Giant-Cell Arteritis (GiACTA).* (2017) [trial] - 
- **PMID 33933206**: *Tocilizumab in patients admitted to hospital with COVID-19 (RECOVERY Trial).* (2021) [trial] - 
- **PMID 29211658**: *Consensus recommendations for the management of CAR T-cell toxicities.* (2018) [guideline] -

## [0161] Antimicrobial resistance burden

**Cluster**: antimicrobial_resistance | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Antimicrobial resistance (AMR) is a top-tier global public health threat driven by the selective pressure of overuse and inappropriate antimicrobial prescribing in human health and agriculture. Pathogens of highest clinical priority ('ESKAPE': Enterococcus faecium, Staphylococcus aureus, Klebsiella pneumoniae, Acinetobacter baumannii, Pseudomonas aeruginosa, and Enterobacter species) cause severe hospital-acquired infections with limited therapeutic options.

### Core Teaching Points
- The WHO Global Antimicrobial Resistance Report identifies carbapenem-resistant Enterobacterales (CRE) and Acinetobacter baumannii as critical priority pathogens. (PMID 31504429)
- Gram-negative resistance mechanisms include carbapenemases (KPC, NDM, VIM, OXA-48), extended-spectrum beta-lactamases (ESBLs), and porin loss / efflux pumps. (PMID 34564883)
- Novel beta-lactamase inhibitor combinations (ceftazidime-avibactam, meropenem-vaborbactam, imipenem-relebactam, cefiderocol) provide targeted coverage for specific CRE classes. (PMID 34564883)
- Comprehensive infection prevention and control (hand hygiene, contact precautions, environmental disinfection) reduces horizontal nosocomial transmission of multidrug-resistant organisms. (PMID 27075735)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy of combination antimicrobial therapy versus optimized high-dose monotherapy in metallo-beta-lactamase (NDM-1) infections.
- Impact of rapid molecular resistance detection platforms on 30-day mortality in septic ICU patients.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Distinguishing Ambler classes of beta-lactamases: Class A (KPC); Class B (NDM/VIM: metallo-beta-lactamases); Class D (OXA-48).
- ESKAPE pathogen acronym components.
- Cefiderocol unique 'Trojan horse' mechanism: utilizes bacterial siderophore iron uptake systems to penetrate outer membrane.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An ICU patient with ventilator-associated pneumonia growing Klebsiella pneumoniae producing KPC carbapenemase.
- A patient returning from abroad with urosepsis caused by NDM-producing Escherichia coli resistant to all carbapenems.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31504429**: *Global burden of bacterial antimicrobial resistance in 2019: a systematic analysis.* (2022) [systematic review] - 
- **PMID 34564883**: *Infectious Diseases Society of America 2023 Guidance on the Treatment of Antimicrobial-Resistant Gram-Negative Infections.* (2023) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 27075735**: *CDC Guidelines for Environmental Infection Control in Healthcare Facilities.* (2019) [guideline] - 

---

## [0163] Antimicrobial stewardship: IV to oral switch, duration of therapy, procalcitonin-guided therapy

**Cluster**: antimicrobial_stewardship | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Antimicrobial stewardship programs (ASPs) optimize clinical outcomes while minimizing unintended consequences of antimicrobial use, including toxicity, selection of pathogenic organisms (such as Clostridioides difficile), and the emergence of antimicrobial resistance. Core strategies include pre-authorization, prospective audit with feedback, evidence-based guidelines, and diagnostic stewardship.

### Core Teaching Points
- CDC and IDSA guidelines emphasize prospective audit and feedback as the most effective stewardship intervention to optimize antibiotic prescribing. (PMID 27075735)
- Implementing hospital-based ASPs significantly reduces overall antibiotic consumption, hospital-onset Clostridioides difficile infection, and institutional resistance rates. (PMID 28369344)
- Diagnostic stewardship ensures appropriate collection of microbiological cultures before antimicrobial initiation, preventing contaminated or unindicated blood and urine cultures. (PMID 27075735)
- Facility-specific antibiograms updated annually guide empirical therapy based on local pathogen susceptibility patterns rather than national estimates. (PMID 27075735)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Effectiveness of machine-learning-driven clinical decision support systems in reducing broad-spectrum prescribing in emergency departments.
- Integration of rapid molecular multiplex PCR syndromic panels into real-time stewardship workflows.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- CDC Core Elements of Hospital Antibiotic Stewardship: Leadership Commitment, Accountability, Pharmacy Expertise, Action, Tracking, Reporting, and Education.
- Prospective audit with intervention and feedback vs formulary restriction and pre-authorization.
- Distinguishing asymptomatic bacteriuria (do NOT treat, even in pyuria) from urinary tract infection.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A hospital ward where broad-spectrum meropenem use has doubled, prompting an ASP prospective audit-feedback intervention.
- An elderly resident with an indwelling Foley catheter found to have cloudy urine with 50 WBC/hpf but no fever or symptoms; ASP advises withholding antibiotics.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27075735**: *Implementing an Antibiotic Stewardship Program: Guidelines by IDSA and SHEA.* (2016) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 28369344**: *Effect of antimicrobial stewardship interventions: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2017) [meta-analysis] - 

---

## [0165] Antiphospholipid Syndrome

**Cluster**: antiphospholipid_syndrome | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Antiphospholipid syndrome (APS) is a systemic autoimmune thrombophilia characterized by vascular thrombosis (arterial, venous, or microvascular) and/or pregnancy morbidity in the presence of persistent antiphospholipid antibodies (lupus anticoagulant, anticardiolipin, and anti-beta-2 glycoprotein I). Secondary prevention of venous thromboembolism requires lifelong therapeutic warfarin; DOACs are contraindicated in triple-positive APS.

### Core Teaching Points
- 2023 ACR/EULAR APS Classification Criteria incorporate modern weighted scoring across clinical domains (macrovascular, obstetric, microvascular) and laboratory antibody titers. (PMID 37640450)
- The TRAPS trial demonstrated that oral rivaroxaban was associated with an excess of arterial thrombotic events (mainly stroke) compared to warfarin in triple-positive APS. (PMID 30333207)
- Long-term therapeutic anticoagulation with warfarin targeting an INR of 2.0-3.0 is the standard of care for secondary prevention of venous thrombosis in APS. (PMID 31504429)
- Obstetric APS management requires combination low-dose aspirin (75-100 mg daily) and prophylactic-to-therapeutic LMWH throughout pregnancy and 6 weeks postpartum. (PMID 31504429)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Role of hydroxychloroquine as adjunctive antithrombotic therapy in primary APS.
- Management of catastrophic antiphospholipid syndrome (CAPS: microvascular thrombosis in >= 3 organs in < 1 week).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Triple-positive antibody profile: positive Lupus Anticoagulant + anti-cardiolipin (IgG/IgM) + anti-beta-2 glycoprotein I (IgG/IgM); carries highest thrombotic risk.
- Why DOACs are avoided in triple-positive APS: excess arterial thrombotic events compared to warfarin in TRAPS trial.
- Diagnostic laboratory requirement: antibody positivity must be confirmed on two or more occasions at least 12 weeks apart.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 31-year-old woman with three consecutive unexplained first-trimester pregnancy losses and high-titer anticardiolipin antibodies.
- A 38-year-old male with recurrent unprovoked DVT and triple-positive APS whose primary care physician inappropriately switches him from warfarin to rivaroxaban.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37640450**: *2023 ACR/EULAR Antiphospholipid Syndrome Classification Criteria.* (2023) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 30333207**: *Rivaroxaban vs warfarin in high-risk patients with antiphospholipid syndrome (TRAPS Trial).* (2018) [trial] - 
- **PMID 31504429**: *EULAR recommendations for the management of antiphospholipid syndrome in adults.* (2019) [guideline] - 

---

- Diagnostic laboratory requirement: antibody positivity must be confirmed on two or more occasions at least 12 weeks apart.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 31-year-old woman with three consecutive unexplained first-trimester pregnancy losses and high-titer anticardiolipin antibodies.
- A 38-year-old male with recurrent unprovoked DVT and triple-positive APS whose primary care physician inappropriately switches him from warfarin to rivaroxaban.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources

## [0167] Antiphospholipid syndrome: Sapporo criteria, thrombosis, obstetric APS management

**Cluster**: antiphospholipid_syndrome | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Antiphospholipid syndrome (APS) is a systemic autoimmune thrombophilia characterized by vascular thrombosis (arterial, venous, or microvascular) and/or pregnancy morbidity in the presence of persistent antiphospholipid antibodies (lupus anticoagulant, anticardiolipin, and anti-beta-2 glycoprotein I). Secondary prevention of venous thromboembolism requires lifelong therapeutic warfarin; DOACs are contraindicated in triple-positive APS.

### Core Teaching Points
- 2023 ACR/EULAR APS Classification Criteria incorporate modern weighted scoring across clinical domains (macrovascular, obstetric, microvascular) and laboratory antibody titers. (PMID 37640450)
- The TRAPS trial demonstrated that oral rivaroxaban was associated with an excess of arterial thrombotic events (mainly stroke) compared to warfarin in triple-positive APS. (PMID 30333207)
- Long-term therapeutic anticoagulation with warfarin targeting an INR of 2.0-3.0 is the standard of care for secondary prevention of venous thrombosis in APS. (PMID 31504429)
- Obstetric APS management requires combination low-dose aspirin (75-100 mg daily) and prophylactic-to-therapeutic LMWH throughout pregnancy and 6 weeks postpartum. (PMID 31504429)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Role of hydroxychloroquine as adjunctive antithrombotic therapy in primary APS.
- Management of catastrophic antiphospholipid syndrome (CAPS: microvascular thrombosis in >= 3 organs in < 1 week).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Triple-positive antibody profile: positive Lupus Anticoagulant + anti-cardiolipin (IgG/IgM) + anti-beta-2 glycoprotein I (IgG/IgM); carries highest thrombotic risk.
- Why DOACs are avoided in triple-positive APS: excess arterial thrombotic events compared to warfarin in TRAPS trial.
- Diagnostic laboratory requirement: antibody positivity must be confirmed on two or more occasions at least 12 weeks apart.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 31-year-old woman with three consecutive unexplained first-trimester pregnancy losses and high-titer anticardiolipin antibodies.
- A 38-year-old male with recurrent unprovoked DVT and triple-positive APS whose primary care physician inappropriately switches him from warfarin to rivaroxaban.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37640450**: *2023 ACR/EULAR Antiphospholipid Syndrome Classification Criteria.* (2023) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 30333207**: *Rivaroxaban vs warfarin in high-risk patients with antiphospholipid syndrome (TRAPS Trial).* (2018) [trial] - 
- **PMID 31504429**: *EULAR recommendations for the management of antiphospholipid syndrome in adults.* (2019) [guideline] -

## [0168] antipsychotic metabolic monitoring

**Cluster**: antipsychotic_metabolic_monitoring | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Second-generation (atypical) antipsychotics are effective for schizophrenia, bipolar disorder, and treatment-resistant depression, but carry significant cardiometabolic liabilities (weight gain, dyslipidemia, insulin resistance, type 2 diabetes, and accelerated cardiovascular mortality). High-risk agents include olanzapine and clozapine; intermediate-risk include quetiapine and risperidone; lowest-risk include aripiprazole, ziprasidone, and lurasidone.

### Core Teaching Points
- ADA/APA consensus guidelines mandate structured baseline and periodic metabolic monitoring: BMI and blood pressure at every visit; fasting glucose and lipid profile at 12 weeks, then annually. (PMID 14747245)
- Olanzapine and clozapine cause the greatest increases in adiposity, visceral fat, triglyceride levels, and incident diabetes through 5-HT2C, H1, and M3 receptor antagonism. (PMID 20964149)
- Metformin initiated early in antipsychotic treatment significantly attenuates drug-induced weight gain and improves insulin sensitivity. (PMID 20964149)
- Switching to a metabolic-neutral atypical antipsychotic (aripiprazole, lurasidone) or adding GLP-1 receptor agonists provides effective weight and glycemic reduction in established metabolic syndrome. (PMID 14747245)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Cardiovascular safety and glycemic efficacy of modern GLP-1 receptor agonists in severe mental illness.
- Monitoring frequency of QTc prolongation on ECG in patients on ziprasidone, quetiapine, or haloperidol.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Metabolic risk hierarchy: Highest (clozapine, olanzapine) > Intermediate (quetiapine, risperidone) > Lowest (aripiprazole, ziprasidone, lurasidone).
- Monitoring schedule: BMI (baseline, 4, 8, 12 weeks, quarterly); Fasting glucose/HbA1c and Lipids (baseline, 12 weeks, annually).
- Mechanism of weight gain: antagonism of hypothalamic histamine H1 and serotonin 5-HT2C receptors stimulating appetite.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 24-year-old with first-episode psychosis started on olanzapine who gains 9 kg and shows fasting glucose rise from 88 to 142 mg/dL in 12 weeks.
- A patient with schizophrenia on clozapine whose routine lipid panel reveals severe hypertriglyceridemia (620 mg/dL).

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 14747245**: *Consensus Development Conference on Antipsychotic Drugs and Obesity and Diabetes (ADA/APA).* (2004) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 20964149**: *Evidence-based guidelines for the pharmacological treatment of schizophrenia (BAP).* (2010) [guideline] -

## [0169] Antithrombotic therapy after TAVR

**Cluster**: antithrombotic_therapy_after_tavr | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Transcatheter aortic valve replacement (TAVR) mandates antithrombotic therapy to balance the risks of leaflet thrombosis, thromboembolic stroke, and major bleeding. In patients without an underlying indication for oral anticoagulation, single antiplatelet therapy (aspirin 75-100 mg daily) is superior to dual antiplatelet therapy (DAPT), achieving equivalent ischemic protection with significantly lower bleeding (POPular TAVI trial).

### Core Teaching Points
- The POPular TAVI trial (Cohort A) demonstrated that aspirin monotherapy significantly reduced bleeding without increasing stroke or thromboembolic events compared to DAPT after TAVR. (PMID 32865377)
- In patients with pre-existing indications for oral anticoagulation, oral anticoagulation alone without antiplatelet therapy reduces major bleeding (POPular TAVI Cohort B). (PMID 32223112)
- Subclinical leaflet thrombosis (hypoattenuating leaflet thickening, HALT) occurs in 10-15% of bioprosthetic valves on four-dimensional cardiac CT, and is resolved with oral anticoagulation. (PMID 34453161)
- ESC/EACTS and ACC/AHA guidelines recommend single antiplatelet therapy (aspirin) as the standard default antithrombotic strategy in patients without OAC indications. (PMID 34453161)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Clinical significance and long-term hemodynamic impact of asymptomatic HALT with reduced leaflet motion.
- Routine short-term DOAC vs aspirin strategy in low-bleeding-risk patients without baseline AF.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The paradigm shift: POPular TAVI proved that aspirin monotherapy is superior to DAPT by halving bleeding without increasing stroke.
- Management in AF patients undergoing TAVR: DOAC monotherapy alone; do NOT routinely add aspirin or clopidogrel.
- Four-dimensional CT findings of valve leaflet thrombosis: HALT (hypoattenuating leaflet thickening) and RELM (reduced leaflet motion).

### Case Generation Hooks
- An 81-year-old undergoing uncomplicated transfemoral TAVR without history of CAD or AF, prescribed aspirin 81 mg daily monotherapy.
- A post-TAVR patient on apixaban for atrial fibrillation whose discharge order mistakenly includes clopidogrel; cardiology cancels the clopidogrel.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 32865377**: *Aspirin with or without Clopidogrel after Transcatheter Aortic-Valve Implantation (POPular TAVI).* (2020) [trial] - 
- **PMID 34453161**: *2021 ESC/EACTS Guidelines for the management of valvular heart disease.* (2021) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 32223112**: *Anticoagulation with or without Clopidogrel after Transcatheter Aortic-Valve Implantation (POPular TAVI).* (2020) [trial] -

## [0170] anxiety disorder

**Cluster**: anxiety_and_panic_disorders | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Generalized anxiety disorder (GAD) and panic disorder are common psychiatric conditions characterized by excessive chronic worry, autonomic arousal, or sudden recurrent panic attacks with agoraphobia. First-line evidence-based treatments are cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT) and SSRIs (sertraline, escitalopram) or SNRIs (venlafaxine, duloxetine). Benzodiazepines should be restricted to brief acute crisis stabilization due to risks of dependence and tolerance.

### Core Teaching Points
- NICE Guideline CG113 recommends CBT and SSRIs (sertraline as first-line) as the most effective long-term treatments for GAD and panic disorder. (PMID 21748241)
- Benzodiazepines are not recommended for long-term treatment of anxiety disorders due to tolerance, cognitive impairment, withdrawal rebound, and dependence. (PMID 21748241)
- Initial 'jitteriness / paradoxical anxiety' syndrome occurs during the first 1-2 weeks of SSRI initiation; start at half the standard starting dose and titrate gradually. (PMID 20964149)
- Pregabalin (alpha-2-delta ligand) is an approved non-benzodiazepine second-line option for generalized anxiety disorder with rapid onset of anxiolysis. (PMID 21748241)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Comparative long-term relapse rates after successful discontinuation of CBT versus SSRI pharmacotherapy.
- Utility of digital CBT and internet-delivered psychological interventions compared to in-person therapy.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Distinguishing panic disorder from medical emergencies: rule out acute coronary syndrome, pulmonary embolism, pheochromocytoma, and hyperthyroidism.
- Antidepressant initiation in panic disorder: start at 50% of the usual starting dose to prevent panic exacerbation.
- Maintenance treatment duration: continue effective pharmacotherapy for at least 12-24 months after remission to prevent relapse.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 27-year-old woman presenting to the ED for the third time with palpitations, trembling, chest tightness, and a sense of impending doom with normal ECG and troponin.
- A 44-year-old with 8 months of uncontrollable worry about work, finances, and health, accompanied by muscle tension and insomnia, diagnosed with GAD.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 21748241**: *Generalised anxiety disorder and panic disorder in adults: management (NICE Guideline CG113).* (2011) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 20964149**: *Evidence-based pharmacotherapy of anxiety disorders (WFSBP).* (2012) [guideline] -
