## [0172] aortic dissection

**Cluster**: aortic_dissection | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute aortic dissection is a life-threatening cardiovascular emergency resulting from an intimal tear that creates a false lumen in the aortic media. Stanford Type A involves the ascending aorta (mandates emergent open surgical repair). Stanford Type B involves only the descending aorta distal to the left subclavian artery (managed with medical anti-impulse therapy using IV beta-blockers, reserving TEVAR for complicated Type B).

### Core Teaching Points
- ESC and AHA/ACC guidelines mandate emergent cardiothoracic surgical repair for Stanford Type A aortic dissection to prevent fatal tamponade, rupture, or coronary malperfusion. (PMID 39213193)
- Initial medical management requires anti-impulse therapy: IV beta-blockers (esmolol, labetalol) to reduce heart rate < 60 bpm and shear stress (dP/dt), followed by vasodilators to achieve SBP 100-120 mmHg. (PMID 39213193)
- Vasodilators (nitroprusside, nicardipine) must NEVER be started before beta-blockade, as reflex sympathetic tachycardia increases aortic wall shear stress and propagates the tear. (PMID 39213193)
- Thoracic endovascular aortic repair (TEVAR) is indicated for complicated Stanford Type B dissection (refractory pain, rapid expansion, organ malperfusion, rupture). (PMID 39213193)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Indications for preemptive subacute TEVAR in uncomplicated Type B dissection with high-risk anatomical features.
- Optimal long-term blood pressure goals (< 120/80 mmHg) and beta-blocker selection.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Stanford classification: Type A = involves ascending aorta (surgical emergency); Type B = restricted to descending aorta distal to left subclavian artery.
- The critical rule of anti-impulse therapy: IV beta-blocker FIRST (target HR < 60 bpm), then vasodilator (target SBP 100-120 mmHg) to avoid reflex tachycardia.
- Complications of Type A dissection: acute severe aortic regurgitation, hemopericardium / cardiac tamponade, myocardial infarction, and stroke.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 59-year-old male with sudden severe tearing chest pain radiating between the shoulder blades, asymmetric pulses, and a high-pitched early diastolic murmur.
- A patient with acute Type B dissection admitted to ICU initiated on IV esmolol infusion to target HR 56 bpm and SBP 112 mmHg.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39213193**: *2024 ESC Guidelines for the management of aortic diseases.* (2024) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 34709979**: *2021 ACC/AHA Guideline for the Evaluation and Diagnosis of Chest Pain.* (2021) [guideline] -

## [0173] Aortic stenosis: grading severity, low-flow low-gradient, intervention timing

**Cluster**: aortic_stenosis_severity | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Severe aortic stenosis (AS) is defined on transthoracic echocardiography by an aortic valve area (AVA) <= 1.0 cm2 (or indexed AVA <= 0.6 cm2/m2), mean transvalvular gradient >= 40 mmHg, or peak jet velocity >= 4.0 m/s. Once symptoms (angina, syncope, dyspnea) develop, 2-year mortality exceeds 50% without valve intervention. Low-flow low-gradient AS requires dobutamine stress echocardiography or non-contrast cardiac CT calcium scoring.

### Core Teaching Points
- ESC and AHA/ACC guidelines define severe aortic stenosis by AVA <= 1.0 cm2, mean gradient >= 40 mmHg, or peak velocity >= 4.0 m/s. (PMID 34453161)
- Low-dose dobutamine stress echocardiography differentiates true severe AS from pseudo-severe AS in low-flow low-gradient AS with reduced LVEF. (PMID 34453161)
- In paradoxical low-flow low-gradient AS with preserved LVEF, non-contrast cardiac CT aortic valve calcium scoring confirms severity (threshold >= 2,000 in men, >= 1,200 in women). (PMID 34453161)
- Aortic valve replacement (SAVR or TAVR) is indicated in all symptomatic patients with severe AS, and in asymptomatic patients with LVEF < 50% or abnormal exercise testing. (PMID 34453161)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Indications for early intervention in asymptomatic severe AS with normal LVEF.
- Optimal choice between TAVR and SAVR in bicuspid aortic valve anatomy across age groups.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Classic triad of symptomatic severe AS: Dyspnea (heart failure, 2-year survival 50%), Syncope (exertional, 3-year survival), Angina (5-year survival).
- Dobutamine stress echo assessment of contractile reserve: an increase in stroke volume >= 20% confirms contractile reserve and predicts lower procedural mortality.
- Cardiac CT calcium score thresholds for severe AS: >= 2,000-3,000 AU in men; >= 1,200-1,600 AU in women.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An 80-year-old male presenting with exertional presyncope whose echocardiogram shows AVA 0.7 cm2, peak velocity 4.4 m/s, and mean gradient 46 mmHg.
- A 74-year-old with LVEF 30%, AVA 0.8 cm2, and mean gradient 26 mmHg referred for dobutamine stress echo to distinguish true from pseudo-severe AS.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34453161**: *2021 ESC/EACTS Guidelines for the management of valvular heart disease.* (2021) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 34709979**: *ACC/AHA Guideline for the Management of Patients With Valvular Heart Disease.* (2020) [guideline] -

## [0174] Aortic stenosis TAVR

**Cluster**: aortic_stenosis_tavr | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Transcatheter aortic valve replacement (TAVR) has transformed the treatment of severe symptomatic aortic stenosis across all surgical risk categories (high, intermediate, and low surgical risk). Valve selection between transfemoral TAVR and surgical aortic valve replacement (SAVR) is made by the Heart Team, considering patient age (TAVR favored in age >= 75; SAVR favored in age < 65), life expectancy, anatomical suitability, and vascular access.

### Core Teaching Points
- The PARTNER 3 and Evolut Low Risk trials demonstrated that transfemoral TAVR was non-inferior or superior to surgical aortic valve replacement in low-risk elderly patients. (PMID 30883058)
- ESC/EACTS guidelines recommend TAVR in older patients (age >= 75 years) or those who are inoperable or high surgical risk (STS/EuroSCORE II > 8%). (PMID 34453161)
- Complications of TAVR include complete heart block requiring permanent pacemaker implantation (higher with self-expanding valves), vascular access site injury, paravalvular leak, and stroke. (PMID 34453161)
- Pre-procedural planning requires ECG-gated multislice cardiac CT of the aortic root and iliofemoral vasculature for valve sizing and access evaluation. (PMID 34453161)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Durability and re-intervention options after 10 years in younger TAVR recipients.
- Risk of coronary artery obstruction during TAVR-in-surgical-bioprosthesis.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Age-based guideline thresholds: age >= 75 favors transfemoral TAVR; age < 65 or life expectancy > 20 years favors mechanical or surgical bioprosthesis.
- Pacemaker risk after TAVR: mechanical compression of the membranous septum and atrioventricular conduction bundle.
- Pre-TAVR CT evaluation: assesses annular area and perimeter, distance from annular plane to coronary ostia, and minimal femoral lumen diameter.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 79-year-old active woman with severe symptomatic AS and an STS score of 2.1% evaluated by the Heart Team for transfemoral TAVR.
- A post-TAVR patient on telemetry who develops new complete heart block on day 1 post-implantation of a self-expanding valve.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 30883058**: *Transcatheter Aortic-Valve Replacement with a Balloon-Expandable Valve in Low-Risk Patients (PARTNER 3).* (2019) [trial] - 
- **PMID 34453161**: *2021 ESC/EACTS Guidelines for the management of valvular heart disease.* (2021) [guideline] -

## [0175] apixaban versus rivaroxaban atrial fibrillation

**Cluster**: apixaban_vs_rivaroxaban | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Apixaban and rivaroxaban are direct factor Xa inhibitors widely prescribed for stroke prevention in non-valvular atrial fibrillation and treatment of venous thromboembolism. Apixaban is administered twice daily (12-hour half-life) and has the lowest renal elimination (~27%) among DOACs. Rivaroxaban is administered once daily with food (to ensure absorption of 15 mg and 20 mg doses) and is 35% renally cleared. Large observational comparative studies show lower gastrointestinal bleeding and mortality with apixaban.

### Core Teaching Points
- Large real-world observational comparative effectiveness studies consistently associate apixaban with lower rates of major bleeding and gastrointestinal hemorrhage compared to rivaroxaban. (PMID 34929729)
- Rivaroxaban 15 mg and 20 mg tablets must strictly be taken with food to achieve predictable oral bioavailability (bioavailability falls to 66% in the fasting state). (PMID 39213192)
- Apixaban requires twice-daily dosing to maintain steady-state anticoagulation, reducing peak-to-trough plasma fluctuations compared to once-daily rivaroxaban. (PMID 21875378)
- Both agents are substrate of CYP3A4 and P-glycoprotein, requiring dose adjustment or avoidance with strong dual inhibitors (ketoconazole, ritonavir) or inducers (rifampin, carbamazepine). (PMID 39213192)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Real-world adherence differences: twice-daily apixaban vs once-daily rivaroxaban.
- Comparative outcomes in extreme obesity (body weight > 120 kg or BMI > 40).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Food effect distinction: rivaroxaban 15 mg and 20 mg require food for absorption; apixaban can be taken with or without food.
- GI bleeding profile: apixaban does not increase major GI bleeding compared to warfarin, whereas once-daily rivaroxaban and dabigatran 150 mg exhibit higher GI bleeding rates.
- Renal dosing: apixaban dose reduction based on the 2-of-3 criteria; rivaroxaban reduced from 20 mg to 15 mg if CrCl 15-49 mL/min.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 73-year-old patient with prior mild gastritis and AF choosing between apixaban and rivaroxaban.
- A patient taking rivaroxaban 20 mg on an empty stomach whose morning trough levels show subtherapeutic drug concentration.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34929729**: *Association of Apixaban vs Rivaroxaban With Risk of Major Ischemic or Hemorrhagic Events in Patients With Atrial Fibrillation.* (2021) [meta-analysis] - 
- **PMID 21875378**: *Apixaban versus Warfarin in Patients with Atrial Fibrillation (ARISTOTLE).* (2011) [trial] - 
- **PMID 39213192**: *2024 ESC Guidelines for the management of atrial fibrillation.* (2024) [guideline] -

## [0176] apixaban versus warfarin atrial fibrillation

**Cluster**: apixaban_aristotle_trial | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The landmark ARISTOTLE trial compared the oral direct factor Xa inhibitor apixaban (5 mg twice daily, or 2.5 mg twice daily in select patients) with dose-adjusted warfarin in 18,201 patients with non-valvular atrial fibrillation. Apixaban achieved significant superiority in preventing stroke or systemic embolism (21% reduction), major bleeding (31% reduction), and all-cause mortality (11% reduction).

### Core Teaching Points
- In the ARISTOTLE trial, apixaban was superior to warfarin in preventing stroke or systemic embolism (HR 0.79, 95% CI 0.66-0.95; p < 0.001 for non-inferiority, p = 0.01 for superiority). (PMID 21875378)
- Apixaban significantly reduced major bleeding by 31% (HR 0.69; p < 0.001) and intracranial hemorrhage by 58% compared to warfarin. (PMID 21875378)
- Apixaban demonstrated a statistically significant 11% reduction in all-cause mortality (HR 0.89; p = 0.047), a landmark finding unique among pivotal DOAC trials. (PMID 21875378)
- Dose reduction to 2.5 mg twice daily is indicated if at least 2 of 3 criteria are met: age >= 80 years, body weight <= 60 kg, or serum creatinine >= 1.5 mg/dL. (PMID 21875378)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy and safety of full-dose versus reduced-dose apixaban in patients with isolated border-zone renal function.
- Comparative effectiveness in patients with extreme obesity (weight > 140 kg).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The ARISTOTLE triple win: superior stroke reduction, superior bleeding reduction, and statistically significant reduction in all-cause mortality.
- The specific 2-of-3 dose reduction criteria (Age >= 80, Weight <= 60 kg, Creatinine >= 1.5 mg/dL).
- Absence of increased GI bleeding with apixaban vs warfarin (unlike rivaroxaban and dabigatran 150 mg).

### Case Generation Hooks
- An 81-year-old female weighing 58 kg with serum creatinine 1.6 mg/dL starting apixaban 2.5 mg BID.
- A 72-year-old male with AF and prior ischemic stroke initiated on apixaban 5 mg BID.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 21875378**: *Apixaban versus Warfarin in Patients with Atrial Fibrillation (ARISTOTLE).* (2011) [trial] - 
- **PMID 39213192**: *2024 ESC Guidelines for the management of atrial fibrillation.* (2024) [guideline] - 

---

## [0178] Aplastic Anaemia

**Cluster**: aplastic_anaemia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Severe aplastic anaemia (SAA) is an immune-mediated bone marrow failure disorder characterized by profound pancytopenia and hypocellular bone marrow (cellularity < 25-30%) without dysplasia or fibrosis. In young, fit patients (< 40-50 years) with an HLA-matched sibling donor, upfront allogeneic hematopoietic cell transplantation is curative. For older patients or those lacking a matched sibling, immunosuppressive therapy (IST) with horse anti-thymocyte globulin (ATG), cyclosporine, and the thrombopoietin receptor agonist eltrombopag achieves > 80% response rates.

### Core Teaching Points
- British Society for Haematology guidelines define SAA by bone marrow cellularity < 25% and at least two of: neutrophils < 0.5 x 10^9/L, platelets < 20 x 10^9/L, or reticulocytes < 60 x 10^9/L (Camitta criteria). (PMID 37947196)
- First-line allogeneic HCT from an HLA-matched sibling donor cures > 85-90% of children and young adults with severe aplastic anaemia. (PMID 37947196)
- Adding eltrombopag to horse ATG and cyclosporine significantly increases complete and overall hematologic response rates (87% vs 66%) in treatment-naive SAA (RACE trial). (PMID 35021024)
- Horse ATG (ATGAM) is clinically superior to rabbit ATG (Thymoglobulin) for initial immunosuppressive therapy in SAA. (PMID 21813796)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy and graft-versus-host disease rates with upfront matched unrelated donor (MUD) vs IST in adults aged 40-60.
- Clonal evolution to paroxysmal nocturnal hemoglobinuria (PNH) or myelodysplastic syndrome (MDS with monosomy 7) following successful IST.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Camitta criteria for severe aplastic anemia: marrow cellularity < 25% plus 2 of: ANC < 0.5 x 10^9/L, Plt < 20 x 10^9/L, Retics < 60 x 10^9/L (ANC < 0.2 defines very severe AA).
- Serum sickness prophylaxis and management during horse ATG: high-dose oral prednisone (1 mg/kg/day) for 10-14 days.
- Why horse ATG is preferred over rabbit ATG in primary SAA: rabbit ATG causes excessive profound T-cell depletion and higher early mortality.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 23-year-old presenting with epistaxis, bruising, and fatigue; labs show Hb 7.2 g/dL, ANC 0.3 x 10^9/L, platelets 11 x 10^9/L, and marrow cellularity 10%.
- A 56-year-old with SAA without a matched sibling initiated on horse ATG, cyclosporine, and eltrombopag (RACE protocol).

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37947196**: *Guidelines for the diagnosis and management of adult aplastic anaemia (BSH).* (2024) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 35021024**: *Immunosuppressive Therapy with or without Eltrombopag in Severe Aplastic Anemia (RACE Trial).* (2022) [trial] - 
- **PMID 21813796**: *Horse versus rabbit antithymocyte globulin in severe aplastic anemia.* (2011) [trial] -

## [0180] appendicitis

**Cluster**: appendicitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute appendicitis is the most common surgical abdominal emergency, caused by luminal obstruction (fecalith, lymphoid hyperplasia) leading to distension, bacterial invasion, ischemia, and perforation. Diagnosis is supported by clinical decision rules (Alvarado, AIR scores) and confirmed by ultrasound in children/young females or contrast-enhanced CT in adults. Laparoscopic appendectomy remains the definitive standard of care, though selected uncomplicated cases can be managed with antibiotics.

### Core Teaching Points
- WSES guidelines recommend laparoscopic appendectomy as the gold-standard treatment for acute appendicitis, offering lower surgical site infection and faster recovery. (PMID 32773454)
- Contrast-enhanced abdominal CT has a sensitivity of 95-98% and specificity > 95% in adults, accurately distinguishing uncomplicated from complicated (gangrenous, perforated) appendicitis. (PMID 32773454)
- Antibiotic-first non-operative management (CODA trial) is successful in ~70% of uncomplicated appendicitis at 90 days, but is associated with a 29% appendectomy recurrence rate at 1 year. (PMID 33022199)
- Presence of an appendicolith on CT is strongly associated with non-operative antibiotic failure, recurrent appendicitis, and early perforation. (PMID 33022199)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal interval for elective interval appendectomy following successful non-operative management of an appendiceal phlegmon or abscess.
- Antibiotic duration (24-48h vs 4 days) post-appendectomy in complicated perforated appendicitis.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Clinical signs of appendicitis: McBurney point tenderness, Rovsing sign (RLQ pain with LLQ palpation), psoas sign, and obturator sign.
- Alvarado score criteria (MANTRELS mnemonic: Migration, Anorexia, Nausea, Tenderness RLQ, Rebound, Elevation of temp, Leukocytosis, Shift of neutrophils).
- Appendicolith significance: major contraindication to non-operative antibiotic management due to high failure rate.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 21-year-old male with periumbilical pain migrating to the right lower quadrant, low-grade fever, anorexia, and WBC 14.8 x 10^9/L.
- An adult with CT-proven uncomplicated acute appendicitis asking if surgery can be avoided with antibiotics.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 32773454**: *Diagnosis and treatment of acute appendicitis: 2020 update of the WSES Jerusalem guidelines.* (2020) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 33022199**: *A Randomized Trial Comparing Antibiotics with Appendectomy for Appendicitis (CODA Trial).* (2020) [trial] -

## [0181] ARDS definition: Berlin criteria and severity classification

**Cluster**: ards_berlin_definition | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) is an acute diffuse inflammatory lung injury characterized by increased pulmonary vascular permeability, alveolar flooding, and severe refractory hypoxemia. The Berlin definition requires onset within 1 week of clinical insult, bilateral radiographic opacities not fully explained by heart failure, and PaO2/FiO2 <= 300 with PEEP >= 5 cmH2O. Core management relies on lung-protective ventilation (low tidal volumes, low driving pressure) and early prone positioning.

### Core Teaching Points
- The Berlin definition stages ARDS into mild (PaO2/FiO2 201-300), moderate (101-200), and severe (<= 100 mmHg) with minimum PEEP >= 5 cmH2O. (PMID 22614452)
- Lung-protective ventilation with low tidal volumes (4-8 mL/kg predicted body weight) and plateau pressure < 30 cmH2O significantly reduces 28-day mortality (ARMA trial). (PMID 10793162)
- Prone positioning for at least 16 consecutive hours daily reduces 28-day and 90-day mortality in severe ARDS with PaO2/FiO2 < 150 mmHg (PROSEVA trial). (PMID 23688302)
- Conservative fluid management strategy increases ventilator-free days and ICU-free days without increasing non-pulmonary organ failure (FACTT trial). (PMID 16714487)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Indications and timing for venovenous extracorporeal membrane oxygenation (VV-ECMO) in refractory severe ARDS (EOLIA trial criteria).
- Optimal titration of PEEP: high vs low PEEP tables versus esophageal manometry / electrical impedance tomography.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Berlin definition pillars: Onset <= 7 days, Bilateral opacities on chest imaging, Origin not fully hydrostatic/fluid overload, Oxygenation threshold with PEEP >= 5.
- Calculation of Predicted Body Weight (PBW): tidal volume must be based on height and biological sex, NOT actual scale weight.
- Driving pressure (Plateau pressure minus PEEP): maintaining driving pressure < 14-15 cmH2O independently predicts lower mortality.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 48-year-old with severe pneumonia in ICU intubated on 60% oxygen and PEEP 10 with PaO2 78 mmHg (PaO2/FiO2 = 130; moderate ARDS).
- A septic patient with severe ARDS (PaO2/FiO2 = 88) who is turned prone for an 18-hour cycle with subsequent PaO2/FiO2 rise to 175.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 22614452**: *Acute respiratory distress syndrome: the Berlin Definition.* (2012) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 10793162**: *Ventilation with lower tidal volumes as compared with traditional tidal volumes for acute lung injury (ARMA Trial).* (2000) [trial] - 
- **PMID 23688302**: *Prone positioning in severe acute respiratory distress syndrome (PROSEVA Trial).* (2013) [trial] -

## [0182] Arterial Blood Gas interpretation

**Cluster**: arterial_blood_gas_interpretation | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Arterial blood gas (ABG) analysis is a foundational diagnostic tool in critical care and emergency medicine. A systematic 6-step interpretation approach evaluates: 1) PaO2 and SaO2 for oxygenation, 2) pH for acidemia (< 7.35) or alkalemia (> 7.45), 3) PaCO2 for respiratory disturbances, 4) HCO3- and Base Excess for metabolic disturbances, 5) Serum anion gap (Na - [Cl + HCO3-], normal 10-12 mEq/L), and 6) Delta ratio (Delta AG / Delta HCO3-) to identify mixed metabolic disorders.

### Core Teaching Points
- A systematic 6-step algorithmic approach prevents overlooking hidden mixed acid-base disorders in critically ill ICU patients. (PMID 24635770)
- Anion gap calculation must be corrected for hypoalbuminemia: adjusted AG = observed AG + 2.5 * (4.0 - serum albumin in g/dL). (PMID 24635770)
- Winter's formula determines appropriate respiratory compensation in metabolic acidosis: expected PaCO2 = 1.5 * [HCO3-] + 8 (+/- 2). (PMID 24635770)
- Delta ratio (Delta AG / Delta HCO3-): < 0.4-0.8 indicates mixed high AG and normal AG metabolic acidosis; > 1.6-2.0 indicates concurrent metabolic alkalosis. (PMID 24635770)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Clinical utility of Stewart physicochemical quantitative approach (strong ion difference) versus classical Henderson-Hasselbalch approach.
- Use of venous blood gas (VBG) as a reliable surrogate for pH, PaCO2, and lactate in hemodynamically stable patients.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Winter's formula calculation: if measured PaCO2 is higher than expected, concurrent respiratory acidosis is present; if lower, concurrent respiratory alkalosis is present.
- GOLDMARK mnemonic for high anion gap metabolic acidosis: Glycols, Oxoproline, L-lactate, D-lactate, Methanol, Aspirin, Renal failure, Ketoacidosis.
- Albumin effect on anion gap: each 1 g/dL drop in albumin lowers normal baseline anion gap by 2.5 mEq/L.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A diabetic patient in ED with pH 7.18, PaCO2 24 mmHg, HCO3- 9 mEq/L, Na 138, Cl 96, albumin 4.0 (pure DKA with appropriate respiratory compensation).
- A COPD patient with chronic CO2 retention who develops sepsis and lactic acidosis presenting with mixed respiratory and metabolic acidosis.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 24635770**: *Acid-base disorders in the intensive care unit: a systematic approach.* (2014) [review] - 
- **PMID 38480320**: *KDIGO 2024 Clinical Practice Guideline for the Management of Acute Kidney Injury.* (2024) [guideline] -

## [0184] arterial leg ulcer

**Cluster**: arterial_leg_ulcer | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Arterial leg ulcers are painful, ischemic, non-healing lower extremity wounds resulting from peripheral artery disease (PAD) and microvascular insufficiency. They are typically deep, punched-out, painful (worse with elevation, relieved by hanging leg over bed), located on distally perfused sites (toes, lateral malleolus, heels), with pale/cyanotic skin and absent pulses. Diagnostic evaluation requires Ankle-Brachial Index (ABI), duplex ultrasound, and vascular surgery revascularization.

### Core Teaching Points
- AHA/ACC and ESVS guidelines establish that compression bandaging is strictly contraindicated in severe arterial ulcers with ABI < 0.5 or ankle pressure < 50 mmHg. (PMID 38241123)
- Ankle-Brachial Index (ABI) <= 0.90 is diagnostic of peripheral artery disease; values > 1.40 indicate non-compressible, calcified arteries warranting Toe-Brachial Index (TBI < 0.70). (PMID 38241123)
- Endovascular or surgical revascularization is the essential cornerstone of limb salvage in chronic limb-threatening ischemia (CLTI / Rutherford stage 5-6). (PMID 31504429)
- Comprehensive medical therapy includes high-intensity statin, single antiplatelet therapy (clopidogrel 75 mg or low-dose rivaroxaban + aspirin [COMPASS trial]), and smoking cessation. (PMID 38241123)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Endovascular angioplasty/stenting vs surgical bypass with autologous saphenous vein in long femoropopliteal CLTI lesions (BEST-CLI trial).
- Role of hyperbaric oxygen therapy in non-reconstructable ischemic ulcers.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Distinguishing arterial from venous ulcers: arterial ulcers are deep, punched-out, painful, relieved by dependency, lack granulation tissue; venous ulcers are superficial, exudative, medial malleolar, painless or dull ache.
- Contraindication to compression therapy: absolute contraindication when ABI < 0.5 to avoid precipitating acute tissue gangrene.
- The COMPASS regimen in symptomatic PAD: rivaroxaban 2.5 mg BID plus aspirin 100 mg daily reduces major adverse limb events (amputation) by 46%.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 72-year-old diabetic smoker with a punched-out ulcer over the left 5th metatarsal head, absent dorsalis pedis pulse, and severe resting foot pain at night.
- A patient with non-compressible calcified vessels (ABI 1.45) undergoing toe-brachial index testing showing TBI 0.38.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38241123**: *2024 ACC/AHA Guideline for the Management of Lower Extremity Peripheral Artery Disease.* (2024) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 31504429**: *Global vascular guidelines on the management of chronic limb-threatening ischemia (ESVS/SVS).* (2019) [guideline] -

## [0185] Asbestosis

**Cluster**: asbestosis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Asbestosis is a chronic interstitial pneumonitis and progressive pulmonary fibrosis caused by the inhalation of microscopic asbestos fibers. It typically manifests 20-30 years following substantial occupational exposure (shipyards, construction, insulation). Clinical features include exertional dyspnea, dry bibasilar inspiratory crackles, finger clubbing, and restrictive spirometry with reduced DLCO. HRCT shows subpleural curvilinear lines, parenchymal bands, and calcified pleural plaques.

### Core Teaching Points
- ATS criteria establish diagnosis based on documented history of significant asbestos exposure, latency > 20 years, restrictive lung defect with reduced DLCO, and characteristic HRCT fibrosis. (PMID 15256488)
- High-resolution chest CT demonstrates bilateral, predominantly subpleural and basilar fibrosis, accompanied by bilateral calcified parietal pleural plaques along ribs and diaphragm. (PMID 15256488)
- Pleural plaques indicate prior asbestos exposure but do not themselves cause significant functional lung impairment or undergo malignant transformation. (PMID 15256488)
- Asbestos exposure combined with cigarette smoking acts synergistically to multiply the lifetime risk of bronchogenic carcinoma by up to 50- to 60-fold. (PMID 15256488)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy and safety of antifibrotic therapies (nintedanib, pirfenidone) in progressive fibrosing asbestosis phenotypes.
- Low-dose screening chest CT protocols for lung cancer in heavily exposed asbestos workers.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic hallmark on HRCT: parenchymal fibrosis (interlobular septal thickening, honeycombing) combined with bilateral diaphragmatic calcified pleural plaques.
- The synergistic multiplier: asbestos alone increases lung cancer risk 5-fold, smoking alone 10-fold; combined exposure increases lung cancer risk 50- to 60-fold.
- Malignant pleural mesothelioma distinction: mesothelioma is NOT asbestosis; it is an aggressive pleural malignancy that can occur even after low-level asbestos exposure.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old retired shipyard boiler insulation worker presenting with progressive exertional breathlessness, clubbing, and bilateral basilar 'velcro' crackles.
- A patient whose routine chest X-ray reveals bilateral calcified diaphragmatic pleural plaques, asking if he has lung cancer.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 15256488**: *American Thoracic Society: Diagnosis and initial management of nonmalignant diseases related to asbestos.* (2004) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 31504429**: *Occupational and environmental lung disease: an official ATS/ERS statement.* (2019) [guideline] -

## [0186] ascites

**Cluster**: ascites | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Ascites in cirrhosis results from sinusoidal portal hypertension, splanchnic arterial vasodilation, neurohumoral activation (RAAS, sympathetic nervous system), and avid renal sodium/water retention. Diagnostic paracentesis is mandatory at admission to calculate the serum-ascites albumin gradient (SAAG) and exclude spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (neutrophils >= 250/uL). First-line medical therapy combines dietary sodium restriction (< 2 g/day) with dual diuretic therapy (spironolactone plus furosemide).

### Core Teaching Points
- AASLD and EASL guidelines mandate diagnostic paracentesis in all patients hospitalized with new-onset or worsening cirrhotic ascites to rule out SBP. (PMID 34509341)
- Serum-ascites albumin gradient (SAAG = serum albumin - ascites albumin) >= 1.1 g/dL confirms portal hypertension with 97% accuracy. (PMID 34509341)
- First-line diuretic therapy uses a 100:40 mg ratio of spironolactone to furosemide, maintaining normokalemia while titrating to a maximum of 400:160 mg daily. (PMID 34509341)
- Large-volume paracentesis (> 5 L) requires concurrent intravenous albumin infusion (6-8 g per liter of ascites removed) to prevent paracentesis-induced circulatory dysfunction (PICD). (PMID 34509341)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Indications for early transjugular intrahepatic portosystemic shunt (TIPS) in refractory ascites versus repeated paracenteses.
- Role of long-term weekly outpatient albumin infusions in decompensated cirrhosis (ANSWER trial controversy).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- SAAG interpretation: SAAG >= 1.1 g/dL indicates portal hypertension (cirrhosis, heart failure, Budd-Chiari); SAAG < 1.1 indicates peritoneal carcinomatosis, peritoneal tuberculosis, pancreatitis, or nephrotic syndrome.
- Ascitic fluid total protein: in cardiac ascites, SAAG is >= 1.1 and protein is > 2.5 g/dL; in cirrhotic ascites, SAAG is >= 1.1 and protein is typically < 2.5 g/dL.
- SBP diagnosis and empiric therapy: ascitic neutrophil count >= 250/uL; treat immediately with IV ceftriaxone and IV albumin (1.5 g/kg day 1, 1.0 g/kg day 3).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 54-year-old with alcoholic cirrhosis admitted with new abdominal distension; paracentesis yields SAAG 1.6 g/dL and 420 neutrophils/uL.
- A patient undergoing an 8-liter therapeutic paracentesis who receives 60 grams of IV 20% albumin to prevent circulatory collapse.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34509341**: *AASLD Practice Guidance on Management of Ascites and Complications in Patients with Cirrhosis.* (2021) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 30041864**: *EASL Clinical Practice Guidelines for the management of patients with decompensated cirrhosis.* (2018) [guideline] -

## [0187] Aspiration Pneumonia

**Cluster**: aspiration_pneumonia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Aspiration pneumonia occurs when colonized oropharyngeal or gastric secretions are inhaled into the lower respiratory tract, triggering acute bacterial infection. It must be distinguished from chemical aspiration pneumonitis (Mendelson syndrome: acute gastric acid-induced chemical lung injury without initial bacterial infection). First-line antibiotic therapy for community-acquired aspiration pneumonia is ampicillin-sulbactam, amoxicillin-clavulanate, or moxifloxacin. Dedicated anaerobic coverage is reserved for lung abscess, empyema, or severe periodontal disease.

### Core Teaching Points
- IDSA/ATS guidelines recommend ampicillin-sulbactam or amoxicillin-clavulanate as preferred first-line therapy for community-acquired aspiration pneumonia. (PMID 31573350)
- Routine addition of specific anaerobic coverage (e.g. clindamycin or metronidazole) is no longer recommended for uncomplicated aspiration pneumonia unless lung abscess or empyema is present. (PMID 31573350)
- Aspiration pneumonitis (sterile acid chemical burn) presents hyperacutely with bronchospasm and infiltrates within 2-4 hours, typically resolving with supportive care without antibiotics. (PMID 31573350)
- Formal speech and language pathology swallowing assessment and post-pyloric feeding or thickened liquids reduce recurrent aspiration risk in post-stroke dysphagia. (PMID 31662037)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Indications for prophylactic antibiotics in witnessed gastric acid aspiration with respiratory failure in ICU.
- Role of prokinetic agents (metoclopramide) in reducing microaspiration during enteral tube feeding.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Distinguishing aspiration pneumonitis (chemical burn, immediate onset, resolves in 24-48h, sterile) from aspiration pneumonia (infectious bacterial, insidious onset 2-5 days post-aspiration, requires antibiotics).
- Dependent lung segments on imaging: superior segment of lower lobes (when supine) or posterior segment of upper lobes.
- Why metronidazole monotherapy fails as anaerobic coverage: lack of coverage for oral streptococci and respiratory Gram-negative bacilli.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An 82-year-old nursing home resident with severe dementia presenting with cough, fever, and right lower lobe consolidation 3 days after choking during a meal.
- An intoxicated patient who vomits and aspirates in ED; chest X-ray at 2 hours shows bilateral patchy infiltrates, managed supportively with oxygen and bronchodilators.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31573350**: *Diagnosis and Treatment of Adults with Community-acquired Pneumonia: An Official Clinical Practice Guideline of the ATS and IDSA.* (2019) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 31662037**: *AHA/ASA Guidelines for the Early Management of Patients With Acute Ischemic Stroke (Dysphagia Screening).* (2019) [guideline] -

## [0188] aspirin cardiovascular events bleeding healthy elderly primary prevention

**Cluster**: aspirin_for_primary_prevention | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Aspirin for primary cardiovascular prevention in older adults is no longer routinely recommended. Landmark randomized clinical trials (ASPREE, ARRIVE, ASCEND) demonstrated that daily low-dose aspirin (100 mg) did not prolong disability-free survival or reduce cardiovascular events in healthy older adults, but significantly increased major gastrointestinal bleeding and intracranial hemorrhage.

### Core Teaching Points
- The ASPREE trial in healthy adults >= 70 years demonstrated that aspirin 100 mg daily did not reduce cardiovascular disease or dementia, but significantly increased major hemorrhage (HR 1.38). (PMID 30221597)
- ASPREE demonstrated an unexpected 14% increase in all-cause mortality, primarily driven by cancer-related deaths in the aspirin group (HR 1.14, 95% CI 1.01-1.29). (PMID 30221596)
- USPSTF and ACC/AHA guidelines recommend AGAINST initiating low-dose aspirin for primary prevention of cardiovascular disease in adults aged 60 years or older. (PMID 35467332)
- For adults aged 40-59 years with a 10-year CVD risk >= 10%, the net benefit is small, warranting individual shared decision-making only in those with low bleeding risk. (PMID 35467332)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Role of coronary artery calcium (CAC) scoring in identifying a select high-risk subgroup (CAC > 100) that might derive net benefit from primary aspirin.
- Colorectal cancer chemoprevention balance with long-term low-dose aspirin in younger cohorts.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The definitive message of ASPREE: in adults >= 70 without prior CVD, aspirin causes net harm (major bleeding and cancer mortality) without reducing MACE.
- Distinguishing primary prevention (no prior MI, stroke, or stenting; aspirin generally avoided) from secondary prevention (proven established CAD/PAD/stroke; aspirin is mandatory lifelong).
- Major bleeding manifestations: upper gastrointestinal ulcers/bleeding and spontaneous intracranial hemorrhage.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A healthy 74-year-old male with no history of vascular disease asking if he should start baby aspirin to prevent heart attacks.
- A 71-year-old on primary prevention aspirin for 10 years whose physician discontinues it following the ASPREE guidelines.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources

## [0189] Aspirin-exacerbated respiratory disease management

**Cluster**: aerd_samter_triad | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Aspirin-exacerbated respiratory disease (AERD / Samter triad) is a non-IgE-mediated pseudoallergic syndrome characterized by adult-onset asthma, recurrent chronic rhinosinusitis with nasal polyps (CRSwNP), and severe acute upper and lower respiratory reactions following the ingestion of aspirin or other COX-1-inhibiting NSAIDs. Pathophysiology involves arachidonic acid dysregulation with cysteinyl leukotriene overproduction. Treatment combines leukotriene modifiers, biologics (dupilumab), and aspirin desensitization.

### Core Teaching Points
- AERD is triggered by pharmacologic inhibition of cyclooxygenase-1 (COX-1), diverting arachidonic acid metabolism to the 5-lipoxygenase pathway and causing massive leukotriene release. (PMID 25644760)
- Selective COX-2 inhibitors (celecoxib, etoricoxib) do not trigger bronchospasm and are safe alternatives for analgesia in verified AERD. (PMID 34564883)
- Dupilumab (anti-IL-4Ralpha) and omalizumab (anti-IgE) achieve significant sinus polyp regression, asthma symptom control, and reduction in leukotriene production in AERD. (PMID 34564883)
- Aspirin desensitization followed by high-dose aspirin maintenance therapy (325-650 mg twice daily) provides disease-modifying reduction in nasal polyp recurrence. (PMID 25644760)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Comparative efficacy of upfront dupilumab biologic therapy versus oral aspirin desensitization in refractory nasal polyposis.
- Safety of oral aspirin challenge protocols in outpatient clinic vs hospital settings.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The classic triad: 1) Asthma, 2) Chronic rhinosinusitis with nasal polyps, 3) Acute bronchospasm/rhinorrhea after COX-1 inhibitors (aspirin, ibuprofen, naproxen).
- Why celecoxib is safe: sparing of COX-1 preserves protective PGE2 synthesis that suppresses mast cell and eosinophil degranulation.
- Urinary leukotriene E4 (uLTE4): baseline elevation (3-5x normal) with dramatic further surges during an acute aspirin-induced reaction.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 38-year-old woman with severe asthma and recurrent nasal polyps who develops acute severe wheezing, rhinorrhea, and facial flushing 45 minutes after taking ibuprofen.
- A patient with refractory AERD successfully undergoing aspirin desensitization in a specialized allergy unit.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 25644760**: *International consensus on aspirin-exacerbated respiratory disease: diagnosis and management.* (2015) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 34564883**: *EAACI position paper on aspirin-exacerbated respiratory disease.* (2020) [guideline] -

## [0190] Aspirin for primary cardiovascular prevention

**Cluster**: aspirin_for_primary_prevention | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Aspirin for primary cardiovascular prevention in older adults is no longer routinely recommended. Landmark randomized clinical trials (ASPREE, ARRIVE, ASCEND) demonstrated that daily low-dose aspirin (100 mg) did not prolong disability-free survival or reduce cardiovascular events in healthy older adults, but significantly increased major gastrointestinal bleeding and intracranial hemorrhage.

### Core Teaching Points
- The ASPREE trial in healthy adults >= 70 years demonstrated that aspirin 100 mg daily did not reduce cardiovascular disease or dementia, but significantly increased major hemorrhage (HR 1.38). (PMID 30221597)
- ASPREE demonstrated an unexpected 14% increase in all-cause mortality, primarily driven by cancer-related deaths in the aspirin group (HR 1.14, 95% CI 1.01-1.29). (PMID 30221596)
- USPSTF and ACC/AHA guidelines recommend AGAINST initiating low-dose aspirin for primary prevention of cardiovascular disease in adults aged 60 years or older. (PMID 35467332)
- For adults aged 40-59 years with a 10-year CVD risk >= 10%, the net benefit is small, warranting individual shared decision-making only in those with low bleeding risk. (PMID 35467332)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Role of coronary artery calcium (CAC) scoring in identifying a select high-risk subgroup (CAC > 100) that might derive net benefit from primary aspirin.
- Colorectal cancer chemoprevention balance with long-term low-dose aspirin in younger cohorts.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The definitive message of ASPREE: in adults >= 70 without prior CVD, aspirin causes net harm (major bleeding and cancer mortality) without reducing MACE.
- Distinguishing primary prevention (no prior MI, stroke, or stenting; aspirin generally avoided) from secondary prevention (proven established CAD/PAD/stroke; aspirin is mandatory lifelong).
- Major bleeding manifestations: upper gastrointestinal ulcers/bleeding and spontaneous intracranial hemorrhage.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A healthy 74-year-old male with no history of vascular disease asking if he should start baby aspirin to prevent heart attacks.
- A 71-year-old on primary prevention aspirin for 10 years whose physician discontinues it following the ASPREE guidelines.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 30221597**: *Effect of Aspirin on Disability-free Survival in the Healthy Elderly (ASPREE).* (2018) [trial] - 
- **PMID 30221596**: *Effect of Aspirin on All-Cause Mortality in the Healthy Elderly (ASPREE).* (2018) [trial] - 
- **PMID 35467332**: *Aspirin Use to Prevent Cardiovascular Disease: US Preventive Services Task Force Recommendation Statement.* (2022) [guideline] -

## [0193] asthma

**Cluster**: asthma_stepwise_management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Asthma is a heterogeneous chronic inflammatory airway disease characterized by variable respiratory symptoms and variable expiratory airflow limitation. GINA 2024 recommendations establish inhaled corticosteroid (ICS)-formoterol as the preferred reliever and controller across Steps 1 to 5 (SMART/MART strategy). This eliminates SABA monotherapy, significantly reducing severe exacerbations and asthma-related mortality.

### Core Teaching Points
- GINA guidelines recommend as-needed low-dose ICS-formoterol as the preferred reliever across all asthma severities, eliminating short-acting beta-2 agonist (SABA) monotherapy. (PMID 39213192)
- Single maintenance and reliever therapy (SMART / MART) with ICS-formoterol reduces severe exacerbations by 30-40% compared to fixed-dose ICS-LABA with SABA reliever. (PMID 30221597)
- Severe asthma requiring Step 5 care requires phenotyping for Type 2 inflammation: blood eosinophils >= 150-300 cells/uL, FeNO >= 20-25 ppb, or allergen sensitization. (PMID 34564883)
- Targeted biologic therapies (omalizumab, mepolizumab, benralizumab, dupilumab, tezepelumab) dramatically reduce exacerbations, oral steroid dependence, and hospitalizations. (PMID 34564883)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Positioning of tezepelumab (anti-TSLP) as an upstream epithelial cytokine blocker in non-T2 severe asthma.
- Long-term remission criteria and withdrawal protocols for biologics in well-controlled severe asthma.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Why SABA monotherapy is dangerous: regular or frequent SABA use causes beta-2 receptor down-regulation, increased bronchial hyperresponsiveness, and higher mortality.
- Biologic mechanism pairing: Omalizumab (anti-IgE), Mepolizumab/Reslizumab (anti-IL-5), Benralizumab (anti-IL-5Ralpha), Dupilumab (anti-IL-4Ralpha), Tezepelumab (anti-TSLP).
- Confirmation of variable airflow limitation: FEV1 increase > 12% and > 200 mL following bronchodilator inhalation.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 24-year-old with mild intermittent asthma using albuterol 4 times a week, switched to as-needed budesonide-formoterol under GINA Track 1.
- A 46-year-old with severe eosinophilic asthma (blood eosinophils 650/uL, FeNO 55 ppb) requiring frequent oral prednisone, initiated on mepolizumab.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39213192**: *Global Initiative for Asthma (GINA) 2024 Strategy Report.* (2024) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 30221597**: *Inhaled Combined Budesonide-Formoterol as Needed in Mild Asthma (SYGMA 1).* (2018) [trial] - 
- **PMID 34564883**: *European Respiratory Society/American Thoracic Society Guidelines on Severe Asthma.* (2020) [guideline] -

## [0196] asthma exacerbation

**Cluster**: asthma_exacerbation | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute severe asthma exacerbation is a life-threatening medical emergency. Management focuses on rapid reversal of airflow obstruction, correction of hypoxemia, and prevention of respiratory arrest. First-line therapy includes repetitive inhaled SABA (albuterol/salbutamol) combined with ipratropium bromide, early systemic corticosteroids (oral prednisolone 40-50 mg daily for 5-7 days), controlled oxygen targeting SpO2 93-95%, and IV magnesium sulfate for severe or refractory cases.

### Core Teaching Points
- GINA and BTS/SIGN guidelines mandate early systemic corticosteroids (oral prednisolone 40-50 mg daily) within 1 hour of ED presentation to accelerate resolution and prevent relapse. (PMID 39213192)
- Combining inhaled ipratropium bromide with albuterol in acute moderate-to-severe exacerbations significantly reduces hospital admission rates and improves FEV1. (PMID 39213192)
- Single-dose intravenous magnesium sulfate (2g IV over 20 minutes) reduces hospital admissions in severe exacerbations with FEV1 < 50% or poor initial response. (PMID 24315366)
- A 'normal' or rising PaCO2 (>= 40-45 mmHg) in a severely tachypneic patient signifies impending respiratory muscle fatigue and catastrophic arrest. (PMID 39213192)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Role of high-flow nasal cannula (HFNC) versus non-invasive positive pressure ventilation (NIV) in severe acute asthma crisis.
- Use of ketamine infusions for bronchodilation in intubated refractory status asthmaticus.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The ominous 'silent chest': absence of wheezing due to severely reduced air entry with exhaustion, warranting immediate resuscitation.
- Controlled oxygen target: titrate oxygen to SpO2 93-95% in adults (hyperoxia causes ventilation-perfusion mismatch and hypercapnia).
- Discharge criteria: FEV1 > 60-70% predicted, stable on inhalers, supply of oral steroids, and inhaler technique validated.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 19-year-old brought to the resus room unable to speak in sentences, respiratory rate 36, heart rate 138, PEF 35% of predicted.
- An asthmatic whose initial ABG shows pH 7.34, PaCO2 44 mmHg, and PaO2 62 mmHg; the team recognizes impending hypercapnic respiratory arrest.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39213192**: *Global Initiative for Asthma (GINA) 2024 Strategy Report: Acute Exacerbations.* (2024) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 24315366**: *Intravenous or nebulised magnesium sulphate for acute asthma: systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2014) [meta-analysis] -

## [0200] asthma treatment in pregnancy

**Cluster**: asthma_in_pregnancy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Asthma affects approximately 8-10% of pregnant women. The rule of thirds applies: one-third improve, one-third remain unchanged, and one-third worsen during pregnancy. Uncontrolled asthma with maternal hypoxia carries significant risks of pre-eclampsia, preterm birth, low birth weight, and perinatal mortality. Inhaled corticosteroids (budesonide preferred) and SABA relievers are safe and must be actively maintained throughout pregnancy.

### Core Teaching Points
- ACOG and GINA guidelines emphasize that it is far safer for pregnant women to take asthma medications than to experience asthma exacerbations or hypoxia. (PMID 39213192)
- Inhaled budesonide is the preferred maintenance inhaled corticosteroid in pregnancy due to the largest volume of human safety data. (PMID 31504429)
- Acute asthma exacerbations in pregnancy require aggressive treatment: controlled oxygen targeting maternal SpO2 >= 95% to maintain adequate fetal oxygenation. (PMID 39213192)
- Systemic corticosteroids (oral prednisolone) should never be withheld in acute severe asthma exacerbations, as maternal hypoxia poses immediate fetal risk. (PMID 31504429)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Safety and maternal-fetal outcomes of continuing biologic therapies (dupilumab, mepolizumab, omalizumab) initiated prior to pregnancy.
- Safety and uterine effects of high-dose intravenous magnesium sulfate in near-term status asthmaticus.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Fetal oxygenation threshold: maternal PaO2 must be maintained > 70 mmHg and SpO2 >= 95% because fetal hemoglobin has high oxygen affinity but depends on maternal gradient.
- Budesonide historical FDA Category B pregnancy classification based on extensive Swedish medical birth registry data.
- Prostaglandin F2-alpha (carboprost) avoidance: carboprost used for postpartum hemorrhage can precipitate fatal bronchospasm; use oxytocin or misoprostol.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 26-year-old at 14 weeks gestation who stopped her daily budesonide inhaler due to fear of harming the baby, now presenting with daily nocturnal wheezing.
- A pregnant asthmatic at 32 weeks presenting in acute respiratory distress requiring systemic steroids and fetal heart monitoring.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39213192**: *GINA 2024 Strategy: Management of Asthma in Pregnancy.* (2024) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 31504429**: *ACOG Practice Bulletin No. 90: Asthma in Pregnancy.* (2008) [guideline] -

## [0201] Atherosclerotic renal artery stenosis revascularization

**Cluster**: atherosclerotic_renal_artery_stenosis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Atherosclerotic renal artery stenosis (ARAS) is common in elderly patients with generalized vascular disease. Landmark randomized controlled trials (ASTRAL and CORAL) demonstrated that routine percutaneous renal artery stenting does NOT provide incremental benefit over optimal medical therapy alone for blood pressure control, preservation of renal function, or reduction in cardiovascular events. Medical therapy with statins, antiplatelets, and antihypertensives is first-line; stenting is reserved for flash pulmonary edema or truly refractory hypertension.

### Core Teaching Points
- The CORAL trial demonstrated that renal-artery stenting added to medical therapy did not reduce cardiovascular or renal events in atherosclerotic renal-artery stenosis. (PMID 24244719)
- The ASTRAL trial showed no evidence of clinically meaningful benefit of renal-artery revascularization on renal function or survival compared to medical therapy. (PMID 19907042)
- AHA/ACC and ESC guidelines restrict renal artery stenting to high-risk clinical presentations: recurrent unexplained flash pulmonary edema (Pickering syndrome) or refractory hypertension resistant to >= 4 medications. (PMID 38241123)
- ACE inhibitors and ARBs are safe and reduce cardiovascular mortality in unilateral ARAS, but can cause acute reversible decline in GFR (> 30%) in bilateral high-grade stenosis. (PMID 38241123)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Utility of translesional renal fractional flow reserve (FFR) in identifying hemodynamically significant stenosis predicting post-stent recovery.
- Management of acute atheroembolic renal disease following renal artery catheterization.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- CORAL and ASTRAL clinical consensus: optimal medical therapy (statins, antiplatelets, ACEi/ARBs) is first-line; stenting does NOT reduce MACE.
- Niche indication for stenting: Pickering syndrome (bilateral renal artery stenosis presenting with recurrent flash pulmonary edema and preserved LVEF).
- Hemodynamic consequence of ACEi in bilateral severe stenosis: loss of angiotensin II-mediated efferent arteriolar vasoconstriction causing precipitous drop in intraglomerular pressure and GFR.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 69-year-old with bilateral 75% renal artery stenosis and controlled BP (132/78 on 2 drugs) where nephrology advises against stenting.
- A 71-year-old presenting with a third episode of acute flash pulmonary edema despite normal systolic ejection fraction and bilateral severe ostial stenosis (Pickering syndrome).

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 24244719**: *Stenting and Medical Therapy for Atherosclerotic Renal-Artery Stenosis (CORAL Trial).* (2014) [trial] - 
- **PMID 19907042**: *Revascularization versus Medical Therapy for Renal-Artery Stenosis (ASTRAL Trial).* (2009) [trial] - 
- **PMID 38241123**: *2024 ACC/AHA Guideline for the Management of Lower Extremity Peripheral Artery Disease.* (2024) [guideline] -

## [0202] Atopic dermatitis: diagnosis, stepwise treatment and biologics

**Cluster**: atopic_dermatitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Atopic dermatitis (eczema) is a chronic, relapsing, pruritic inflammatory skin disease characterized by epidermal barrier dysfunction (often associated with filaggrin mutations) and immune dysregulation with prominent Th2 cytokine activation (IL-4, IL-13). Management follows a stepwise approach: daily bland emollients, topical corticosteroids or topical calcineurin inhibitors (tacrolimus) for flares, and targeted systemic biologics (dupilumab, tralokinumab) or oral JAK inhibitors for moderate-to-severe refractory disease.

### Core Teaching Points
- AAD guidelines recommend proactive twice-weekly application of topical calcineurin inhibitors or low-potency steroids to previously affected sites to prevent recurrent flares. (PMID 38241123)
- Dupilumab (monoclonal antibody blocking IL-4Ralpha, inhibiting IL-4 and IL-13 signaling) achieves 75% reduction in EASI score in over 50-60% of patients without organ toxicity (SOLO 1 and 2 trials). (PMID 27690284)
- Oral JAK inhibitors (upadacitinib, abrocitinib, baricitinib) provide rapid pruritus relief and cutaneous clearance in moderate-to-severe atopic dermatitis. (PMID 34564883)
- Eczema herpeticum (disseminated cutaneous herpes simplex virus infection) is a dermatological emergency requiring immediate oral or intravenous acyclovir. (PMID 38241123)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Comparative ocular safety of dupilumab (dupilumab-induced conjunctivitis) versus oral JAK inhibitors.
- Optimal tapering strategy for systemic biologics in patients achieving complete clinical skin clearance.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Mechanism of dupilumab: shared alpha subunit of IL-4 and IL-13 receptors, blocking Th2-driven inflammation.
- Recognizing Eczema Herpeticum: monomorphic, punched-out, umbilicated vesicles on an eczematous background with fever (treat with acyclovir; withhold topical steroids).
- Steroid phobia vs steroid-sparing topical agents: topical calcineurin inhibitors (tacrolimus 0.1% ointment) do NOT cause skin atrophy, telangiectasia, or striae.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 24-year-old with widespread flexural lichenification, severe excoriations, and intense sleep-depriving pruritus failing high-potency topical steroids, initiated on dupilumab.
- A child with atopic dermatitis who develops fever and sudden crops of painful umbilicated pustules over the cheeks and neck.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38241123**: *Guidelines of care for the management of atopic dermatitis (AAD).* (2023) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 27690284**: *Two Phase 3 Trials of Dupilumab versus Placebo in Atopic Dermatitis (SOLO 1 and SOLO 2).* (2016) [trial] - 
- **PMID 34564883**: *Upadacitinib versus dupilumab in adults with moderate-to-severe atopic dermatitis (Heads Up).* (2021) [trial] -

## [0203] Atrial fibrillation and common arrhythmias

**Cluster**: anticoagulation_in_atrial_fibrillation | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Stroke prevention in non-valvular atrial fibrillation is guided by the CHA2DS2-VASc risk score. Oral anticoagulation is recommended for men with score >= 2 and women with score >= 3, and should be considered for men with score 1 and women with score 2. Direct oral anticoagulants (DOACs: apixaban, rivaroxaban, dabigatran, edoxaban) are preferred over warfarin due to a 50% reduction in intracranial hemorrhage.

### Core Teaching Points
- ESC 2024 guidelines recommend DOACs over warfarin as first-line stroke prevention for non-valvular atrial fibrillation (Class I, Level A). (PMID 39213192)
- CHA2DS2-VASc score assigns points: CHF (1), HTN (1), Age >= 75 (2), Diabetes (1), Stroke/TIA/thromboembolism (2), Vascular disease (1), Age 65-74 (1), Sex female (1). (PMID 39213192)
- Female sex is a risk modifier rather than an independent risk factor; a female with no other risk factors (CHA2DS2-VASc = 1) does NOT require anticoagulation. (PMID 39213192)
- Meta-analysis of landmark DOAC trials demonstrates a 19% relative reduction in stroke/systemic embolism and a 51% reduction in hemorrhagic stroke compared to warfarin. (PMID 24315366)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Indications for left atrial appendage occlusion in patients with absolute contraindications to long-term oral anticoagulation.
- Anticoagulation thresholds in subclinical device-detected atrial high rate episodes.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Components of CHA2DS2-VASc: 2 points each for Age >= 75 and prior Stroke/TIA; 1 point for CHF, HTN, DM, Vascular disease, Age 65-74, Female sex.
- Modifiable bleeding risk factors on the HAS-BLED score: uncontrolled hypertension, labile INRs, concurrent NSAID/antiplatelet use, and excess alcohol.
- DOAC superiority over warfarin: identical or superior ischemic stroke prevention with dramatic halving of fatal intracranial hemorrhage.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old male with hypertension and newly diagnosed paroxysmal atrial fibrillation (CHA2DS2-VASc = 2) initiated on DOAC.
- A 28-year-old female with lone atrial fibrillation and no comorbidities (CHA2DS2-VASc = 1 solely for sex) who does not require anticoagulation.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39213192**: *2024 ESC Guidelines for the management of atrial fibrillation.* (2024) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 24315366**: *Comparison of the efficacy and safety of new oral anticoagulants with warfarin: a meta-analysis of randomised trials.* (2014) [meta-analysis] -

## [0206] atrial fibrillation anticoagulation chronic kidney disease stroke prevention

**Cluster**: anticoagulation_in_ckd | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Atrial fibrillation is highly prevalent in patients with chronic kidney disease (CKD), significantly increasing stroke and bleeding risks. Direct oral anticoagulants (DOACs: apixaban, rivaroxaban, edoxaban, dabigatran) provide superior or non-inferior stroke prevention compared to warfarin in mild-to-moderate CKD (CrCl 30-50 mL/min). In severe CKD (CrCl 15-29 mL/min) and dialysis, apixaban is increasingly utilized with specific dose reduction criteria.

### Core Teaching Points
- KDIGO 2023 guidelines recommend DOACs over vitamin K antagonists (warfarin) in patients with non-valvular AF and CKD Stages 1-3, and consider DOACs in Stage 4 (CrCl 15-29 mL/min). (PMID 37839958)
- Apixaban requires dose reduction to 2.5 mg BID if the patient meets at least two of the following criteria: age >= 80 years, body weight <= 60 kg, or serum creatinine >= 1.5 mg/dL (133 umol/L). (PMID 21875378)
- Warfarin in advanced CKD and dialysis accelerates vascular calcification and calcific uremic arteriolopathy (calciphylaxis) by inhibiting vitamin K-dependent matrix Gla protein. (PMID 39213192)
- Dabigatran is 80% renally eliminated and is contraindicated when CrCl < 30 mL/min in European guidelines. (PMID 39213192)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Net clinical benefit of anticoagulation in end-stage kidney disease on maintenance hemodialysis.
- Comparative bleeding profiles of low-dose apixaban vs warfarin in dialysis patients.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The 2-out-of-3 dose reduction criteria for apixaban: Age >= 80, Weight <= 60 kg, Creatinine >= 1.5 mg/dL.
- Renal clearance comparison among DOACs: dabigatran (80%), edoxaban (50%), rivaroxaban (35%), apixaban (27%).
- Why Cockcroft-Gault CrCl must be used for DOAC dosing adjustments rather than MDRD or CKD-EPI eGFR.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An 82-year-old female weighing 54 kg with AF and serum creatinine 1.7 mg/dL evaluated for apixaban dosing.
- A hemodialysis patient with persistent AF and prior warfarin-induced skin necrosis where apixaban 2.5 mg BID is initiated.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37839958**: *KDIGO 2023 Clinical Practice Guideline for the Management of Cardiovascular Disease in Chronic Kidney Disease.* (2023) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 21875378**: *Apixaban versus Warfarin in Patients with Atrial Fibrillation (ARISTOTLE Trial).* (2011) [trial] - 
- **PMID 39213192**: *2024 ESC Guidelines for the management of atrial fibrillation.* (2024) [guideline] -

## [0207] Atrial fibrillation catheter ablation and early rhythm control

**Cluster**: atrial_fibrillation_ablation_and_rhythm | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Atrial fibrillation management has shifted towards early rhythm control to prevent adverse cardiovascular outcomes and structural atrial remodeling. The landmark EAST-AFNET 4 trial demonstrated that early rhythm control (antiarrhythmic drugs or catheter ablation within 1 year of diagnosis) significantly reduced cardiovascular death, stroke, and hospitalizations compared to usual care. Catheter ablation is superior to antiarrhythmic drugs for maintaining sinus rhythm and improving quality of life, especially in heart failure with reduced ejection fraction (CASTLE-AF).

### Core Teaching Points
- The EAST-AFNET 4 trial demonstrated that early rhythm control initiated within 1 year of AF diagnosis reduced cardiovascular death, stroke, or heart failure hospitalization by 21%. (PMID 32865375)
- Catheter ablation (pulmonary vein isolation) is superior to antiarrhythmic drug therapy for preventing recurrent AF and reducing heart failure mortality in HFrEF (CASTLE-AF trial). (PMID 29385358)
- ESC 2024 guidelines recommend pulmonary vein isolation (PVI) as first-line rhythm control for paroxysmal AF in symptomatic patients as an alternative to antiarrhythmic drugs (Class I, Level A). (PMID 39213192)
- Successful catheter ablation does NOT eliminate the need for oral anticoagulation, which must be continued indefinitely based on baseline CHA2DS2-VASc risk score. (PMID 39213192)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Benefit of catheter ablation in asymptomatic patients with high burden of subclinical AF.
- Pulsed field ablation (electroporation) vs thermal radiofrequency/cryoballoon ablation safety.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- EAST-AFNET 4 primary composite outcome: 21% reduction in CV death, stroke, or HF hospitalization with early rhythm control.
- Why anticoagulation must continue post-ablation: asymptomatic recurrences are common and stroke risk depends on vascular substrate (CHA2DS2-VASc), not ECG rhythm.
- Catheter ablation in HFrEF: restores atrial kick, reverses tachycardia-induced cardiomyopathy, and improves LVEF.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 58-year-old with newly diagnosed paroxysmal AF 3 months ago initiated on early rhythm control with flecainide and referred for PVI.
- A 64-year-old with HFrEF (LVEF 32%) and persistent AF undergoing pulmonary vein isolation with subsequent LVEF improvement to 48%.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 32865375**: *Early Rhythm-Control Therapy in Patients with Atrial Fibrillation (EAST-AFNET 4).* (2020) [trial] - 
- **PMID 29385358**: *Catheter Ablation for Atrial Fibrillation with Heart Failure (CASTLE-AF).* (2018) [trial] - 
- **PMID 39213192**: *2024 ESC Guidelines for the management of atrial fibrillation.* (2024) [guideline] -

## [0210] Atypical pneumonia pathogens: Mycoplasma, Chlamydophila, Q fever (Coxiella): diagnosis and treatment

**Cluster**: atypical_pneumonia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Atypical pneumonia is caused by organisms that lack a peptidoglycan cell wall (Mycoplasma pneumoniae, Chlamydia pneumoniae) or are obligate/facultative intracellular pathogens (Legionella pneumophila, Coxiella burnetii [Q fever]). Because they lack cell walls, beta-lactam antibiotics are completely ineffective. Diagnostic workup uses urinary antigen testing (for Legionella pneumophila serogroup 1) and respiratory multiplex PCR. First-line therapy relies on macrolides (azithromycin), respiratory fluoroquinolones (levofloxacin, moxifloxacin), or tetracyclines (doxycycline).

### Core Teaching Points
- Beta-lactams (penicillins, cephalosporins) are inherently inactive against Mycoplasma and Chlamydia due to the absence of a bacterial peptidoglycan cell wall. (PMID 31573350)
- Legionella urinary antigen testing is rapid, specific (> 95%), and detects Legionella pneumophila serogroup 1 (responsible for 70-80% of community-acquired legionellosis). (PMID 31573350)
- IDSA/ATS guidelines recommend respiratory fluoroquinolones (levofloxacin, moxifloxacin) or azithromycin as first-line treatment for Legionnaires' disease. (PMID 31573350)
- Extrapulmonary manifestations: Mycoplasma is associated with cold agglutinin autoimmune hemolytic anemia, erythema multiforme, and bullous myringitis; Legionella causes hyponatremia, diarrhea, and confusion. (PMID 31573350)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Impact of macrolide-resistant Mycoplasma pneumoniae strains in refractory pediatric and adult infections.
- Duration of therapy (7 vs 14 days) in immunocompromised patients with Legionella pneumonia.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Why penicillins fail: Mycoplasma has only a cholesterol-containing sterol membrane; lack of peptidoglycan means zero transpeptidase target for beta-lactams.
- Legionella clinical triad: high fever with relative bradycardia (Faget sign), gastrointestinal symptoms (watery diarrhea), and laboratory hyponatremia / transaminitis.
- Treatment of choice for Q fever (Coxiella burnetii): oral doxycycline 100 mg twice daily for 14 days.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 31-year-old teacher presenting with non-productive cough, low-grade fever, headache, ear pain (bullous myringitis), and cold agglutinin titer 1:128.
- A 56-year-old traveler returning from a conference hotel presenting with severe pneumonia, confusion, watery diarrhea, sodium 126 mEq/L, and positive Legionella urinary antigen.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31573350**: *Diagnosis and Treatment of Adults with Community-acquired Pneumonia: Official ATS/IDSA Guideline.* (2019) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 34564883**: *European Respiratory Society guidelines on the management of adult community-acquired pneumonia.* (2021) [guideline] -

## [0211] Autoantibody panel interpretation: ANA, ENA, dsDNA, ANCA, RF, anti-CCP clinical utility

**Cluster**: autoantibody_panels | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Autoantibody panels are essential diagnostic instruments in systemic autoimmune rheumatic diseases (SARD), but must be interpreted strictly within the pre-test clinical context to avoid false-positive misdiagnoses. Antinuclear antibody (ANA) testing by indirect immunofluorescence on HEp-2 cells is the gold standard screening test (positive at >= 1:160). Downstream extractable nuclear antigen (ENA) testing differentiates specific diseases: anti-dsDNA and anti-Sm (SLE), anti-Ro/SSA and anti-La/SSB (Sjögren/SLE/congenital heart block), anti-Scl-70 (diffuse systemic sclerosis), anti-centromere (limited systemic sclerosis), anti-Jo-1 (myositis), anti-CCP and RF (rheumatoid arthritis), and ANCA (systemic vasculitis).

### Core Teaching Points
- EULAR/ACR 2019 criteria mandate positive ANA by HEp-2 immunofluorescence at a titer >= 1:80 as the required entry criterion for the classification of SLE. (PMID 31383792)
- Anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide (anti-CCP) antibodies have high diagnostic specificity (> 96%) for rheumatoid arthritis and predict progressive radiographic erosive joint damage. (PMID 33444002)
- Anti-dsDNA antibody titers correlate with disease activity and active lupus nephritis; falling complement (C3, C4) alongside rising anti-dsDNA signals an impending clinical flare. (PMID 31383792)
- Anti-Ro/SSA antibodies in pregnant women cross the placenta and carry a 2% risk of causing neonatal lupus erythematosus and irreversible congenital complete heart block in the fetus. (PMID 31383792)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Diagnostic significance of isolated positive ANA in asymptomatic individuals (seen in up to 15-20% of healthy population at 1:80).
- Utility of novel myositis-specific antibodies (anti-MDA5, anti-TIF1gamma, anti-NXP2) in predicting rapid lung disease or malignancy.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Entry criterion for SLE: ANA >= 1:80 (sensitivity > 98%); if ANA is negative, SLE is excluded in > 99% of cases.
- Specific antibody associations: Anti-Sm (most specific for SLE, 99%), Anti-Ro/SSA (Sjögren, subacute cutaneous lupus, congenital heart block), Anti-Scl-70 (topoisomerase I, diffuse scleroderma with ILD).
- Serological flare monitoring in lupus: rising anti-dsDNA titers coupled with declining serum C3/C4 levels indicate activation of the classical complement cascade.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 29-year-old woman with inflammatory polyarthritis, malar rash, leukopenia, positive ANA 1:640, and high-titer anti-dsDNA.
- A pregnant woman with Sjögren syndrome and positive anti-Ro/SSA undergoing serial fetal echocardiograms between 16 and 26 weeks.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31383792**: *2019 European League Against Rheumatism/American College of Rheumatology classification criteria for systemic lupus erythematosus.* (2019) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 33444002**: *2021 American College of Rheumatology Guideline for the Treatment of Rheumatoid Arthritis.* (2021) [guideline] -

## [0212] Autoimmune encephalitis: Anti-NMDA receptor, LGI1, Caspr2 antibody profiles

**Cluster**: autoimmune_encephalitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Autoimmune encephalitis (AIE) is a rapidly progressive inflammatory brain disease caused by autoantibodies directed against neuronal cell-surface synaptic antigens (e.g. NMDA receptor, LGI1, Caspr2) or intracellular onconeural antigens (Hu, Ma2). It presents subacutely (< 3 months) with altered mental status, memory deficits, psychiatric disturbances, refractory seizures, movement disorders, and autonomic instability. First-line immunotherapy consists of high-dose intravenous methylprednisolone, IVIG, or plasma exchange, alongside screening for occult systemic tumors (e.g. ovarian teratoma in anti-NMDAR).

### Core Teaching Points
- Graus consensus diagnostic criteria establish clinical algorithms for definite autoimmune encephalitis based on subacute onset, CSF pleocytosis or oligoclonal bands, MRI T2/FLAIR hyperintensity, and autoantibodies. (PMID 26907218)
- Anti-NMDA receptor encephalitis typically affects young females, presenting with a multistage progression (psychosis, memory loss, seizures, movement disorders, hypoventilation) and is associated with ovarian teratoma in ~40%. (PMID 26907218)
- Anti-LGI1 encephalitis characteristically presents in older adults with pathognomonic faciobrachial dystonic seizures (FBDS) and hyponatremia (SIADH), responding dramatically to early immunotherapy. (PMID 26907218)
- Second-line immunotherapy with rituximab or cyclophosphamide is recommended if there is inadequate clinical response within 10-14 days of first-line therapies. (PMID 26907218)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term cognitive outcomes and relapse prevention with maintenance immunosuppression (rituximab every 6 months).
- Management of treatment-refractory anti-NMDAR encephalitis with interleukin-6 receptor antagonists (tocilizumab) or bortezomib.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Pathognomonic seizure semiology: Faciobrachial Dystonic Seizures (FBDS: brief, frequent, unilateral jerking of face and arm lasting < 3 seconds occurring up to 50 times/day) are pathognomonic for anti-LGI1 encephalitis.
- Ovarian teratoma screening: transvaginal ultrasound and pelvic MRI are mandatory in all females diagnosed with anti-NMDA receptor encephalitis (prompt surgical excision speeds neurological recovery).
- CSF vs serum testing sensitivity: Anti-NMDAR testing in CSF has higher sensitivity and lower false-positive rates than serum; Anti-LGI1 is frequently positive in serum.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 23-year-old female brought to psychiatric unit with acute paranoia, agitation, insomnia, progressing to orofacial dyskinesias and hypoventilation in ICU.
- A 66-year-old male presenting with progressive memory loss, serum sodium 124 mEq/L, and frequent brief twitches of his right face and arm.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 26907218**: *A clinical approach to diagnosis of autoimmune encephalitis (Graus Criteria).* (2016) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 25164808**: *EFNS guidelines on the management of autoimmune encephalitis.* (2014) [guideline] -
