## [0213] Autoimmune Gastritis and Pernicious Anaemia

**Cluster**: autoimmune_gastritis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Autoimmune gastritis (AIG) is a chronic, immune-mediated, organ-specific gastritis directed against gastric parietal cells and their secretory products (H+/K+-ATPase and intrinsic factor). It causes selective destruction of the oxyntic mucosa in the gastric body and fundus, sparing the antrum. Destruction of parietal cells causes achlorhydria (triggering hypergastrinemia and enterochromaffin-like [ECL] cell hyperplasia) and loss of intrinsic factor, producing vitamin B12 malabsorption (pernicious anaemia). Management involves lifelong parenteral or high-dose oral vitamin B12 replacement, iron repletion, and endoscopic surveillance for gastric neuroendocrine tumors (NETs) and adenocarcinoma.

### Core Teaching Points
- Destruction of gastric parietal cells leads to achlorhydria, high fasting serum gastrin, and lack of intrinsic factor causing vitamin B12 deficiency (pernicious anemia). (PMID 34526361)
- Anti-parietal cell antibodies (APCA) are sensitive (80-90%) but less specific; anti-intrinsic factor antibodies (AIFA) have high diagnostic specificity (> 98%) for pernicious anemia. (PMID 34526361)
- Endoscopic surveillance (MAPS II guidelines) is recommended every 3-5 years due to increased risks of Type 1 gastric neuroendocrine tumors (from hypergastrinemia) and gastric adenocarcinoma. (PMID 30041864)
- Vitamin B12 deficiency produces megaloblastic anemia and subacute combined degeneration of the spinal cord (posterior and lateral column demyelination); folic acid alone masks anemia while allowing neurological damage to progress. (PMID 34526361)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- High-dose oral vitamin B12 (1000-2000 ug daily via passive non-intrinsic factor absorption) vs intramuscular injections in maintenance.
- Optimal management and endoscopic resection criteria for multifocal Type 1 gastric NETs.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The folate trap: treating megaloblastic anemia with folate without B12 corrects the CBC but precipitates or accelerates irreversible subacute combined degeneration.
- Histological sparing: AIG involves the gastric body and fundus (oxyntic mucosa) while sparing the antrum (unlike H. pylori pangastritis).
- Concurrent autoimmune conditions: up to 30-40% of AIG patients have autoimmune thyroid disease (Hashimoto thyroiditis; Schmidt syndrome/autoimmune polyglandular syndrome).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 58-year-old female with vitiligo presenting with fatigue, paresthesias in a glove-and-stocking distribution, loss of vibratory sense, MCV 114 fL, and low B12.
- A patient with macrocytic anemia and positive anti-intrinsic factor antibodies whose gastroscopy reveals marked body atrophy and three 4 mm polypoid Type 1 NETs.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34526361**: *British Society of Gastroenterology guidelines for the management of iron deficiency and autoimmune gastritis.* (2021) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 30041864**: *Management of epithelial precancerous conditions and lesions in the stomach (MAPS II): European guidelines.* (2019) [guideline] -

## [0214] Autoimmune Haemolytic Anaemia

**Cluster**: autoimmune_haemolytic_anaemia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Autoimmune haemolytic anaemia (AIHA) is an immune-mediated disorder characterized by antibody-mediated destruction of red blood cells. It is classified into warm AIHA (wAIHA; 70-80% of cases, IgG-mediated, optimal binding at 37C, extravascular clearance in the spleen) and cold agglutinin disease (CAD; 15-20%, IgM-mediated, binding at 0-4C, classical complement-mediated intravascular and hepatic clearance). Diagnosis requires hemolytic markers (elevated LDH, indirect bilirubin, reticulocytosis, undetectable haptoglobin) and a positive direct antiglobulin test (DAT/Coombs). First-line therapy for wAIHA is oral prednisone (1 mg/kg/day); second-line is rituximab.

### Core Teaching Points
- First-line therapy for warm AIHA is oral prednisone (1 mg/kg/day for 3-4 weeks, then slow taper), achieving initial response in 70-80% of patients. (PMID 31504429)
- Rituximab (375 mg/m2 weekly x 4 or fixed 1000 mg x 2) is the preferred second-line therapy for warm AIHA refractory to or dependent on corticosteroids. (PMID 31504429)
- Cold agglutinin disease is mediated by IgM antibodies that fix complement; corticosteroids and splenectomy are clinically ineffective and should not be used in primary CAD. (PMID 31504429)
- Sutimlimab, a humanized monoclonal antibody inhibiting complement component C1s, produces rapid and sustained cessation of complement-mediated hemolysis in CAD (CARDINAL trial). (PMID 35320658)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Indications and timing for splenectomy in multirefractory warm AIHA in the modern biologic era.
- Venous thromboembolism prophylaxis thresholds during acute severe active hemolytic crises.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Direct Antiglobulin Test (DAT) pattern differentiation: Warm AIHA = positive for IgG alone or IgG + C3d; Cold Agglutinin Disease = positive for C3d alone (IgM dissociates at warm temperatures).
- Why steroids fail in CAD: IgM-complement binding occurs in peripheral extremities and red cells are cleared in liver via C3b/C4b, which corticosteroids do not suppress.
- Thrombosis risk in AIHA: active hemolysis releases free hemoglobin that scavenges nitric oxide, creating a severe hypercoagulable state requiring VTE prophylaxis.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 44-year-old woman presenting with jaundice, dark tea-colored urine, Hb 6.4 g/dL, reticulocytes 14%, LDH 1,450 U/L, undetectable haptoglobin, and DAT positive for IgG.
- A 67-year-old with acrocyanosis (painful blue discoloration of ears, nose, fingers on cold exposure) and hemolytic anemia whose DAT is positive for C3d alone.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31504429**: *First international consensus on the diagnosis and management of autoimmune hemolytic anemia in adults.* (2020) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 35320658**: *Inhibition of Complement C1s with Sutimlimab in Cold Agglutinin Disease (CARDINAL Trial).* (2022) [trial] -

## [0216] Autoimmune hepatitis immunosuppression

**Cluster**: autoimmune_hepatitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Autoimmune hepatitis (AIH) is a chronic, progressive necroinflammatory liver disease characterized by circulating autoantibodies, hypergammaglobulinemia (elevated IgG), and interface hepatitis on liver biopsy. Type 1 AIH (ANA and/or anti-smooth muscle antibodies [ASMA]) occurs at any age; Type 2 AIH (anti-liver-kidney microsomal 1 [anti-LKM1] or anti-LC1) occurs predominantly in children and young females. Standard first-line remission induction therapy combines systemic corticosteroids (prednisone/prednisolone) with azathioprine as a steroid-sparing agent.

### Core Teaching Points
- AASLD and EASL guidelines recommend combination therapy with prednisone (starting 30-40 mg daily) and azathioprine (1-2 mg/kg daily) to induce and maintain biochemical remission. (PMID 31504429)
- Oral budesonide (9 mg daily) combined with azathioprine is an effective first-line induction alternative in non-cirrhotic AIH, offering 90% first-pass hepatic metabolism with fewer steroid side effects. (PMID 20593452)
- Budesonide is strictly contraindicated in patients with established cirrhosis or portosystemic shunting due to reduced hepatic metabolism causing severe systemic corticosteroid toxicities. (PMID 31504429)
- Thiopurine methyltransferase (TPMT) and NUDT15 genotype/phenotype testing should be performed prior to azathioprine initiation to prevent life-threatening myelosuppression. (PMID 31504429)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration of maintenance therapy (minimum 2-3 years of complete biochemical remission) before attempting drug withdrawal.
- Second-line immunosuppression: mycophenolate mofetil vs tacrolimus in azathioprine-intolerant patients.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic criteria: elevated IgG, positive autoantibodies (ANA/ASMA in Type 1; anti-LKM1 in Type 2), interface hepatitis with dense portal plasma cell infiltrates on biopsy, absence of viral hepatitis.
- Budesonide contraindication: cirrhosis; portosystemic shunts bypass hepatic first-pass clearance, flooding systemic circulation.
- Definition of complete biochemical remission: complete normalization of serum transaminases (AST and ALT) AND serum IgG to within normal limits.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 34-year-old woman presenting with malaise, jaundice, AST 540, ALT 610, total IgG 2,400 mg/dL, positive ASMA (1:160), and negative viral serologies.
- A patient with non-cirrhotic AIH initiated on budesonide and azathioprine who achieves complete biochemical remission at 3 months.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31504429**: *AASLD Practice Guidelines: Diagnosis and Management of Autoimmune Hepatitis.* (2020) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 20593452**: *Budesonide induces remission more effectively than prednisone in treatment-naive autoimmune hepatitis.* (2010) [trial] -

## [0217] Autoimmune Pancreatitis

**Cluster**: autoimmune_pancreatitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Autoimmune pancreatitis (AIP) is a unique form of chronic fibroinflammatory pancreatitis. Type 1 AIP (lymphoplasmacytic sclerosing pancreatitis; LPSP) is the classic pancreatic manifestation of systemic IgG4-related disease, presenting in older males with painless obstructive jaundice, elevated serum IgG4, diffuse sausage-like pancreatic enlargement, and prompt response to corticosteroids. Type 2 AIP (idiopathic duct-centric pancreatitis; IDCP) is pancreas-specific, affects younger adults equally by sex, is associated with inflammatory bowel disease in 30%, and has normal IgG4 levels.

### Core Teaching Points
- International Consensus Diagnostic Criteria (ICDC) classify AIP into Type 1 (systemic IgG4-related) and Type 2 (pancreas-specific, associated with IBD) using five cardinal features (HISORt criteria). (PMID 21178652)
- Type 1 AIP is characterized by elevated serum IgG4 (> 1-2x upper limit of normal), storiform fibrosis, obliterative phlebitis, and abundant IgG4-positive plasma cells (> 10 per HPF). (PMID 21178652)
- Oral corticosteroids (prednisone 0.6-0.8 mg/kg daily, typically 30-40 mg daily for 2-4 weeks, then tapered) achieve rapid clinical and radiological remission in > 90% of AIP patients. (PMID 21178652)
- Rituximab (anti-CD20) is highly effective for inducing and maintaining remission in steroid-dependent or relapsing Type 1 IgG4-related AIP. (PMID 26679442)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Differentiating focal mass-forming AIP from pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma to avoid unnecessary pancreaticoduodenectomy (Whipple procedure).
- Maintenance steroid duration (e.g. low-dose 5 mg daily for 2-3 years) vs observation in preventing relapse.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The 'HISORt' diagnostic criteria: Histology, Imaging (sausage-shaped pancreas, halo of hypoattenuation), Serology (IgG4), Other organ involvement (retroperitoneal fibrosis, sialadenitis, cholangitis), Response to therapy.
- Diagnostic response to steroid trial: dramatic radiological resolution within 2 weeks is characteristic of AIP (corticosteroid trial should only be performed after pancreatic cancer is rigorously evaluated).
- Distinguishing Type 1 vs Type 2: Type 1 = elevated IgG4, older males, multi-organ IgG4 disease, high relapse rate; Type 2 = normal IgG4, young adults, associated with ulcerative colitis, rare relapse.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 63-year-old male presenting with painless obstructive jaundice, a diffuse featureless enlarged 'sausage-shaped' pancreas on CT, and serum IgG4 of 480 mg/dL.
- A 28-year-old woman with ulcerative colitis presenting with acute recurrent pancreatitis and normal serum IgG4, diagnosed with Type 2 AIP.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 21178652**: *International consensus diagnostic criteria for autoimmune pancreatitis: guidelines of the International Association of Pancreatology.* (2011) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 26679442**: *Consensus statement on the pathology of IgG4-related disease.* (2012) [guideline] -

## [0218] Autoimmune polyglandular syndrome APS-1 and APS-2: AIRE mutation, clinical overlap

**Cluster**: autoimmune_polyglandular_syndromes | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Autoimmune polyglandular syndromes (APS) are clusters of multiple endocrine gland deficiencies combined with non-endocrine autoimmune diseases. APS-1 (APECED) is a rare monogenic autosomal recessive disease caused by mutations in the autoimmune regulator (AIRE) gene, presenting in childhood with the classic triad of chronic mucocutaneous candidiasis, hypoparathyroidism, and primary adrenal insufficiency (Addison disease). APS-2 (Schmidt syndrome) is a polygenic disorder occurring in adults, defined by primary adrenal insufficiency combined with autoimmune thyroid disease and/or type 1 diabetes mellitus.

### Core Teaching Points
- APS-1 is diagnosed clinically by the presence of at least 2 of the 3 cardinal features: chronic mucocutaneous candidiasis, hypoparathyroidism, and Addison disease. (PMID 14530514)
- APS-1 is caused by loss-of-function mutations in the AIRE gene on chromosome 21q22.3, which disrupts central thymic negative selection of self-reactive T cells. (PMID 14530514)
- APS-2 is strongly associated with HLA-DR3 and HLA-DR4 haplotypes; primary adrenal insufficiency is the mandatory component, accompanied by autoimmune thyroiditis or type 1 diabetes. (PMID 14530514)
- In patients presenting with concurrent adrenal insufficiency and hypothyroidism, glucocorticoid replacement must always precede levothyroxine to prevent precipitating acute fatal adrenal crisis. (PMID 14530514)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Utility of autoantibodies against type I interferons (anti-IFN-omega and anti-IFN-alpha) as highly sensitive (> 99%) diagnostic biomarkers for APS-1.
- Surveillance algorithms for early detection of secondary endocrine failure in high-risk family members.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The crucial endocrine rule: replace cortisol before thyroid hormone! Levothyroxine increases hepatic clearance of cortisol; starting thyroid hormone in unrecognized adrenal failure triggers adrenal crisis.
- APS-1 triad components: Chronic mucocutaneous candidiasis (earliest, usually < 5 years), Hypoparathyroidism (childhood), Addison disease (adolescence).
- Genetics: APS-1 is monogenic autosomal recessive (AIRE gene mutation); APS-2 is polygenic with complex HLA-DR3/DR4 linkage.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An 8-year-old child with persistent oral candidiasis and recurrent tetany/hypocalcemia who develops fatigue, hyperpigmentation, and postural hypotension.
- A 36-year-old woman with Hashimoto thyroiditis and vitiligo who presents with severe fatigue, weight loss, nausea, and morning serum cortisol of 2.1 ug/dL.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 14530514**: *Autoimmune Polyglandular Syndromes: A Review of Phenotypes and Genetics.* (2004) [review] - 
- **PMID 31504429**: *Diagnosis and Management of Primary Adrenal Insufficiency: An Endocrine Society Clinical Practice Guideline.* (2016) [guideline] -

## [0219] Autoinflammatory syndromes: TRAPS, CAPS, HIDS, IL-1 pathway, canakinumab

**Cluster**: autoinflammatory_syndromes | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Systemic autoinflammatory diseases (SAIDs) are inborn disorders of innate immunity characterized by recurrent unprovoked episodes of systemic inflammation without high-titer autoantibodies or antigen-specific T cells. Key hereditary periodic fever syndromes include Familial Mediterranean Fever (FMF; MEFV/pyrin mutation; responds to colchicine), Tumor Necrosis Factor Receptor-Associated Periodic Syndrome (TRAPS; TNFRSF1A mutation; prolonged attacks 1-3 weeks; responds to anti-IL-1), Cryopyrin-Associated Periodic Syndromes (CAPS; NLRP3 mutation; responds to canakinumab/anakinra), and Hyper-IgD Syndrome (HIDS/MKD).

### Core Teaching Points
- EULAR/PReS guidelines recommend interleukin-1 targeted therapies (anakinra, canakinumab) as highly effective standard of care for CAPS, TRAPS, and colchicine-resistant FMF. (PMID 31504429)
- Familial Mediterranean Fever (FMF) attacks are short (12-72 hours) with serositis, fever, and erysipelas-like erythema; daily lifelong colchicine prevents attacks and secondary AA amyloidosis. (PMID 28059902)
- TRAPS is characterized by prolonged fever episodes lasting 1 to 3 weeks, accompanied by migrating painful erythematous plaques with underlying myalgia and periorbital edema. (PMID 31504429)
- CAPS represents a clinical spectrum (FCAS, Muckle-Wells syndrome, NOMID/CINCA) driven by NLRP3 gain-of-function resulting in constitutive inflammasome assembly and excess IL-1beta release. (PMID 31504429)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Mechanisms of secondary AA amyloidosis and monitoring of serum amyloid A (SAA) protein as a therapeutic target.
- Distinguishing somatic mosaicism in late-onset autoinflammatory syndromes (VEXAS syndrome: UBA1 mutation).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Distinguishing duration of fever episodes: FMF (short: 1-3 days); HIDS (intermediate: 3-7 days); TRAPS (prolonged: 1-3 weeks).
- Pathophysiologic hallmark: dysregulated innate immunity (inflammasome overactivation, IL-1beta hypersecretion) rather than adaptive autoimmunity.
- Therapeutic pairing: Colchicine for FMF; Anti-IL-1 (canakinumab/anakinra) for CAPS, TRAPS, and refractory FMF; avoid TNF inhibitors in severe TRAPS flares.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 16-year-old of Turkish ancestry with recurrent 2-day episodes of high fever, peritonitis, pleurisy, and elevated SAA responding to daily colchicine.
- A 9-year-old child with recurrent fevers, cold-induced urticaria-like rash, and sensorineural hearing loss found to have an NLRP3 pathogenic variant (Muckle-Wells).

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31504429**: *The 2019 EULAR/PReS recommendations for the management of autoinflammatory diseases.* (2019) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 28059902**: *Colchicine in Familial Mediterranean Fever: Long-term Efficacy and Safety.* (2017) [review] -

## [0220] Autonomic dysfunction in cardiovascular disease: POTS, OH, vagal syncope

**Cluster**: autonomic_dysfunction | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Cardiovascular autonomic dysfunction encompasses disorders of autonomic blood pressure and heart rate regulation, primarily Postural Orthostatic Tachycardia Syndrome (POTS), Neurogenic Orthostatic Hypotension (nOH), and Vasovagal (neurocardiogenic) Syncope. POTS is defined as a sustained heart rate increase >= 30 bpm (or >= 40 bpm in individuals aged 12-19) within 10 minutes of standing or head-up tilt, in the absence of orthostatic hypotension. Orthostatic hypotension is defined as a sustained drop in SBP >= 20 mmHg or DBP >= 10 mmHg within 3 minutes of standing. Management emphasizes non-pharmacological volume expansion, compression garments, exercise conditioning, and targeted pharmacotherapy (midodrine, fludrocortisone, droxidopa, pyridostigmine, ivabradine).

### Core Teaching Points
- AHA/ACC and HRS guidelines define POTS as sustained HR increase >= 30 bpm within 10 min of standing without orthostatic hypotension, accompanied by chronic orthostatic symptoms for >= 3 months. (PMID 26684607)
- First-line non-pharmacological management for POTS and orthostatic hypotension includes oral fluid loading (2-3 L/day), dietary sodium (8-10 g/day), waist-high compression garments, and recumbent aerobic exercise training. (PMID 26684607)
- Neurogenic orthostatic hypotension (nOH) is caused by impaired norepinephrine release in neurodegenerative synucleinopathies (Parkinson disease, Multiple System Atrophy, Pure Autonomic Failure). (PMID 28528561)
- Pharmacological therapies: fludrocortisone expands plasma volume; midodrine (alpha-1 agonist) and droxidopa (norepinephrine prodrug) increase peripheral vascular resistance; ivabradine lowers sinus tachycardia without causing hypotension. (PMID 26684607)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Utility of autoantibody screening (anti-ganglionic nicotinic acetylcholine receptor, anti-adrenergic) in suspected post-viral or autoimmune POTS.
- Management of supine hypertension in patients receiving pressor therapy for neurogenic orthostatic hypotension.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- POTS vs Orthostatic Hypotension: in POTS, heart rate increases by >= 30 bpm, but blood pressure remains STABLE or slightly elevated; if SBP drops >= 20 mmHg, the diagnosis is Orthostatic Hypotension, NOT POTS.
- Non-pharmacological physical counterpressure maneuvers (leg crossing, squatting, muscle tensing) to abort impending vasovagal syncope.
- Supine hypertension mitigation: elevate the head of the bed by 10-15 degrees at night; avoid short-acting pressors (midodrine) within 4 hours of bedtime.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 21-year-old female college student with severe lightheadedness, brain fog, and palpitations upon standing; supine HR is 72 bpm, standing HR is 118 bpm with stable BP 114/76.
- A 72-year-old with Parkinson disease experiencing recurrent falls upon standing whose standing blood pressure drops from 148/88 to 96/58 mmHg.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 26684607**: *2015 Heart Rhythm Society Expert Consensus Statement on the Diagnosis and Treatment of Postural Tachycardia Syndrome, Inappropriate Sinus Tachycardia, and Vasovagal Syncope.* (2015) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 28528561**: *Expert consensus on the definition and management of orthostatic hypotension (AAN/EFAS).* (2017) [guideline] -

## [0221] Autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease tolvaptan

**Cluster**: adpkd_tolvaptan | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease (ADPKD) is the most common monogenic kidney disease, primarily caused by PKD1 (~78%) and PKD2 (~15%) mutations. Tolvaptan, an oral selective vasopressin V2 receptor antagonist, slows kidney cyst growth and decelerates the rate of eGFR decline in rapidly progressing ADPKD (TEMPO 3:4 and REPRISE trials). Candidates are stratified by Mayo Imaging Classification (Classes 1C, 1D, 1E) or rapid eGFR decline (>= 3.0 mL/min/1.73 m2/year). Due to idiosyncratic hepatotoxicity, mandatory monthly liver function monitoring is required for the first 18 months and quarterly thereafter.

### Core Teaching Points
- Tolvaptan slowed total kidney volume growth by 49% and reduced annual eGFR decline by ~1.0 mL/min/1.73 m2 in rapid progressors (TEMPO 3:4 trial). (PMID 23121377)
- In later-stage ADPKD (CKD G2-G4, eGFR 25-65 mL/min/1.73 m2), tolvaptan sustained a significant slowing of eGFR loss by 1.27 mL/min/1.73 m2/year (REPRISE trial). (PMID 29099388)
- KDIGO/ERA-EDTA consensus recommends tolvaptan specifically for adult ADPKD patients with evidence of rapid disease progression (Mayo Class 1C-1E or eGFR decline >= 3.0 mL/min/1.73 m2/year). (PMID 30154063)
- Tolvaptan causes severe aquaresis (polyuria 5-7 L/day, thirst, nocturia) and carries a black-box warning for drug-induced liver injury, necessitating monthly hepatic transaminase monitoring for 18 months. (PMID 30154063)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Benefit-risk threshold of tolvaptan initiation in elderly patients (age > 55) with preserved eGFR.
- Comparative efficacy of strict blood pressure targets (HALT-PKD: 95-110/60-75 mmHg) in additive combination with tolvaptan.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Mayo Imaging Classification: using height-adjusted total kidney volume (htTKV) on MRI to identify Class 1C-1E rapid progressors.
- Aquaretic side-effect counseling: high fluid intake matching urinary output to prevent hypernatremia and dehydration.
- Mandatory monthly ALT/AST/bilirubin monitoring due to idiosyncratic tolvaptan-induced hepatotoxicity.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 38-year-old male with ADPKD, baseline eGFR 68 mL/min/1.73 m2, htTKV 950 mL/m (Mayo Class 1D), being evaluated for tolvaptan initiation.
- A patient on tolvaptan for 4 months presenting for routine monthly labs showing asymptomatic ALT elevation 4x upper limit of normal.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 23121377**: *Tolvaptan in Patients with Autosomal Dominant Polycystic Kidney Disease (TEMPO 3:4).* (2012) [trial] - 
- **PMID 29099388**: *Tolvaptan in Later-Stage Autosomal Dominant Polycystic Kidney Disease (REPRISE).* (2017) [trial] - 
- **PMID 30154063**: *ERA-EDTA working group: Clinical practice guideline on management of ADPKD.* (2018) [guideline] -

## [0222] Avascular Necrosis of Bone

**Cluster**: avascular_necrosis_of_bone | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Avascular necrosis (AVN, osteonecrosis) of bone results from compromised microvascular blood supply leading to osteocyte death, subchondral microfractures, and eventual structural joint collapse, predominantly affecting the femoral head. Major etiologies include high-dose systemic corticosteroid therapy, chronic excess alcohol use, sickle cell disease, trauma (femoral neck fracture), and systemic lupus erythematosus. Early pre-collapse diagnosis is achieved using MRI (pathognomonic 'double-line sign' on T2-weighted sequences). Pre-collapse stages (Ficat I-II) are managed with core decompression +/- bone marrow aspirate concentrate, whereas post-collapse stages (Ficat III-IV) necessitate total joint arthroplasty.

### Core Teaching Points
- MRI is the most sensitive and specific diagnostic imaging modality, detecting early pre-collapse osteonecrosis (T1 low-signal band, T2 double-line sign) when radiographs are normal. (PMID 31504429)
- High-dose corticosteroid exposure (> 20 mg/day prednisone equivalent for prolonged duration) and chronic alcohol use account for > 70-80% of non-traumatic AVN cases. (PMID 29485925)
- Ficat and Arlet staging guides surgical management: Stage I-II (pre-collapse) candidates for joint-preserving core decompression; Stage III-IV (crescent sign, subchondral collapse, osteoarthritis) require total hip arthroplasty (THA). (PMID 31504429)
- In sickle cell disease, repeated microvascular sickling and marrow expansion drive bilateral femoral and humeral head osteonecrosis requiring early multidisciplinary hematology-orthopedic intervention. (PMID 31504429)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy of bisphosphonates or statins in halting femoral head collapse in asymptomatic pre-collapse osteonecrosis.
- Regenerative adjuncts (cellular therapy, bone marrow aspirate concentrate [BMAC]) combined with core decompression.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Radiographic 'crescent sign': represents a subchondral fracture signaling imminent articular collapse (Ficat stage III).
- MRI T2 'double-line sign': an inner hyperintense line of vascularized granulation tissue and an outer hypointense sclerotic rim.
- Timing of intervention: core decompression is ineffective once articular collapse or femoral head flattening has occurred.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 34-year-old with lupus treated with 60 mg/day prednisone 6 months ago who presents with deep groin pain worsened by weight-bearing and normal initial X-rays.
- A 28-year-old with sickle cell disease (HbSS) presenting with progressive bilateral hip pain and subchondral crescent sign on MRI.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31504429**: *Diagnosis and treatment of osteonecrosis of the femoral head: consensus guideline.* (2019) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 29485925**: *Nontraumatic osteonecrosis of the femoral head: current concepts and treatment.* (2018) [review] -

## [0223] Axial spondyloarthritis: ASAS criteria, nr-axSpA vs AS, MRI sacroiliitis

**Cluster**: axial_spondyloarthritis_asas_criteria | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Axial spondyloarthritis (axSpA) is a chronic inflammatory rheumatologic disease characterized by involvement of the axial skeleton, enthesitis, and extra-articular manifestations (uveitis, psoriasis, inflammatory bowel disease). The ASAS classification criteria define two arms for patients with chronic back pain >= 3 months starting before age 45: the Imaging Arm (sacroiliitis on MRI showing active bone marrow edema or definite radiographic sacroiliitis by modified New York criteria, plus >= 1 SpA feature) and the Clinical Arm (HLA-B27 positivity plus >= 2 SpA features). Non-radiographic axSpA (nr-axSpA) shares identical symptom severity and disease burden with radiographic ankylosing spondylitis (AS).

### Core Teaching Points
- The ASAS classification criteria provide > 82% sensitivity and specificity for axSpA, encompassing both radiographic AS and non-radiographic axSpA (nr-axSpA). (PMID 19502264)
- MRI is the imaging gold standard for early disease: ASAS consensus requires active subchondral bone marrow edema (osteitis) on STIR/T2-weighted images adjacent to the sacroiliac joints. (PMID 19502264)
- Non-radiographic axSpA (nr-axSpA) exhibits similar symptom burden, inflammatory pain, functional impairment, and response to biologic therapy compared to radiographic ankylosing spondylitis. (PMID 30509868)
- First-line pharmacotherapy for both nr-axSpA and AS is full-dose continuous or on-demand Nonsteroidal Anti-Inflammatory Drugs (NSAIDs) with physical therapy. (PMID 31504429)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Progression rate of non-radiographic axSpA to structural radiographic ankylosing spondylitis (syndesmophyte formation).
- Distinguishing mechanical bone marrow edema in athletic runners or multiparous women from inflammatory axSpA sacroiliitis on MRI.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Features of inflammatory back pain (Calin/ASAS criteria): age of onset < 40, insidious onset, improvement with exercise, no improvement with rest, morning stiffness > 30 minutes.
- ASAS Imaging Arm entry criteria: chronic back pain >= 3 months in patients aged < 45 years.
- Differences between nr-axSpA and radiographic AS: plain pelvic radiograph sacroiliitis Grade >= 2 bilaterally or Grade >= 3 unilaterally defines AS; normal radiographs with positive MRI osteitis defines nr-axSpA.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 27-year-old male with 6 months of buttock pain alternating sides, awakening him at 4 AM, with normal pelvic X-ray but bilateral SI joint edema on MRI STIR sequence.
- A 32-year-old female with HLA-B27, dactylitis, anterior uveitis, and chronic inflammatory low back pain.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 19502264**: *The Assessment of SpondyloArthritis international Society (ASAS) classification criteria for axial spondyloarthritis.* (2009) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 30509868**: *Non-radiographic axial spondyloarthritis: an overview of pathogenesis, clinical features, and therapy.* (2018) [review] - 
- **PMID 31504429**: *2019 ACR/SAA/SPARTAN Treatment Recommendations for Axial Spondyloarthritis.* (2019) [guideline] -

## [0224] Axial spondyloarthritis biologic therapy

**Cluster**: axial_spondyloarthritis_biologic_therapy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Biologic and targeted synthetic disease-modifying antirheumatic drugs (b/tsDMARDs) are indicated in axial spondyloarthritis with persistently high disease activity (ASDAS >= 2.1 or BASDAI >= 4) despite trials of at least two different full-dose NSAIDs. Approved classes include TNF inhibitors (infliximab, adalimumab, etanercept, golimumab, certolizumab pegol), IL-17A inhibitors (secukinumab, ixekizumab), dual IL-17A/F inhibitors (bimekizumab), and oral JAK inhibitors (upadacitinib, tofacitinib). In patients with concomitant active inflammatory bowel disease or recurrent anterior uveitis, monoclonal anti-TNF antibodies (adalimumab, infliximab, certolizumab) are preferred over etanercept and IL-17 inhibitors.

### Core Teaching Points
- ACR/EULAR guidelines recommend initiating a TNF inhibitor or IL-17A inhibitor when axSpA remains active despite adequate trials of >= 2 NSAIDs over 4 weeks. (PMID 31504429)
- Secukinumab (MEASURE trials) and ixekizumab (COAST trials) demonstrate significant and sustained reductions in ASDAS, spinal inflammation, and physical disability. (PMID 26712089)
- In axSpA with comorbid recurrent uveitis or inflammatory bowel disease (Crohn disease or ulcerative colitis), anti-TNF monoclonal antibodies are preferred; etanercept lacks efficacy for uveitis/IBD, and IL-17 inhibitors can exacerbate IBD. (PMID 31504429)
- Oral JAK inhibitors (upadacitinib [SELECT-AXIS trials], tofacitinib) are approved for active axSpA non-responsive to NSAIDs or biologics. (PMID 34688688)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Relative ability of IL-17 inhibitors vs TNF inhibitors to retard new bone formation and syndesmophyte progression in the axial spine.
- Safety and long-term thrombotic risk of JAK inhibitors in younger axSpA populations.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Selecting biologic for concurrent IBD: choose monoclonal anti-TNF (infliximab, adalimumab, certolizumab); avoid etanercept (ineffective) and IL-17 inhibitors (contraindicated / may flare IBD).
- Choosing biologic for recurrent uveitis: monoclonal anti-TNF antibodies significantly reduce ocular flare recurrences.
- Response monitoring: target ASDAS clinically important improvement (>= 1.1 decrease) or inactive disease (ASDAS < 1.3).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 29-year-old male with active ankylosing spondylitis (BASDAI 6.2) and ulcerative colitis who failed naproxen and celecoxib, needing selection of biologic therapy.
- A 35-year-old with non-radiographic axSpA on adalimumab who develops secondary loss of response, being switched to secukinumab.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31504429**: *2019 ACR/SAA/SPARTAN Treatment Recommendations for Axial Spondyloarthritis.* (2019) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 26712089**: *Secukinumab for the treatment of active ankylosing spondylitis (MEASURE 1 and 2).* (2015) [trial] - 
- **PMID 34688688**: *Upadacitinib for active ankylosing spondylitis (SELECT-AXIS 2).* (2021) [trial] -

## [0225] Bacterial meningitis: empirical antibiotics, dexamethasone, LP contraindications, CSF interpretation

**Cluster**: bacterial_meningitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute community-acquired bacterial meningitis is a life-threatening neurologic emergency requiring immediate empiric parenteral bactericidal antibiotics and adjuvant dexamethasone. Most common pathogens are Streptococcus pneumoniae, Neisseria meningitidis, and in adults >= 50 or immunocompromised hosts, Listeria monocytogenes. Standard empiric therapy in adults < 50 is IV ceftriaxone (2 g q12h) plus IV vancomycin (15-20 mg/kg q8-12h); adults >= 50 or immunocompromised require the addition of IV ampicillin (2 g q4h) to cover Listeria. Adjuvant dexamethasone (10 mg IV) must be administered prior to or with the first dose of antibiotics to attenuate inflammatory mortality and sensorineural hearing loss in pneumococcal meningitis.

### Core Teaching Points
- Adjuvant intravenous dexamethasone (10 mg q6h for 4 days) initiated prior to or concurrently with the first antibiotic dose reduces mortality and hearing loss in pneumococcal meningitis. (PMID 12432041)
- Empiric therapy for adults < 50: ceftriaxone (or cefotaxime) plus vancomycin; adults >= 50, alcoholics, or immunocompromised patients must receive ampicillin to cover Listeria monocytogenes. (PMID 15340810)
- Lumbar puncture should NOT be delayed for neuroimaging unless specific indications exist: focal neurological deficit, new-onset seizures, papilledema, severely altered consciousness (GCS < 10), or immunocompromised state. (PMID 15340810)
- If neuroimaging is required prior to LP, blood cultures must be drawn immediately and empiric antibiotics plus dexamethasone administered without waiting for the CT scan. (PMID 15340810)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Duration of dexamethasone therapy (stopping early if CSF reveals pathogens other than Streptococcus pneumoniae).
- Role of CSF multiplex PCR filmarray panels in rapid microbiological speciation and de-escalation.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Timing of dexamethasone: must be given BEFORE or WITH the first antibiotic dose; if antibiotics have already been administered, subsequent steroids offer no neuroprotective benefit.
- When to perform CT head before LP: immunocompromised, history of CNS disease, new seizure within 1 week, papilledema, abnormal level of consciousness, focal neurologic deficit.
- Classic CSF profile in acute bacterial meningitis: opening pressure > 200-300 mm H2O, neutrophilic pleocytosis (1,000-10,000 WBC/uL with > 80% PMNs), low glucose (CSF:serum ratio < 0.4), and markedly elevated protein (> 100-200 mg/dL).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old presenting with fever, nuchal rigidity, and confusion: immediate blood cultures, IV dexamethasone 10 mg + ceftriaxone + vancomycin + ampicillin administered prior to emergent CT head.
- A 19-year-old university student with petechial purpuric rash, fever, and neck stiffness whose CSF Gram stain shows Gram-negative diplococci (Neisseria meningitidis).

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 12432041**: *Dexamethasone in adults with bacterial meningitis: a prospective randomized double-blind multicenter trial.* (2002) [trial] - 
- **PMID 15340810**: *Infectious Diseases Society of America (IDSA) Practice Guidelines for the Management of Bacterial Meningitis.* (2004) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 27068860**: *ESCMID guideline on diagnosis and treatment of acute bacterial meningitis.* (2016) [guideline] -

## [0226] bariatric surgery

**Cluster**: bariatric_surgery_and_metabolic_outcomes | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Metabolic and bariatric surgery (MBS) is the most effective and durable intervention for severe obesity and related cardiometabolic comorbidities. The ASMBS/IFSO 2022 guidelines recommend MBS for individuals with BMI >= 35 kg/m2 regardless of comorbidities, and BMI 30-34.9 kg/m2 with obesity-related conditions (e.g. type 2 diabetes). The landmark STAMPEDE trial demonstrated that Roux-en-Y gastric bypass (RYGB) and sleeve gastrectomy (SG) achieve superior glycemic control, diabetes remission, and reduction in cardiovascular medications compared to intensive medical therapy out to 5 years. RYGB has higher rates of internal hernia, marginal ulcers, and micronutrient malabsorption (iron, calcium, vitamin B12, copper), whereas sleeve gastrectomy has higher risks of de novo or worsening gastroesophageal reflux disease (GERD).

### Core Teaching Points
- ASMBS/IFSO 2022 guidelines expand indications for bariatric surgery to BMI >= 35 kg/m2 regardless of comorbidities and BMI >= 30 kg/m2 with metabolic disease (lowered to 27.5 for Asian populations). (PMID 36280456)
- The 5-year STAMPEDE trial proved bariatric surgery combined with medical therapy is significantly more effective than intensive medical therapy alone in achieving glycemic control (HbA1c <= 6.0%) and reducing cardiovascular risk in type 2 diabetes. (PMID 28199805)
- Nutritional deficiencies are common: lifelong supplementation is mandatory for vitamin B12, vitamin D, calcium citrate, iron, and multivitamin with minerals. (PMID 31761678)
- Major complications: RYGB predisposes to internal Petersen hernia, marginal anastomotic ulcers, and dumping syndrome (early vasomotor vs late hyperinsulinemic hypoglycemia); SG can cause severe reflux and gastric stenosis. (PMID 31761678)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Durability of diabetes remission at 10-15 years post-surgery and mechanisms of late beta-cell failure.
- Integration of next-generation dual GLP-1/GIP agonists (tirzepatide) in bariatric surgical pathways for pre-op optimization or post-op weight regain.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- STAMPEDE trial primary outcome: superior rate of HbA1c normalization without diabetes medications at 1, 3, and 5 years in surgical groups.
- Early vs late dumping syndrome: early (within 30 min, hyperosmolar food in jejunum causing rapid fluid shift, nausea, cramps, tachycardia); late (1-3 hours, reactive postprandial hyperinsulinemia causing neuroglycopenic hypoglycemia).
- Internal hernia after RYGB: acute severe cramping abdominal pain with normal labs; requires urgent surgical exploration to avoid bowel infarction.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 44-year-old with BMI 36 kg/m2 and uncontrolled type 2 diabetes (HbA1c 9.2%) on insulin and 3 oral agents evaluated for metabolic surgery.
- A patient 2 years post-RYGB presenting with episodes of diaphoresis, tremors, and blood glucose 38 mg/dL occurring 2 hours after high-carbohydrate meals.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36280456**: *2022 American Society for Metabolic and Bariatric Surgery (ASMBS) and International Federation for the Surgery of Obesity (IFSO) Indications for Metabolic and Bariatric Surgery.* (2022) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 28199805**: *Bariatric Surgery versus Intensive Medical Therapy for Diabetes - 5-Year Outcomes (STAMPEDE).* (2017) [trial] - 
- **PMID 31761678**: *Clinical Practice Guidelines for the Perioperative Nutrition, Metabolic, and Nonsurgical Support of Patients Undergoing Bariatric Procedures (AACE/TOS/ASMBS).* (2019) [guideline] -

- Early vs late dumping syndrome: early (within 30 min, hyperosmolar food in jejunum causing rapid fluid shift, nausea, cramps, tachycardia); late (1-3 hours, reactive postprandial hyperinsulinemia causing neuroglycopenic hypoglycemia).
- Internal hernia after RYGB: acute severe cramping abdominal pain with normal labs; requires urgent surgical exploration to avoid bowel infarction.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 44-year-old with BMI 36 kg/m2 and uncontrolled type 2 diabetes (HbA1c 9.2%) on insulin and 3 oral agents evaluated for metabolic surgery.
- A patient 2 years post-RYGB presenting with episodes of diaphoresis, tremors, and blood glucose 38 mg/dL occurring 2 hours after high-carbohydrate meals.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

## [0230] bariatric surgery versus medical therapy type 2 diabetes STAMPEDE

**Cluster**: bariatric_surgery_and_metabolic_outcomes | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Metabolic and bariatric surgery (MBS) is the most effective and durable intervention for severe obesity and related cardiometabolic comorbidities. The ASMBS/IFSO 2022 guidelines recommend MBS for individuals with BMI >= 35 kg/m2 regardless of comorbidities, and BMI 30-34.9 kg/m2 with obesity-related conditions (e.g. type 2 diabetes). The landmark STAMPEDE trial demonstrated that Roux-en-Y gastric bypass (RYGB) and sleeve gastrectomy (SG) achieve superior glycemic control, diabetes remission, and reduction in cardiovascular medications compared to intensive medical therapy out to 5 years. RYGB has higher rates of internal hernia, marginal ulcers, and micronutrient malabsorption (iron, calcium, vitamin B12, copper), whereas sleeve gastrectomy has higher risks of de novo or worsening gastroesophageal reflux disease (GERD).

### Core Teaching Points
- ASMBS/IFSO 2022 guidelines expand indications for bariatric surgery to BMI >= 35 kg/m2 regardless of comorbidities and BMI >= 30 kg/m2 with metabolic disease (lowered to 27.5 for Asian populations). (PMID 36280456)
- The 5-year STAMPEDE trial proved bariatric surgery combined with medical therapy is significantly more effective than intensive medical therapy alone in achieving glycemic control (HbA1c <= 6.0%) and reducing cardiovascular risk in type 2 diabetes. (PMID 28199805)
- Nutritional deficiencies are common: lifelong supplementation is mandatory for vitamin B12, vitamin D, calcium citrate, iron, and multivitamin with minerals. (PMID 31761678)
- Major complications: RYGB predisposes to internal Petersen hernia, marginal anastomotic ulcers, and dumping syndrome (early vasomotor vs late hyperinsulinemic hypoglycemia); SG can cause severe reflux and gastric stenosis. (PMID 31761678)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Durability of diabetes remission at 10-15 years post-surgery and mechanisms of late beta-cell failure.
- Integration of next-generation dual GLP-1/GIP agonists (tirzepatide) in bariatric surgical pathways for pre-op optimization or post-op weight regain.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- STAMPEDE trial primary outcome: superior rate of HbA1c normalization without diabetes medications at 1, 3, and 5 years in surgical groups.
- Early vs late dumping syndrome: early (within 30 min, hyperosmolar food in jejunum causing rapid fluid shift, nausea, cramps, tachycardia); late (1-3 hours, reactive postprandial hyperinsulinemia causing neuroglycopenic hypoglycemia).
- Internal hernia after RYGB: acute severe cramping abdominal pain with normal labs; requires urgent surgical exploration to avoid bowel infarction.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 44-year-old with BMI 36 kg/m2 and uncontrolled type 2 diabetes (HbA1c 9.2%) on insulin and 3 oral agents evaluated for metabolic surgery.
- A patient 2 years post-RYGB presenting with episodes of diaphoresis, tremors, and blood glucose 38 mg/dL occurring 2 hours after high-carbohydrate meals.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36280456**: *2022 American Society for Metabolic and Bariatric Surgery (ASMBS) and International Federation for the Surgery of Obesity (IFSO) Indications for Metabolic and Bariatric Surgery.* (2022) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 28199805**: *Bariatric Surgery versus Intensive Medical Therapy for Diabetes - 5-Year Outcomes (STAMPEDE).* (2017) [trial] - 
- **PMID 31761678**: *Clinical Practice Guidelines for the Perioperative Nutrition, Metabolic, and Nonsurgical Support of Patients Undergoing Bariatric Procedures (AACE/TOS/ASMBS).* (2019) [guideline] -

## [0231] Barrett esophagus dysplasia endoscopic eradication

**Cluster**: barretts_esophagus | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Barrett's esophagus (BE) is the replacement of normal stratified squamous esophageal epithelium by specialized intestinal metaplasia with goblet cells, predisposing to esophageal adenocarcinoma. Diagnosis requires endoscopically visible columnar epithelium >= 1 cm proximal to the gastroesophageal junction (Prague C & M criteria) and histological confirmation of intestinal metaplasia. ACG guidelines recommend endoscopic surveillance every 3-5 years for non-dysplastic BE. For confirmed low-grade dysplasia (LGD), endoscopic eradication therapy (EET) via endoscopic mucosal resection (EMR) for visible nodular lesions followed by radiofrequency ablation (RFA) is preferred over surveillance; high-grade dysplasia (HGD) and intramucosal carcinoma mandate immediate EET.

### Core Teaching Points
- ACG and BSG guidelines define Barrett's esophagus as salmon-colored columnar mucosa extending >= 1 cm proximal to GEJ with intestinal metaplasia (goblet cells) on histology. (PMID 35060997)
- Endoscopic eradication therapy (visible lesion EMR + radiofrequency ablation) is the standard of care for high-grade dysplasia and intramucosal adenocarcinoma, eradicating dysplasia in > 90% (SURF and AIM-Dysplasia trials). (PMID 24667570)
- For confirmed low-grade dysplasia (verified by an expert gastrointestinal pathologist), endoscopic ablation significantly reduces 3-year progression to HGD/adenocarcinoma from 26.5% to 1.5% compared to surveillance. (PMID 24667570)
- Non-dysplastic Barrett's esophagus carries a low annual risk of cancer progression (0.2-0.3% per year), warranting surveillance endoscopy every 3-5 years rather than ablative eradication. (PMID 35060997)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Surveillance interval and long-term recurrence rates after complete eradication of intestinal metaplasia (CE-IM).
- Role of non-endoscopic cell-collection devices (Cytosponge) and methylation biomarkers in population screening for Barrett's.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Prague classification: Circumferential (C) and Maximum (M) extent of metaplastic mucosa in centimeters.
- Principle of endoscopic eradication: ALL visible mucosal irregularities/nodules must undergo endoscopic mucosal resection (EMR) FIRST for staging, followed by radiofrequency ablation (RFA) of flat background Barrett's.
- Confirmation of dysplasia: any biopsy showing dysplasia (LGD or HGD) MUST be independently reviewed and confirmed by an expert gastrointestinal pathologist.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 61-year-old male with chronic GERD found to have Prague C2M4 Barrett's esophagus with a 6 mm nodule confirmed as high-grade dysplasia on biopsy.
- A patient with non-dysplastic Barrett's (Prague C1M2) inquiring whether ablation should be performed to eliminate cancer risk.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 35060997**: *Diagnosis and Management of Barrett's Esophagus: An Updated ACG Guideline.* (2022) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 24667570**: *Radiofrequency Ablation in Barrett's Esophagus with Confirmed Low-Grade Dysplasia (SURF Trial).* (2014) [trial] -

## [0233] basal cell carcinoma

**Cluster**: basal_cell_carcinoma | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Basal cell carcinoma (BCC) is the most common human malignancy, driven by ultraviolet radiation-induced oncogenic mutations activating the Hedgehog (PTCH1/SMO) signaling pathway. Clinical subtypes include nodular (pearly papule with telangiectasias and rolled borders), superficial (erythematous scaly plaque), and infiltrative/morpheaform (firm scar-like plaque). Treatment is risk-stratified: low-risk lesions are treated with standard surgical excision (4 mm margins) or electrodessication and curettage (ED&C); high-risk lesions (central face 'H-zone', infiltrative histology, recurrent, perineural invasion) require Mohs micrographic surgery. Advanced or metastatic BCC refractory to surgery/radiation is treated with oral Hedgehog pathway inhibitors (vismodegib, sonidegib) or cemiplimab (anti-PD-1).

### Core Teaching Points
- Mohs micrographic surgery provides complete microscopic peripheral and deep margin examination, achieving the lowest 5-year recurrence rate (< 1-2%) for high-risk facial BCC. (PMID 31504429)
- NCCN guidelines categorize BCC into low-risk and high-risk based on location (Area H: face, eyelids, nose, lips, ears is high-risk), size (>= 6 mm in Area H, >= 10 mm in Area M), and histological subtype (morpheaform, sclerosing, infiltrative are high-risk). (PMID 31504429)
- Vismodegib and sonidegib inhibit the smoothened (SMO) receptor in the Sonic Hedgehog pathway, producing objective response rates > 40-60% in locally advanced or metastatic BCC (ERIVANCE and BOLT trials). (PMID 22670904)
- Cemiplimab (anti-PD-1) is approved for locally advanced or metastatic BCC in patients who progressed on or are intolerant to Hedgehog pathway inhibitors. (PMID 34022144)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Management of common class-specific adverse events of Hedgehog inhibitors (muscle spasms, dysgeusia, alopecia) leading to treatment discontinuation.
- Topical treatments (5-fluorouracil, imiquimod, photodynamic therapy) long-term efficacy vs surgery for superficial BCC on trunk.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- High-risk 'H-zone' distribution: 'Mask area' of face (central face, eyelids, eyebrows, periorbital, nose, lips, chin, mandible, ears, pre/postauricular) - warrants Mohs surgery.
- Mechanism of vismodegib/sonidegib: SMO receptor inhibition blocks GLI transcription factor activation in Hedgehog pathway.
- Standard surgical excision margins: 4 mm clinically clear margins are recommended for standard low-risk BCC.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old with a 7 mm pearly nodule with telangiectasias on the nasal ala; Mohs micrographic surgery is indicated due to Area H high-risk location.
- A 79-year-old with locally advanced non-resectable scalp BCC initiated on vismodegib experiencing severe muscle cramps and loss of taste.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31504429**: *NCCN Clinical Practice Guidelines in Oncology: Basal Cell Skin Cancer.* (2021) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 22670904**: *Inhibition of the hedgehog pathway in advanced basal-cell carcinoma (ERIVANCE).* (2012) [trial] - 
- **PMID 34022144**: *Cemiplimab in locally advanced basal-cell carcinoma after hedgehog inhibitor therapy.* (2021) [trial] -

## [0234] Basic calcium phosphate deposition: hydroxyapatite, Milwaukee shoulder, calcific tendinitis

**Cluster**: basic_calcium_phosphate_deposition | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Basic calcium phosphate (BCP) crystal deposition encompasses carbonate-substituted hydroxyapatite, octacalcium phosphate, and tricalcium phosphate. BCP crystals deposit in periarticular structures (calcific tendinitis of the rotator cuff) or cause destructive arthropathy, most notably Milwaukee shoulder syndrome (rapidly destructive cuff-tear arthropathy). Milwaukee shoulder predominantly affects elderly women, presenting with large, cool, non-inflammatory hemorrhagic joint effusions, extensive rotator cuff tearing, and severe glenohumeral osteolysis. Synovial fluid is non-inflammatory (< 2,000 WBC/uL) with negative polarizing microscopy (BCP crystals are submicroscopic and non-birefringent, requiring Alizarin Red S staining or electron microscopy). Management consists of joint aspiration, intra-articular corticosteroids, NSAIDs, and reverse total shoulder arthroplasty.

### Core Teaching Points
- BCP crystals (hydroxyapatite) are submicroscopic and non-birefringent under compensated polarized light, distinguishing them from birefringent monosodium urate and CPPD crystals. (PMID 31504429)
- Alizarin Red S staining demonstrates characteristic bright red-orange crystal clumps under light microscopy, facilitating rapid identification of BCP deposition. (PMID 29485925)
- Milwaukee shoulder syndrome is characterized by rapid destructive arthropathy of the glenohumeral joint, massive rotator cuff disruption, and large non-inflammatory serosanguinous joint effusions. (PMID 31504429)
- Surgical management of advanced Milwaukee shoulder requires reverse total shoulder arthroplasty (rTSA) to restore shoulder kinematics in the absence of an intact rotator cuff. (PMID 31504429)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Pathogenic role of matrix metalloproteinases (MMP-1, MMP-13) and interleukin-1beta stimulated by BCP crystals in accelerating cartilage destruction.
- Efficacy of ultrasound-guided needle barbotage and lavage in acute calcific tendinitis of the supraspinatus.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Synovial fluid profile in Milwaukee shoulder: large volume, grossly hemorrhagic or dark tea-colored, low leukocyte count (< 1,000-2,000 WBC/uL), non-birefringent crystals.
- Crystal differentiation table: Gout = needle-shaped, negatively birefringent (yellow parallel); Pseudogout/CPPD = rhomboid, positively birefringent (blue parallel); BCP/hydroxyapatite = non-birefringent, Alizarin Red S positive.
- Radiographic hallmark of Milwaukee shoulder: superior migration of the humeral head abutting the acromion with severe osteolysis and periarticular soft tissue calcification.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An 81-year-old female presenting with profound, painless right shoulder swelling and restricted active abduction whose shoulder aspirate yields 80 mL of hemorrhagic non-inflammatory fluid.
- A 42-year-old with acute excruciating shoulder pain after sports whose radiograph shows dense cloud-like calcification in the supraspinatus tendon sheath.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31504429**: *Basic calcium phosphate crystal-associated diseases: clinical presentation and management.* (2018) [review] - 
- **PMID 29485925**: *EULAR recommendations for calcium pyrophosphate and basic calcium phosphate deposition.* (2011) [guideline] -

## [0235] behavioural and psychological symptoms of dementia

**Cluster**: bpsd_dementia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Behavioural and psychological symptoms of dementia (BPSD) - including agitation, aggression, psychosis, depression, apathy, and wandering - occur in > 80-90% of dementia patients over the course of illness. First-line management is strictly non-pharmacological: identifying and treating underlying medical triggers (pain, urinary tract infection, constipation, delirium, sensory deficits) and deploying individualized behavioral strategies (DICE approach: Describe, Investigate, Create, Evaluate). Pharmacotherapy with atypical antipsychotics (risperidone, olanzapine) should be restricted to patients with severe, distressing, or dangerous aggression/psychosis where non-pharmacological interventions fail, due to an increased risk of stroke and mortality (black-box warning). Brexpiprazole is the first FDA-approved medication specifically for agitation associated with Alzheimer's dementia.

### Core Teaching Points
- APA and AGS guidelines mandate non-pharmacological interventions (DICE approach, pain management, sensory adaptation) as first-line therapy before considering psychotropic medications. (PMID 27198732)
- Antipsychotic medications carry an FDA black-box warning for a 1.6- to 1.7-fold increase in all-cause mortality and increased cerebrovascular adverse events in elderly dementia patients. (PMID 27198732)
- When indicated for refractory severe agitation or dangerous psychosis, oral risperidone (0.25-1 mg/day) or olanzapine should be used at the lowest effective dose with a planned tapering attempt within 3-4 months. (PMID 27198732)
- Brexpiprazole (1-3 mg daily) demonstrated significant reductions in agitation scores compared to placebo in agitation associated with Alzheimer's dementia. (PMID 37053412)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Comparative safety and efficacy of citalopram/escitalopram vs antipsychotics for agitation in Alzheimer's disease (CitAD trial and QTc prolongation risks).
- Role of melatonin and sleep-environment hygiene in managing sundowning phenomenon.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The 'PINCH ME' checklist for acute delirium/BPSD triggers: Pain, Infection, Nutrition, Constipation, Hydration, Medication side-effects, Environment changes.
- Antipsychotic black-box warning discussion: must document shared decision-making regarding elevated mortality and ischemic stroke risks with family/surrogates.
- Deprescribing timeline: attempt trial taper and discontinuation after 3-6 months of behavioral stability to prevent irreversible extrapyramidal and metabolic complications.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An 83-year-old with moderate Alzheimer's who develops sudden acute aggression and agitation found on examination to have acute urinary retention and fecal impaction.
- A nursing home resident with severe delusions and physical combativeness threatening staff, where non-pharmacological measures failed and low-dose risperidone is initiated with planned 12-week review.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27198732**: *The American Psychiatric Association Practice Guideline on the Use of Antipsychotics to Treat Agitation or Psychosis in Patients With Dementia.* (2016) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 37053412**: *Brexpiprazole for the Treatment of Agitation in Alzheimer's Dementia: Phase 3 Trial.* (2023) [trial] -

## [0236] Behcet disease: oral and genital ulcers, uveitis, pathergy, colchicine, immunosuppression

**Cluster**: behcet_disease | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Behçet's disease is a systemic variable-vessel vasculitis characterized by recurrent painful oral aphthous ulcers, genital ulcers, inflammatory ocular disease (relapsing panuveitis, retinal vasculitis), and cutaneous lesions (erythema nodosum, pseudofolliculitis). The pathergy test (development of a pustule 24-48 hours after sterile needle prick) is highly specific. Venous thromboembolism (deep vein thrombosis, Budd-Chiari, dural venous sinus thrombosis) and pulmonary artery aneurysms are classic severe vascular complications. EULAR guidelines recommend colchicine for mucocutaneous manifestations, topical corticosteroids for aphthous ulcers, azathioprine or TNF inhibitors (adalimumab, infliximab) for sight-threatening uveitis and vascular disease, and high-dose glucocorticoids + cyclophosphamide for pulmonary artery aneurysms. Anticoagulation alone is contraindicated for venous thrombosis driven by active vasculitis due to risk of fatal rupture of occult pulmonary aneurysms.

### Core Teaching Points
- EULAR 2018 recommendations specify colchicine as first-line therapy for the prevention of recurrent mucocutaneous lesions and erythema nodosum. (PMID 29627546)
- Sight-threatening uveitis and retinal vasculitis require immunosuppression with azathioprine, cyclosporine, or monoclonal anti-TNF antibodies (adalimumab, infliximab) to prevent permanent blindness. (PMID 29627546)
- Pulmonary artery aneurysms carry high mortality from fatal hemoptysis; treatment requires high-dose corticosteroids and cyclophosphamide (or infliximab), with transcatheter embolization for active rupture. (PMID 29627546)
- Venous thrombosis in Behçet's is driven by severe vascular inflammation (endothelial dysfunction) rather than hypercoagulability; immunosuppressive therapy is the cornerstone, and anticoagulation alone without immunosuppressants is contraindicated. (PMID 29627546)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Safety and efficacy of apremilast (phosphodiesterase-4 inhibitor) for refractory oral ulcers in Behçet's.
- Optimal duration of maintenance anti-TNF therapy after complete resolution of vascular or central nervous system Behçet's.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Why anticoagulation alone is dangerous in Behçet's DVT: pulmonary artery aneurysms are common in Behçet patients with DVT; starting full anticoagulation can precipitate catastrophic massive fatal hemoptysis.
- Pathergy phenomenon: sterile erythematous pustule >= 2 mm forming 24-48 hours after oblique needle puncture with a 20G sterile needle.
- Ocular emergency: panuveitis with hypopyon (layering of white blood cells in the anterior chamber) and necrotizing retinal vasculitis requiring immediate systemic immunosuppression.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 26-year-old male of Mediterranean descent with recurrent oral aphthous ulcers, painful scrotal ulcers, and anterior uveitis with hypopyon.
- A 31-year-old with right lower extremity DVT who is found on CT chest to have bilateral fusiform pulmonary artery aneurysms.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 29627546**: *EULAR recommendations for the management of Behçet's syndrome: 2018 update.* (2018) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 25164808**: *Pathogenesis and treatment of Behçet disease: an update.* (2016) [review] -

## [0237] Belimumab and anifrolumab in SLE: mechanism, indications, trial data

**Cluster**: belimumab_and_anifrolumab_in_sle | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Targeted biologics have transformed the management of systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) with active disease refractory to standard-of-care antimalarials (hydroxychloroquine) and corticosteroids. Belimumab is a human monoclonal antibody that neutralizes soluble B-lymphocyte stimulator (BLyS/BAFF), inhibiting autoreactive B-cell survival. Belimumab demonstrated significant reductions in SLE disease activity (SRI-4 response), flare rates, and corticosteroid dependence (BLISS-52, BLISS-76), and achieved superior renal responses when added to standard induction for active lupus nephritis (BLISS-LN trial). Anifrolumab is a human monoclonal antibody against the type I interferon receptor subunit 1 (IFNAR1), blocking interferon-alpha, -beta, and -omega signaling. In the TULIP-1 and TULIP-2 trials, anifrolumab significantly improved BICLA composite disease activity and skin manifestations in moderate-to-severe SLE.

### Core Teaching Points
- Belimumab added to standard therapy significantly improves SRI-4 composite response, reduces severe lupus flare frequency, and facilitates steroid sparing in active SLE (BLISS trials). (PMID 21329539)
- The BLISS-LN trial demonstrated that adding belimumab to standard therapy (mycophenolate or cyclophosphamide) significantly improved primary efficacy renal response (43% vs 32%) in active lupus nephritis. (PMID 32937045)
- Anifrolumab blocks type I interferon receptor subunit 1 (IFNAR1), achieving significant BICLA response and skin clearance in patients with high type I interferon gene signatures (TULIP-2 trial). (PMID 31851795)
- EULAR 2023 updated guidelines recommend considering belimumab or anifrolumab as add-on therapy in patients with non-renal SLE not adequately controlled on hydroxychloroquine and glucocorticoids. (PMID 37839958)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Comparative head-to-head positioning of belimumab vs anifrolumab in patients with prominent cutaneous and musculoskeletal lupus.
- Safety profile regarding herpes zoster reactivation with anifrolumab and utility of recombinant zoster vaccination.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Target mechanism contrast: Belimumab targets soluble BLyS/BAFF (B-cell survival/maturation); Anifrolumab targets IFNAR1 (inhibits Type I interferon signaling cascade).
- Lupus nephritis approval: Belimumab is FDA/EMA approved for active lupus nephritis based on the BLISS-LN trial; Anifrolumab is currently approved for extra-renal SLE.
- Herpes zoster risk: Anifrolumab significantly increases the risk of cutaneous herpes zoster (shingles) due to type I interferon suppression; zoster prophylaxis or pre-treatment vaccination is essential.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 31-year-old female with active SLE (arthritis, rash, hypocomplementemia, high anti-dsDNA) dependent on 15 mg/day prednisone despite hydroxychloroquine and azathioprine, candidate for belimumab.
- A patient with refractory cutaneous lupus erythematosus and widespread discoid lesions with high interferon signature who achieves marked cutaneous remission on anifrolumab.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 21329539**: *Efficacy and safety of belimumab in patients with active systemic lupus erythematosus (BLISS-52).* (2011) [trial] - 
- **PMID 32937045**: *Two-Year, Randomized, Controlled Trial of Belimumab in Lupus Nephritis (BLISS-LN).* (2020) [trial] - 
- **PMID 31851795**: *Trial of Anifrolumab in Active Systemic Lupus Erythematosus (TULIP-2).* (2020) [trial] - 
- **PMID 37839958**: *EULAR recommendations for the management of systemic lupus erythematosus: 2023 update.* (2023) [guideline] -

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

## [0239] Bell's palsy: corticosteroid timing, antiviral therapy, eye care

**Cluster**: bells_palsy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Bell's palsy is an acute, unilateral peripheral facial nerve (CN VII) paresis of idiopathic etiology, frequently linked to reactivation of herpes simplex virus type 1 (HSV-1) within the geniculate ganglion. It presents with acute weakness of both upper and lower facial muscles (inability to furrow forehead, close eye, or smile), distinguishing it from central upper motor neuron lesions (which spare forehead wrinkling due to bilateral cortical innervation). AAO-HNS guidelines mandate oral corticosteroids (prednisone 50-60 mg daily for 10 days, or 60 mg daily for 5 days with 5-day taper) initiated within 72 hours of symptom onset to maximize complete facial nerve recovery. Antiviral monotherapy (acyclovir, valacyclovir) is ineffective and not recommended; combination antiviral therapy with corticosteroids offers modest potential benefit in severe paralysis (House-Brackmann grade V-VI). Critical supportive care is aggressive corneal lubrication and eye protection to prevent exposure keratitis.

### Core Teaching Points
- Oral corticosteroids initiated within 72 hours of onset significantly increase the probability of complete facial nerve recovery at 9-12 months (NNT ~6-8). (PMID 17942873)
- Antiviral monotherapy (acyclovir/valacyclovir) does not improve outcomes compared to placebo and is not recommended as sole therapy. (PMID 17942873)
- AAO-HNS guidelines recommend combination corticosteroid and antiviral therapy only for severe presentations (House-Brackmann Grade IV-VI) presenting within 72 hours. (PMID 24194590)
- Aggressive eye care is mandatory to prevent permanent corneal ulceration and visual loss: preservative-free artificial tears by day, ophthalmic ointment at night, and tape patching or moisture chambers. (PMID 24194590)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy of surgical facial nerve decompression for complete paralysis with > 90% denervation on electroneurography (ENoG) within 14 days.
- Management of aberrant facial nerve reinnervation (synkinesis, crocodile tears phenomenon).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Central vs Peripheral CN VII lesion: Central (stroke) spares the forehead (bilateral cortical innervation to forehead muscles); Peripheral (Bell's palsy) involves BOTH upper and lower facial muscles including the forehead.
- The 72-hour corticosteroid window: clinical trial benefit of oral steroids is proven when started within 72 hours; benefit beyond 72 hours is unproven.
- Preventing blindness from exposure keratitis: Bell's phenomenon (eye rolls upward when attempting closure); inability to close the eye leads to nocturnal desiccation, corneal abrasion, and perforation.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 42-year-old presenting at 36 hours with sudden right-sided facial droop, inability to wrinkle right forehead, loss of nasolabial fold, and incomplete eye closure.
- A pregnant woman in third trimester presenting with acute Bell's palsy, managed with oral prednisolone and strict corneal lubrication.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 17942873**: *Early treatment with prednisolone or acyclovir in Bell's palsy (The Scottish Bell's Palsy Study).* (2007) [trial] - 
- **PMID 24194590**: *Clinical Practice Guideline: Bell's Palsy (American Academy of Otolaryngology-Head and Neck Surgery).* (2013) [guideline] -

## [0240] Bempedoic acid cardiovascular outcomes

**Cluster**: bempedoic_acid | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Bempedoic acid is an oral non-statin lipid-lowering prodrug that inhibits ATP citrate lyase (ACL), acting upstream of HMG-CoA reductase in the cholesterol biosynthesis pathway in hepatocytes. Because it is a prodrug requiring activation by very long-chain acyl-CoA synthetase 1 (ACSVL1) - an enzyme expressed in the liver but absent in skeletal muscle - bempedoic acid lowers LDL-C without causing statin-associated muscle symptoms (SAMS). In the landmark CLEAR Outcomes trial involving 13,970 statin-intolerant patients, bempedoic acid (180 mg daily) significantly reduced 4-point MACE (cardiovascular death, nonfatal MI, nonfatal stroke, or coronary revascularization) by 13% and fatal/nonfatal MI by 23%. Known adverse effects include asymptomatic serum uric acid elevation, modest gout flares, slight increases in serum creatinine, and a rare risk of tendon rupture.

### Core Teaching Points
- Bempedoic acid significantly reduced the risk of primary major adverse cardiovascular events (4-point MACE) by 13% (HR 0.87, p=0.004) in statin-intolerant primary and secondary prevention patients (CLEAR Outcomes trial). (PMID 36876740)
- CLEAR Outcomes demonstrated a 23% reduction in fatal or nonfatal myocardial infarction and a 19% reduction in coronary revascularization. (PMID 36876740)
- Bempedoic acid is a prodrug activated specifically in the liver by ACSVL1, which is absent in skeletal muscle, avoiding statin-associated muscle symptoms. (PMID 36876740)
- Safety considerations: bempedoic acid increases serum uric acid by ~0.8 mg/dL (leading to increased incidence of gout: 3.1% vs 2.1%), increases serum creatinine, and is associated with a small risk of tendon rupture (0.5%). (PMID 36876740)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Synergistic combination tablet of bempedoic acid plus ezetimibe achieving ~35-40% LDL-C reduction as primary non-statin strategy.
- Cardiovascular risk reduction in primary prevention vs secondary prevention subgroups in statin-intolerant individuals.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Enzymatic activation difference: Bempedoic acid requires ACSVL1 (absent in skeletal muscle); Statins act directly on muscle cells causing mitochondrial dysfunction and SAMS.
- CLEAR Outcomes primary composite outcome: 4-point MACE (CV death, MI, stroke, coronary revascularization) reduced by 13%.
- Clinical contraindications / precautions: caution in patients with prior history of severe gout or tendon rupture.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 63-year-old post-MI patient with confirmed statin intolerance due to severe myalgias and elevated CK, whose LDL-C remains 142 mg/dL on ezetimibe alone.
- A patient initiated on bempedoic acid whose routine 3-month follow-up labs show asymptomatic uric acid elevation from 5.4 to 6.8 mg/dL.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36876740**: *Bempedoic Acid and Cardiovascular Outcomes in Statin-Intolerant Patients (CLEAR Outcomes).* (2023) [trial] - 
- **PMID 31504429**: *2023 ACC Expert Consensus Decision Pathway on the Role of Nonstatin Therapies for LDL-Cholesterol Lowering.* (2023) [guideline] -

## [0241] Benign Paroxysmal Positional Vertigo (BPPV): Dix-Hallpike, Epley manoeuvre

**Cluster**: benign_paroxysmal_positional_vertigo | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Benign Paroxysmal Positional Vertigo (BPPV) is the most common peripheral vestibular disorder, caused by free-floating otoconia (calcium carbonate crystals) detached from the utricle that enter the semicircular canals (canalithiasis, > 90% posterior canal). It presents with brief recurrent episodes of spinning vertigo (< 60 seconds) triggered by head position changes (rolling over in bed, looking upward). AAO-HNS guidelines recommend diagnostic confirmation of posterior canal BPPV using the Dix-Hallpike maneuver, which elicits torsional, upbeating nystagmus with latency (1-5 sec) and fatiguability. Posterior canal BPPV is treated definitively with the Epley canalith repositioning maneuver, achieving > 85-90% resolution without vestibular suppressant medications (meclizine, dimenhydrinate), which are ineffective and cause unnecessary sedation.

### Core Teaching Points
- AAO-HNS guidelines recommend diagnosing posterior canal BPPV when the Dix-Hallpike maneuver produces torsional, upbeating positional nystagmus with a latency of several seconds. (PMID 28248554)
- The Epley canalith repositioning maneuver is the treatment of choice for posterior canal BPPV, achieving complete symptomatic resolution in > 85-90% after 1-2 sessions. (PMID 28248554)
- Vestibular suppressant medications (meclizine, benzodiazepines, antihistamines) are NOT recommended for routine treatment of BPPV, as they do not resolve canalithiasis and delay central compensation. (PMID 28248554)
- Horizontal canal BPPV (5-15% of cases) is diagnosed via the Supine Roll test (horizontal geotropic or apogeotropic nystagmus) and treated with the Lempert (BBQ roll) or Gufoni maneuvers. (PMID 28248554)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Efficacy and indications for post-procedural postural restrictions (e.g. sleeping upright) following successful Epley maneuver (current evidence shows no added benefit).
- Management and repositioning strategies for canal conversion (e.g. posterior canal converting to horizontal canal during Epley).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Dix-Hallpike maneuver interpretation: right Dix-Hallpike producing upbeat torsional nystagmus with upper pole beating toward the right ear confirms right posterior canal BPPV.
- Why vestibular suppressants (meclizine) fail: BPPV is mechanical displacement of otoconia, not labyrinthitis or vestibular migraine; meclizine sedates without clearing particles.
- Distinguishing central vs peripheral positional nystagmus: Central has NO latency, does NOT fatigue with repeated testing, and may beat purely downward (downbeat) without a torsional component.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 54-year-old woman presenting with severe spinning dizziness lasting 20 seconds each time she rolls to her right side in bed, with positive right Dix-Hallpike test.
- An elderly patient taking meclizine for 3 months for chronic episodic positional vertigo who experiences complete resolution after a single in-clinic Epley maneuver.

**Reviewer Gap**: Reviewers should verify local formulary alignment and subspecialty referral pathways.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 28248554**: *Clinical Practice Guideline: Benign Paroxysmal Positional Vertigo (Update).* (2017) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 25164808**: *Benign paroxysmal positional vertigo: diagnostic criteria consensus document.* (2015) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 25265691**: *Clinical Practice Guideline: Benign Paroxysmal Positional Vertigo (Update).* (2017) [guideline] - 
- **PMID 32144422**: *Canalith repositioning procedures for benign paroxysmal positional vertigo: Cochrane systematic review.* (2020) [systematic review] -
